The stochastic six vertex model and discrete Orthogonal Polynomial Ensembles
Promit Ghosal, Guilherme L. F. Silva

Abstract
. Stochastic growth models in the Kardar–Parisi–Zhang (KPZ) universality class exhibit remarkable fluctuation phenomena. While a variety of powerful methods have led to a detailed understanding of their typical fluctuations or large deviations, much less is known about behavior on intermediate, or moderate deviation, scales. Addressing this problem requires refined asymptotic control of the integrable structures underlying KPZ models. Motivated by this perspective, we study multiplicative statistics of discrete orthogonal polynomial ensembles (dOPEs) in different scaling regimes, with a particular focus on applications to tail probabilities of the height function in the stochastic six-vertex model. For a large class of dOPEs, we obtain robust singular asymptotic estimates for multiplicative statistics critically scaled near a saturated-to-band transition. These asymptotics exhibit universal crossover behavior, interpolating between Airy, Painlevé XXXIV, and Bessel-type regimes. Our proofs employ the Riemann-Hilbert Problem (RHP) approach to obtain asymptotics for the correlation kernel of a deformed version of the dOPE across the critical scaling windows. These asymptotics are then used on a double integral formula relating this kernel to partition function ratios, which may be of independent interest. At the technical level, the RHP analysis requires a novel parameter-dependent local parametrix, which needs a separate asymptotic analysis of its own. Using these results, together with a known identity relating a Laplace-type transform of the stochastic six-vertex model height function to a multiplicative statistic of the Meixner point process, we derive moderate deviation estimates for the height function in both the upper and lower tail regimes, with sharp exponents and constants.



1.
Introduction

The six-vertex model is one of the most ubiquitous lattice models in modern mathematical physics, being a cornerstone in the study of two-dimensional lattice models and their phase transitions. From the algebraic structures that arise from the Yang-Baxter equations, the unraveling of rich phase transitions, to its deep connections to random point processes, it displays an overarching range of connections to several areas of mathematics and physics.

Given a graph with vertices in the square lattice
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
, a configuration of the six-vertex model in this graph consists of an assignment of orientation to each of the edges, in such a way that the number of arrows pointing inwards equals the number of arrows pointing outwards of each vertex.

This means that each vertex is assigned one choice among six possible incident edges’ configurations, as shown in Figure
1
.

Figure 1
.
On the left, the six possible configurations of individual vertices in the six-vertex model in its classical formulation. On the right, the corresponding configurations of vertices used in the representation as a random path configuration.

To each one of the 6 possible vertex configurations, one assigns a (positive) weight, and the total weight of a configuration is the product of the weights in each vertex of the configuration. The probability of a configuration is then proportional to the total weight of the configuration. This way, one may view the distribution on the configurations as a probability that depends on 6 initial parameters, the weights of the model. Obviously, depending on the boundary conditions prescribed, one may reduce this number of parameters.

In our case, we are interested in the
stochastic
six-vertex model on the quadrant
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and step initial condition, which may be seen as a random growth model. This version of the model was introduced by Gwa and Spohn in 1992. Rather than viewing it as a model in equilibrium statistical mechanics, it is instructive to describe it as a Markovian evolution of non-intersecting lines, obtained when we replace arrows pointing west and south by empty (or rather dashed) edges, and arrows pointing north or east by solid edges, we refer the reader again to Figure
1
for the display of all possible configurations at a given vertex. In this version of the model, the vertices are stochastic, meaning that for fixed inputs at a given vertex, the sum of weights over all possible outputs is 1, with each weight being non-negative. By adding arrows to the solid lines, we may view a given configuration of this model as a set of directed up-right random paths, see Figure
2
.

Figure 2
.
A configuration of the stochastic six-vertex model in the random path representation.

The step initial condition is then obtained by setting that paths emerge from every vertex in the vertical axis, and that there are no paths entering from the horizontal axis. Configurations with this initial condition, as well as its stochastic evolution, are depicted in Figure
4
.

Given a down-right diagonal path on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and specified boundary conditions along the path, the S6V model generates a measure on the vertices located above and to the right of the path, see Figure
4
below for more details. Equivalently, it produces a measure on the configuration of solid lines that extend from the boundary inputs and continue upwards and rightwards. The measure is defined recursively: starting with vertices where inputs are provided, the outputs are randomly selected from all possible configurations, with probabilities determined by the associated vertex weights. In this framework, the weights of the six-vertex model may be all described by two independent parameters, which are usually parametrized by
\mathsf{q}\in(0,1)
and
u>0
. More details will be given in Section
2.1
below.

The height function
\mathfrak{h}(x,y)
for the S6V model is defined as the net number of particles that have crossed the time-space line between
(0,0)
and
(x,y)
, where each left-to-right move contributes
+1
and each right-to-left move contributes
-1
. For a more detailed description of the S6V model, as well as the construction of
\mathfrak{h}(x,y)
for bi-infinite configurations, we refer to Section
2.1
below.

The graph of the height function
\mathfrak{h}(x,y)
may be viewed as a random interface that encodes all statistics of the model. Naturally, one is asked about its
typical behavior
, that is, about a Law of Large Numbers for it, and also about the
fluctuations
around this typical behavior. In the seminal work
[
19
]
, Borodin, Corwin and Gorin answer both of these questions. Their Law of Large Numbers reads
[
19
, Theorem 1.1]

\lim_{N\to+\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(Nx,Ny)}{N}=\mathcal{H}(x,y),\quad\text{in probability},

for an explicit limiting function
\mathcal{H}(x,y)
. This deterministic function
\mathcal{H}(x,y)
is curved in a conic region with vertex at the origin, and it is flat outside this region. The curved region is referred to as the
liquid
region, and the flat regions are termed the
frozen
regions. A simulation of the S6V model is displayed in Figure
3
, where these regions become apparent.

Figure 3
.
A simulation of the stochastic six-vertex model with narrow wedge initial condition, in a grid of size
200\times 200
, performed with the software Mathematica. Dark (blue) lines correspond to the paths in the model. The region filled with paths in the upper left corner, as well as the empty region in the lower right corner, correspond to the frozen regions. In these regions, the behavior of paths is determined with probability exponentially close to
1
. In the roughly conic region between the frozen ones we see a true stochastic behavior of paths. This region corresponds to the liquid region.

Within the solid region, the fluctuations of
\mathfrak{h}
are expected to be exponentially small. On the other hand, for
(x,y)
within the liquid region the fluctuations are nontrivial, and given by the celebrated
Tracy-Widom distribution

F_{\rm GUE}

[
19
, Theorem 1.2]
,

(1.1)

\lim_{N\to+\infty}{\mathbb{P}}\left(-\frac{\mathfrak{h}(Nx,Ny)-N\mathcal{H}(x,y)}{\sigma N^{1/3}}\leq\mathsf{h}\right)=F_{\rm GUE}(\mathsf{h}).

In the convergence above,
\sigma=\sigma(x,y)
is an explicit scaling constant.

The original appearance of the Tracy-Widom distribution in the literature goes back to the 1990s
[
51
,
77
]
, where it was found that it describes the limiting fluctuations for the largest eigenvalue of Hermitian random matrices with independent Gaussian entries, the so-called
Gaussian unitary ensemble
of random matrices, in the limit of large matrix size. As shown by Tracy and Widom
[
77
]
, this distribution can be computed through the Painlevé II equation: it admits the representation

F_{\rm GUE}(s)=\exp\left(-\int_{s}^{\infty}(u-s)^{2}q(u)^{2}\mathrm{d}u\right),

where
q
is the Hastings-McLeod solution to the Painlevé II equation,

q^{\prime\prime}(s)=sq(s)+2q(s)^{3},\quad\text{with boundary behavior }q(s)\sim\ai(s)\text{ as }s\to+\infty,

\ai(\cdot)
being the Airy function. Alternatively, it is given by the Fredholm determinant

F_{\rm GUE}(s)=\det\left(\mathsf{I}-\mathsf{A}\right)_{L^{2}(s,\infty)},

where
\mathsf{A}:L^{2}(s,\infty)\to(s,\infty)
is the integral operator with integral kernel given by the
Airy kernel

\mathsf{A}
,

(1.2)

\mathsf{A}(x,y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\ai(x)\ai^{\prime}(y)-\ai(y)\ai^{\prime}(x)}{x-y},\quad x\neq y,\qquad\mathsf{A}(x,x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(\ai^{\prime}(x))^{2}-\ai(x)\ai^{\prime\prime}(x).

The scaling exponents
N^{1}
and
N^{1/3}
, as well as the appearance of the limit
F_{\rm GUE}
are not an accident, and instead are manifestations of the fact that the stochastic six-vertex model belongs to the
Kardar-Parisi-Zhang
(KPZ) universality class.

In this paper, the main question we want to address is: how far away from its fluctuation scale can the height function still feel the KPZ universality statistics?

Our answer will be given through the study of tail probabilities for the height function
\mathsf{h}
. Our first main result for the stochastic six-vertex model deals with the upper tail distribution of the height function.

Theorem A
.

Let
\mathfrak{h}(x,y)
be the height function for the stochastic six-vertex model evaluated at a point
(x,y)
in the liquid region. For any
\varepsilon,\delta>0
sufficiently small, there exists a (large) constant
\mathsf{h}_{0}>0
for which

\exp\left(-(1+\varepsilon)\frac{4}{3}\mathsf{h}^{3/2}\right)\leq{\mathbb{P}}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{h}(Nx,Ny)-N\mathcal{H}(x,y)}{\sigma N^{1/3}}\leq-\mathsf{h}\right)\leq\exp\left(-(1-\varepsilon)\frac{4}{3}\mathsf{h}^{3/2}\right),

for every
\mathsf{h}
with
\mathsf{h}_{0}\leq\mathsf{h}\leq N^{\tfrac{1}{6}-\delta}
.

Our second main result for the stochastic six-vertex model deals with the lower tail distribution of the height function.

Theorem B
.

Let
\mathfrak{h}(x,y)
be the height function for the stochastic six-vertex model evaluated at a point
(x,y)
in the liquid region. For any
\varepsilon,\delta>0
sufficiently small, there exists a (large) constant
\mathsf{h}_{0}>0
for which

\exp\left(-(1+\varepsilon)\frac{1}{12}\mathsf{h}^{3}\right)\leq{\mathbb{P}}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{h}(Nx,Ny)-N\mathcal{H}(x,y)}{\sigma N^{1/3}}\geq\mathsf{h}\right)\leq\exp\left(-(1-\varepsilon)\frac{1}{12}\mathsf{h}^{3}\right),

for every
\mathsf{h}
with
\mathsf{h}_{0}\leq\mathsf{h}\leq N^{\tfrac{1}{6}-\delta}
.

Our methods also extend the tail bounds in Theorems
A
and
B
to the full range
\mathsf{h}_{0}\leq\mathsf{h}\leq\mathsf{h}^{-1}_{0}N^{1/6}
with the same power laws
\mathsf{h}^{3}
and
\mathsf{h}^{3/2}
, at the cost of changing the universal constants
4/3
and
1/12
when we go beyond the regime
\mathsf{h}\leq N^{1/6-\delta}
. See Remark
2.3
for a detailed discussion.

Technically speaking, Theorem
A
describes deviations in the direction of the lower tail of the random variable
\mathfrak{h}(\nu N,N)
, whereas Theorem
B
describes the upper tail of it. The swapped terminology as used here is common in KPZ-type models, and refers more to the lower/upper tail directions of the limiting Tracy-Widom distribution, as we discuss next. We opt to follow this common terminology throughout our paper.

A more precise version of Theorems
A
and
B
that includes more explicit expressions for the scaling parameters is given in Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
below.

The Tracy-Widom distribution has tail behavior
[
77
]

(1.3)

\displaystyle 1-F_{\rm GUE}(\mathsf{h})=\exp\left({-\frac{2}{3}\mathsf{h}^{3/2}(1+\mathit{o}(1))}\right),\quad\mathsf{h}\to+\infty,\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle F_{\rm GUE}(-\mathsf{h})=\exp\left({-\frac{1}{12}\mathsf{h}^{3}(1+\mathit{o}(1))}\right),\quad\mathsf{h}\to+\infty.

More refined versions of these estimates are obtained in
[
6
,
42
]
.

Thus, Theorems
A
and
B
complement the CLT convergence (
1.1
) drastically, in the sense that
F_{\rm GUE}
provides a good approximation to the distribution of
\mathsf{h}
not only in the fluctuation limit, but also for the tail probabilities when
|\mathsf{h}|
is large, and deeper down all the way to the moderate deviation regime when
|\mathsf{h}|=\mathcal{O}(N^{1/6})
. In other words, the KPZ universality limit
F_{\rm GUE}
still governs the leading behavior of the height function down to the moderate deviations scale.

The investigation of tail probabilities for models in the Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) universality class has attracted considerable attention, revealing that the Tracy-Widom distributions governs not only fluctuations at the typical scale but also provide accurate asymptotic descriptions deep into the moderate deviations regime. For zero-temperature models such as random matrix ensembles and last passage percolation, upper tail inequalities for GUE were established with optimal constants by Paquette and Zeitouni
[
73
]
, following earlier work by Johansson
[
61
]
using sub-additivity and large deviation arguments. Similar optimal bounds for GOE and LOE were obtained in
[
49
,
10
]
. For general Dyson index
\beta
, Ramírez, Rider and Virág
[
75
]
proved convergence to the stochastic Airy operator spectrum, with sharper upper tail estimates later obtained by Dumaz-Virág
[
48
]
. Ledoux and Rider
[
69
]
established tail bounds with correct exponents
3/2
(upper tail) and
3
(lower tail), though without optimal constants
2\beta/3
and
\beta/24
. The left tail bounds, as always, require more sophisticated tools. For geometric last passage percolation they were proven by Baik, Deift, McLaughlin, Miller and Zhou
[
7
]
, and for longest increasing subsequences (related to Poissonian last passage percolation) by Löwe and Merkl
[
67
]
as well as Löwe, Merkl and Rolles
[
66
]
. A lower bound for the lower tail of the general
\beta
Laguerre ensembles was obtained by Basu, Ganguly, Hegde and Krishnapur
[
11
]
and optimal bounds (with the appropriate exponents and the constants) for both the tails by Baslingker, Basu, Bhattacharjee and Krishnapur
[
9
]
.

Beyond zero-temperature models, tail estimates for positive-temperature models in the KPZ universality class have been pursued in several recent works, notably for the KPZ equation itself
[
34
,
32
,
52
,
35
]
. For last passage percolation, the correct tail exponents
3/2
and
3
(without optimal constants) were conditionally obtained via bootstrapping and geodesic geometry methods by Ganguly and Hegde
[
53
]
. However, prior to our work, no rigorous proof of tight optimal tail asymptotics extending into the moderate deviations regime had been established for any positive-temperature model except the KPZ equation. Our Theorems
A
and
B
provide the first such result for the stochastic six-vertex model, confirming that the Tracy-Widom universality persists down to the moderate deviations scale
|\mathsf{h}|=\mathcal{O}(N^{1/6})
and that thermal fluctuations do not alter the leading-order tail behavior given in (
1.3
).

Our starting mathematical point in this paper is a relation between the six-vertex model and the so-called
Meixner ensemble
. The latter is an interacting particle system
\mathcal{X}
of
n
particles on the lattice of strictly positive integers
1
1

1

In
[
22
]
, the Meixner ensemble has state space
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, but for us it is more convenient to shift their lattice and work with the Meixner ensemble in
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
instead.

\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, whose joint distribution of points may be described by Meixner orthogonal polynomials, and it depends on two parameters
\alpha>0
and
\beta\in(0,1)
. Recall that
\mathsf{q}\in(0,1)
and
u>0
are the parameters of the stochastic six-vertex model. Borodin and Olshanski
[
22
, Corollary 8.5]
showed the identity of observables

(1.4)

\mathbb{E}_{\rm S6V}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{1+\zeta\mathsf{q}^{\mathfrak{h}(x,y)+j}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\rm Meixner}\left[\prod_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\setminus\mathcal{X}}\frac{1}{1+\zeta\mathsf{q}^{\lambda-1}}\right],\quad\text{for every }\zeta\in\mathbb{C},

with correspondence of parameters

(1.5)

n=x,\quad\alpha=y-x\quad\text{and}\quad\beta=\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1}.

The variable
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}
may be seen as a spectral variable, and both expectations in (
1.4
) may be interpreted as generating functions in this spectral variable. As we will prove in Lemmas
3.2
and
3.3
, if we choose
\zeta=\mathsf{q}^{-N\mathcal{H}(x,y)-\sigma N^{1/3}\mathsf{h}}
, then the approximation

(1.6)

{\mathbb{P}}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{h}(Nx,Ny)-N\mathcal{H}(x,y)}{\sigma N^{1/3}}\geq\mathsf{h}\right)\approx\mathbb{E}_{\rm S6V}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{1+\zeta\mathsf{q}^{\mathfrak{h}(Nx,Ny)+j}}\right]

is valid thanks to soft probabilistic arguments. As a consequence, we learn from (
1.4
) that proving Theorems
B
and
A
boils down to obtaining asymptotics for the multiplicative statistic of the Meixner ensemble in the right-hand side of (
1.4
). In fact, we will do more: we will obtain
precise and universal asymptotics
for multiplicative statistics of the form

(1.7)

\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}_{n}}\left[\prod_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{n}}\frac{1}{1+\ee^{-t(\lambda-\mathsf{a}N)-s}}\right],\quad n=\lfloor Nx\rfloor,

for
\mathsf{a},t>0,s\in\mathbb{R}
, and a large family of
discrete orthogonal polynomial ensembles

\mathcal{X}_{n}
with
n=\lfloor Nx\rfloor
particles. For the record, the statistics needed when applying (
1.6
) are recovered from the Meixner ensemble with the choice of parameters

(1.8)

\displaystyle\alpha=N(y-x),\quad\beta=\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1},\quad t=\log(\mathsf{q}^{-1}),

\displaystyle\mathsf{a}=\mathsf{a}(x,y)=\mathcal{H}(x,y),\quad\text{and}\quad s=-\left(1+\sigma\mathsf{h}N^{1/3}\right)\log(\mathsf{q}^{-1}).

Discrete orthogonal polynomial ensembles form a class of point processes which are natural discrete analogues of eigenvalues of random matrices. The connection between discrete orthogonal polynomial ensembles and growth models has been extensively used since the early 2000s, but to our knowledge the term discrete orthogonal polynomial ensemble was first coined in the seminal work of Johansson
[
62
]
, who among other results showed that, for instance, the Meixner ensemble admits a reduction to the classical Plancherel measure on random partitions. Several notable findings in the KPZ universality theory, both old and new, may be traced back to such type of connections.

The
large deviations theory
for discrete orthogonal polynomial ensembles may be considered by now well developed, and allows one to extract asymptotic limits of statistics for these models. In fact, the recent work
[
40
]
by Das, Liao and Mucciconi also utilizes the identity (
1.4
) as their starting point. Fluctuations of
\mathfrak{h}
in the large deviations scale lead to multiplicative statistics of the Meixner which also live in a large deviations (global) scale of the point process. As a consequence, the scaling limit of the Meixner component in the right-hand side of (
1.4
) is an immediate consequence of an earlier theory by Johansson
[
61
]
(see also the more recent work
[
38
]
), and most of the work in
[
40
]
lies on deriving properties of this limit, and on obtaining the analogue of the approximation (
1.6
) in the large deviation scale they focus on.

In contrast, the choice of parameters in (
1.5
) that we need to consider implies that the multiplicative statistics
\mathsf{L}_{N}
live on
local singular scales
. Still as a consequence of the potential theory developed in
[
61
]
, points in the Meixner ensemble have three distinguished behaviors in the large. First off, for the value
\mathsf{a}>0
in (
1.8
), random points fill out all sites in
(0,n\mathsf{a})\cap\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
with exponentially large probability. For a second value
\mathsf{b}>\mathsf{a}
, all sites in
(\mathsf{b}n,+\infty)
remain free of points, also with exponentially large probability. Finally, in the middle interval
(n\mathsf{a},n\mathsf{b})
, points accumulate with nontrivial fluctuations, following a certain strictly positive density. The intervals
(0,\mathsf{a})
,
(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b})
and
(\mathsf{b},+\infty)
are called
saturated
,
band
, and
gap
regions in the limiting (scaled) spectrum of points, respectively. In our scale, the endpoint
\mathsf{a}
that marks the transition between the saturated and band regions coincides precisely with the value of
\mathsf{a}
in (
1.5
), and the random points may be described globally through a density function
\phi(nz)
, the so-called equilibrium density. This density
\phi(z)
is constant and strictly positive for
z\in(0,\mathsf{a})
, it decays from this constant value to
0
along the interval
(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b})
, and it remains equal to
0
on the interval
(\mathsf{b},+\infty)
.
Roughly, each term in the product in (
1.7
) either goes exponentially to
0
, or converges to
1
. In other words, at a formal level, the term inside the expectation in (
1.7
) reads

(1.9)

\frac{1}{1+\ee^{-t(\lambda-\mathsf{a}N)-s}}\approx{\mathds{1}}_{(0,\mathsf{a}-\frac{s}{tN})}(\lambda/N)\ee^{t(\lambda-\mathsf{a}N)+s}+{\mathds{1}}_{(\mathsf{a}-\frac{s}{tN},+\infty)}(\lambda/N),

where
{\mathds{1}}_{J}
denotes the indicator function of the interval
J
. Therefore,

(1.10)

\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)\approx\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}_{n}}\left[\exp\left(\sum_{{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{n},\,\lambda\leq N\mathsf{a}-\frac{s}{t}}}\left(t(\lambda-\mathsf{a}N)+s\right)\right)\right]

For values
s=\mathcal{O}(N)
(corresponding to the large deviations scale
\mathsf{h}=\mathcal{O}(N^{2/3})
in the S6VM language), the large
N
behavior of statistics in (
1.7
) may be determined from the mentioned large deviations theory for orthogonal polynomial ensembles. Thanks to the approximation (
1.10
),
\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)
essentially becomes a gap probability which sits in the global scale
s=\mathcal{O}(N)
, and
\log\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)/N^{2}
has leading behavior governed by a modification of the equilibrium problem that incorporates the exponential factor approximation (
1.10
).

If, instead,
s
is of order strictly smaller than
N
,
\log\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)
leaves on a scale lower than
N^{2}
, and the mentioned potential-theoretic methods fail to capture its limit. In fact, in such smaller scales of
s
, one sees that
{\mathds{1}}_{(\mathsf{a}-\frac{s}{tN},+\infty)}
becomes the indicator function of an interval shrinking to the point
\mathsf{a}
, and the leading behavior of
\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)
comes effectively from the fluctuations of particles near the point
\mathsf{a}
rather than from fluctuations of all the particles at the global scale
\mathcal{O}(N)
.

This effect of local fluctuations is also at the core of our main results on the asymptotics of
\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)
. To explain the nature of such asymptotics, it is instructive to look at the empty sites
\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\circ}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{n}
that appear in the expectation (
1.7
) as a new point process; this is the
hole
point process. For clarity, for this discussion we will still call these empty sites as random particles. Observe however that
\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\circ}
always has infinitely many particles, and this point process is not an orthogonal polynomial ensemble.

For particles in the process
\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\circ}
the roles of the bands, gaps and saturated regions are interchanged, and the intervals
(0,\mathsf{a})
,
(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b})
and
(\mathsf{b},+\infty)
now form gap, band and saturated regions. In particular, there are no particles in
(0,n\mathsf{a})\cap\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\circ}
with probability exponentially close to
1
, and the density of particles in
(n\mathsf{a},n(\mathsf{a}+\varepsilon))\cap\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\circ}
is positive. With this interpretation in mind, we may view the indicator function in (
1.10
) as creating a pushing effect in particles. When
s>0
, essentially all particles in
\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\circ}
are inside
(\mathsf{a}-s/tN,+\infty)
, the indicator function in (
1.10
) evaluates to
1
, and the corresponding expectation in (
1.7
) becomes close to
1
: it should be close to the gap probability of the interval
(0,\mathsf{a}-s/tN)
. However, when
s<0
, with positive probability one finds particles for which the indicator function
{{\mathds{1}}_{(\mathsf{a}-\frac{s}{tN},+\infty)}(\lambda/N)}
in (
1.10
) vanishes, one has to account for such cancellations in the expectation (
1.7
), and the asymptotic behavior of
\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)
changes drastically.

A lot of our work here is to rigorously quantify the heuristics we just described. With our results, we learn that the behavior of
\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)
is governed by the variation of
\left(1+\ee^{-t(\lambda-\gamma N)-s}\right)^{-1}
as
s
ranges over the interval
(-\delta\,N^{1/2},\,\delta\,N^{1/2})
, and three distinct asymptotic regimes emerge: (i)
subcritical
, when
s\in(s_{0}\,N^{1/3},\,s_{0}^{-1}N^{1/2})
; (ii)
critical
, when
s\in(-s_{0}\,N^{1/3},\,s_{0}\,N^{1/3})
; and (iii)
supercritical
, when
s\in(-s_{0}^{-1}\,N^{1/2},\,-s_{0}\,N^{1/3})
, where
s_{0}>0
is an arbitrary large constant. In terms of the height variable
\mathsf{h}=\mathcal{O}(s/N^{1/3})
of the six-vertex model in (
1.8
), the subcritical regime corresponds to the upper moderate deviations regime, the critical regime corresponds to the CLT scale, and the supercritical regime corresponds to the lower moderate deviations regime.

A deeper explanation for the emergence of these three different regimes, and the underlying mechanisms in the corresponding asymptotics, may be described again by random matrix theory. In random matrix theory language, the point
\mathsf{a}
is a (regular) soft edge point of the original discrete orthogonal polynomial ensemble
\mathcal{X}_{n}
, and statistics of
\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\circ}
near
\mathsf{a}
may be computed in terms of the Airy kernel (
1.2
). Still in this language, one may view the endpoint
\mathsf{a}-s/(tN)
in the approximation (
1.9
) as a moving wall. When
s\gg 0
, this moving wall is to the left of the soft edge point
\mathsf{a}
, and asymptotics of
\mathsf{L}_{N}
are expected to be governed by the Airy kernel as well. However, when
s\ll 0
, this moving wall gets inside the band region
(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b})
, and in configurations giving the leading contribution to
\mathsf{L}_{N}
particles are now pushed to the right of this wall at
\mathsf{a}-s/(tN)
. This push phenomenon creates a hard edge effect at
\mathsf{a}-s/(tN)
. Heuristically, one then expects that the leading contributions should be coming from typical hard edge contributions, and governed now by the
Bessel kernel
,

(1.11)

\mathsf{J}_{0}(x,y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\Jb_{0}(\sqrt{x})\Jb^{\prime}_{0}(\sqrt{y})\sqrt{y}-\Jb_{0}(\sqrt{y})\Jb^{\prime}_{0}(\sqrt{x})\sqrt{x}}{x-y},\quad x\neq y,\qquad\mathsf{J}_{0}(x,x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{4}\left(\Jb_{0}^{\prime}(\sqrt{x})^{2}+\Jb_{0}(\sqrt{x})^{2}\right).

In the formula above,
\Jb_{\nu}
denotes the Bessel function of first kind and order
\nu
. Obviously, there is also a transitional asymptotic effect on
\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)
for intermediate values of
s
, which should somehow capture a “soft-edge to hard-edge” transition. According to random matrix theory, this type of transition should be governed by kernels related to Painlevé equations, as we now describe.

Let
u=u(y)
be the solution to the Painlevé XXXIV equation

(1.12)

\partial_{y}^{2}u=4u^{2}+2yu+\frac{(\partial_{y}u)^{2}}{2u}\quad\text{with}\quad u(y)=\frac{\ee^{-\frac{4}{3}y^{3/2}}}{4\pi y^{1/2}}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(y^{-1/4})\right),\;y\to+\infty.

This solution is uniquely characterized by its behavior at
+\infty
, it is real-valued and free of poles over the real line, and satisfies

(1.13)

u(y)=-\frac{y}{2}-\frac{1}{8y^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(y^{-7/2}),\quad y\to-\infty.

With this Painlevé transcendent, we consider a particular solution
\psi=\psi(\zeta,y)
of the linear Schrödinger equation with potential
2u(y)
of the form

\partial_{y}^{2}\psi=\left(\zeta+y+2u(y)\right)\psi,\quad\text{with}\quad\psi(\zeta,y)=(1+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-1}))\frac{\zeta^{-1/4}}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\ee^{-\frac{2}{3}\zeta^{3/2}-y\zeta^{1/2}}\text{ as }\zeta\to+\infty.

The Painlevé XXXIV kernel is then given by

(1.14)

\mathsf{K}_{\rm P34}(\zeta,\xi{\,|\,}y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\psi(\zeta,y)\partial_{y}\psi(\xi,y)-\psi(\xi,y)\partial_{y}\psi(\zeta,y)}{\zeta-\xi},\quad\zeta,\xi\in\mathbb{R},\;y\in\mathbb{R},\;\zeta\neq\xi,

and extended by continuity to
\zeta=\xi
.

Typically, Painlevé-type kernels are expressed in terms of the matrix-valued solution to the Lax Pair equations characterizing the Painlevé equation itself, rather than in terms of a stand-alone equation. The more direct construction we just provided in terms of the linear Schrödinger equation is proved in Section
7.1
below.

Still in the context of random matrix theory, other kernels related to the Painlevé XXXIV equation have appeared, to our knowledge, originally in
[
59
]
, as the limiting correlation kernel for a random matrix model in a suitable double scaling regime near a regular soft edge. Since then, Painlevé XXXIV kernels (or related quantities) have resurfaced in random matrix theory in other works
[
81
,
18
,
24
]
. Such Painlevé kernels depend on certain monodromy parameters, and to our knowledge the Painlevé XXXIV kernel with the specific monodromy parameters that we consider here have not appeared before.

The precise conceptual reason underneath the appearance of the Airy, Bessel and Painlevé XXXIV kernels is explained by the exact solvability structure of discrete orthogonal polynomial ensembles. As we discuss in detail in Section
2.2
, correlation functions for discrete orthogonal polynomial ensembles may be computed through the Christoffel-Darboux kernel
\mathsf{K}_{n}
of orthogonal polynomials with respect to a discrete weight
w(x)
on
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. We will show that if
\Theta=\Theta(\cdot{\,|\,}s)
is any sufficiently regular function depending smoothly on a parameter
s
, then

(1.15)

\log\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}_{n}}\left[\prod_{\lambda\in\mathcal{X}_{n}}\Theta(\lambda{\,|\,}s)\right]=-\int_{s}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}\mathsf{K}_{n}^{\Theta}(x,x{\,|\,}u)w(x)(\partial_{s}\Theta)(x{\,|\,}s=u)\right)\mathrm{d}u,

where
\mathsf{K}_{n}^{\Theta}(\cdot{\,|\,}s)
is the Christoffel-Darboux kernel associated to the new weight
\Theta(x{\,|\,}s)w(x)
, which we view as as a
deformation
of the original weight
w(x)
. This formula is not directly applicable to the expectation in (
1.7
) because it runs over the hole point process
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{n}
, which is not an orthogonal polynomial ensemble. Nevertheless, a simple observation (see (
2.31
) below) will yield that
\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)
is equal to the sum of an explicit growing term and the right-hand side of (
1.15
) with choice

(1.16)

\Theta(x{\,|\,}s)=\Theta_{N}(x{\,|\,}s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=1+\ee^{-t(x-\mathsf{a}N)-s}.

Most of our effort is devoted to analyzing the right-hand side of (
1.15
) with this choice
\Theta=\Theta_{N}
. Notice however that the function
\Theta_{N}
itself diverges for values
x<\mathsf{a}N-s/t
. In fact, after performing the scaling
\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
of the original lattice, this blow up of the deformation
\Theta_{N}
contributes nontrivially exactly at the scale of local correlations near the point
\mathsf{a}
. In turn, this blow up means that the corresponding scaled kernel also blows up, and the asymptotics involved in the formula (
1.15
) are in fact
singular
. As a consequence, a lot of effort has to be put into the required asymptotic analysis. Notably, first, we have to compute the growing terms coming from the right-hand side of (
1.15
) quite precisely, to show that they cancel exactly the explicit blown-up terms previously mentioned and, second, unravel that the next order term yields the leading asymptotics we are seeking for.

Our main findings on asymptotics of
\mathsf{L}_{N}
explain the above heuristics in precise terms, not only for the Meixner ensemble but for any discrete orthogonal polynomial ensemble displaying a saturated-to-band transition in the limiting spectrum.

To state a simplified version of our findings, let
\mathcal{X}_{n}
be a discrete orthogonal polynomial ensemble of
n
particles on
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, for which the associated limiting particle density has the interval
(0,\mathsf{a})
as a saturated region and
(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b})
as a band interval, and let
\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)
be the associated multiplicative statistic (
1.7
).

Theorem C
.

Let
s<S
be given numbers with
|s|,|S|\leq\frac{1}{M}N^{1/2}
,
M>0
sufficiently large but fixed.
For some constant
\mathsf{c}>0
and any
\delta>0
sufficiently small, the multiplicative statistic
\mathsf{L}_{N}
has asymptotics described by

(1.17)

\log\frac{\mathsf{L}_{N}(S)}{\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)}\approx\frac{\mathsf{c}}{N^{1/3}}\int_{s}^{S}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{Z}\\
|x-\mathsf{a}N|\leq N\delta\end{subarray}}\mathsf{H}_{N}\left(-\frac{\mathsf{c}}{N^{1/3}}\left(x-N\mathsf{a}+\frac{v}{t}\right){\,|\,}v\right)\frac{\ee^{-t(x-N\mathsf{a}+v/t)}}{(1+\ee^{-t(x-N\mathsf{a}+v/t)})^{2}}\mathrm{d}v,

as
N\to\infty
, where the function
\mathsf{H}_{N}(\zeta{\,|\,}\mathsf{s})
has the following asymptotic behavior

(i)

For any sufficiently large constant
s_{0}>0

\mathsf{H}_{N}(\zeta{\,|\,}s)\approx\mathsf{A}\left(\zeta+\frac{\mathsf{c}}{t}\frac{s}{N^{1/3}},\zeta+\frac{\mathsf{c}}{t}\frac{s}{N^{1/3}}\right),

uniformly for
s_{0}N^{1/3}\leq s\leq\frac{1}{s_{0}}N^{1/2}
, where
\mathsf{A}(x,x)
is the diagonal of the Airy kernel (
1.2
).

(ii)

For any
s_{0}>0
,

\mathsf{H}_{N}(\zeta{\,|\,}s)\approx\mathsf{K}_{\rm P34}\left(\zeta,\zeta{\,|\,}\frac{\mathsf{c}}{t}\frac{s}{N^{1/3}}\right),

uniformly for
-s_{0}N^{1/3}\leq s\leq s_{0}N^{1/3}
, where
\mathsf{K}_{\rm P34}(\cdot,\cdot{\,|\,}y)
is the Painlevé XXXIV kernel in the Painlevé variable
y
.

(iii)

For any sufficiently large constant
s_{0}>0
,

\mathsf{H}_{N}(\zeta{\,|\,}s)\approx-\frac{\mathsf{c}^{2}}{t^{2}}\frac{s^{2}}{N^{2/3}}\mathsf{J}_{0}\left(\frac{\mathsf{c}^{2}}{t^{2}}\frac{s^{2}}{N^{2/3}}\zeta,\frac{\mathsf{c}^{2}}{t^{2}}\frac{s^{2}}{N^{2/3}}\zeta\right),

uniformly for
-\frac{1}{s_{0}}N^{1/2}\leq s\leq-s_{0}N^{1/3}
, where
\mathsf{J}_{0}
is the Bessel (
1.11
).

A refined version of Theorem
C
, including estimates for the error terms in each of the cases and an expression for the constant
\mathsf{c}
in terms of the equilibrium measure of the system, is given in Theorem
2.8
below. In particular, the error terms we obtain are robust enough so that we can in fact integrate these asymptotic identities and recover leading asymptotics for
\log\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)
; we will say more about this in a moment.

As we said earlier, the correlation kernel appearing in (
1.15
)–(
1.16
) blows up as
N
grows. The term
\mathsf{H}_{N}
appearing in Theorem
C
rises up as a surviving subleading term in the asymptotics of this correlation kernel, after accounting for explicit cancellations from the mechanisms mentioned earlier.

At a formal level, the exponential factor

\frac{\ee^{-t(x-N\mathsf{a}+v/t)}}{(1+\ee^{-t(x-N\mathsf{a}+v/t)})^{2}}=\frac{\ee^{-t|x-N\mathsf{a}+v/t|}}{(1+\ee^{-t|x-N\mathsf{a}+v/t|})^{2}},

acts as a delta function, in the sense that non-negligible terms in the sum (
1.17
) must come from terms
x-N\mathsf{a}+v/t=\mathcal{O}(1)
.
A naive Taylor expansion suggests that at these points,

(1.18)

\mathsf{H}_{N}\left(-\frac{\mathsf{c}}{tN^{1/3}}\left(x-N\mathsf{a}+\frac{v}{t}\right){\,|\,}v\right)\approx\mathsf{H}_{N}(0{\,|\,}v).

Sending formally
S\to+\infty
in (
1.17
) and neglecting for a moment boundary terms, the ansatz (
1.18
) yields the approximation

(1.19)

\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)\approx-\frac{\mathsf{c}}{tN^{1/3}}\int_{s}^{\infty}\mathsf{H}_{N}(0{\,|\,}v)\mathrm{d}v,

and the asymptotic behavior of
\mathsf{H}_{N}
from Theorem
C
leads to the next main result.

Theorem D
.

For a constant
\mathsf{c}>0
, the multiplicative statistics
\mathsf{S}_{N}
has asymptotic behavior described by

(i)

For any sufficiently large constant
s_{0}>0

\log\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)\approx-\int_{\mathsf{c}s/(tN^{1/3})}^{+\infty}\mathsf{A}\left(y,y\right)\mathrm{d}y,

uniformly for
s_{0}N^{1/3}\leq s\leq\frac{1}{s_{0}}N^{1/2}
, where
\mathsf{A}(x,x)
is the diagonal of the Airy kernel (
1.2
).

(ii)

For any
s_{0}>0
,

\log\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)\approx-\int_{\mathsf{c}s/(tN^{1/3})}^{+\infty}\mathsf{K}_{\rm P34}\left(y,y\right)\mathrm{d}y,

uniformly for
-s_{0}N^{1/3}\leq s\leq s_{0}N^{1/3}
, where
\mathsf{K}_{\rm P34}(\cdot,\cdot{\,|\,}y)
is the Painlevé XXXIV kernel in the Painlevé variable
y
.

(iii)

For any sufficiently large constant
s_{0}>0
,

\log\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)\approx\mathsf{J}_{0}(0,0)\frac{\mathsf{c}^{3}}{t^{3}}\frac{|s|^{3}}{3N},

uniformly for
-\frac{1}{s_{0}}N^{1/2}\leq s\leq-s_{0}N^{1/3}
, where
\mathsf{J}_{0}
is the Bessel (
1.11
).

A detailed version of Theorem
D
, with appropriate bounds for the error terms in the claimed approximations, is given in Theorem
2.9
below.

Theorems
A
and
B
become essentially corollaries of Theorem
D
, as we now indicate.

We start with the regime
s_{0}N^{1/3}\leq s\leq N^{1/2}/s_{0}
, which is the range in Theorem
C
–(i). Writing
s=\frac{t}{\mathsf{c}}N^{1/3}\mathsf{h}
with
\mathsf{h}_{0}\leq\mathsf{h}\leq N^{1/6}/\mathsf{h}_{0}
(recall (
1.5
)), and accounting for the asymptotic behavior of
\mathsf{A}(v,v)
for
v
large (see (
2.55
) below), we obtain

(1.20)

\log\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)\approx-\frac{1}{8\pi\mathsf{h}}\ee^{-\frac{4}{3}\mathsf{h}^{3/2}},\quad\text{that is},\quad\log(1-\mathsf{L}_{N}(s))\approx-\frac{4}{3}\mathsf{h}^{3/2}.

Thanks to (
1.6
) and (
1.4
), this asymptotic approximation eventually leads to Theorem
A
.

Next, we look at the regime from Theorem
D
–(ii). As a novel result, we will show in Section
7.1
below the non-trivial identity

(1.21)

\mathsf{K}_{\rm P34}(0,0{\,|\,}y)=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{y}^{2}u(y)-3u(y)^{2}-2yu(y)

between the PXXXIV kernel and the Painlevé transcendent
u(y)
, and from this identity and (
1.12
), (
1.13
),

(1.22)

\mathsf{K}_{\rm P34}(0,0{\,|\,}y)\approx-\frac{1}{2\pi}y^{1/2}\ee^{-\frac{4}{3}y^{3/2}}\quad\text{as}\quad y\to+\infty,

and

(1.23)

\mathsf{K}_{\rm P34}(0,0{\,|\,}y)\approx\frac{y^{2}}{4}\quad\text{as}\quad y\to-\infty.

Write, as before,
s=\frac{t}{\mathsf{c}}N^{1/3}\mathsf{h}
, but now with
|\mathsf{h}|\leq\mathsf{h}_{0}
and
\mathsf{h}_{0}
sufficiently large. In the upper tail regime when
\mathsf{h}
is very large and positive, we use (
1.22
) in Theorem
D
–(ii), and obtain again that
\log(1-\mathsf{L}_{N}(s))\approx-\frac{4}{3}\mathsf{h}^{3/2}
, in agreement with (
1.20
). In the other extreme when
\mathsf{h}
is large but negative, we instead use (
1.23
), and after
y
-integration approximate

\log\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)\approx-\frac{|\mathsf{h}|^{3}}{12},

which is the tail order in Theorem
B
. This behavior matches with Theorem
D
–(iii) once we observe that
\mathsf{J}_{0}(0,0)=1/4
. In other words, it is precisely the kernel
\mathsf{K}_{\rm P34}
in Theorem
C
–(ii) and the identity (
1.21
) that allow us to interpolate between the asymmetrical upper and lower tail decays in Theorems
A
and
B
.

The heart of the paper lies in the proofs of Theorems
C
and
D
, while Theorems
A
and
B
follow naturally from them. Before turning to the detailed analysis, we briefly outline some core ideas behind the proofs of the first two results.

The approximation (
1.18
) is, at first sight, reasonable to be expected. But it turned out to be rather cumbersome to rigorously derive Theorem
D
from Theorem
C
. Aside from the technical aspects naturally involved, we believe there is a more conceptual reason underneath this difficulty. After scaling the lattice
\mathbb{Z}\mapsto N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, the sum in (
1.17
) may be asymptotically approximated by

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathsf{H}_{N}\left(\zeta_{k}{\,|\,}v\right)\frac{\ee^{-N^{1/3}t\zeta_{k}}}{(1+\ee^{-N^{1/3}t\zeta_{k}})^{2}},\qquad\text{with}\quad\zeta_{k}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathsf{-}cN^{2/3}\left(\frac{k}{N}-\mathsf{a}+\frac{v}{tN}\right),\;k\in\mathbb{Z}.

at the cost of exponentially negligible error terms. Such scaling
x\mapsto\zeta
sends the points in the original lattice
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
to scaled points
\zeta_{k}
which live in the same local scale of fluctuations near
x=\mathsf{a}
. Our first try was to approximate this sum by the integral

(1.24)

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathsf{H}_{N}\left(\zeta_{k}{\,|\,}v\right)\frac{\ee^{-N^{1/3}t\zeta_{k}}}{(1+\ee^{-N^{1/3}t\zeta_{k}})^{2}}\approx\int p(\zeta)\mathsf{H}_{N}(\zeta{\,|\,}v)\frac{\ee^{-N^{1/3}t\zeta}}{(1+\ee^{-N^{1/3}t\zeta})^{2}}\mathrm{d}\zeta,

where
p(\zeta)
is the density of points
\zeta
, which in our case can be computed explicitly. Performing a classical steepest descent argument on the remaining integral, we are left with the same approximation (
1.19
) that eventually leads to Theorem
D
. However, the approximation (
1.24
) does not actually hold true, and it turns out that the cancellations that lead to the approximation (
1.19
) do not come from approximating the sum in (
1.17
) by an integral. Instead, the approximation (
1.19
) comes from cancellations that emerge from the periodicity of the lattice structure, or in other words from the discrete nature of the original model. At the core of these estimates lies the identity

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\ee^{-tk+u}}{(1+\ee^{-tk+u})^{2}}=\frac{1}{t}+\frac{8\pi}{t^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{k\cos(2\pi ku/t)}{\ee^{2\pi^{2}k/t}-\ee^{-2\pi^{2}k/t}},

which is computed using Poisson’s summation formula (see Lemma
13.6
below, as well as Remark
13.7
which expresses this identity in terms of a Jacobi Theta function). With this identity at hand, we essentially show that the right-hand side of (
1.17
) coincides (up to exponentially small terms) with

\frac{\mathsf{c}}{tN^{1/3}}\int_{s}^{S}\mathsf{H}_{N}(0{\,|\,}v)\mathrm{d}v+\frac{8\pi}{t^{2}}\frac{\mathsf{c}}{N^{1/3}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{k}{\ee^{2\pi^{2}k/t}-\ee^{-2\pi^{2}k/t}}\int_{s}^{S}\mathsf{H}_{N}(0{\,|\,}v)\cos\left(\frac{2\pi k}{t}(Nt\mathsf{a}-v)\right)\mathrm{d}v,

and a simple integration by parts shows that the integrals with trigonometric terms leave on a smaller scale that the first term, leading to (
1.19
) and the proof of Theorem
D
.

The proof of Theorem
C
, in turn, takes up most of the technical effort developed in this paper. As said earlier, it relies on the asymptotic analysis of the right-hand side of (
1.15
) with the choice (
1.16
). The kernel
\mathsf{K}_{n}^{\Theta}
appearing in (
1.15
) is expressed in terms of discrete orthogonal polynomials for the deformed weight
\Theta_{N}(x)w(w)
on
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, and the analysis of (
1.15
) will be performed through the so-called Riemann-Hilbert approach to (discrete) orthogonal polynomials. The modern asymptotic theory for Riemann-Hilbert problems was initiated by Deift and Zhou in the context of integrable systems
[
45
,
46
]
and shortly afterwards extended to orthogonal polynomials as well
[
44
,
43
]
. For discrete orthogonal polynomials, fundamental works include their large-degree asymptotic analysis carried out by Baik, Kriecherbauer, McLaughlin and Miller
[
5
]
for orthogonality on finite lattices, and by Bleher and Liechty for orthogonality on infinite lattices
[
14
]
, in great part also motivated by the six-vertex model
[
16
]
. We also refer to
[
23
,
79
,
72
,
4
,
71
,
36
]
for similar works deriving asymptotics for discrete orthogonal polynomials and related quantities through the Riemann-Hilbert approach.

Aside from technical modifications that we need to cope with because, as said, our deformation
\Theta_{N}
of the weight blows up, the most fundamental difference of our asymptotic analysis of discrete RHPs with previous literature lies in the construction of the so-called local parametrix, and its needed analysis. In short terms, this local parametrix serves as a local approximation to the orthogonal polynomials near the point
z=\mathsf{a}
.

In the classical setup when
\Theta\equiv 1
, corresponding to the asymptotic analysis of discrete orthogonal polynomials with weight
w(x)
, the local parametrix at
z=\mathsf{a}
may be constructed with the aid of Airy functions. However, over here this is no longer possible: the effects of the deformation
\Theta_{N}
are felt through down to the local scale of fluctuations of the model, and the local parametrix has to account for such effects. As a consequence, we need to consider a novel model problem in the construction of the local parametrix. This novel model problem contains two essential information. First off, its jump matrix involves the deformed weight
\Theta_{N}
in the appropriate local scale, encoded through a function that still depends on
N
, and which has infinitely many poles accumulating on the real axis as
N
grows. Second, its jump matrix also involves another function which encodes the fact that the original model was defined through discrete orthogonality rather than continuous. This second function has infinitely many poles on the real axis that become dense as
N
grows. In particular, we have to deal with a local parametrix with non-constant and non-homogeneous jumps, which even at the local scale still depends on the large
\tau=N^{1/3}
parameter, and moreover the jump matrices involved have
infinitely many poles
on the complex plane, which interplay all the way throughout our analysis.

At the end, and in much as a consequence of the presence of the mentioned poles, we have to deal with the nonlinear steepest descent method for Riemann-Hilbert problems at 5 different stages: at the level of the RHP for orthogonal polynomials, at the level of the model problem required, which in itself involves 3 separate asymptotic analysis, one for each of the regimes (i)–(iii) from Theorem
C
, and a fifth and last analysis, necessary for the local parametrix needed to complete regime (iii). We give more details about these aspects, including a comparison with previous RHP literature and possible connections with integrable systems, in Section
2.6
below.

2.
Statement of precise results

In this section we introduce the models under study in detail and present our main results in precise terms. We begin with the stochastic six-vertex model, then develop the general theory of discrete orthogonal polynomial ensembles, and finally specialize to the Meixner ensemble which provides the key connection between these two subjects.

2.1.
The stochastic six-vertex model

The six-vertex model is a fundamental lattice model in statistical mechanics, first introduced by Pauling
[
74
]
in the context of ice-type models and subsequently studied extensively in the physics and mathematics literature
[
70
,
12
,
76
]
. The stochastic version we consider was introduced by Gwa and Spohn
[
57
]
and has attracted significant recent attention due to its connections with integrable probability and the KPZ universality class
[
19
,
3
,
33
]
.

2.1.1.
Definition of the model

Let
\mathfrak{P}
be an infinite up-right oriented line ensemble such that from each of the points on the line
\{(1,m):m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq}\}
originates one path (this corresponds to the
step initial condition
) and they do not share any horizontal edge.

We endow the space of such path ensemble with a probability measure in the following Markovian way (see Figure
4
). This measure depends on two parameters
b_{1},b_{2}\in(0,1)
, which may be alternatively described by parameters
\mathsf{q}\in(0,1)
and
u>\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}
, see Table
1
for this correspondence.

\scriptstyle b_{2}
\scriptstyle b_{2}
\scriptstyle b_{2}
\scriptstyle 1-b_{2}
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle b_{1}

Figure 4
.
Markovian evolution of upright paths in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{\geq}
, starting from the narrow wedge initial condition.

We assume that the region
T_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{(x,y){\,|\,}x+y\leq n\}
is endowed with the probability measure
\mathfrak{P}_{n}
, and construct a probability measure on
T_{n+1}
.

Fix a particular configuration on the region
T_{n}
which is sampled under the measure
\mathfrak{P}_{n}
. Fix any vertex
v
on the line
\{(x,y):x+y=n+1\}
. Denote the number of horizontal (resp. vertical) arrows coming into the site
v
from the line
\{(x,y)|x+y=n\}
by
i_{1}
(resp.
j_{1}
). Similarly, denote the number of horizontal (resp. vertical) arrows going out of the the vertex
v
by
i_{2}
(resp.
j_{2}
). We restrict our discussion to the case
i_{1},i_{2},j_{1},j_{2}\in\{0,1\}
. Now, the probabilities of the transitions
(i_{1},j_{1})\to(j_{1},j_{2})
is given by the following weights:

(2.1)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}((i_{1},0)\to(i_{2},0))

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\mathsf{q}^{i_{1}}\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u}{1-\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u}\mathbbm{1}_{i_{1}=i_{2}},

(2.2)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}((i_{1},0)\to(i_{2},1))

\displaystyle=\frac{(\mathsf{q}^{i_{1}}-1)\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u}{1-\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u}\mathbbm{1}_{i_{1}=i_{2}-1},

(2.3)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}((i_{1},1)\to(i_{2},1))

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathsf{q}^{i_{1}-1}-\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u}{1-\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u}\mathbbm{1}_{i_{1}=i_{2}},

(2.4)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}((i_{1},1)\to(i_{2},0))

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\mathsf{q}^{i_{1}-1}}{1-\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u}\mathbbm{1}_{i_{1}=i_{2}-1}.

Note that
\sum_{i_{2},j_{2}}{\mathbb{P}}((i_{1},j_{1})\to(i_{2},j_{2}))=1
where
i_{1},i_{2},j_{1},j_{2}\in\{0,1\}
in the case of the stochastic six vertex model. This defines a probability measure
\mathfrak{P}_{n+1}
on the region
\{(x,y):x+y\leq n+1\}
if all the weights are assumed to be positive. Owing to the order
\mathfrak{P}_{n}\subset\mathfrak{P}_{m}
for all
n\leq m
(i.e.,
\mathfrak{P}_{m}|T_{n}=\mathfrak{P}_{n}
), the infinite volume limit
\mathfrak{P}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathfrak{P}_{n}
is well defined and is called the
stochastic six vertex model
. See Table
1
for the weight configuration of individual vertices.

1

1

\frac{u\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}-\mathsf{q}^{-1}}{u\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}-1}

\frac{(u\mathsf{q}^{1/2}-1)}{u\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}-1}

\frac{\mathsf{q}^{-1}-1}{u\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}-1}

\frac{u\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}(1-\mathsf{q})}{u\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}-1}

1

1

b_{1}

b_{2}

1-b_{1}

1-b_{2}

Table 1
.
Table of vertex weights. The correspondence of weights
(b_{1},b_{2})\mapsto(\mathsf{q},u)
with
\mathsf{q}\in(0,1)
and
u>\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}
assumes that
b_{1}>b_{2}
.

When needed to specify parameters, we use the notation
\mathfrak{P}(b_{1},b_{2})
or
\mathfrak{P}(\mathsf{q},u)
for the limiting measure
\mathfrak{P}
. The measure
\mathfrak{P}(\mathsf{q},u)
of the stochastic six vertex on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{\geq 0}
can also be interpreted using an interacting particle system, see for instance
[
19
]
for details.

2.1.2.
The height function

Each path ensemble
\mathfrak{P}
is encoded by a
height function

\mathfrak{h}:\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, defined by setting
\mathfrak{h}(M,N)
equal to the number of paths passing through or to the right of the vertex
(M,N)
. Equivalently, viewing the model as an interacting particle system where particle positions are given by the intersections of paths with horizontal lines,
\mathfrak{h}(M,N)
counts the particles weakly to the right of position
M
at time
N
.

The height function satisfies the boundary conditions
\mathfrak{h}(M,N)=N
for
M=1
(step initial condition) and exhibits a law of large numbers as established by Borodin, Corwin, and Gorin
[
19
, Theorem 1.1]
:

(2.5)

\lim_{N\to+\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{h}(Nx,Ny)}{n}=\mathcal{H}(x,y),\quad\text{in probability},

for an explicit limiting function
\mathcal{H}(x,y)
. The function
\mathcal{H}(x,y)
possesses regions of nontrivial curvature and regions where it is affine.
These are traditionally called the
liquid region
and the
frozen regions
, which we now describe in the setting of the stochastic six vertex model with parameters
(b_{1},b_{2})
.

Liquid region.

For fixed vertex weights
(b_{1},b_{2})
with
0<b_{1},b_{2}<1
, define the
liquid region
as the set of macroscopic points
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2}
where the slope of the limit shape

(\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}(x,y),\;\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}(x,y))

takes values strictly inside the admissible range imposed by arrow conservation.
Equivalently, this is the region where the local proportions of horizontal and vertical arrows are strictly between
0
and
1
.
In probabilistic terms, the paths remain genuinely random, e.g., both horizontal and vertical edges appear with positive asymptotic frequencies determined by
(b_{1},b_{2})
, and the macroscopic limit shape is
curved
.
This is the “disordered” or “liquid” part of the model, and assuming
b_{2}<b_{1}
it is the conic region given by

\left\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{>0}\;\Big|\;\frac{1-b_{1}}{1-b_{2}}<\frac{x}{y}<\frac{1-b_{2}}{1-b_{1}}\right\}=\left\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{>0}\;\Big|\;\frac{1}{u\mathsf{q}^{1/2}}<\frac{x}{y}<u\mathsf{q}^{1/2}\right\}.

Frozen regions.

The
frozen regions
are the zones in the first quadrant complementary to the liquid region, where at least one of the local arrow densities reaches an extremal value.
Equivalently, in these regions the limit shape
\mathcal{H}(x,y)
is
affine
and the local configuration of edges freezes into a deterministic pattern (all edges pointing in the same macroscopic direction).
For step initial data, there are two such frozen phases:
one dominated by vertical edges (close to the
y
–axis) and the other by horizontal edges (close to the
x
–axis).
In both frozen regions, the height function fluctuations collapse to deterministic (non-random) linear evolution.

When scaled around points in the liquid region, the stochastic six vertex height function exhibits nontrivial random fluctuations:
in particular, along any characteristic direction inside this region the model is in the KPZ class and one observes

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}(Nx,Ny)=N\mathcal{H}(x,y)+\mathcal{O}(N^{1/3}),

with fluctuations governed by Tracy–Widom distributions or their inhomogeneous generalizations (depending on direction). In contrast, in the frozen regions the randomness disappears at the macroscopic scale:
\mathfrak{h}(Nx,Ny)=N\mathcal{H}(x,y)+\mathcal{O}(1)
and the local configuration is deterministic. Fluctuations there are of the order
\mathcal{O}(1)
and do not exhibit KPZ-type behavior. Thus the interface between the curved liquid region and the affine frozen regions describes the transition from KPZ-type random behavior to deterministic crystal-like behavior in the stochastic six vertex model. Our main results about the stochastic six vertex model pertains to the liquid region of the height function.

2.1.3.
Fluctuations and the Tracy-Widom distribution

Fix
\nu>1
satisfying
1<\nu<\mathsf{q}^{1/2}u
, and consider the height function at the point
(\nu N,N)
for large
N
. Define the centering and scaling constants:

(2.6)

{\mathsf{a}_{\nu}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{(1-\sqrt{\nu\kappa_{\mathsf{q}}})^{2}}{1-\kappa_{\mathsf{q}}},\qquad\mathsf{c}_{\nu}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\kappa_{\mathsf{q}}^{1/2}\nu^{-1/6}}{1-\kappa_{\mathsf{q}}}\left((1-\sqrt{\nu\kappa_{\mathsf{q}}})(\sqrt{\nu/\kappa_{\mathsf{q}}}-1)\right)^{2/3},\quad\text{with}\quad\kappa_{\mathsf{q}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1}.

The Central Limit Theorem obtained by Borodin, Corwin and Gorin
[
19
, Theorem 1.2]
states that the rescaled height function

(2.7)

\mathfrak{H}_{N}(\nu)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\mathfrak{h}(\nu N,N)-{\mathsf{a}_{\nu}}N}{\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}}

converges in distribution to the GUE Tracy-Widom distribution:

(2.8)

\lim_{N\to+\infty}{\mathbb{P}}\left(-\mathfrak{H}_{N}(\nu)\leq x\right)=F_{\rm GUE}(x).

Our main results quantify this convergence down to the level of the moderate deviation regimes.

Theorem 2.1

(Upper tail for the stochastic six-vertex model)
.

Let
\mathfrak{h}(\nu N,N)
be the height function for the stochastic six-vertex model with parameters satisfying
\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1}<\nu<\mathsf{q}^{1/2}u
(i.e.,
(\nu N,N)
is in the liquid region). For any
\varepsilon,\delta>0
sufficiently small, there exists a constant
\mathsf{h}_{0}=\mathsf{h}_{0}(\varepsilon,\delta,\mathsf{q},u)>0
such that

(2.9)

\exp\left(-(1+\varepsilon)\frac{4}{3}\mathsf{h}^{3/2}\right)\leq{\mathbb{P}}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{h}(\nu N,N)-{\mathsf{a}_{\nu}}N}{\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}}\leq-\mathsf{h}\right)\leq\exp\left(-(1-\varepsilon)\frac{4}{3}\mathsf{h}^{3/2}\right),

uniformly for
\mathsf{h}_{0}\leq\mathsf{h}\leq\mathsf{h}_{0}^{-1}N^{\tfrac{1}{6}-\delta}
.

Theorem 2.2

(Lower tail for the stochastic six-vertex model)
.

Under the same hypotheses as Theorem
2.1
, for any
\varepsilon,\delta>0
sufficiently small, there exists a constant
\mathsf{h}_{0}=\mathsf{h}_{0}(\varepsilon,\delta,\mathsf{q},u)>0
such that

(2.10)

\exp\left(-(1+\varepsilon)\frac{1}{12}\mathsf{h}^{3}\right)\leq{\mathbb{P}}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{h}(\nu N,N)-{\mathsf{a}_{\nu}}N}{\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}}\geq\mathsf{h}\right)\leq\exp\left(-(1-\varepsilon)\frac{1}{12}\mathsf{h}^{3}\right),

uniformly for
\mathsf{h}_{0}\leq\mathsf{h}\leq\mathsf{h}_{0}^{-1}N^{\tfrac{1}{6}-\delta}
.

Remark 2.3

(Extension up to the region
\mathsf{h}\leq\mathsf{h}_{0}^{-1}N^{\tfrac{1}{6}}
)
.

Our results in fact give that the conclusions of Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
remain valid for
\mathsf{h}\leq h_{0}^{-1}N^{1/6}
, with the same exponential growth rates (exponents
3/2
for the upper tail and
3
for the lower tail), though the precise values of constants
4/3
and
1/12
in the rate functions get lost. More precisely, one can derive from Theorem
2.9
that for
\mathsf{h}_{0}\leq\mathsf{h}\leq\mathsf{h}_{0}^{-1}N^{1/6}
with
\mathsf{h}_{0}
sufficiently small, there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
(depending on
\varepsilon,\mathsf{q},u,\nu
) such that

\exp(-c_{1}\mathsf{h}^{3/2})\leq{\mathbb{P}}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{h}(\nu N,N)-{\mathsf{a}_{\nu}}N}{\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}}\leq-\mathsf{h}\right)\leq\exp(-c_{2}\mathsf{h}^{3/2})

and

\exp(-c_{1}\mathsf{h}^{3})\leq{\mathbb{P}}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{h}(\nu N,N)-{\mathsf{a}_{\nu}}N}{\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}}\geq\mathsf{h}\right)\leq\exp(-c_{2}\mathsf{h}^{3}).

The restriction
\mathsf{h}\leq N^{1/6-\delta}
in Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
ensures that we remain in the moderate deviations regime where the Tracy-Widom rate functions with their universal constants
4/3
and
1/12
continue to govern the tail behavior, as established through our precise asymptotic analysis.

For their exploration of the stochastic six-vertex speed process, Drillick and Haunschmid-Sibitz
[
47
]
obtained
one-sided
upper and lower tail bounds (in the CLT scale) for the height function of the narrow wedge stochastic six-vertex model we consider here. For the upper tail, their bound gets the optimal
3/2
-power law, but they do not get the
4/3
optimal constant as we obtain. For the lower tail, they get a much weaker power law
c\,\mathsf{h}
for some undetermined constant
c
, instead of our optimal
\frac{1}{12}\mathsf{h}^{3}
factor. Estimates similar to
[
47
]
have been obtained by Landon and Sosoe
[
68
]
for the stationary stochastic six-vertex model. We are unaware of moderate deviation results for positive temperature models in the KPZ universality class (except the KPZ equation) that are sharp in the power law as we obtain here.

On a somewhat opposite extremal scaling, and as mentioned at the introduction, Das, Liao, and Mucciconi obtained lower tail large deviation results for the stochastic six-vertex model with narrow wedge initial condition
[
40
]
. We discuss further connections in Section
2.5
below.

2.2.
Discrete orthogonal polynomial ensembles

The proofs of Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
proceed through a connection with discrete orthogonal polynomial ensembles, which we now develop in generality.

2.2.1.
Definition and basic properties

Following Johansson
[
62
]
, a
discrete orthogonal polynomial ensemble
(dOPE) on
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
is a probability measure on
(\mathbb{Z}_{>0})^{N}
of the form

(2.11)

\frac{1}{\mathsf{Z}_{N}(\omega)}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\omega(\lambda_{j}),\qquad\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{N}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},

where
\omega:\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\to[0,+\infty)
is the
weight function
and

(2.12)

\mathsf{Z}_{N}(\omega)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{N}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\omega(\lambda_{j})

is the
partition function
. Such ensembles arise naturally in combinatorics, representation theory, and random matrix theory; see
[
63
,
21
]
for surveys.

A fundamental property is that dOPEs are
determinantal point processes
: the measure (
2.11
) admits the representation

(2.13)

\frac{1}{N!}\det\left(\mathsf{K}_{N}(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\omega(\lambda_{j}),

where
\mathsf{K}_{N}
is the
Christoffel-Darboux kernel

(2.14)

\mathsf{K}_{N}(x,y)=\mathsf{K}_{N}(x,y{\,|\,}\omega)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\gamma_{k}^{2}(\omega)\,p_{k}(x{\,|\,}\omega)p_{k}(y{\,|\,}\omega).

Here
p_{k}=p_{k}(\cdot{\,|\,}\omega)
denotes the monic orthogonal polynomial of degree
k
with respect to
\omega
on
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, and
\gamma_{k}=\gamma_{k}(\omega)>0
is the inverse of its weighted norm:

(2.15)

\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}x^{j}p_{k}(x)\omega(x)=0\quad\text{for }j=0,\ldots,k-1,\qquad\frac{1}{\gamma_{k}^{2}}=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}p_{k}(x)^{2}\omega(x).

The partition function relates to the norming constants via

(2.16)

\mathsf{Z}_{N}(\omega)=N!\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}\gamma_{k}^{-2}(\omega).

2.2.2.
Assumptions on the weight

We consider weights whose rescaled versions become exponential weights in the large-
N
limit.

Assumptions 2.4

(Assumptions on the weight)
.

The rescaled weight

(2.17)

\Omega(x)=\Omega_{N}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{N}\omega(Nx),\qquad x\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0},

admits the representation

(2.18)

\Omega(x)=\ee^{-N\mathsf{V}(x)-2\mathsf{C}+\mathsf{E}(x)},\qquad x\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0},

where:

(i)

The
external field

\mathsf{V}
is real-valued, independent of
N
, continuous on
[0,+\infty)
, analytic in a complex neighborhood
U
of
(0,\infty)
, bounded as
x\to 0
along
U
, and satisfies the growth condition

(2.19)

\liminf_{x\to+\infty}\frac{\mathsf{V}(x)}{x}>0.

(ii)

The
correction term

\mathsf{E}
is real-valued, analytic in a complex neighborhood of
(0,+\infty)
, satisfies

(2.20)

\mathsf{E}(x)={\mathsf{E}}_{0}(x)+O(N^{-1})\quad\text{as }N\to\infty,

locally uniformly in a complex neighborhood
U
of
(0,\infty)
, where
{\mathsf{E}}_{0}
is analytic in
U
, and

(2.21)

\sup_{N}\sup_{z\in U,|z|\leq\rho}\left(|\mathsf{E}(z)|+|\mathsf{E}^{\prime}(z)|\right)\leq c\rho

for some constant
c>0
.

(iii)

The
normalization constant

\mathsf{C}
is independent of
x
but may depend on
N
.

2.2.3.
The constrained equilibrium measure

The large-
N
behavior of dOPEs is governed by a variational problem from logarithmic potential theory. The
constrained equilibrium measure
in the external field
\mathsf{V}
is the unique Borel probability measure
\mu_{\mathsf{V}}
on
[0,+\infty)
minimizing the energy functional

(2.22)

I[\mu]=\iint\log\frac{1}{|x-y|}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)+\int\mathsf{V}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

over all Borel probability measures
\mu
on
[0,\infty)
satisfying the
upper constraint

(2.23)

\mu([a,b])\leq b-a\quad\text{for every }b>a>0.

Under Assumption
2.4
, the equilibrium measure
\mu_{\mathsf{V}}
exists uniquely, is absolutely continuous with a piecewise real-analytic density, and has support consisting of finitely many intervals. We impose the following regularity conditions.

Assumptions 2.5

(Assumptions on the equilibrium measure)
.

The potential
\mathsf{V}
satisfies Assumption
2.4
and its constrained equilibrium measure
\mu_{\mathsf{V}}
satisfies:

(i)

Connected support containing the origin:

\supp\mu_{\mathsf{V}}=[0,\mathsf{b}]
for some
\mathsf{b}>0
.

(ii)

Single saturated region:
The upper constraint (
2.23
) is active on an interval
[0,\mathsf{a}]
with
0<\mathsf{a}<\mathsf{b}
:

(2.24)

\mu_{\mathsf{V}}|_{[0,\mathsf{a}]}=\text{Lebesgue measure on }[0,\mathsf{a}].

(iii)

Regularity at the transition points:
The density of
\mu_{\mathsf{V}}
vanishes as a square root at
\mathsf{b}
, and
1-\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathsf{V}}/\mathrm{d}x
vanishes as a square root at
\mathsf{a}
.

(iv)

Regularity of the variational inequalities:
The Euler-Lagrange conditions hold strictly: there exists
\ell=\ell_{\mathsf{V}}
such that

(2.25)

\int\log\frac{1}{|x-y|}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathsf{V}}(y)+\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{V}(x)+\ell\begin{cases}<0,&0<x<\mathsf{a},\\
=0,&\mathsf{a}\leq x\leq\mathsf{b},\\
>0,&x>\mathsf{b}.\end{cases}

Under these assumptions, we call
[0,\mathsf{a}]
the
saturated region
,
[\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}]
the
band region
, and
(\mathsf{b},+\infty)
the
gap region
. The equilibrium measure admits a probabilistic interpretation: for random points
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{N}
distributed according to (
2.11
), the empirical measure
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j}\delta_{\lambda_{j}/N}
converges almost surely to
\mu_{\mathsf{V}}
.

The regularity condition (iii) ensures that near the transition point
\mathsf{a}
,

(2.26)

1-\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathsf{V}}}{\mathrm{d}x}(x)=\frac{\mathsf{c}_{0}}{\pi}(x-\mathsf{a})^{1/2}(1+\mathcal{O}(x-\mathsf{a})),\quad x\searrow\mathsf{a},

for some constant
\mathsf{c}_{0}>0
. This behavior is crucial for the appearance of Airy-type asymptotics.

2.2.4.
Deformed weights

To analyze the six-vertex model through its connection to discrete orthogonal polynomial ensembles, we introduce a deformation of the weight
\omega
that encodes the relevant multiplicative observables. For parameters
t>0
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
, and with the size parameter
N
eventually tending to infinity, define the
deformed weight

(2.27)

\omega^{\sigma}(x)=\omega^{\sigma}(x{\,|\,}s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\left(1+\ee^{-t(x-\mathsf{a}N)-s}\right)\omega(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}.

The dependence of
\omega^{\sigma}
on
t,s
, and
N
is intentional: the shift
t\mathsf{a}N
could be absorbed into
s
, but keeping the two parameters separate is crucial for the asymptotic regimes we study. The dependence on
N
indicates additionally that we are working with a
varying weight
.

Let
\mathsf{Z}_{N}^{\sigma}(s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathsf{Z}_{N}(\omega^{\sigma})
denote the partition function of the discrete orthogonal polynomial ensemble with weight (
2.27
) (recall (
2.11
)). The undeformed partition function corresponds to the limit
s\to+\infty
:

\mathsf{Z}_{N}^{\sigma}(+\infty)=\mathsf{Z}_{N}(\omega).

Here and below, the index “
\sigma
” simply indicates that a quantity is defined with respect to the deformed weight
\omega^{\sigma}
.

2.2.5.
Multiplicative statistics

The deformation (
2.27
) naturally gives rise to the multiplicative statistic

(2.28)

\mathsf{S}_{N}(s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}_{N}}\!\left[\prod_{\lambda\in\mathcal{X}_{N}}\left(1+\ee^{-t(\lambda-\mathsf{a}N)-s}\right)\right],

where
\mathcal{X}_{N}
is a random configuration sampled from the discrete orthogonal polynomial ensemble. A direct application of the definition (
2.11
) shows that this expectation is precisely the ratio of deformed to undeformed partition functions:

(2.29)

\mathsf{S}_{N}(s)=\frac{\mathsf{Z}_{N}^{\sigma}(s)}{\mathsf{Z}_{N}^{\sigma}(+\infty)},\qquad\frac{\mathsf{S}_{N}(s)}{\mathsf{S}_{N}(S)}=\frac{\mathsf{Z}_{N}^{\sigma}(s)}{\mathsf{Z}_{N}^{\sigma}(S)}\quad\text{for any }s,S\in\mathbb{R}.

A closely related statistic probes the
hole process
:

(2.30)

\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}_{N}}\!\left[\prod_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{N}}\frac{1}{1+\ee^{-t(\lambda-\mathsf{a}N)-s}}\right].

The statistics
\mathsf{S}_{N}(s)
and
\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)
are linked through the identity

(2.31)

\log\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)=\log\mathsf{S}_{N}(s)-\sum_{x=1}^{\infty}\log\bigl(1+\ee^{-t(x-\mathsf{a}N)-s}\bigr),

which reflects the complementary contributions of particles and holes.

2.2.6.
The deformation formula

For the deformed weight
\omega^{\sigma}
, let
p_{N,k}^{\sigma}
denote the
k
th monic orthogonal polynomial and let
\gamma_{N,k}^{\sigma}>0
be its (inverse) norming constant (recall (
2.15
)). Denote by
{\mathsf{K}}_{N}^{\sigma}
the associated
deformed Christoffel–Darboux kernel
,

(2.32)

\mathsf{K}_{N}^{\sigma}(x,y)=\mathsf{K}_{N}^{\sigma}(x,y{\,|\,}s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(\gamma_{N,k}^{\sigma})^{2}p_{N,k}^{\sigma}(x)\,p_{N,k}^{\sigma}(y).

The asymptotic analysis of
\mathsf{S}_{N}(s)
and
\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)
and hence their connection to the stochastic six-vertex model, will begin from the properties of these deformed orthogonal polynomials and their kernels.

The starting point for our asymptotic analysis is a formula expressing the ratio of partition functions in terms of an integral involving the deformed Christoffel-Darboux kernel.

Proposition 2.6

(Deformation formula)
.

Let
\omega
satisfy Assumption
2.4
, and let
\omega^{\sigma}
be defined by (
2.27
). For any
s\leq S
,

(2.33)

\log\frac{\mathsf{Z}_{N}^{\sigma}(s)}{\mathsf{Z}_{N}^{\sigma}(S)}=\int_{s}^{S}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}\frac{\ee^{-t(x-\mathsf{a}N)-u}}{1+\ee^{-t(x-\mathsf{a}N)-u}}\mathsf{K}_{N}^{\sigma}(x,x{\,|\,}u)\omega(x)\,\mathrm{d}u,

where
\mathsf{K}_{N}^{\sigma}(\cdot,\cdot{\,|\,}u)
is the Christoffel-Darboux kernel for the deformed weight
\omega^{\sigma}(\cdot{\,|\,}u)
.

For continuous weights, analogues of this deformation formula have appeared recently in the literature
[
30
,
55
]
, see Remark
4.2
below for further details. We show here that Identity (
2.33
) is a special case of a generalized deformation formula applicable to a broad class of orthogonality weights; specifically, we can replace Assumption
2.4
with substantially weaker regularity assumptions. In Section
4
, we adopt the ideas from
[
55
]
to state and prove this general version, utilizing only orthogonality properties, regardless of whether it is discrete or continuous orthogonality.

Proposition
2.6
, together with (
2.29
) and (
2.31
), explains the appearance of the summand in Theorem
C
: aside from explicit prefactors arising in the derivation, the right-hand side in (
1.17
) is essentially the scaling limit of the sum on the right-hand side of (
2.33
). To present the full version of Theorem
C
, we first formalize the three distinct regimes that enter into its formulation as a set of assumptions.

2.2.7.
Parameter regimes

The asymptotic behavior of
\mathsf{S}_{N}(s)
depends critically on the scale of the parameter
s
relative to
N
.

Assumptions 2.7

(Parameter regimes)
.

The parameters
t>0
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
in (
2.27
) satisfy:

(a)

The parameter
t=t(N)
satisfies
T^{-1}\leq t\leq T
for some fixed
T>1
.

(b)

The parameter
s=s(N)
lies in one of the following regimes:

(i)

Subcritical regime:

s_{0}N^{1/3}\leq s\leq s_{0}^{-1}N^{1/2}
for
some large fixed

s_{0}>0
.

(ii)

Critical regime:

|s|\leq s_{0}N^{1/3}

for any fixed

s_{0}>0
.

(iii)

Supercritical regime:

-s_{0}^{-1}N^{1/2}\leq s\leq-s_{0}N^{1/3}

for some large fixed

s_{0}>0
.

When connecting dOPs back to the stochastic six vertex model, we will choose
\mathsf{h}=\mathcal{O}(N^{1/3}s)
. This way, the subcritical, critical, and supercritical regimes correspond respectively to the upper moderate deviations, fluctuation scale, and lower moderate deviations of the height function in the six-vertex model.

2.3.
Universal asymptotics for multiplicative statistics

We now state our main results on the asymptotics of
\mathsf{S}_{N}(s)
, which hold for any dOPE satisfying Assumptions
2.4
and
2.5
. These results reveal a remarkable universality: the asymptotic behavior depends on the specific ensemble only through a single constant
\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}>0
determined by the equilibrium measure. Define

(2.34)

\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\left(\mathsf{c}_{0}\right)^{2/3},

where
\mathsf{c}_{0}
is the constant appearing in (
2.26
). In the result that follows we will use a local coordinate
z\mapsto\varphi(z)
, which is constructed from the equilibrium measure in a canonical way, and it essentially provides an analytic continuation for the density of
\mu_{\mathsf{V}}
, see Proposition
11.15
below for further details. For now it suffices to say that
\varphi
is a conformal map from a neighborhood of
z=\mathsf{a}
with
\varphi(\mathsf{a})=0
and
\varphi^{\prime}(\mathsf{a})=-\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}
, it is independent of
N
, and it is also independent of the deformation parameters
s,t
in (
2.27
).

Our first main result provides leading order asymptotic estimates for
\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)/\mathsf{L}_{N}(S)
, when
s,S
both fall within the same parameter regime. In essence, it says that this quotient has leading asymptotics localized near the transition point
\mathsf{a}
, and that such asymptotics are governed by Airy, Painlevé, or Bessel-type kernels, depending on whether we are in the sub, critical, or supercritical regimes, respectively.

Theorem 2.8

(Localized asymptotics for multiplicative statistics)
.

Let
\omega
satisfy Assumptions
2.4
and
2.5
, and let
s,S
satisfy Assumption
2.7
with
s<S
and
|s|,|S|\leq M^{-1}N^{1/2}
for some large fixed
M>0
. Then

(2.35)

\log\frac{\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)}{\mathsf{L}_{N}(S)}=\\
\frac{1}{N^{1/3}}\int_{s}^{S}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{Z}\\
|x-\mathsf{a}N|<N\delta\end{subarray}}\varphi^{\prime}\left(\frac{x}{N}\right)\mathsf{H}_{N}\left(N^{2/3}\left(\varphi\left(\frac{x}{N}\right)-\varphi\left(\mathsf{a}-\frac{v}{tN}\right)\right){\,|\,}v\right)\frac{\ee^{-t(x-N\mathsf{a}+v/t)}}{(1+\ee^{-t(x-N\mathsf{a}+v/t)})^{2}}\mathrm{d}v+\mathsf{R},

as
N\to\infty
, for any
\delta>0
sufficiently small. The function
\mathsf{H}_{N}(\zeta{\,|\,}\mathsf{s})
and the error term
\mathsf{R}=\mathsf{R}(s,S)
have the following asymptotic behavior in each regime:

(i)

Subcritical regime:

For some
\eta>0
and
s,S
in the subcritical regime, the asymptotic formula

\mathsf{H}_{N}\left(\zeta-\frac{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}}{t}\frac{s}{N^{1/3}}{\,|\,}s\right)=\mathsf{A}\left(\zeta,\zeta\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\ee^{-\eta s/N^{1/3}}\right)

is valid uniformly for
\zeta
in compacts of
\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathsf{A}(x,x)
is the diagonal of the Airy kernel (
1.2
), and the estimate

\mathsf{R}(S,s)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\ee^{-\frac{4}{3}s_{N}^{3/2}}-\ee^{-\frac{4}{3}S_{N}^{3/2}}}{N^{1/3}}\right),\qquad s_{N}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}}{t}\frac{s}{N^{1/3}},\quad S_{N}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}}{t}\frac{S}{N^{1/3}},

is also valid.

(ii)

Critical regime:

For
s,S
in the critical regime, the asymptotic formula

\mathsf{H}_{N}(\zeta{\,|\,}s)=\mathsf{K}_{\rm P34}\left(\zeta,\zeta{\,|\,}\frac{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}}{t}\frac{s}{N^{1/3}}\right)+\mathcal{O}(N^{-1/3}),

is valid uniformly for
\zeta
in compacts of
\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathsf{K}_{\rm P34}(\cdot,\cdot{\,|\,}y)
is the Painlevé XXXIV kernel of (
1.14
) in the Painlevé variable
y
, and the estimate

\mathsf{R}(S,s)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{|S|^{3/2}+|s|^{3/2}}{N^{5/6}}\right)

is also valid.

(iii)

Supercritical regime:

For
s,S
in the supercritical regime, the asymptotic formula

\mathsf{H}_{N}(\zeta{\,|\,}s)=-\frac{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}^{2}}{t^{2}}\frac{s^{2}}{N^{2/3}}\mathsf{J}_{0}\left(\frac{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}^{2}}{t^{2}}\frac{s^{2}}{N^{2/3}}\zeta,\frac{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}^{2}}{t^{2}}\frac{s^{2}}{N^{2/3}}\zeta\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\max\left\{\frac{|s|}{N^{1/3}},\frac{s^{4}}{N^{5/3}}\right\}\right),

is valid uniformly for
|\zeta|\leq\delta
and
\delta>0
sufficiently small, where
\mathsf{J}_{0}
is the Bessel kernel (
1.11
), and the estimate

\mathsf{R}(S,s)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{|s|^{9/4}-|S|^{9/4}}{N^{13/12}}\right)

is also valid.

All error terms are uniform in the stated parameter ranges.

A Taylor expansion around
x=\mathsf{a}
yields

\varphi^{\prime}\left(\frac{x}{N}\right)=-\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{x}{N}-\mathsf{a}\right),

as well as

\varphi\left(\frac{x}{N}\right)-\varphi\left(\mathsf{a}-\frac{v}{tN}\right)=-\frac{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}}{N}\left(x-N\mathsf{a}+\frac{v}{t}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\max\left\{\left(\frac{x}{N}-\mathsf{a}\right)^{2},\frac{s^{2}}{N^{2}}\right\}\right).

These estimates explain how to go from (
2.35
) to its informal version stated in (
1.17
).

The universal character of random matrix theory statistics is reflected upon Theorem
2.8
in the leading behavior of
\mathsf{H}_{N}
: in the subcritical regime it is described by the Airy kernel, the (regular) soft-edge universal limit of random matrix theory, in the supercritical regime it is described by the Bessel kernel, the (regular) hard-edge universal limit, and in the transitional critical regime it is described by the Painlevé XXXIV kernel, the transitional soft-to-hard edge universal limit of RMT
[
31
]
.

In the leading term on the right-hand side of (
2.35
), however, there are two non-universal quantities. The first one is the conformal map
\varphi
, which is constructed from the equilibrium measure of the system. Even though it is non-universal, it should only be viewed as providing the correct coordinate system for the emergence of universality. The second non-universal factor is the exponential term, and in the local coordinate system
\zeta=N^{2/3}\varphi(z)\approx-N^{2/3}\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}(z-\mathsf{a})
with
z=x/N
and
c=\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}/t
it writes as

\frac{\ee^{-t(x-N\mathsf{a}+v/t)}}{(1+\ee^{-t(x-N\mathsf{a}+v/t)})^{2}}\approx\frac{\ee^{cN^{1/3}\zeta-v}}{(1+\ee^{cN^{1/3}\zeta-v})^{2}}.

The right-hand side has two competing quantities. As a function of
\zeta
, it converges pointwise to
0
, indicating that this quantity should become trivial in the local scale of fluctuations
\zeta=\mathcal{O}(N^{2/3})
. However, as a function of
v
, it may well diverge in our regimes of interest.

Nevertheless, after a careful analysis of all the terms involved, the local asymptotics of Theorem
2.8
yield explicit asymptotic formulas for
\log\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)
