. Such formulas are stated in our next result, which is the complete version of Theorem
D
.

Theorem 2.9

(Universal global asymptotics)
.

Under the same hypotheses of Theorem
2.8
,

(i)

Subcritical regime:
There exists
\eta>0
such that for
s
in the subcritical regime, the estimate

(2.36)

\log\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)=-\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\ee^{-\eta s/N^{1/3}}+N^{-\nu}\right)\right)\int_{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}s/(tN^{1/3})}^{\infty}\mathsf{A}(y,y)\,\mathrm{d}y+\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta N^{1/4}}),\quad N\to\infty,

is valid for any
\nu\in(0,1/6)
.

(ii)

Critical regime:
For
s
in the critical regime, the estimate

(2.37)

\log\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)=-\left(1+\mathcal{O}(N^{-\nu})\right)\int_{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}s/(tN^{1/3})}^{\infty}\mathsf{K}_{\rm P34}(0,0{\,|\,}y)\,\mathrm{d}y,\quad N\to\infty,

is valid for any
\nu\in(0,1/3)
.

(iii)

Supercritical regime:
For
s
in the supercritical regime, the estimate

(2.38)

\log\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)=-\frac{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}^{3}}{12t^{3}N}|s|^{3}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{s^{4}}{N^{3/2}}\right),\quad N\to\infty,

is valid.

The transition between the subcritical and supercritical regimes is mediated by the Painlevé XXXIV kernel. Using the identity (
1.21
) and the asymptotic behavior (
1.22
)–(
1.23
), one verifies that the critical regime formulas interpolate smoothly between the other two regimes.

A natural analogue of
\mathsf{L}_{N}
is obtained when replacing the symbol
\sigma_{N}
in the multiplicative statistic by a true characteristic function, say with an endpoint exactly at
\mathsf{a}-s/(tN)
. In such a case,
\mathsf{L}_{N}(\mathsf{s})
consists of the quotient of the partition function of the undeformed weight with a Hankel determinant for a truncated weight. In the setup for continuous weights, analogues of Theorem
2.9
for such type of Hankel determinants have been considered vastly in the literature in recent times, in particular for weights exhibiting Fisher-Hartwig singularities, see for instance
[
18
,
13
,
28
]
and the references therein. However, to our knowledge hard-to-soft edge transition results have been restricted to the analysis of kernels
[
31
,
29
]
. We are unaware of asymptotic results similar to our Theorem
2.9
covering a soft-to-hard edge phase transition, even for continuous weights.

2.4.
The Meixner ensemble and connection to the six-vertex model

We now specialize to the Meixner ensemble, which provides the key link between the general theory of dOPEs and the stochastic six-vertex model.

2.4.1.
Definition of the Meixner ensemble

The
Meixner ensemble
with
N
particles, strength parameter
\alpha>0
, and shape parameter
\beta\in(0,1)
is the dOPE on
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
with weight function
2
2

2

The Meixner ensemble is commonly defined on
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, but for technical reasons for us it is convenient to define it on
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
: in this case, after scaling, during the RHP analysis the lower point in the lattice is
1/N
instead of
0
, which avoids the need of a technical and unimportant analysis around
0
.

(2.39)

\omega_{N}(x)=\omega_{N}(x{\,|\,}\alpha,\beta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+x-1)}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(x)}\beta^{x-1},\qquad x\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}.

The corresponding probability measure is

(2.40)

{\mathbb{P}}_{\rm Meixner(N,\alpha,\beta)}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{N})=\frac{1}{\mathsf{Z}_{N}(\omega_{N})}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\omega_{N}(\lambda_{j}).

The Meixner polynomials, which are the orthogonal polynomials with respect to this weight, form one of the classical families in the Askey scheme
[
64
]
. They satisfy a three-term recurrence relation with explicit coefficients, enabling detailed asymptotic analysis via classical methods
[
60
]
. More detailed asymptotic information for them may also be obtained through Riemann-Hilbert methods
[
5
,
14
]
. In our context here, we are interested in the deformations of the Meixner, and we now verify that they indeed satisfy the conditions required for our main results to hold.

Proposition 2.10
.

For
\mathsf{q}\in(0,1)
,
u>\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}
and
1<\nu<u\mathsf{q}^{1/2}
, consider the Meixner ensemble with parameters

\alpha=(\nu-1)\,N,\beta=\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1}\in(0,1),
and define the rescaled weight

\Omega_{N}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{N}\omega_{N}(Nx),
for
x\in\tfrac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}.

Then Assumptions
2.4
and
2.5
hold with:

(i)

External field

(2.41)

\mathsf{V}(x)=x\log\frac{x}{\beta(\nu+x-1)}+(\nu-1)\log\frac{\nu-1}{\nu+x-1},\qquad x>0.

(ii)

Saturated region
[0,\mathsf{a}]
and band
[\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}]
given by

(2.42)

\mathsf{a}=\frac{(1-\sqrt{\beta})^{2}}{1-\beta}\,(\nu-1),\qquad\mathsf{b}=\frac{(1+\sqrt{\beta})^{2}}{1-\beta}\,(\nu-1).

In particular,
\mathsf{V}
satisfies Assumption
2.4
, and the associated constrained equilibrium measure
\mu_{\mathsf{V}}
satisfies Assumption
2.5
with
\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}=\mathsf{c}_{\nu}^{-1}
and
\mathsf{c}_{\nu}
as in (
2.6
).

The proof follows from standard potential-theoretic arguments; see
[
61
,
38
]
. For the sake of completeness, we outline its proof in Section
3.1
below.

2.4.2.
The Borodin-Olshanski identity

The connection between the stochastic six-vertex model and the Meixner ensemble was discovered by Borodin and Olshanski
[
22
]
.

Proposition 2.11

(Borodin-Olshanski
[
22
]
)
.

For the stochastic six-vertex model with parameters
(\mathsf{q},u)
satisfying
\mathsf{q}\in(0,1)
and
u>\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}
, and any
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{-\mathsf{q}^{\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}}\}
,

(2.43)

\mathbb{E}_{\rm S6V}\left[\prod_{i\geq 1}\frac{1}{1+\zeta\mathsf{q}^{\mathfrak{h}(N,M)+i}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\rm Meixner(N,M-N,\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1})}\left[\prod_{x\in X}(1+\zeta\mathsf{q}^{x})\right]\prod_{i\geq 0}\frac{1}{1+\zeta\mathsf{q}^{i}},

where the expectation on the right is over the Meixner ensemble with the indicated parameters.

This identity expresses a
\mathsf{q}
-Laplace transform of the height function in terms of a multiplicative functional of the Meixner ensemble. The correspondence of parameters is:

(2.44)

n=N,\qquad\alpha=M-N,\qquad\beta=\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1}.

2.4.3.
Asymptotics for the six-vertex model via the Meixner ensemble

Combining Proposition
2.11
with Theorems
2.8
and
2.9
and the choice of parameters

(2.45)

\zeta=\mathsf{q}^{-{\mathsf{a}_{\nu}}N-\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad s=-(\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}+1)\log(1/\mathsf{q})

yields the following result, from which Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
follow.

Theorem 2.12

(Asymptotics of the six-vertex height function)
.

Consider the stochastic six-vertex model with parameters
\mathsf{q}\in(0,1)
and
u>\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}
, and fix
\nu
satisfying
1<\nu<\mathsf{q}^{1/2}u
. Recall
{\mathsf{a}_{\nu}}
and
\mathsf{c}_{\nu}
from (
2.6
) and scale
\zeta
as in (
2.45
).
Then, the following asymptotic estimates hold true.

(i)

Upper tail regime:
For any
\mathsf{h}_{0}>0
sufficiently large, there exists
\eta>0
such that the estimate

(2.46)

\log\mathbb{E}_{\rm S6V}\left[\prod_{i\geq 1}\frac{1}{1+\zeta\mathsf{q}^{\mathfrak{h}(\nu N,N)+i}}\right]=-\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\ee^{-\eta s/N^{1/3}}+N^{-\nu}\right)\right)\int_{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}s/(tN^{1/3})}^{\infty}\mathsf{A}(y,y)\,\mathrm{d}y+\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta N^{1/4}}),

is valid for any
\nu\in(0,1/6)
, uniformly for
\mathsf{h}_{0}\leq\mathsf{h}\leq\mathsf{h}_{0}^{-1}N^{1/6}
.

(ii)

Fluctuations regime:
Given
\mathsf{h}_{0}>0
, the estimate

(2.47)

\log\mathbb{E}_{\rm S6V}\left[\prod_{i\geq 1}\frac{1}{1+\zeta\mathsf{q}^{\mathfrak{h}(\nu N,N)+i}}\right]=-\left(1+\mathcal{O}(N^{-\nu})\right)\int_{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}s/(tN^{1/3})}^{\infty}\mathsf{K}_{\rm P34}(0,0{\,|\,}y)\,\mathrm{d}y

holds true for any
\nu\in(0,1/3)
, uniformly for
|\mathsf{h}|\leq\mathsf{h}_{0}
.

(iii)

Lower tail regime:
For any
\mathsf{h}_{0}>0
sufficiently large, the estimate

(2.48)

\log\mathbb{E}_{\rm S6V}\left[\prod_{i\geq 1}\frac{1}{1+\zeta\mathsf{q}^{\mathfrak{h}(\nu N,N)+i}}\right]=-\frac{\mathsf{c}_{\nu}^{-3}}{12t^{3}N}|s|^{3}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{s^{4}}{N^{3/2}}\right),

is valid uniformly for
-\mathsf{h}_{0}^{-1}N^{1/6}\leq\mathsf{h}\leq-\mathsf{h}_{0}
.

The derivation of Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
from Theorem
2.12
proceeds via soft probabilistic arguments relating the
\mathsf{q}
-Laplace transform to tail probabilities. Assuming Theorem
2.12
, we complete their proofs in Section
3
.

The starting input in the proof of Theorem
2.12
is the connection between the stochastic six-vertex model and the Meixner ensemble, as outlined in Proposition
2.11
. Because of this, the slope parameter
\nu
must satisfy
\nu>1
. Indeed, the strength parameter
\alpha
of the associated Meixner orthogonal polynomial ensemble takes the form
(\nu-1)N
, which must be positive. Although this may at first appear to be a restriction, the regime
\nu<1
can also be investigated. In that case, an analogue of Theorem
2.12
may be obtained by exploiting the link between the stochastic six-vertex model and the Krawtchouk orthogonal polynomial ensemble, as we explain next.

Remark 2.13

(Extension to
\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1}<\nu\leq 1
)
.

While Theorem
2.12
is stated for the case
\nu>1
, the techniques developed in this paper can be extended to the regime
\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1}<\nu\leq 1
using a variant of the Borodin-Olshanski identity. Specifically, for
M\leq N
,
[
22
, Remark 8.6]
establishes that

(2.49)

\mathbb{E}_{\rm S6V}\left[\prod_{i\geq 1}\frac{1}{1+\zeta\mathsf{q}^{\mathfrak{h}(M,N)+i}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\rm Meixner^{\circ}(M-1,N-M+2,\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1})}\left[\prod_{x\in X+(N-M+1)}\frac{1}{1+\zeta\mathsf{q}^{x}}\right],

where
X+S
denotes the deterministic shift of all points in the random configuration
X
by
S
. The deterministic shift by
N-M+1
reflects the fact that
\mathfrak{h}(M,N)\geq N-M+1
in this regime. The asymptotic analysis of the multiplicative statistics in (
2.49
) requires verifying a modified version of Proposition
2.10
for the shifted Meixner ensemble, as detailed in Proposition
3.1
below. In this modified setup, the parameter
N
is replaced by
\widetilde{N}=\lfloor\nu N\rfloor-1
, after scaling particles start at positions to the right of
\nu^{-1}-1
, the saturated region becomes
[\nu^{-1}-1,\mathsf{a}]
where
\mathsf{a}=\frac{(1-\sqrt{\beta})}{(1-\beta)}(\nu^{-1}-1)
, and the band region becomes
[\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}]
where
\mathsf{b}=\frac{(1+\sqrt{\beta})}{(1-\beta)}(\nu^{-1}-1)
. The external field
\mathsf{V}(x)
is modified by shifting its argument by
(\nu^{-1}-1)
. Once this verification is complete, the general framework of Theorem
2.8
applies directly, yielding the analogue of Theorem
2.12
for
\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1}<\nu\leq 1
. We omit the detailed calculations for brevity, focusing instead on the case
\nu>1
.

In the same paper
[
22
, Remark 8.7]
, the authors showed that

(2.50)

\mathbb{E}_{\rm S6V}\left[\prod_{i\geq 1}\frac{1}{1+\zeta\mathsf{q}^{\mathfrak{h}(N,M)+i}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\rm Krawtchouk(M-1,(1+\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1})^{-1},N+M-2)}\left[\prod_{x\in X}\frac{1}{1+\zeta\mathsf{q}^{x}}\right],

where
\rm Krawtchouk(M-1,(1+\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1})^{-1},N+M-2)
is the
(M-1)
-particle orthogonal polynomial ensemble on
\{0,\ldots,N+M-2\}
with weight function

w(x)=\binom{N+M-2}{x}(1+\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1})^{-x}\left(\frac{\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1}}{1+\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1}}\right)^{N+M-2-x},\qquad x\in\{0,\ldots,N+M-2\}.

One may alternatively use (
2.50
) to derive the asymptotics for the
\mathsf{q}
-Laplace transform of the stochastic six-vertex model height function. However, we note that such an asymptotic result will not directly follow from our general theorem on multiplicative statistics for discrete orthogonal polynomial ensembles (Theorem
2.8
), because the Krawtchouk ensemble violates one of the assumptions of that theorem, namely, that the ensemble be supported on a half-infinite integer lattice rather than on a finite interval. Nevertheless, Theorem
2.8
can be extended to include the Krawtchouk case, with appropriate modifications to the proof.

In
[
22
]
, Borodin and Olshanski also obtain a reduction to (
2.43
),(
2.50
), corresponding to the degeneration of the stochastic six-vertex model to the ASEP. We foresee the ideas developed in this paper to also apply to this reduction, and yield similar results for the ASEP. However, such reduction requires analyzing also the limit
t\to 0
simultaneously with
N\to\infty
, so unlike the mentioned Krawtchouk situation the rigorous analysis requires investigation that goes beyond what we do here, and we leave it to a future work.

2.5.
Further connections

Tail probabilities of stochastic processes are a fundamental tool in understanding the qualitative behaviors of various random models, particularly those in the Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) universality class. For models within this class, tail probabilities have been extensively studied through diverse mathematical frameworks, such as random matrix theory, Riemann-Hilbert analysis, Schrödinger operators, weak noise theory, and the Gibbs property of the Brownian ensemble, among others. These approaches have enabled insights into the asymptotic behaviors and extremal statistics of these models.

A prominent example is the last passage percolation model with exponential weights, which belongs to the KPZ universality class. The foundational work of Johansson
[
61
]
initiated the study of tail probabilities for this model, with large deviation results. About the same time, Löwe and Merkl
[
67
,
67
]
obtained optimal moderate deviations for the length of the longest increasing subsequence in random permutations. For last passage percolation with geometric weights, Baik, Deift, McLaughlin, Miller and Zhou
[
7
]
obtained optimal moderate deviation estimates utilizing Riemann-Hilbert methods. Later contributions by Ledoux and Rider
[
69
]
established upper bounds for both the upper and lower tail probabilities, valid also in the moderate deviations regime for beta ensembles, which by distributional identities include some of the mentioned discrete growth models.

For the KPZ equation itself, studies of tail properties were pioneered by Corwin and the first-named author, in
[
34
]
, where the authors derived tight bounds on the lower and upper tail probabilities for the narrow wedge initial condition. A crucial aspect of this analysis involved an identity analogue to (
2.43
), connecting the double exponential moments of the KPZ equation, with the expectation of a multiplicative functional of the Airy
2
point process. This connection, originally noted by Borodin and Gorin in
[
21
]
, facilitated the application of random matrix techniques to derive precise tail bounds, including in the large deviation regime
[
34
]
.

In subsequent work, Corwin and the first-named author
[
32
]
leveraged the Brownian Gibbs property of the KPZ line ensemble to generalize these tail probability bounds to a broader set of initial conditions. This research opened the door to numerous further studies on the exact characterization of tail probabilities for the KPZ equation. Notable advancements in this direction include works by Cafasso and Claeys
[
27
]
, Cafasso, Claeys, and Ruzza
[
26
]
, Tsai
[
78
]
, Das and Tsai
[
37
]
, Ghosal and Lin
[
54
]
, and Ganguly and Hegde
[
52
]
. These studies have employed refined techniques from integrable systems, probabilistic analysis, and determinantal point processes to further elucidate the precise asymptotic behavior and distributional properties of the KPZ equation’s tail probabilities.

As mentioned earlier, Das, Liao and Mucciconi obtained recently lower tail large deviation results for the stochastic six-vertex model
[
40
]
. Their starting point is also Borodin-Olshanski’s identity (
2.43
), but their asymptotic techniques are very distinct from ours. Shortly before, in
[
39
]
the same authors also obtained large deviation results for the
q
-deformed polynuclear growth (PNG) model, a system introduced in
[
2
]
that generalizes PNG to include a deformation parameter
q
, using a similar identity relating the
q
-PNG height function to a multiplicative statistics of a point process. For the six-vertex model speed process, Drillick and Haunschmid-Sibitz
[
47
]
derived tail bounds by leveraging Borodin-Olshanski’s formula combined with concentration inequalities, though their bounds do not capture the precise exponential rate. In related work on discrete models, Corwin and Hegde
[
35
]
obtained tail bounds for the
q
-pushTASEP using a combination of coupling arguments with last passage percolation and moment bound techniques, demonstrating yet another approach to understanding tail behavior in integrable stochastic systems.

2.6.
Outline of the proof and organization of the paper

The paper is organized naturally into four parts. Each part is largely self-contained, although a few key results and pieces of notation are used across different parts. Conceptually, each part may be viewed as providing a “black box” that is invoked in the subsequent parts. These parts are roughly described as follows.

(1)

Part 1: Proof of the tail bounds of S6V; a general deformation formula for the partition functions of OP ensemble; rough estimates for
\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)
, when
s\geq\delta N^{1/2}
.

(2)

Part 2: introduction of a novel Riemann-Hilbert problem, and its asymptotic analysis.

(3)

Part 3: analysis of the Riemann-Hilbert problem for the deformed orthogonal polynomials, for
s
in one of the regimes from Assumption
2.7
.

(4)

Part 4: asymptotic analysis of the deformation formula (
2.33
).

We now elaborate on each of these parts.

2.6.1.
About Part 1

Part 1 consists of Sections
3
,
4
and
5
, which are three independent sections.

Assuming the asymptotics of the multiplicative statistics of Meixner ensemble from Theorem
2.9
, Section
3
explains how these results yield the large deviation principles for the stochastic six-vertex model stated in Theorems
2.2
and
2.1
. The proofs of both upper and lower tail results follow a three-step strategy that bridges probability tail bounds with moment asymptotics. The key technical bridge is provided by Lemmas
3.2
and
3.3
, which relate tail probabilities of the rescaled height fluctuation
\mathfrak{h}
to the multiplicative statistic
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{S6V}}[\cdot]
appearing on the left-hand side of (
2.43
) with a key choice of
\zeta
. The proofs of these comparison lemmas exploit the concentration of the product in the expectation on events where the height function takes specific values; by carefully splitting this product at an appropriate cutoff index and using logarithmic inequalities, we show that deviations of the height function away from the target value lead to exponentially suppressed contributions that are negligible compared to the main deviation exponents of order
\mathsf{h}^{3}
or
\mathsf{h}^{3/2}
. Once the tail probabilities are expressed in terms of this expectation
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{S6V}}[\cdot]
, we apply the asymptotic formulas from Theorem
2.12
to conclude Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
.

Section
4
establishes Proposition
4.1
, which provides the general deformation formula that underlies our entire asymptotic analysis and is of independent interest beyond the specific application to the Meixner ensemble and S6VM model. The setup considers a reference Borel measure
\mu_{0}
with finite moments and a one-parameter family of deformed measures. The key result expresses the logarithmic derivative of the partition function as an integral involving the kernel diagonal and the derivative of the weight. This deformation formula allows us to translate the asymptotic analysis of the RHP for deformed orthogonal polynomials (Part 3 & 4) into asymptotic formulas for the partition function ratios, and hence for the multiplicative statistics that encode the tail probabilities of the S6VM height function. The proof of the deformation formula we provide starts from a known relation between the log derivative of the partition function and a sum of log derivatives of norming constants. The latter are then related to a total weighted integral of a derivative of the deformed kernel. From there, we use integration by parts and the reproducing property of the kernel to arrive at the result.

While Theorem
2.8
itself provides precise asymptotics for the multiplicative statistics
\mathsf{S}_{N}(s)
when
|s|\leq\delta N^{1/2}
, its proof also requires obtaining a rough bound for the regime
s=\delta N^{1/2}
separately, which Section
5
addresses through a probabilistic analysis exploiting the determinantal structure of OPEs.

To obtain such bound, we work directly with determinantal structure. At a scale
s=\delta N^{1/2}
, the leading nontrivial contributions to the statistic
\mathsf{S}_{N}(s)
from (
2.28
) comes at particles that sit far to the left of the saturated-to-band edge
\mathsf{a}
. In this region, particles are sitting at every site with very high probability, and we are able to localize
\mathsf{S}_{N}(s)
for contributions coming solely from this region. We then use arguments relying on the determinantal structure, together with basic probabilistic inequalities, to reduce the analysis of
\mathsf{S}_{N}(s)
to the analysis of the counting function of an appropriately chosen interval that sits within the saturated region. By then exploiting the Fredholm series representation, together with known asymptotics for the correlation kernel of the undeformed point process, we are able to obtain sufficient control on this counting function, that ultimately translates into a for
\mathsf{S}_{N}(\delta N^{1/2})
which is not sharp, but sufficient for our purposes.

2.6.2.
About Part 2

Part 2 consists of Sections
6
,
7
,
8
and
9
. As usual in RHP literature, the asymptotic analysis of the RHP for the deformed orthogonal polynomials requires the construction of a so-called local parametrix. In our case, thanks to both the discrete nature of the original RHP and the deformed weight that lives on a critical scale, this local parametrix is based on a novel model RHP, and Part 2 is devoted to studying this RHP, as we now outline.

In Section
6
we introduce this model problem in a self-contained language, and obtain some auxiliary results for quantities related to it. But unlike previous literature, this model problem is not independent of the large parameter
N
: depends on the parameter
s
of the deformation, which grows with
N
. To our knowledge, the first appearance of such
N
-dependent model problems in the context of OPs was observed in our previous work
[
55
]
. In the mentioned work the model problem used essentially had a regular large
N
-limit without the need of performing any other scaling. However, over here this is not quite the case. As a consequence of the fact that we are dealing with weights that blow up with
N
, the asymptotic analysis of the model problem is a task on its own, and has to be performed separately for each of the regimes in
s
.

To cope with the asymptotic analysis of the model problem, three different RHPs have to be studied, and in Section
7
we discuss them. The first two of them are related to the integro-differential Painlevé II, and to the Painlevé XXXIV equations. These RHPs are not new, but we need to tailor several aspects of them to our needs in the asymptotic analysis of the model problem. For the former, we perform a quick asymptotic analysis to show that it is asymptotically described by the Airy RHP. For the latter, we collect some basic facts, in particular showing the relations (
1.21
) and (
1.14
), which to the best of our knowledge are novel.

But, as it turns out, the analysis of the model problem in the supercritical regime is more subtle, and requires the introduction of an yet new model problem, which we call the modified model problem, and which differs from our initial model problem by its asymptotic behavior at
\infty
. The modified model problem is introduced Section
7.3
, and its necessary asymptotic analysis is performed in the same section. As a result, we show that it is asymptotically close to the Bessel RHP.

In Section
8
, we perform the asymptotic analysis of the model problem, in which the aforementioned two RHPs are used. In the subcritical regime, we compare it with the RHP for the integro-differential Painlevé II, but in a regime where the latter is asymptotically described by the Airy RHP. In the critical regime, we need the construction of a local parametrix and a global parametrix. The local parametrix is explicitly constructed through Cauchy integrals. The global parametrix, instead, is constructed using the Painlevé XXXIV RHP mentioned. Continuing in Section
8
, the asymptotic analysis of the model problem in the supercritical regime requires the construction of an explicit global parametrix, and a local parametrix in terms of the modified model problem.

Section
9
may be seen as the black-box from part 2: in this section we summarize all the asymptotic results from the previous sections of this part, tailored for direct application in the subsequent parts.

Apart from minor technical assumptions, the model problem that we introduce may be seen as a generalization of an RHP introduced by Cafasso, Claeys and Ruzza for the integro-differential Painlevé II equation
[
27
,
26
]
. This extension is nontrivial, in the sense that it involves one more function (spectral data) and jumps in additional contours when compared to
[
27
,
26
]
. Such extra data essentially encodes the fact that, at its core, we are using the model problem for a problem of discrete nature. A detailed comparison is explained in Remark
6.13
below.

The asymptotic analysis of the model problem we just outlined follows the major lines performed in
[
26
]
, and the final outputs are described by the same objects, the Airy, Painlevé XXXIV and Bessel RHPs. However, in virtue of this additional spectral data, several technical constructions have to be performed substantially differently than in the mentioned work, and the quantities we need from the model RHP are different than the ones studied in
[
26
]
. In particular, in
[
26
]
the analysis of the RHP yields asymptotics for their functions of interest in a straightforward manner, as they are contained in a certain residue of the RHP at
\infty
. In contrast, in Part 2 the major quantity of interest for us is essentially the function
\mathsf{H}_{N}
from Theorem
2.8
, and even after completing the asymptotic analysis of the model problem there is still considerable labor needed to extract asymptotics for
\mathsf{H}_{N}
in the detailed manner we need.

2.6.3.
About Part 3

Part 3 comprises Sections
10
,
11
, and
12
. In Section
10
, we recall the Riemann-Hilbert problem for discrete orthogonal polynomials, list relevant identities, and implement the scaling corresponding to the lattice transformation
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\mapsto\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.

In Section
11
, we perform the Deift-Zhou nonlinear steepest descent analysis for the discrete orthogonal polynomials associated with the deformed weight
\omega^{\sigma}
in (
2.27
). While the transformations generally follow standard literature
[
5
,
14
]
, we introduce necessary modifications to address specific obstructions. First, the deformation component
(1+\ee^{\bullet})
of the deformed weight
\omega^{\sigma}
introduces poles in the complex plane for the inverse
(\omega^{\sigma})^{-1}
. As
N\to\infty
, these poles, after scaling, accumulate near the real axis at
z=\mathsf{a}-s/(tN)
. Consequently, we cannot open lenses around this point, and our transformations must accommodate this singularity structure. Second, due to these poles, the local parametrix near
z=\mathsf{a}
must be constructed using the novel model RHP developed in Part 2. Crucially, this local parametrix retains dependence on the large parameter
N
, even at the local scale.

The asymptotic analysis concludes at the end of Section
11
. Subsequently, in Section
12
, we trace back the explicit transformations. Our primary objective is to evaluate the right-hand side of (
2.33
), which requires the values of the kernel
\mathsf{K}_{N}^{\sigma}(x,x{\,|\,}u)
along the entire positive axis. We therefore invert the transformations in all regions of the positive axis, accounting for cancellations between various terms. The final result of Section
11
expresses the right-hand side of (
2.33
) via quantities defined in Part 2, in a format suitable for the application of the framework established therein.

2.6.4.
About Part 4

Part 4 consists of Sections
13
and
14
. Having established the asymptotic behavior of the RHP for deformed orthogonal polynomials and an appropriate expression for the right-hand side of (
2.33
) in terms of RHP quantities in Part 3, we now turn to the asymptotic analysis of the latter. This part constitutes the final step in proving our main results.

The starting point is the rewriting of formula from Section
12.2.2
, which at the end takes the form

\log\frac{\mathsf{Z}_{N}^{\sigma}(s)}{\mathsf{Z}^{\sigma}_{N}(S)}=\mathsf{S}(S,s)+\mathsf{R}(S,s)+\text{explicit terms},

where
\mathsf{S}
captures the main asymptotic contribution and
\mathsf{R}
represents terms arising from the RHP analysis, which are to be shown to be error term. All these terms are expressed in quantities related to the model problem from Part 2 and the RHP for OPs from part 3, in a format ready for application of the black box summarized in Section
12.2.2
. Section
13
is devoted to the asymptotic analysis of these terms, through applications of results from Section
12.2.2
together with further asymptotic methods as we now explain.

We begin by showing that certain explicit terms involving sums over the gap region are exponentially negligible. The analysis then splits into estimating
\mathsf{R}
and
\mathsf{S}
separately, with the key challenge being that their asymptotic behavior depends critically on which regime (subcritical, critical, or supercritical) the parameter
s
belongs to.

For the error term
\mathsf{R}
, we exploit the estimates from Part 3 on the transformed RHP solution. The main observation is that contributions from points far from the band edge
\mathsf{a}
decay exponentially due to the Euler-Lagrange conditions. For points near
\mathsf{a}
but outside a small neighborhood (the “outer” region), we use crude bounds on the model RHP showing exponential decay. The dominant contribution comes from the “inner” region near
\alpha
, where we apply the precise asymptotics from Part 2. However, even these inner contributions are shown to be subleading compared to
\mathsf{S}
in each regime. The key technical tool is a careful decomposition of the summation domain combined with the growth/decay estimates on the conformal map
\varphi
and the model problem controlling the deformation.

\mathsf{b}

Figure 5
.
Spatial decomposition for analyzing the error term
\mathsf{R}
. The summation domain is divided into regions based on distance from the band edge
\mathsf{a}
. Far regions (saturated, gap and band, depicted in blue and green) contribute exponentially small terms
O(\ee^{-\eta N})
via Euler-Lagrange inequalities. The outer region (orange) near
\mathsf{a}
yields exponential decay through crude bounds on the model RHP. The inner region (red) requires precise asymptotics from Part 3, but remains subleading to the main term
\mathsf{S}
. The conformal map
\varphi
and the model problem from Part 2 are essential technical tools.

The analysis of
\mathsf{S}
is more delicate and constitutes the heart of Part 4. The quantity
\mathsf{S}
is expressed as a sum involving the function
\mathsf{H}_{N}
, whose asymptotics were established in Part 2. Specifically, from the deformation formula we obtain

(2.51)

\mathsf{S}(S,s)=-\frac{1}{N}\int_{s}^{S}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm in})}}\zeta^{\prime}(x)\mathsf{H}_{N}(\zeta(x)-\zeta_{N}(v){\,|\,}v)\frac{\ee^{-Nt(x-z_{N}(v))}}{(1+\ee^{-Nt(x-z_{N}(v))})^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}v.

As mentioned earlier, the sum over spatial points cannot be approximated by a Riemann integral because the weight factor
\ee^{-Nt(x-z_{N}(v))}/(1+\ee^{-Nt(x-z_{N}(v))})^{2}
already lives at the correct scale. Instead, we employ a strategy based on Poisson summation.

The key insight is that this weight factor is sharply concentrated around the point
z_{N}(v)
where the deformation parameter vanishes. Near this point, both the conformal map derivative
\varphi^{\prime}
and the function
\mathsf{H}_{N}
are approximately constant, allowing us to factor them out. The remaining sum over lattice points is then exactly computed using the Poisson summation formula (Lemma
13.6
), which gives

(2.52)

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\ee^{-tk+u}}{(1+\ee^{-tk+u})^{2}}=\frac{1}{t}+\frac{8\pi}{t^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{k\cos(2\pi ku/t)}{\ee^{2\pi^{2}k/t}-\ee^{-2\pi^{2}k/t}}.

The leading term
1/t
provides the main contribution, while the oscillatory cosine terms are shown to be negligible through integration by parts
a là
stationary phase arguments.

For the subcritical regime, where
N^{1/3}\ll s\leq cN^{1/2}
, the function
\mathsf{H}_{N}
is asymptotically given by the diagonal Airy kernel
\mathsf{A}(\zeta,\zeta)
. We show that contributions from points where
\zeta(x)
is far from
\zeta_{N}(v)
are exponentially suppressed by combining the exponential decay in
\mathsf{A}
with the exponential concentration of the weight factor. The dominant contribution comes from points where
|\zeta(x)-\zeta_{N}(v)|\ll N^{-\nu}
for small
\nu>0
. After applying Poisson summation, the oscillatory terms are shown to be negligible via integration by parts. The leading term reduces to an integral of
\mathsf{A}(y,y)
with respect to the rescaled variable
y=\mathsf{c}_{\varphi}v/(tN^{1/3})
, as stated in Theorem
13.8
.

In the critical regime, where
-CN^{1/3}\leq s\leq CN^{1/3}
, the function
\mathsf{H}_{N}
is instead approximated by the Painlevé XXXIV kernel
\mathsf{K}_{{\rm P34}}(0,0|\zeta_{N}(v))
. The parameter
\zeta_{N}(v)
remains bounded in this regime, which allows for a simpler error analysis. Similar concentration arguments show that the sum is dominated by points where
|x-z_{N}(v)|\ll N^{-2/3-\nu}
, and again Poisson summation combined with integration by parts eliminates the oscillatory terms. The result is Theorem
13.9
, expressing
\mathsf{S}
as an integral of
\mathsf{K}_{{\rm P34}}(0,0|y)
.

The supercritical regime, where
-cN^{1/2}\leq s\ll-N^{1/3}
, requires yet different estimates. Here
\mathsf{H}_{N}
involves the Bessel kernel
\mathsf{J}_{0}
, but with an additional rescaling through the variable
\xi={\zeta_{N}(s)}^{2}\zeta
. We decompose the summation domain into regions where
|\xi-\xi_{N}(s)|
is very small (
\ll N^{-1/6}
) versus moderately small (
\ll 1
). For the very small region, the Bessel kernel is approximately constant at
1/4
, while for the moderate region, we use bounds on the oscillatory behavior plus small growth of Bessel functions to show exponential decay when combined with the weight factor. After Poisson summation, the leading term is a cubic polynomial in
s
, as shown in Theorem
13.10
.

A crucial ingredient throughout these calculations is Proposition
13.5
, which provides precise bounds on the error term
\mathsf{R}
in each regime. These bounds are always subleading compared to the main term
\mathsf{S}
, confirming that the asymptotics are indeed captured by the latter.

Finally, Section
14
assembles all the pieces. We combine the asymptotics of
\mathsf{S}
from the three regimes with the error estimates on
\mathsf{R}
and the analysis of the explicit terms. The relation between
\mathsf{L}_{N}
and the partition functions established in (
2.29
) and (
2.31
)
allows us to translate results about
\mathsf{Z}_{N}^{\sigma}
into statements about the multiplicative statistics. Taking appropriate derivatives with respect to the parameter
s
yields Theorem
2.8
, while direct integration gives Theorem
2.9
. The key observation is that certain logarithmic sums telescope or are exponentially small, leaving only the terms involving
\mathsf{S}
and
\mathsf{R}
that we have already analyzed.

2.7.
Basic notations

Values
\varepsilon,\delta,\epsilon>0
are used to represent small values, independent of other parameters unless otherwise explicitly mentioned. Their values may change from line to line, and in each occurrence they can always be taken sufficiently small. Likewise,
M,\eta>0
are also used to represent positive constants, independent of other parameters, whose values may change from line to line.

For us, the set of non-negative integer numbers is
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
, and the set of strictly positive numbers is
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{1,2,\ldots\}
. Likewise, we denote
\mathbb{R}_{\geq}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=[0,\infty)
and
\mathbb{R}_{>}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(0,\infty)
.

Unless otherwise stated, every branch of
z^{\alpha}
, as well as of
\log z
, is considered to be the principal value, that is, coming from
\arg z\in(-\pi,\pi)
, with a branch cut along the negative real axis. We reserve the notation
\sqrt{\cdot}
for the positive square root, which is defined only for non-negative real values.

For us
D_{r}(p)
denotes the open disk centered at
p\in\mathbb{C}
and radius
r>0
. For a set
G\subset\mathbb{C}
, its topological closure is denoted by
\overline{G}
. The closed disk, however, is denoted by
\overline{D}_{r}(p)
rather than the clumsier notation
\overline{D_{r}(p)}
.

During the whole paper, we will be handling both scalar and
2\times 2
matrix-valued RHPs that depend on complex variables and parameters. Matrices will always be denoted by bold letters, such as
\mathbf{R},\mathbf{G},\mathbf{\Phi}
etc. In particular, we use extensively the notation

\mathbf{E}_{11}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{E}_{21}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{E}_{12}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{E}_{22}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

We also denote the
2\times 2
identity matrix by
\mathbf{I}=\mathbf{E}_{11}+\mathbf{E}_{22}
, and make extensive use of the Pauli matrices

\bm{\sigma}_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{E}_{12}+\mathbf{E}_{21},\quad\bm{\sigma}_{2}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=-\mathrm{i}\mathbf{E}_{12}+\mathrm{i}\mathbf{E}_{21},\quad\bm{\sigma}_{3}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{E}_{11}-\mathbf{E}_{22},

and the auxiliary matrix

\mathbf{U}_{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathrm{i}\bm{\sigma}_{1}\right).

We usually refer to the complex variables as spectral variables, and denote them by letters
z,\zeta,w,\xi
. Derivatives with respect to the spectral variables are always denoted by
′
, whereas derivatives with respect to parameters are denoted using differentials, e.g.
\partial=\partial_{y},\partial_{s}
etc.

This paper will deal with matrix-valued Riemann-Hilbert problems, and as such it will involve
2\times 2
matrix-valued functions
\mathbf{M}:G\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
, with
G
being a subset of the complex plane, typically a union of domains or a contour. We view the entries of
\mathbf{M}
as functions in
L^{p}=L^{p}(\mathrm{d}\mu)
with
\mu
being the planar Lebesgue measure when
G
is a domain, or
\mu
being the arc-length measure when
G
is a union of contours. As such, we use the pointwise norm

|\mathbf{M}(z)|\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{i,j=1,2}|\mathbf{M}_{ij}(z)|,\quad z\in G,

and the induced
L^{p}
norms

\|\mathbf{M}\|_{\infty}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{i,j=1,2}\|\mathbf{M}_{ij}\|_{L^{p}}.

We will mostly use
p=\infty
when
G
is a union of domains, or
p=1,2,\infty
when
G
is a union of contours.

Often, we will deal with matrices depending on parameters, say
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}_{t}
with
t
large, and we will seek for precise asymptotics in different
L^{p}
norms. For given
p_{1},p_{2}\in[1,\infty]
, a matrix
\mathbf{M}_{0}
and a positive real-valued function
f(t)
, when we say that
\mathbf{M}_{t}=\mathbf{M}_{0}+\mathcal{O}(f(t))
in
L^{p_{1}}\cap L^{p_{2}}(G)
we mean that

\|\mathbf{M}_{t}-\mathbf{M}_{0}\|_{L^{p_{j}}(G)}=\mathcal{O}(f(t))\quad\text{as }t\to\infty,\text{ for both }j=1,2.

For a matrix-valued function
\mathbf{M}(z)
which is invertible and has analytic entries, we denote

(2.53)

\mathbf{\Delta}\mathbf{M}(z)=\mathbf{\Delta}_{z}\mathbf{M}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{M}(z)^{-1}\mathbf{M}^{\prime}(z).

Observe that if
z\mapsto\zeta=\varphi(z)
is a conformal change of variables and
\mathbf{N}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{M}(\varphi(z))
, then

(2.54)

\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{N}(\zeta)\mathrm{d}\zeta=\mathbf{\Delta}_{z}\mathbf{M}(z)\mathrm{d}z.

We will also use certain canonical functions.
\ai
denotes the Airy function, and for
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
we use
\mathsf{A}(x,y)
to denote the Airy kernel as in (
1.2
).

A direct calculation with asymptotics of Airy functions show that

(2.55)

\mathsf{A}(x,x)=\frac{1}{8\pi x}\ee^{-\frac{4}{3}x^{3/2}}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(x^{-3/2})\right),\quad x\to+\infty,

as well as

(2.56)

\mathsf{A}(x,x)=\frac{|x|^{1/2}}{\pi}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(|x|^{-3/2})\right),\quad x\to-\infty.

With
\Jb_{\nu}
being the Bessel function of first kind and order
\nu>-1
, the Bessel kernel is defined for
x,y>0
as

(2.57)

\mathsf{J}_{\nu}(x,y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\Jb_{\nu}(\sqrt{x})\Jb^{\prime}_{\nu}(\sqrt{y})\sqrt{y}-\Jb_{\nu}(\sqrt{y})\Jb^{\prime}_{\nu}(\sqrt{x})\sqrt{x}}{x-y},\quad x\neq y,\qquad\mathsf{J}_{\nu}(x,x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{4}\left(\Jb_{\nu}^{\prime}(\sqrt{x})^{2}+\Jb_{\nu}(\sqrt{x})^{2}\right).

We are particularly interested in the Bessel kernel of order
\nu=0
. Even though the Bessel kernel is defined for probabilistic reasons only for
x,y>0
, the function
x\mapsto\mathsf{J}_{0}(x,x)
is an entire function of
x\in\mathbb{C}
. For this particular case,

(2.58)

\mathsf{J}_{0}(x,x)=\frac{1}{2\pi x^{1/2}}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(x^{-1/2})\right),\quad x\to+\infty,\qquad\mathsf{J}_{0}(x,x)=\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{16}x+\mathcal{O}(x^{2}),\quad x\to 0,

(2.59)

\mathsf{J}_{0}(x,x)=\frac{1}{8\pi}\frac{\ee^{2|x|^{1/2}}}{|x|}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(|x|^{-1/2})\right),\quad x\to-\infty.

3.
Proof of Theorem
2.1
and
2.2

Assuming Theorem
2.9
, the overall outline of the proof of Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
is the following:

(1)

Step (a): verify that the conditions for Theorem
2.9
are met for the Meixner weight. That is, verify Proposition
2.10
.

(2)

Step (b): establish upper and lower tail bounds for the tail probabilities of the stochastic six-vertex model in terms of
\mathsf{q}
-Laplace transform
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{S6M}}
in (
2.43
).

(3)

Step (c): Apply Theorem
2.12
carefully.

Each of the steps above is established in the subsequent subsections.

3.1.
Proof of Proposition
2.10

We first verify Assumptions
2.4
followed by Assumption
2.5
.

1. Verification of Assumption
2.4
.

Write

\omega_{N}(k)=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+k)}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(k+1)}\,\beta^{\,k-1},\qquad\alpha=(\nu-1)N,\quad 0<\beta<1,

and set
k=Nx
with
x\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Then

(3.1)

\log\Omega_{N}(x)=-\log N+\log\Gamma(\alpha+Nx)-\log\Gamma(\alpha)-\log\Gamma(Nx+1)+(Nx-1)\log\beta.

We use Stirling’s formula with a remainder that is analytic and uniformly bounded on sectors:

\log\Gamma(z)=\Big(z-\tfrac{1}{2}\Big)\log z-z+\tfrac{1}{2}\log(2\pi)+r(z),\qquad r(z)=O(1/|z|),

valid uniformly in any fixed sector containing the positive real axis.
Apply this to
\Gamma(\alpha+Nx)
,
\Gamma(\alpha)
and
\Gamma(Nx+1)
.
With
\alpha=(\nu-1)N
we obtain, after an elementary but slightly long algebra (all
N
–independent terms in
x
can be grouped into the normalization
\mathsf{C}_{N}
),

\frac{1}{N}\big(\log\Gamma(\alpha+Nx)-\log\Gamma(\alpha)-\log\Gamma(Nx+1)\big)=F(x)+O(N^{-1}),

where

F(x)=(\nu-1)\log\frac{\nu+x-1}{\nu-1}+x\log(\nu+x-1)-x\log x,

and the
O(N^{-1})
term is analytic in
x
on any compact subset of a complex neighborhood of
(0,\infty)
, uniformly bounded together with its derivative. Adding the contribution
(Nx-1)\log\beta
from (
3.1
), we get

\frac{1}{N}\log\Omega_{N}(x)=F(x)+x\log\beta+O(N^{-1}).

Define the external field by
\mathsf{V}(x)=-F(x)-x\log\beta
, which is precisely the expression in (
2.41
). Then we may rewrite

\Omega_{N}(x)=\exp\!\big(-N\mathsf{V}(x)-2\mathsf{C}_{N}+\mathsf{E}_{N}(x)\big),

where
\mathsf{C}_{N}
absorbs all
x
–independent terms and
\mathsf{E}_{N}(x)
collects the
O(1)
remainder coming from the Stirling remainders and the
O(N^{-1})
term multiplied by
N
(hence still
O(1)
). By standard properties of
r(z)
, the functions
\mathsf{E}_{N}
are analytic in a complex neighborhood
U
of
(0,\infty)
and satisfy the bounds in (
2.20
)–(
2.21
).

The analyticity and boundedness of
\mathsf{V}
on
[0,\infty)
and on a complex neighborhood of
(0,\infty)
are immediate from (
2.41
). For the growth condition, observe that as
x\to\infty
,

\mathsf{V}(x)=x\log\frac{x}{\beta(\nu+x-1)}+O(1)=x(-\log\beta)+O(1),

so
\mathsf{V}(x)/x\to-\log\beta>0
. Thus Assumption
2.4
is satisfied.

2. Verification of Assumption
2.5
.

We now consider the constrained logarithmic energy problem with external field
\mathsf{V}
and upper constraint
0\leq\mathrm{d}\mu/\mathrm{d}x\leq 1
on
[0,\infty)
, as in the general theory of discrete orthogonal polynomial ensembles. The equilibrium measure
\mu_{\mathsf{V}}
minimizes

I[\mu]=\iint\log\frac{1}{|x-y|}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)+\int\mathsf{V}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

among probability measures
\mu
on
[0,\infty)
with density at most
1
. The Euler–Lagrange conditions for this constrained problem read

(3.2)

2\int\log\frac{1}{|x-y|}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathsf{V}}(y)+\mathsf{V}(x)+2\ell\begin{cases}<0,&0<x<\mathsf{a},\\
=0,&\mathsf{a}\leq x\leq\mathsf{b},\\
>0,&x>\mathsf{b},\end{cases}

for some constant
\ell
, together with the pointwise constraint

0\leq\rho(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathsf{V}}}{\mathrm{d}x}(x)\leq 1.

For the potential (
2.41
), the associated scalar singular integral equation for the Cauchy transform of
\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathsf{V}}
can be solved explicitly by the standard one-band ansatz used for discrete orthogonal polynomial ensembles (see, e.g., Baik–Kriecherbauer–McLaughlin–Miller
[
5
]
). One looks for a measure
\mu_{\mathsf{V}}
supported on
[0,\mathsf{b}]
with a single saturated interval
[0,\mathsf{a}]
, so that

\rho(x)=\begin{cases}1,&0<x<\mathsf{a},\\[3.00003pt]
\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\arccos\!\Big(\frac{x(1-\beta)-(\nu-1)(1+\beta)}{2\sqrt{\beta(\nu-1)x}}\Big),&\mathsf{a}<x<\mathsf{b},\\[6.99997pt]
0,&x>\mathsf{b},\end{cases}

for some
0<\mathsf{a}<\mathsf{b}
, and then determine
\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}
by imposing:

(a)

the endpoint behavior (square-root vanishing of
\rho
at
\mathsf{b}
and of
1-\rho
at
\mathsf{a}
);

(b)

the mass constraint
\int_{0}^{\mathsf{b}}\rho(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=1
;

(c)

the matching of the jump of
G
with
\mathsf{V}^{\prime}(x)
on
(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b})
.

Carrying out this computation (see
[
5
,
14
]
for details) yields precisely

\mathsf{a}=\frac{(1-\sqrt{\beta})^{2}}{1-\beta}(\nu-1),\qquad\mathsf{b}=\frac{(1+\sqrt{\beta})^{2}}{1-\beta}(\nu-1),

which are the endpoints stated in (
2.42
). The explicit expression of
\rho
shows that

•

\supp\mu_{\mathsf{V}}=[0,\mathsf{b}]
, so Assumption
2.5
(i) holds.

•

\rho(x)\equiv 1
on
(0,\mathsf{a})
, i.e.
\mu_{\mathsf{V}}
coincides there with Lebesgue measure, so the upper constraint is active exactly on
[0,\mathsf{a}]
and Assumption
2.5
(ii) holds.

•

As
x\to\mathsf{a}^{+}
,
1-\rho(x)\sim c\sqrt{x-\mathsf{a}}
, and as
x\to\mathsf{b}^{-}
,
\rho(x)\sim c^{\prime}\sqrt{\mathsf{b}-x}
, for some
c,c^{\prime}>0
, giving the required square-root behavior at the transition points.

A direct check (or, equivalently, the analysis of the scalar Riemann–Hilbert problem in
[
5
,
14
]
) shows that the Euler–Lagrange inequalities in (
3.2
) are strict away from
[\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}]
, which is Assumption
2.5
(iii).
Altogether, Assumptions
2.4
and
2.5
hold for the Meixner ensemble with parameters

\alpha=(\nu-1)N
and
\beta=q^{-1/2}u^{-1}
, with external field and band/saturated intervals given by (
2.41
) and (
2.42
).

Proposition
2.10
was needed so that we can apply Identity
2.43
and recover asymptotics for the
\mathsf{q}
-Laplace transform
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{S6V}}[\cdot]
from Theorem
2.9
. But, we stress again, Identity
2.43
is only valid for
1<\nu<\mathsf{q}^{1/2}u
. For the complementary regime
\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1}<\nu\leq 1
, we have to use instead (
2.43
), and our next result ensures that the Meixner ensemble on the right-hand side of (
2.43
) still satisfies our working Assumptions
2.4
and
2.5
.

Proposition 3.1

(Meixner ensemble for
\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1}<\nu\leq 1
)
.

For
\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1}<\nu\leq 1
,
\mathsf{q}\in(0,1)
and
u>\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}
, consider the shifted Meixner ensemble
\rm Meixner^{\circ}(M-1,N-M+2,\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1})+(N-M+1)
with parameters

\widetilde{N}=\lfloor\nu N\rfloor-1,\qquad\alpha=\left(\frac{1}{\nu}-1\right)\widetilde{N},\qquad\beta=\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1}\in(0,1),

and define the rescaled weight

\Omega_{\widetilde{N}}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{\widetilde{N}}\omega_{N}(\widetilde{N}x),\qquad x\in\tfrac{1}{\widetilde{N}}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}.

Then Assumptions
2.4
and
2.5
hold, as a particle system with particles starting to the right of
\nu^{-1}-1
, and with:

(i)

External field

(3.3)

\mathsf{V}(x)=\left(x-\tfrac{1}{\nu}+1\right)\log\frac{x-\nu^{-1}+1}{\beta x}+\left(\tfrac{1}{\nu}-1\right)\log\frac{\nu^{-1}-1}{x},\qquad x>\tfrac{1}{\nu}-1.

(ii)

Saturated region
\left[\nu^{-1}-1,\,\mathsf{a}\right]
and band
[\mathsf{a},\,\mathsf{b}]
given by

(3.4)

\mathsf{a}=\frac{(1-\sqrt{\beta})}{(1-\beta)}(\nu^{-1}-1),\qquad\mathsf{b}=\frac{(1+\sqrt{\beta})}{(1-\beta)}(\nu^{-1}-1).

In particular,
\mathsf{V}
satisfies Assumption
2.4
, and the associated constrained equilibrium measure
\mu_{\mathsf{V}}
satisfies Assumption
2.5
with
\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}=\mathsf{c}_{\nu}^{-1}
and
\mathsf{c}_{\nu}
as in (
2.6
).

The proof of Proposition
3.1
follows the same arguments as the proof of Proposition
2.10
, with the appropriate shifts and parameter substitutions. We omit the details for brevity. Furthermore, applying Theorem
2.8
with Proposition
3.1
in place of Proposition
2.10
yields the analogue of Theorem
2.12
for the regime
\mathsf{q}^{-1/2}u^{-1}<\nu\leq 1
.

3.2.
Comparison lemmas for the
q
–Laplace transform

The goal of this subsection is to establish bounds between the tail probabilities of the S6V model and the
\mathsf{q}
-Laplace transform
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{S6V}}
of it that appears in the asymptotic result of Theorem
2.12
. We use the following shorthand below

(3.5)

\mathcal{I}_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{1+\zeta\mathsf{q}^{\mathfrak{h}(\nu N,N)+n}},\qquad\mathcal{J}_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\log\bigl(1+\zeta\mathsf{q}^{\mathfrak{h}(\nu N,N)+n}\bigr).

Lemma 3.2

(Upper-tail comparison via the
q
–Laplace transform)
.

Fix
\nu>1
in the liquid region,
\mathsf{h}>0
and
\mathsf{q}\in(0,1)
. Recall that
\zeta=\mathsf{q}^{-{\mathsf{a}_{\nu}}N-\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}}
.
Then for every
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and all
N
large enough (depending on
\varepsilon
and
q
) one has

\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathfrak{H}_{N}(\nu)\geq(1+\varepsilon)\mathsf{h}\bigr)\;\leq\;\exp\Bigl(\tfrac{q^{\varepsilon\mathsf{h}N^{1/3}}}{(1-\mathsf{q})}\Bigr)\,\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{S6V}}\Bigl[\prod_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{I}_{n}\bigr]

and

\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathfrak{H}_{N}(\nu)\geq(1-\varepsilon)\mathsf{h}\bigr)\;\geq\;\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{S6V}}\Bigl[\prod_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{I}_{n}\Bigr]\;-\;\exp\Bigl(-\tfrac{1}{2}\log(\mathsf{q}^{-1})(\varepsilon\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu})^{2}N^{2/3}\Bigr).

Proof.

For the upper bound, define the random variable

Y^{(1)}_{t}:=\mathbf{1}\bigl(\mathfrak{H}_{N}(\nu)\geq(1+\varepsilon)\mathsf{h}\bigr)\prod_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{I}_{n}=\mathbf{1}\bigl(\mathfrak{H}_{N}(\nu)\geq(1+\varepsilon)\mathsf{h}\bigr)\exp\Bigl(-\sum_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{J}_{n}\Bigr).

Since on the event
\{\mathfrak{H}_{N}(\nu)\geq(1+\varepsilon)\mathsf{h}\}
, we have
\mathfrak{h}(\nu N,N)\geq{\mathsf{a}_{\nu}}N+(1+\varepsilon)\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}
,
so for each
n\geq 1
we may write

\zeta\mathsf{q}^{\mathfrak{h}(\nu N,N)+n}=\mathsf{q}^{\mathfrak{h}(\nu N,N)-{\mathsf{a}_{\nu}}N-\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}+n}\leq\mathsf{q}^{\varepsilon\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}+n}.

For
t
large this quantity is in
(0,1)
, uniformly in
n
, and by the inequality
\log(1+z)\leq z
for
z\in(0,1)
we obtain

\mathcal{J}_{n}=\log\bigl(1+\zeta\mathsf{q}^{\mathfrak{h}(\nu N,N)+n}\bigr)\;\leq\;\zeta\mathsf{q}^{\mathfrak{h}(\nu N,N)+n}\;\leq\;\mathsf{q}^{\varepsilon\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}+n}.

Summing over
n
gives

\sum_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{J}_{n}\;\leq\;\mathsf{q}^{\varepsilon\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}}\sum_{n\geq 1}\mathsf{q}^{n}=\frac{1}{(1-\mathsf{q})}\mathsf{q}^{\varepsilon\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}}.

Substituting this into the definition of
Y^{(1)}_{t}
shows

Y^{(1)}_{t}\geq\exp\Bigl(-\tfrac{1}{(1-\mathsf{q})}\mathsf{q}^{\varepsilon\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}}\Bigr),

and hence

\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathfrak{H}_{N}(\nu)\geq(1+\varepsilon)\mathsf{h}\bigr)\leq\exp\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{(1-\mathsf{q})}\mathsf{q}^{\varepsilon\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}}\Bigr)\,\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{S6V}}\Bigl[Y^{(1)}_{t}\Bigr]\leq\exp\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{(1-\mathsf{q})}\mathsf{q}^{\varepsilon\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}}\Bigr)\,\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{S6V}}\Bigl[\prod_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{I}_{n}\Bigr],

which is the first inequality.

For the lower bound, define

Y^{(2)}_{t}:=\mathbf{1}\bigl(\mathfrak{H}_{N}(\nu)<(1-\varepsilon)\mathsf{h}\bigr)\prod_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{I}_{n},\qquad\widetilde{Y}^{(2)}_{t}:=\prod_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{I}_{n}-Y^{(2)}_{t}=\mathbf{1}\bigl(\mathfrak{H}_{N}(\nu)\geq(1-\varepsilon)\mathsf{h}\bigr)\prod_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{I}_{n}.

Since
0<\mathcal{I}_{n}\leq 1
, we have

\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathfrak{H}_{N}(\nu)\geq(1-\varepsilon)\mathsf{h}\bigr)\geq\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{S6V}}[\widetilde{Y}^{(2)}_{t}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{S6V}}\Bigl[\prod_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{I}_{n}\Bigr]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{S6V}}[Y^{(2)}_{t}].

It remains to bound
\mathbb{E}[Y^{(2)}_{t}]
from above. Since on the event
\{\mathfrak{H}_{N}(\nu)<(1-\varepsilon)\mathsf{h}\}
we have

\mathfrak{h}(\nu N,N)\leq{\mathsf{a}_{\nu}}N+(1-\varepsilon)\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3},

so we can estimate the product
\prod_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{I}_{n}
by splitting the sum in
\sum_{n}\mathcal{J}_{n}
at the index
M\approx\varepsilon\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}
.
Using that
\log(1+z)\geq(\varepsilon\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}-n)\log(\mathsf{q}^{-1})
when
n\leq M
and
\log(1+z)\geq\frac{1}{2}q^{n-\varepsilon\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}}
when
n>M
, we obtain a Gaussian-type lower bound

\sum_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{J}_{n}\;\geq\;\tfrac{1}{2}\log(\mathsf{q}^{-1})(\varepsilon\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu})^{2}N^{2/3}

for
t
large. This yields

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{S6V}}[Y^{(2)}_{t}]\leq\exp\Bigl(-\tfrac{1}{2}\log(\mathsf{q}^{-1})(\varepsilon\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu})^{2}N^{2/3}\Bigr).

Plugging this into the previous inequality gives the desired lower bound.
∎

Lemma 3.3

(Lower-tail comparison via the
q
–Laplace transform)
.

Fix
\nu>1
in the liquid region,
\mathsf{h}>0
and
\mathsf{q}\in(0,1)
. Define
\zeta=\mathsf{q}^{-{\mathsf{a}_{\nu}}N+\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}}
.
Then for every
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and all
N
large enough (depending on
\varepsilon
and
q
) one has

\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathfrak{H}_{N}(\nu)\leq-(1-\varepsilon)\mathsf{h}\bigr)\;\geq\;1-\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{S6V}}\Bigl[\prod_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{I}_{n}\Bigr]\;+\;\exp\Bigl(-\tfrac{1}{2}\log(\mathsf{q}^{-1})(\varepsilon\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu})^{2}N^{2/3}\Bigr)

and

\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathfrak{H}_{N}(\nu)\leq-(1+\varepsilon)\mathsf{h}\bigr)\;\leq\;1-\exp\Bigl(\tfrac{q^{\varepsilon\mathsf{h}N^{1/3}}}{(1-\mathsf{q})}\Bigr)\,\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{S6V}}\Bigl[\prod_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{I}_{n}\bigr]

Proof.

The argument is completely analogous to that of Lemma
3.2
: one repeats the same estimates with the substitutions
\mathsf{h}\mapsto-\mathsf{h}
and
\varepsilon\mapsto-\varepsilon
, which reverse the direction of the inequalities in the bounds for the factors
\mathcal{J}_{n}
.
Since no new ideas are required, we omit the details for brevity.
∎

3.3.
Proof of Theorems
2.1
and
2.2

Recall the notation introduced in (
3.5
).

The proofs of the upper and lower tails follow the same strategy:
(1) rewrite the tail probabilities in terms of the
q
–Laplace transform
\mathbb{E}_{\rm S6V}\!\left[\prod_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{I}_{n}\right]
using Lemmas
3.2
and
3.3
;
(2) apply the asymptotic formulas of Theorem
2.12
for
\log\mathbb{E}_{\rm S6V}[\prod\mathcal{I}_{n}]
in the appropriate regime of the parameter
s
;
(3) convert these asymptotics into the required large-deviation exponents. Recall that the rescaled height function
\mathcal{h}_{N}
was introduced in (
2.7
), and from now on consider
\zeta
and
s
as in (
2.45
). In addition, set

F_{N}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\log\mathbb{E}_{\rm S6V}\!\Bigl[\prod_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{I}_{n}\Bigr].

Assuming Theorem
2.9
, we now complete the proof of Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
.

Step 1. From tail probabilities to the
q
–Laplace transform.

Lemma
3.2
gives, for any fixed
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{H}_{N}(\nu)\geq(1+\varepsilon)\mathsf{h})\;\leq\;\exp\bigl(q^{\varepsilon\mathsf{h}N^{1/3}}/(1-\mathsf{q})\bigr)\,\ee^{F_{N}},

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{H}_{N}(\nu)\geq(1-\varepsilon)\mathsf{h})\;\geq\;\ee^{F_{N}}-\exp\bigl(-\tfrac{1}{2}\log(\mathsf{q}^{-1})(\varepsilon\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu})^{2}N^{2/3}\bigr).

An entirely analogous pair of inequalities for the lower tail is supplied by Lemma
3.3
.
Thus the problem reduces to determining the large-
N
asymptotics of
F_{N}
.

Step 2. Asymptotics of
F_{N}
from Theorem
2.12
.

There are three regimes, determined by the size of the parameter
s
.

Supercritical regime
(
s<-s_{0}N^{1/3}
, equivalently
\mathsf{h}>0
for the upper tail).
By Theorem
2.12
(iii),

F_{N}=-\frac{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}^{3}}{12t^{3}N}|s|^{3}+O\!\left(\frac{s^{4}}{N^{3/2}}\right).

Using that
s=-(\mathsf{h}\mathsf{c}_{\nu}N^{1/3}+1)\log(1/\mathsf{q})
and that
\mathsf{c}_{\nu}=\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}^{-1}
, the cubic term simplifies to

\,F_{N}=-(1+o(1))\,\mathsf{h}^{3}/12
.
Since the extra prefactors in Lemma
3.2
are exponentially negligible on the
\mathsf{h}^{3}
-scale, we obtain

\exp\!\left(-(1+\varepsilon)\frac{\mathsf{h}^{3}}{12}\right)\;\leq\;\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{H}_{N}(\nu)\geq\mathsf{h})\;\leq\;\exp\!\left(-(1-\varepsilon)\frac{\mathsf{h}^{3}}{12}\right),

uniformly for
\mathsf{h}_{0}\leq\mathsf{h}\leq\mathsf{h}_{0}^{-1}N^{1/6}
.
This proves the upper-tail bound of Theorem
2.2
.

Subcritical regime
(
s>s_{0}N^{1/3}
, equivalently
\mathsf{h}>0
for the lower tail).
Theorem
2.12
(i) gives

F_{N}=-\left(1+O\!\left(e^{-\eta s/N^{1/3}}+N^{-\nu}\right)\right)\int_{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}s/(tN^{1/3})}^{\infty}\mathsf{A}(y,y)\,\mathrm{d}y+O(\ee^{-\eta N^{1/4}}).

Since the lower limit of integration is proportional to
\mathsf{h}
and
\mathsf{A}(y,y)
is the Airy kernel diagonal, the integral equals
(4/3)\,\mathsf{h}^{3/2}(1+o(1))
.
Thus
F_{N}=-(4/3)\mathsf{h}^{3/2}+o(1)
.
Insert this into Lemma
3.3
(whose prefactors are negligible on the
\mathsf{h}^{3/2}
-scale).
We obtain

\exp\!\left(-(1+\varepsilon)\frac{4}{3}\mathsf{h}^{3/2}\right)\;\leq\;\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathfrak{H}_{N}(\nu)\leq-\mathsf{h}\bigr)\;\leq\;\exp\!\left(-(1-\varepsilon)\frac{4}{3}\mathsf{h}^{3/2}\right),

establishing Theorem
2.1
.

Critical regime
(
|s|\leq s_{0}N^{1/3}
).
Here Theorem
2.12
(ii) expresses
F_{N}
in terms of an integral of the Painlevé P
34
kernel.
The width of this region is
O(N^{1/3})
, and integrating the kernel on this scale contributes only
o(1)
to
F_{N}
.
Thus the subcritical and supercritical asymptotics match smoothly across the critical window and do not affect the dominant large-deviation exponents.

Combining these three steps completes the proof of both upper and lower tail theorems.

4.
A general deformation formula for partition functions of OP ensembles

In this section, we prove a more general version of the deformation formula (
2.33
). This section is independent of other sections and may be of independent interest, and we start setting up appropriate independent notation.

Start with a Borel measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}
with all moments finite. We associate to it the orthogonal polynomial
P_{k}=P_{k}(\cdot{\,|\,}\mu)
to be the monic polynomial of degree
k
that satisfies

\int x^{j}P_{k}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=0,\quad j=0,\ldots,k-1.

The corresponding norming constant
\gamma_{k}=\gamma_{k}(\mu)>0
is then defined via the identity

\frac{1}{\gamma_{k}^{2}}=\int(P_{k}(x))^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

The associated Christoffel-Darboux kernel is

(4.1)

K_{n}(x,y)=K_{n}(x,y{\,|\,}\mu)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\pi_{k}(x)\pi_{k}(y)=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\gamma_{k}^{2}P_{k}(x)P_{k}(y).

From the orthogonality for the
P_{k}
’s, it follows that this kernel satisfies the identity

(4.2)

\int K_{n}(x,x)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=n.

The partition function of the associated orthogonal polynomial ensemble is

(4.3)

Z_{n}=Z_{n}(\mu)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\prod_{1\leq j<k\leq n}(x_{k}-x_{j})^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu(x_{1})\cdots\mathrm{d}\mu(x_{n}),

which is a Hankel determinant, and relates to the norming constants via the formula

(4.4)

Z_{n}=n!\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}\gamma_{k}^{-2}.

In particular, for every
k
it is valid

(4.5)

\gamma_{k}^{2}=\frac{Z_{k+1}}{Z_{k}}.

Also,
P_{n}
may be computed through Heine’s formula

(4.6)

P_{n}(z)=\frac{1}{n!Z_{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}(z-x_{j})\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(x_{j}-x_{i})^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu(x_{1})\cdots\mathrm{d}\mu(x_{n}).

Writing
P_{n}(x)=x^{n}+a^{(n)}_{1}x^{n-1}+\cdots+a^{(n)}_{n-1}x+a^{(n)}_{n}
, we obtain the expression for
a_{k}^{(n)}=a_{k}^{(n)}(\mu)
,

a_{k}^{(n)}(\mu)=\frac{(-1)^{k}}{n!Z_{n}(\mu)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e_{k}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu(x_{1})\cdots\mathrm{d}\mu(x_{n}),

where
e_{k}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
is the
k
-th elementary symmetric polynomial in
n
variables. From the straightforward inequality

|e_{k}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})|=\left|\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq n}x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{k}}\right|\leq\frac{1}{k!}\binom{n}{k}\prod_{j=1}^{n}(1+|x_{j}|)

we obtain the bound

(4.7)

|a_{k}^{(n)}(\mu)|\leq\frac{1}{n!Z_{n}(\mu)}\frac{1}{k!}\binom{n}{k}Z_{n}((1+|x|)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)).

We now talk about deformations of measures. Fix an interval
[s_{0},s_{*}]
with
s_{0}<s_{*}\leq+\infty
. When
s_{*}=+\infty
, we convention that
[s_{0},s_{*}]=[s_{0},+\infty)
, and that all the evaluations at
s=s_{*}
mean taking limits
s\to+\infty
.

We fix a reference Borel measure
\mu_{0}
with all moments finite, and for a function
w=w(x{\,|\,}s)
, we consider a deformed measure

\mathrm{d}\mu_{s}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=w(x{\,|\,}s)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x),\quad s\in[s_{0},s_{*}].

We assume that
x\mapsto x^{k}w(x{\,|\,}s)
is a non-negative
\mu_{0}
-integrable function for every
k\geq 0
, so that
\mu_{s}
is a measure with finite moments for every fixed
s
. In particular, we may talk about

Z_{n}(s)=Z_{n}(\mu_{s}),\quad\gamma_{n}(s)=\gamma_{n}(\mu_{s}),\quad P_{n}(x{\,|\,}s)=P_{n}(x{\,|\,}\mu_{s}),\quad K_{n}(x,y{\,|\,}s)=K_{n}(x,y{\,|\,}\mu_{s})

as the partition function, norming constants, monic orthogonal polynomials and Christoffel-Darboux kernels, respectively, for the measure
\mathrm{d}\mu_{s}(x)
.

We prove

Proposition 4.1
.

For
\partial=\partial_{s}
being the derivative with respect to
s
, assume that there exist non-negative functions
f=f(x),F=F(x)
and a constant
\delta>0
, for which the inequalities

(4.8)

\delta\leq w(x{\,|\,}s)\leq f(x),\quad\text{and}\quad|\partial w(x{\,|\,}s)|\leq F(x)

hold true for
\mu_{0}
-a.e.
x\in\mathbb{R}
and Lebesgue a.e.
s\in[s_{0},s_{*}]
, and satisfying the condition that

|x|^{k}f(x),|x|^{k}F(x)\in L^{1}(\mathrm{d}\mu_{0},\mathbb{R}),

for every
k\geq 0
. Then the identity

\log\frac{Z_{n}(s)}{Z_{n}(s_{*})}=-\int_{s}^{s_{*}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K_{n}(x,x{\,|\,}u)(\partial w)(x{\,|\,}u)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)\mathrm{d}u

holds true for every
s\in[s_{0},s_{*}]
.

Remark 4.2
.

For
\mu_{0}
being a specific weighted version of the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
and
w(x{\,|\,}s)
a particular choice of weight, Proposition
4.1
coincides with
[
55
, Proposition 9.1]
. For IIKS integrable kernels, which include the Christoffel-Darboux kernel for OPs for continuous weights (but not for discrete ones as needed in the present work), a similar result was obtained by Claeys and Glesner in
[
30
]
, see the last displayed equation in page 2216 therein.

Proposition
2.6
follows from Proposition
4.1
, setting
\mu_{0}(x)
to be the counting measure of
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
weighted by
\omega
and
w(x{\,|\,}s)=(1+\ee^{-t(x-\mathsf{a}N)-s})
, so that
\mu_{s}
is the counting measure on
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
weighted by
\omega^{\sigma}(x)=(1+\ee^{-t(x-\mathsf{a}N)-s})\omega(x)
. Indeed, in this case
\mu_{0}
has all its moments finite because this is an assumption we are working with for
\omega
, the inequalities

1\leq w(x{\,|\,}s)\leq 1+\ee^{t\mathsf{a}N-s}

are immediate, and the term
1+\ee^{t\mathsf{a}N-s}
is bounded from above as a function of
s
on any interval of the form
[s_{0},+\infty)
, and finally

\left|\partial_{s}w(x{\,|\,}s)\right|\leq\ee^{t\mathsf{a}N-s},

so (
4.8
) is satisfied with
f,F
being constant functions.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the result for
s_{*}
finite, the general result then follows taking
s_{*}\to+\infty
.

First off, we use the orthonormality to obtain the identity

\int\partial(\pi_{k}(x{\,|\,}s)^{2})\mathrm{d}\mu_{s}(x)=2\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\pi_{k}(x{\,|\,}s)(\partial\gamma_{k}(s)x^{k}+(\text{pol degree}\leq k-1))\mathrm{d}\mu_{s}(x)=2\frac{\partial\gamma_{k}(s)}{\gamma_{k}(s)}

We then take logarithms in (
4.4
) and differentiate, obtaining

\partial\log Z_{n}(s)=-2\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{\partial\gamma_{k}(s)}{\gamma_{k}(s)}=-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\partial K_{n}(x,x{\,|\,}s)\mathrm{d}\mu_{s}(x).

To our knowledge, the steps we performed so far were first observed by Krasovsky
[
65
, Equation (14)]
. Next, we integrate from the given value
s
to
s_{*}
, obtaining

\log\frac{Z_{n}(s)}{Z_{n}(s_{*})}=-\int_{s}^{s_{*}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K_{n}(x,x{\,|\,}u)\partial w(x{\,|\,}u)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)\mathrm{d}u\\
+\int_{s}^{s_{*}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\partial(K_{n}(x,x{\,|\,}u)w(x{\,|\,}u))\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)\mathrm{d}u,

where we also used the trivial identity
(\partial K_{n})w=\partial(K_{n}w)-K_{n}\partial w
.

The final step is to show that the last integral is zero. Assuming that we can apply Fubini’s Theorem,

(4.9)

\int_{s}^{s_{*}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\partial(K_{n}(x,x{\,|\,}u)w(x{\,|\,}u))\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)\mathrm{d}u=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(K_{n}(x,x{\,|\,}s_{*})w(x{\,|\,}s_{*})-K_{n}(x,x{\,|\,}s)w(x{\,|\,}s)\right)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x),

and using (
4.2
) we see that the right-hand side above vanishes.

To conclude, we justify why Fubini’s Theorem could be applied. To that end, observe first that the identities

\delta^{n}Z_{n}(\mathrm{d}\mu_{0})\leq Z_{n}(s)\leq Z_{n}(f^{n}\mathrm{d}\mu_{0})\quad\text{and}\quad|\partial Z_{n}(s)|\leq Z_{n}(F^{n}\mathrm{d}\mu_{0})

are a mere consequence of (
4.3
) and (
4.8
). Thanks to (
4.5
), we thus see that both
\gamma_{k}(s)^{2}
and
\partial_{s}(\gamma_{k}(s)^{2})
are bounded by a uniform constant for
s\in[s_{0},s_{*}]
.

Next, from (
4.7
), we also see that
P_{k}(x{\,|\,}s)
is bounded by a factor of the form
M(1+|x|^{k})
, for some
M>0
. In an analogous way, proceeding as we did to obtain (
4.7
), we see that
\partial P_{k}(x{\,|\,}s)
is bounded by
M^{\prime}(1+|x|^{k-1})
for some
M^{\prime}>0
.

All in all, combining such conclusions with (
4.1
), and using again (
4.8
), we obtain that
\partial(K_{n}w)=\partial K_{n}w+K_{n}\partial w
is bounded by a factor of the form
M(1+|x|^{m})f(x)+M^{\prime}(1+|x|^{m^{\prime}})F(x)
, for some new values
M,M^{\prime},m,m^{\prime}>0
. By assumption and the fact that
s_{*}
is finite, these factors are
\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)\times\mathrm{d}s
integrable on
\mathbb{R}\times[s_{0},s_{*}]
, and therefore the application of Fubini’s Theorem in (
4.9
) is justified.

∎

5.
A priori estimates for multiplicative statistics

Theorem
2.8
deals with asymptotics for
\mathsf{L}_{N}(s)
when
|s|\leq\delta N^{1/2}
. However, we will also need to obtain rough estimates when
s\gg N^{1/2}
, and we pursue them in this section.

Set

\kappa(S)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\left\lfloor N\left(\mathsf{a}-\frac{S}{2tN}\right)\right\rfloor,\quad S>0.

The mentioned needed estimate is summarized with the following result.

Theorem 5.1
.

For any
\delta>0
sufficiently small, there exists
\eta>0
such that the estimate

\mathsf{S}_{N}(S)=\left[\prod_{x=1}^{\kappa(S)}\left(1+\ee^{-t(x-N(\mathsf{a}-\frac{S}{Nt}))}\right)\right]\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\ee^{-\eta S^{3/2}N^{-1/2}}\right)\right),\quad\text{as}\quad N\to+\infty,

holds true uniformly for
\delta N^{1/2}\leq S\leq\delta N
.

The proof of Theorem
5.1
is split into different lemmas and will be concluded at the end of the present section. From now on we always assume that
\delta>0
is fixed in such a way that

0<\kappa(S)<\mathsf{a},\quad\text{for every }S\in[\delta N^{1/2},\delta N].

Such
\delta>0
will be made sufficiently smaller whenever needed.

For
\mathcal{X}_{N}
the discrete orthogonal polynomial ensemble for the weight
\omega
in
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, let

\#(S)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=|\mathcal{X}_{N}\cap(0,\kappa(S))|

be the counting function of the interval
(0,\kappa(S))
. The random variable
\#
will be used in the proofs that follow.

Lemma 5.2
.

For any
S\in[\delta N^{1/2},\delta N]
, the inequality

\mathsf{S}_{N}(S)\leq\prod_{x=1}^{\kappa(S)}\left(1+\ee^{-t(x-N(\mathsf{a}-\frac{S}{Nt}))}\right)\exp\left(\ee^{-\frac{S}{2}+\frac{1}{t}}\frac{\ee^{-t}}{1-\ee^{-t}}\right)

holds true.

Proof.

Having in mind that
\#\leq\kappa(S)
, we expand in conditional expectation,

\mathsf{S}_{N}(S)=\sum_{k=0}^{\kappa(S)}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{N}}\left(1+\ee^{-t(x-N(\mathsf{a}-\frac{S}{Nt}))}\right)\Big|\#(S)=k\right]{\mathbb{P}}\left(\#(S)=k\right).

Split the product inside the expectation into two products, one over
\mathcal{X}_{N}\cap(0,\kappa(S)]
, and the second one over
\mathcal{X}_{N}\cap(\kappa(S),+\infty)
.
The particles
x\in\mathcal{X}_{N}
take values on the integers, and the exponential inside the expectation is a decreasing function of
x
. Therefore, once
\#=k
, the largest value of the product over
\mathcal{X}_{N}\cap(0,\kappa(S)]
is attained when all the
k
particles in this set are at the positions
1,2,\ldots,k
. The remaining
N-k
particles have to be in the interval
(\kappa(S),\infty)
, and once again the corresponding product is maximized when they are positioned at the points
\kappa(S)+1,\ldots,\kappa(S)+N-k
. Hence,

\mathsf{S}_{N}(S)\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\kappa(S)}\prod_{x=1}^{k}\left(1+\ee^{-t(x-N(\mathsf{a}-\frac{S}{Nt}))}\right)\prod_{x=\kappa(S)+1}^{\kappa(S)+N-k}\left(1+\ee^{-t(x-N(\mathsf{a}-\frac{S}{Nt}))}\right){\mathbb{P}}\left(\#(S)=k\right).

The first product above increases when we increase
k
, so each of these products is bounded by the product with
k=\kappa(S)
. Next, we rewrite

\prod_{x=\kappa(S)+1}^{\kappa(S)+N-k}\left(1+\ee^{-t(x-N(\mathsf{a}-\frac{S}{Nt}))}\right)=\prod_{x=1}^{N-k}\left(1+\ee^{-t(x+\kappa(S)-N(\mathsf{a}-\frac{S}{Nt}))}\right)

From the basic inequality
1+y\leq e^{y}
for
y\geq 0
, we learn that

\prod_{x=1}^{N-k}\left(1+\ee^{-t(x+\kappa(S)-N(\mathsf{a}-\frac{S}{Nt}))}\right)\leq\prod_{x=1}^{\infty}\left(1+\ee^{-t(x+\kappa(S)-N(\mathsf{a}-\frac{S}{Nt}))}\right)\leq\exp\left(\ee^{-t\left[\kappa(S)-N\left(a-\frac{S}{Nt}\right)\right]}\sum_{x=1}^{\infty}\ee^{-tx}\right).

Now,

\kappa(S)-N\left(\mathsf{a}-\frac{S}{Nt}\right)=N\left(\mathsf{a}-\frac{S}{2Nt}\right)-N\left(\mathsf{a}-\frac{S}{Nt}\right)+\kappa(S)-N\left(\mathsf{a}-\frac{S}{2Nt}\right)\geq\frac{S}{2t}-1.

Hence, we just obtained the inequality

\mathsf{S}_{N}(S)\leq\prod_{x=1}^{\kappa(S)}\left(1+\ee^{-t(x-N(\mathsf{a}-\frac{S}{Nt}))}\right)\exp\left(\ee^{-\frac{S}{2}+\frac{1}{t}}\frac{\ee^{-t}}{1-\ee^{-t}}\right)\sum_{k=0}^{\kappa(S)}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\#(S)=k\right),

and the remaining sum is identically
1
, concluding the proof.
∎

The next result gives a lower bound for
\mathsf{S}_{N}
.

Lemma 5.3
.

For any
S\in[\delta N^{1/2},\delta N]
, the inequality

\mathsf{S}_{N}(S)\geq\prod_{x=1}^{\kappa(S)}\left(1+\ee^{-t(x-N(\mathsf{a}-\frac{S}{Nt}))}\right){\mathbb{P}}(\#(S)=\kappa(S))

holds true.

Proof.

Applying again conditional expectation, we see that

\mathsf{S}_{N}(S)\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{N}}\left(1+\ee^{-t(x-N(\mathsf{a}-\frac{S}{Nt}))}\right)\Big|\#(S)=\kappa(S)\right]{\mathbb{P}}\left(\#(S)=\kappa(S)\right).

Using that all the terms in the product are greater than
1
, we further bound

\mathsf{S}_{N}(S)\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{N}\cap(0,\kappa(S))}\left(1+\ee^{-t(x-N(\mathsf{a}-\frac{S}{Nt}))}\right)\Big|\#(S)=\kappa(S)\right]{\mathbb{P}}\left(\#(S)=\kappa(S)\right].

There are exactly
\kappa(S)
integer numbers in the interval
(0,\kappa(S)]
, so under the conditional
\#(S)=\kappa(S)
the position of the particles is now deterministic, and we obtain that

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{N}\cap(0,\kappa(S))}\left(1+\ee^{-t(x-N(\mathsf{a}-\frac{S}{Nt}))}\right)\Big|\#(S)=\kappa(S)\right]=\prod_{x=1}^{\kappa(S)}\left(1+\ee^{-t(x-N(\mathsf{a}-\frac{S}{Nt}))}\right),

concluding the proof.
∎

Finally, it remains to estimate the probability of the counting function that appears in the last result. For that, we will explore the underlying determinantal structure. First of all, we use Markov’s inequality and for any given
v>0
we express

(5.1)

{\mathbb{P}}(\#(S)\leq\kappa(S)-1)={\mathbb{P}}\left(\ee^{-v\#(S)}\leq\ee^{-v(\kappa(S)-1)}\right)\leq\ee^{v(\kappa(S)-1)}\mathbb{E}\left[\ee^{-v\#(S)}\right].

The expectation on the right-hand side is the moment generating function of the counting function
\#(S)
. From the theory of determinantal point processes we obtain

(5.2)

\mathbb{E}\left[\ee^{-v\#(S)}\right]=\det\left(\mathsf{I}-(1-\ee^{-u})\mathsf{K}_{N}\right)_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\cap(0,\kappa(S)])},

where
\mathsf{K}_{N}
is the correlation kernel of the Meixner point process, obtained when
\sigma\equiv 1
, or in other words by making
s\to+\infty
in (
2.32
). We now estimate this moment generating function exploring this determinantal representation.

Lemma 5.4
.

Given any
\delta>0
, there exist
\eta>0
and
v_{0}>0
such that the estimate

\mathbb{E}\left[\ee^{-v\#(S)}\right]=\ee^{-v\kappa(S)}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta N^{-1/2}S^{3/2}})\right)

holds true uniformly for
\delta N^{1/2}\leq S\leq\delta N
and uniformly for
0\leq v\leq v_{0}S^{3/2}N^{-1/2}
.

Proof.

We first recall some facts about the kernel
\mathsf{K}_{N}
. Since
\kappa(\delta)<\mathsf{a}
, the interval
(0,\kappa(\delta)]
is inside the saturated region of
\mathcal{X}_{N}
, where the position of the particles is essentially deterministic, up to exponentially small corrections. Concretely, this means that the kernel
\mathsf{K}_{N}
writes as

(5.3)

\mathsf{K}_{N}(x,y)=\delta_{x,y}-\mathsf{R}_{N}(x,y),\quad x,y\in(0,\kappa(\delta)]\cap\mathbb{Z}_{>0},

where
\mathsf{R}_{N}
satisfies an estimate of the form

|\mathsf{R}_{N}(x,y)|\leq M\ee^{-Nf(\kappa(S))},\quad\text{uniformly for }x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\cap[0,\kappa(S)],

for some value
M>0
, and the function
f(x)
is strictly positive on
[0,\mathsf{a})
with

f(x)=c(\mathsf{a}-x)^{3/2}(1+\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{a}-x))\quad\text{as}\quad x\nearrow\mathsf{a},

where
c>0
. Under our working assumptions and the condition
S=\delta N
, the estimate we just described can be obtained with a canonical Riemann-Hilbert/nonlinear steepest descent analysis, and
f
appears as the
+
-boundary value of the
\phi
-function that appears in this analysis. In the larger regime
\delta N^{1/2}\leq\delta N
we are working here, the standard analysis may be modified, with a shrinking neighborhood for the Airy parametrix at
z=\mathsf{a}
, and the estimate is still valid at the cost of making
f
a positive multiple of the
\phi
-function. The arguments needed in this RHP analysis are technical, somewhat lengthy, but standard, and we skip details.

With the local behavior of
f
in mind, the estimate on
\mathsf{R}_{N}
updates to

(5.4)

|\mathsf{R}_{N}(x,y)|\leq M\ee^{-\eta N^{-1/2}S^{3/2}},\quad\text{uniformly for }x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\cap[0,\kappa(S)].

Back to the estimate of the moment generating function, we use the Fredholm determinant representation (
5.2
). We view (
5.3
) as the operator identity
\mathsf{K}_{N}=\mathsf{I}-\mathsf{R}_{N}
on the finite-dimensional space
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\cap(0,\kappa(S)])
of dimension
\kappa(S)
, and manipulate

\ee^{v\kappa(S)}\mathbb{E}\left[\ee^{-v\#(S)}\right]=\ee^{v\kappa(S)}\det\left(\mathsf{I}-(1-\ee^{-v})\mathsf{K}_{N}\right)=\det\left(\ee^{v}\left(\mathsf{I}-(1-\ee^{-v})\mathsf{K}_{N}\right)\right)=\det\left(\mathsf{I}-(\ee^{v}-1)\mathsf{R}_{N}\right),

where in the above all determinants are on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\cap(0,\kappa(S)])
. The Fredholm determinant expansion of the right-hand side yields

\ee^{v\kappa(S)}\mathbb{E}\left[\ee^{-v\#(S)}\right]=1+\sum_{k=1}^{\kappa(S)}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\sum_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}=1}^{\kappa(S)}(\ee^{v}-1)^{k}\det\left(\mathsf{R}_{N}(x_{i},x_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{k}

Hadamard’s inequality combined with (
5.4
) yields that

|\det\left(\mathsf{R}_{N}(x_{i},x_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{k}|\leq M^{k}\ee^{-\eta kN^{-1/2}S^{3/2}},

and we obtain that

(5.5)

\left|\ee^{v\kappa(S)}\mathbb{E}\left[\ee^{-v\#(S)}\right]-1\right|\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\kappa(S)}\frac{1}{k!}\left[M\kappa(S)(\ee^{v}-1)\ee^{-\eta N^{-1/2}S^{3/2}}\right]^{k}

For
S
in the working range, we choose
v_{0}=\eta/2
, so that for some new value
\eta^{\prime}>0
,

M\kappa(S)(\ee^{v}-1)\ee^{-\eta N^{-1/2}S^{3/2}}\leq M\kappa(S)(\ee^{-\frac{\eta}{2}N^{-1/2}S^{3/2}}-\ee^{-\eta N^{-1/2}S^{3/2}})\leq\ee^{-\eta^{\prime}N^{-1/2}S^{3/2}},

which implies that the right-hand side of (
5.5
) is bounded by
\ee^{-\eta^{\prime}N^{-1/2}S^{3/2}}
, concluding the proof.
∎

We are finally ready to conclude this section with the final proof.

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

Having in mind that
\#(S)\leq\kappa(S)
, a combination of Lemmas
5.3
and
5.2
gives that

1-{\mathbb{P}}\left(\#(S)\leq\kappa(S)-1\right)\leq\dfrac{\mathsf{L}_{N}(S)}{\prod_{x=1}^{\kappa(S)}\left(1+\ee^{-t(x-N(\mathsf{a}-\frac{S}{Nt}))}\right)}\leq\exp\left(\ee^{-\frac{S}{2}+\frac{1}{t}}\frac{\ee^{-t}}{1-\ee^{-t}}\right).

In our range of values of
S
, we in fact have that
S
