is larger than
\eta S^{3/2}N^{-1/2}
, for some
\eta>0
, and therefore

\exp\left(\ee^{-\frac{S}{2}+\frac{1}{t}}\frac{\ee^{-t}}{1-\ee^{-t}}\right)=1+\mathcal{O}\left(\ee^{-\eta S^{3/2}N^{-1/2}}\right).

On the other hand, thanks to (
5.1
) and Lemma
5.4
,

{\mathbb{P}}\left(\#(S)\leq\kappa(S)-1\right)=\ee^{-v}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta S^{3/2}N^{-1/2}})\right),

This last estimate is uniform for
v\leq v_{0}S^{3/2}N^{-1/2}
, and so we choose
v=v_{0}S^{3/2}N^{-1/2}
to conclude the proof.
∎

The following result is the main output we will need for later.

Corollary 5.5
.

For any
\delta>0
, there exists
\eta>0
such that the estimate

\log\mathsf{L}_{N}(\delta N^{1/2})=\mathcal{O}\left(\ee^{-\eta N^{1/4}}\right),\quad N\to\infty,

is valid

Proof.

Using (
2.31
) and Theorem
5.1
with
S=\delta N^{1/2}
, we obtain

\log\mathsf{L}_{N}(\delta N^{1/2})=\sum_{x=\kappa(S)+1}^{\infty}\log\left(1+\ee^{-t(x-N(\mathsf{a}-S/(tN)))}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta N^{1/4}}),\quad N\to\infty.

A simple argument shows that the remaining sum is in fact
\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta N})
, for some
\eta>0
, concluding the proof.
∎

6.
The model problem

At the core of our arguments lies in a RHP - the model RHP - that we introduce and study in-depth in this section. This model problem is an extension of several previously known RHPs from the literature, and it depends on two given power series
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{F}
defined near the origin. In Section
6.1
we introduce the necessary conditions in such power series, and obtain some straightforward but important estimates on related quantities. Next, in Section
6.2
we then introduce the model problem itself, and quantities related to it that will be of particular relevance for us.

6.1.
The class of admissible data for the model problem

As mentioned, the model problem we are about to introduce will depend on a pair of power series that we introduce next.

Definition 6.1
.

We say that a pair of functions
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{F})
is
admissible
if they are defined and analytic on a disk
D_{\delta}(0)
, are real-valued over
(-\delta,\delta)
, and

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}(0)=0,\quad\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathsf{P}^{\prime}(0)>0,

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}(0)=0,\quad\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathsf{F}^{\prime}(0)>0.

Fix a value
\lambda_{0}\in(0,\delta/2)
, which will be made arbitrarily small but fixed. Given
\lambda\in[-\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}]
, we introduce the contours

(6.1)

\Gamma_{k}^{\lambda}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\lambda+(0,\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}k/3}\infty),\quad k=0,\ldots,5,\quad\Gamma^{\lambda}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\bigcup_{k=0}^{5}\Gamma_{k}^{\lambda},

and the sectors

(6.2)

\Omega_{j}^{\lambda}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\left\{\zeta\in\mathbb{C}{\,|\,}\arg(\zeta-\lambda)\in\left(\frac{\pi j}{3},\frac{\pi(j+1)}{3}\right)\right\}.

We orient
\Gamma_{0}^{\lambda}
,
\Gamma_{1}^{\lambda}
and
\Gamma_{5}^{\lambda}
from the origin to
\infty
, and
\Gamma_{2}^{\lambda}
,
\Gamma_{3}^{\lambda}
and
\Gamma_{4}^{\lambda}
from
\infty
to the origin. These sets are displayed in Figure
6
.

Figure 6
.
The contours
\Gamma_{j}^{\lambda}
and regions
\Omega_{j}^{\lambda}
introduced in (
6.1
) and (
6.2
), respectively. The angle between two consecutive
\Gamma_{j}^{\lambda}
’s is always
\frac{\pi}{3}
.

The model problem will be defined on
\Gamma^{\lambda}
for appropriate choices of
\lambda\in[-\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}]\subset D_{\delta}(0)
, and will involve admissible functions
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{P})
which are originally defined only on a neighborhood
D_{\delta}(0)
of the origin. By fixing the pair
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{P})
we are in principle also fixing the radius of convergence
\delta>0
of their power series, and we now extend these functions to a full neighborhood of
\Gamma^{\lambda}
in an appropriate way. Nevertheless, we stress that along the way it will turn out convenient to choose the values
\delta>0
and
\lambda_{0}\in(0,\delta/2)
to be arbitrarily small, but they should always be seen as fixed.

Proposition 6.2
.

Given any pair of admissible functions
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{F})
, we can choose
\delta>0
and
\lambda_{0}>0
sufficiently small, such that
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{F})
admit
C^{\infty}
extensions from
D_{\delta}(0)
to
\mathbb{C}
which are real-valud over the real line, and with the following additional properties.

(i)

The function
\mathsf{P}
is increasing over the real line, and

\displaystyle\re\mathsf{P}(w)\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}\re w,\quad\text{for }\re w>0,\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\re\mathsf{P}(w)\leq-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}|\re w|,\quad\text{for }\re w<0,

are valid.

(ii)

The inequalities

\displaystyle\im\mathsf{F}(w)\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}\im w,\quad\text{for }\im w>0,\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\im\mathsf{F}(w)\leq-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}|\im w|,\quad\text{for }\im w<0,

are valid.

(iii)

Set
\epsilon_{j}=1
for
j=1,2
, and
\epsilon_{j}=-1
for
j=4,5
. There exists
\eta>0
such that the inequalities

(6.3)

\frac{4}{3}\re\left(w^{3/2}\right)+\epsilon_{j}\im\mathsf{F}\left(w\right)\geq\eta|w-\lambda|,\quad w\in\Gamma_{j}^{\lambda},

are valid whenever
\lambda\in[-\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}]
and
\tau>0
is chosen sufficiently large.

Proof.

We verify that properties (i)–(ii) are valid for
w\in D_{\delta}(0)
, eventually taking
\delta,\lambda_{0}
sufficiently small. Once this is established, the fact that we can extend such properties to the whole complex plane is a cumbersome but straightforward construction using basic interpolation techniques, and we skip it.

Both
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{F}
are real-valued on
\mathbb{R}\cap D_{\delta}(0)
, by their very definition as real power series near the origin. Furthermore, since we are assuming
\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}=\mathsf{P}^{\prime}(0)>0
and
\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}=\mathsf{F}^{\prime}(0)>0
, the claimed properties (i) and (ii) are valid in
D_{\delta}(0)
, eventually making
\delta>0
sufficiently small. Observe that these properties considered so far do not depend on
\lambda
.

Property (iii), however, also depends on
\lambda
as it is a property along
\Gamma_{j}^{\lambda}
. Once again, we prove it for
w\in\Gamma_{j}^{\lambda}\cap D_{\delta}(0)
, and because we are assuming that
\lambda\in[-\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}]
, the proof for general
w\in\Gamma_{j}^{\lambda}
follows by adjusting
\lambda_{0}>0
if necessary.

For the sake of clarity in the arguments, we prove (
6.3
) for
j=1,2
, the proof for
j=4,5
is analogous.

Write
w\in D_{\delta}(0)
with
\im w>0
as
w=r\ee^{\mathrm{i}\theta}
,
r=|w|
,
\theta=\arg w\in(0,\pi)
. Using (ii), we bound

\frac{4}{3}\re\left(w^{3/2}\right)+\im\mathsf{F}\left(w\right)\geq\frac{4}{3}\re\left(w^{3/2}\right)+\frac{1}{2}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}}\im w=\frac{4}{3}r^{3/2}\cos\left(\frac{3}{2}\theta\right)+\frac{1}{2}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}}r\sin\theta.

Using that
\cos(3\theta/2)\geq-2\sin\theta
for
\theta\in[0,\pi]
, we obtain

\frac{4}{3}\re\left(w^{3/2}\right)+\im\mathsf{F}\left(w\right)\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}}-\frac{8}{3}r^{1/2}\right)r\sin\theta.

Thus, by making
\delta>0
sufficiently small, we can make sure that
{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}}/2-8r^{1/2}/3\geq{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}}/4
for every
r\in[0,\delta]
, concluding that

\frac{4}{3}\re\left(w^{3/2}\right)+\im\mathsf{F}\left(w\right)\geq\frac{1}{8}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}}r\sin\theta=\frac{1}{8}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}}\im w,\quad\text{for every }w\in D_{\delta}(0).

The proof is then completed by noticing that for
w\in\Gamma^{\lambda}_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}^{\lambda}
, we have
\im w=\im(w-\lambda)=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}|w-\lambda|
.
∎

From now on, given admissible functions
\mathsf{P},\mathsf{F}
, we already assume that they have been extended to
\mathbb{C}
, as ensured by Proposition
6.2
.

For real-valued parameters
s\in\mathbb{R},u,\tau>0
, we introduce scaled versions of admissible functions:

(6.4)

\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=-\frac{s}{\tau}+\tau^{2}\mathsf{P}\left(\frac{\zeta}{\tau^{2}}\right),\quad\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=-u\tau^{2}+\tau^{2}\mathsf{F}\left(\frac{\zeta}{\tau^{2}}\right),\quad\zeta\in\mathbb{C}.

For us, we allow
u>0
to vary with
\tau
as
\tau\to+\infty
, but remaining in a fixed compact set of
(0,+\infty)
, but we never stress this fact further. In our original context of dOPEs and the stochastic six-vertex model, we will in fact choose
\tau=N^{1/3}
, but in our study of the model problem we opt for using this parameter
\tau
as a dummy parameter, which may be seem as a (large) time parameter. The value
u
itself will later be chosen as
2\pi\mathsf{a}
.

The parameter
s\in\mathbb{R}
will also be allowed to vary with
\tau
, but within the following regime: for
any

T>0
sufficiently large but fixed, we assume that

(6.5)

-\frac{1}{T}\tau^{3/2}\leq s\leq\frac{1}{T}\tau^{3/2}.

Under the identification
\tau=N^{1/3}
, such restriction is simply capturing that we are working under Assumptions
2.7

When we say that a property holds uniformly for
s
, we always mean that the property holds uniformly for
s
as in (
6.5
), with
T
fixed but possibly made sufficiently large. Later on, we will further split this regime into three distinct sub-regimes in terms of
\tau
, to reflect the sub-regimes from Assumptions
2.7
.

Throughout the whole paper, a zero of
\mathsf{P}_{\tau}
on the real line will play a major role, and we now introduce it.

Proposition 6.3
.

Suppose that
s
is within the regime (
6.5
). The equation

\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)=0

has exactly one solution
\zeta=\zeta_{\tau}(s)
on the real axis, and for
\zeta\in\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
,

\re\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)>0\quad\text{if }\re\zeta>\zeta_{\tau}(s),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\re\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)<0\quad\text{if }\re\zeta<\zeta_{\tau}(s).

Furthermore, this solution
\zeta_{\tau}(s)
is a real analytic function of
s\in\mathbb{R}
, and it satisfies

(6.6)

\zeta_{\tau}(s)=\frac{1}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}}\frac{s}{\tau}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(s\tau^{-3})\right),\quad\tau\to\infty,

uniformly for
s
within the regime (
6.5
), and where we recall that
\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}>0
is as in Definition
6.1
.

Proof.

The zeros of
\mathsf{P}_{\tau}
are solutions to

(6.7)

\mathsf{P}\left(\frac{\zeta}{\tau^{2}}\right)=\frac{s}{\tau^{3}}

Thanks to Proposition
6.2
–(i), the function
\zeta\mapsto\mathsf{P}
is strictly increasing, becomes negative for very negative values of
\zeta
and positive for very positive values of
\zeta
. Thus, there is exactly one such solution
\zeta_{\tau}(s)
, and we may invert
\mathsf{P}
on the real axis and conclude that

\zeta_{\tau}(s)=\tau^{2}\mathsf{P}^{-1}\left(\frac{s}{\tau^{3}}\right)

The local behavior of
\mathsf{P}
near the origin (see Definition
6.1
) ensures that
\mathsf{P}
is an analytic invertible function in a neighborhood of the origin, say
D_{\varepsilon}(0)
. Thanks to (
6.5
), by making
T
sufficiently large we can be sure that
|s/\tau^{3}|<\varepsilon
for
\tau
sufficiently large, and the Implicit Function Theorem gives us that
\zeta_{\tau}(s)
is an analytic function of
s
that satisfies (
6.6
) uniformly as claimed.

Finally, the inequalities on
\re\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)
also follow from the fact that
\mathsf{P}
is increasing over the real line.
∎

We will also need a certain control on the values of the functions
\mathsf{P}_{\tau}
and
\mathsf{F}_{\tau}
over the contour
\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
. We collect these properties as separate results. Many of the properties that we now collect are based on the following observation. For
s
within the critical regime, a Taylor expansion shows that the approximations

(6.8)

\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)=\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}(\zeta-\zeta_{\tau}(s))+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{s^{2}}{\tau^{4}}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)=-u\tau^{2}+\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}\zeta+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{s^{2}}{\tau^{4}}\right)

are valid as
\tau\to\infty
, uniformly for
(1+|\zeta_{\tau}(s)|)^{-1}\zeta
in compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}
. Thus, in essence, in the analysis that follows we are always guided by these linear approximations to
\mathsf{P}_{\tau}
and
\mathsf{F}_{\tau}
.

For instance, this approximation indicates that

\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}}\approx\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}(\zeta-\zeta_{\tau}(s))}}

Hence, any disc centered at
\zeta_{\tau}(s)
of radius
\mathcal{O}(1)
contains
\mathcal{O}(\tau)
poles of the left-hand side. We will have to control the location of such poles, and to do it we first establish a general lemma.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
G\subset\mathbb{C}
be an arbitrary subset of the complex plane. Suppose that

(6.9)

\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in G\\
k\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}|w-(2k+1)\pi\mathrm{i}|\geq\epsilon,

for some
\epsilon>0
. Then there exists
\delta>0
such that

\inf_{w\in G}|1+\ee^{w}|\geq\delta.

Proof.

Suppose, to the contrary, that there exists a sequence
(w_{n})\subset G
for which

|1+\ee^{w_{n}}|\to 0,\quad n\to\infty.

Write
w_{n}=x_{n}+\mathrm{i}y_{n}
,
x_{n},y_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
. From the basic inequality
|\ee^{x_{n}}-1|=||\ee^{w_{n}}|-|-1||\leq|\ee^{w_{n}}+1|
we learn that
x_{n}\to 0
as well, and from (
6.9
) we see that there exists
n_{0}>0
such that

(6.10)

\inf_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}|y_{n}-(2k+1)\pi\mathrm{i}|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{2},\quad\text{ for every }n\geq n_{0}.

From a Taylor expansion,

\ee^{w_{n}}=\ee^{x_{n}}\ee^{\mathrm{i}y_{n}}=(1+\mathcal{O}(x_{n}))\ee^{\mathrm{i}y_{n}}=\ee^{\mathrm{i}y_{n}}+\mathcal{O}(x_{n}),

and therefore

(6.11)

|1+\ee^{\mathrm{i}y_{n}}|\to 0

we must have
|1+\ee^{\mathrm{i}y_{n}}|\to 0
. We view
(y_{n})
as a sequence of angles, and denote
\theta_{n}=y_{n}\mod 2\pi
, with
\theta_{n}\in[0,2\pi)
. By passing to a subsequence if necessary, we may assume that
(\theta_{n})
converges in
[0,2\pi]
, and from (
6.11
) we learn that
\theta_{n}\to-\pi
. In particular, this convergence ensures that

\inf_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}|y_{n}-(2k+1)\pi|=\inf_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}|\theta_{n}-(2k+1)\pi|\to 0,\quad n\to\infty,

where the first equality above is valid because
\theta_{n}=y_{n}\mod 2\pi
. But this last conclusion is in contradiction with (
6.10
), concluding the proof.
∎

With the next result, we ensure that the factor
(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})^{-1}
remains bounded along the contour
\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
, in spite of having poles accumulating near
\zeta_{\tau}(s)
as
\tau\to\infty
.

Proposition 6.5
.

Suppose that
s
satisfies (
6.5
).

Then, there exist
\tau_{0}>0
and
\delta>0
such that

(6.12)

|1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}|\geq\delta,\quad\tau\geq\tau_{0},\quad\text{for every }\zeta\in\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)},

and for every
\tau\geq\tau_{0}
.

Proof.

The proof will be split into two parts. In the first part, we prove that if
|\re\zeta|
is sufficiently large in an explicit manner, then the mild bound from Proposition
6.2
will give that
|1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}|
has to be bounded from below. In the second part, we consider
|\re\zeta|
to be of moderate size, and then use Lemma
6.4
in combination with the power series (
6.8
) to conclude the proof.

Proceeding with the first part, assume that
\zeta\in\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
satisfies
|\re\zeta|\geq M|\zeta_{\tau}(s)|+1
with
M=\frac{8}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}}+2
, say first with
\re\zeta\geq M|\zeta_{\tau}(s)|+1>0
. Using Proposition
6.2
–(i) we compute

\displaystyle\re\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{s}{\tau}+\frac{1}{2}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}}\re\zeta=\frac{1}{4}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}}\re(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})+\frac{1}{4}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}}\re\zeta+\frac{1}{4}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}}{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}-\frac{s}{\tau}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}}\re(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})+\frac{1}{4}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}}M|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|+\frac{1}{4}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}}{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}-\frac{s}{\tau}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}}\re(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})+\frac{1}{4}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}}(2|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})+2|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|-\frac{s}{\tau}

(6.13)

\displaystyle>\frac{1}{4}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}}\re(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})+2|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|-\frac{s}{\tau}.

From the expansion (
6.6
) we learn that
2|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|-\frac{s}{\tau}\geq 0
for
\tau
sufficiently large, and therefore we just learnt that in this case

(6.14)

\re\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)\geq\frac{1}{4}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}}\re(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})\geq\frac{1}{4}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}},

where in the last step we used that
\re\zeta\geq M|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|+1\geq{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}+1
. Hence, still in the case
\re\zeta\geq M|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|+1
we learn that

|1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}|\geq\ee^{\tau{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}}/4}-1,

and the right-hand side can be made strictly positive by choosing
\tau>0
sufficiently large. In a completely analogous way, in the case when
\re\zeta\leq-(M|\zeta_{\tau}(s)|+1)<0
we use again Proposition
6.2
–(i) to obtain

(6.15)

\re\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)\leq-\frac{1}{4}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}}|\re(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})|\leq-\frac{1}{4}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}},

and therefore

|1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}|\geq 1-\ee^{-\tau{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}}/4},

which again can be made strictly positive. This finishes the first part of the proof.

For the second part, assume that
\zeta\in\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
with
|\re\zeta|\leq(M|\zeta_{\tau}(s)|+1)
. Our next goal is to show that for such values of
\zeta
, the condition (
6.9
) for
w=\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)
is satisfied for some
\epsilon>0
.

If
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}
, then because
\mathsf{P}_{\tau}
is real-valued we simply bound
|1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}|=1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}\geq 1
, and from now on we assume that
\zeta\in\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\setminus\mathbb{R}
.

Because
\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\setminus\mathbb{R}
consists of straight lines with positive angle from the real axis, we can find a new value
M^{\prime}>0
, independent of other parameters, such that

|\zeta|\leq M^{\prime}|\re\zeta|\leq M^{\prime}(M|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|+1).

For later use, denote

G=\{\zeta\in\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\setminus\mathbb{R}{\,|\,}|\zeta|\leq M^{\prime}|\re\zeta|\leq M^{\prime}(M|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|+1)\}.

In particular, for
\zeta\in G
we have that
|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|^{-1}\zeta
remains within a compact set of the complex plane, and the expansion (
6.8
) can be used. We write
\zeta\in G
as
\zeta={\zeta_{\tau}(s)}+r\omega
, with
r=|\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|
,
|\omega|=1
with
|\re\omega|=\frac{1}{2}
and
|\im\omega|=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
. In particular, we may write
\im\omega=\varsigma\re w
with
\varsigma\in\{\sqrt{3},-\sqrt{3}\}
. In these coordinates, the expansion (
6.8
) then says that

\im\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)=\varsigma\re\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{s^{2}}{\tau^{4}}\right).

Hence, we can choose
T>0
such that for
s
within the regime (
6.5
),

(6.16)

|\im\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)-\varsigma\re\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(s)|\leq\frac{1}{10\tau},\quad\zeta\in G.

The value
1/10
here is chosen just for concreteness, any sufficiently small value would suffice for what follows.

Now, using the inequality
|w|\geq\frac{1}{2}(|\re w|+|\im w|),w\in\mathbb{C}
, we write for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\zeta\in G
,

|\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)-(2k+1)\pi\mathrm{i}|\geq\frac{1}{2}\left(\Big||(2k+1)\pi\mathrm{i}|-|\tau\im\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)|\Big|+|\tau\re\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)|\right)

If, say,
|\tau\re\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)|\geq\frac{1}{10\tau}
, then the right-hand side above is obviously bounded by
1/10
. If, on the other hand,
|\tau\re\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)|\leq\frac{1}{10\tau}
, then (
6.16
) ensures that
|\im\mathsf{P}_{\tau}|\leq\frac{1+|\varsigma|}{10\tau}=\frac{1+\sqrt{3}}{10\tau}
, and we bound the right-hand side above obtaining

|\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)-(2k+1)\pi\mathrm{i}|\geq\frac{1}{2}|2k+1|\pi-|\tau\im\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)|\geq\frac{1}{2}\pi-\frac{1+\sqrt{3}}{10}\geq 1

We just verified that

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\zeta\in G\\
k\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}|\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)-(2k+1)\pi\mathrm{i}|\geq\frac{1}{10}.

The proof is now finally completed using Lemma
6.4
.
∎

Remark 6.6
.

The proof of Proposition
6.5
actually shows an additional fact that will be useful later: given
\varepsilon>0
, there exist
\eta>0
and
C>0
such that

(6.17)

\displaystyle|\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}|\geq C\ee^{\tau\eta|\re(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})|},\quad\text{for every }\zeta\in\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\text{ with }\re(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})\geq 0,

\displaystyle|\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}|\leq C\ee^{-\tau\eta|\re(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})|},\quad\text{for every }\zeta\in\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\text{ with }\re(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})\leq 0,

and these inequalities are valid for every
s
within the regime (
6.5
).

Indeed, if
|\re\zeta|\geq M|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|+1
, then the developments in (
6.13
), (
6.14
) and (
6.15
) already show that

\displaystyle\ee^{\tau\re\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}\geq\ee^{\frac{1}{4}\tau{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}}\re(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})},\quad\text{if }\re(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})>0,\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\quad\ee^{\tau\re\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}\leq\ee^{-\frac{1}{4}\tau{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}}|\re(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})|},\quad\text{if }\re(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})<0.

If, on the other hand,
|\re\zeta|\leq M|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|+1
, the expansion (
6.8
) yields

(6.18)

\re\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)-{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}}\re(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{s^{2}}{\tau^{4}}\right).

For
s
within the critical regime, the error term on the right-hand side is
\mathcal{O}(\tau^{-1})
. Multiplying across by
\tau
and taking exponentials, we see that in this case

|\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}|=\ee^{\mathcal{O}(1)}\ee^{{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}}\re(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})},

and the claim (
6.17
) follows.

The next result is the analogue of Proposition
6.5
for factors of the form
(1+\ee^{\pm\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}})^{-1}
. In virtue of the factors
\mathrm{i}
and
u
in the exponent, we cannot ensure this term is bounded away from zero everywhere, but only outside a small neighborhood of the point
\zeta_{\tau}(s)
. Nevertheless, this will suffice for our purposes.

Proposition 6.7
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, there exist
\tau_{0}>0
and
\delta>0
such that

(6.19)

|1-\ee^{\mp\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)}|\geq\delta,\quad\text{for every }\zeta\in\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\text{ with }\pm\im\zeta>0\text{ and }|\zeta-\zeta_{\tau}(s)|\geq\varepsilon,

for every
\tau>\tau_{0}
and for every
s
within the regime (
6.5
).

Proof.

Write
\zeta={\zeta_{\tau}(s)}+r\omega\in\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\setminus\mathbb{R}
with
r>0
,
\im\omega=\varsigma\re\omega
,
|\omega|=1
and
\varsigma\in\{\sqrt{3},-\sqrt{3}\}
. We verify the claim for
\im\zeta=r\im\omega>0
, so that
\im\omega=\sqrt{3}/2
. The claim when
\zeta=r\im\omega=-r\sqrt{3}/2<0
follows analogously.

Using Proposition
6.2
–(ii), we compute that

(6.20)

\im\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)=\tau^{2}\im\mathsf{F}\left(\frac{\zeta}{\tau^{2}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}}\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}r\geq{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}}r.

Therefore, using that
r=|\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|\geq\varepsilon
,

|1-\ee^{-\tau\mathrm{i}\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)}|\geq|\ee^{-\tau\mathrm{i}\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)}|-1=\ee^{\tau\im\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)}-1\geq\ee^{\tau{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}}r}-1\geq\ee^{\tau{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}}\varepsilon}-1

Hence, by choosing
\tau\geq\tau_{0}
with
\tau_{0}>0
sufficiently large, we can make sure that the right-hand side above is strictly positive, concluding the proof.
∎

Remark 6.8
.

Equation (
6.20
) actually shows that the inequality

(6.21)

|\ee^{\pm\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)}|\leq\ee^{-{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}}\tau|\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|},\quad\zeta\in\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\text{ with }\pm\im\zeta>0,

is valid for every
\tau>0
and every
s
.

At the technical level, the so-called small norm estimates that will appear in the asymptotic analysis of
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
will involve the scalar factors in the jump
\mathbf{J}_{\tau}
from (
6.23
), and before starting with the asymptotic analysis of
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
we collect such estimates with the next result.

Proposition 6.9
.

Suppose that
s
is in the regime (
6.5
). There exists
\eta=\eta(T)>0
such that the functions
\mathsf{P}_{\tau}
and
\mathsf{F}_{\tau}
satisfy the following estimates as
\tau\to+\infty
.

(i)

The estimate

\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\ee^{-\tau\eta|\zeta-\zeta_{\tau}(s)|}\right)

holds true uniformly for
\zeta\in\Gamma_{0}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cup\Gamma_{1}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cup\Gamma_{5}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
.

(ii)

The estimates

\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}=\mathcal{O}\left(\ee^{-\tau\eta|\zeta-\zeta_{\tau}(s)|}\right),\quad\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}}=1+\mathcal{O}\left(\ee^{-\tau\eta|\zeta-\zeta_{\tau}(s)|}\right)

hold true uniformly for
\zeta\in\Gamma_{2}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cup\Gamma_{3}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cup\Gamma_{4}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
.

(iii)

Set
\epsilon_{j}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=1
for
j=1,2
, and
\epsilon_{j}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=-1
for
j=4,5
. The estimates

\ee^{\epsilon_{j}\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)}=\mathcal{O}\left(\ee^{-\tau\eta|\zeta-\zeta_{\tau}(s)|}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\ee^{-\left(\frac{4}{3}\zeta^{3/2}-\epsilon_{j}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)\right)}=\mathcal{O}\left(\ee^{-\tau\eta|\zeta-\zeta_{\tau}(s)|}\right),

hold true uniformly for
\zeta\in\Gamma_{j}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
and
j=1,2,4,5
, as well as for any
\rho>0
, the estimate

\frac{1}{1-\ee^{\epsilon_{j}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\ee^{-\tau\eta|\zeta-\zeta_{\tau}(s)|}\right),

holds true uniformly for
\zeta\in\Gamma_{j}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\setminus D_{\rho}({\zeta_{\tau}(s)})
,
j=1,2,4,5
.

Proof.

Thanks to Proposition
6.5
and Lemma
A.1
, we see that in order to estimate terms of the form
1/(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})
along
\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
, it suffices to provide estimates for
\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}
. Having in mind that for
\zeta\in\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
, we have
|\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|=\varsigma|\re(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})|
with
\varsigma\in\{1/2,1\}
, Equations (
6.17
) provide the estimates claimed in (i) and (ii).

The first and third estimates in (iii) follow similarly, applying Lemma
A.1
and (
6.21
).

It remains to prove the second estimate in (iii). For that, we use (
6.4
) and write

\re\left(\frac{4}{3}\zeta^{3/2}-\mathrm{i}\epsilon_{j}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)\right)=\tau^{3}\left(\re\left(\frac{\zeta}{\tau^{2}}\right)^{3/2}+\epsilon_{j}\im\mathsf{F}\left(\frac{\zeta}{\tau^{2}}\right)\right)

Now we apply Proposition
6.2
–(iii) with
\lambda={\zeta_{\tau}(s)}/\tau^{2}=\mathcal{O}(\tau^{-1/2})
and
w=\zeta/\tau^{2}\in\Gamma^{\lambda}_{j}
.

∎

6.2.
The model problem

We are finally ready to introduce the model problem. The RHP we are about to introduce depends on a parameter
\tau>0
, which will eventually be made sufficiently large. In everything that follows, we assume that the value
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
in (
6.1
) satisfies

(6.22)

\frac{|\lambda|}{\tau^{2}}\leq\lambda_{0},\quad\text{with}\quad\lambda_{0}\leq\frac{\delta}{2},

for the value
\lambda_{0}>0
from Proposition
6.1
. Such condition ensures, for instance, that when evaluating
\mathsf{P}(\zeta/\tau^{2})
and
\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta/\tau^{2})
at
\zeta=\lambda
, the particular value of the argument
\lambda/\tau^{2}
falls within the disk where
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{F}
are analytic. We will eventually make such
\delta
arbitrarily small (but fixed) throughout the whole analysis.

Let
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{F})
be a pair of admissible functions as in Definition
6.1
and Proposition
6.2
,
\mathsf{P}_{\tau}
and
\mathsf{F}_{\tau}
as in (
6.4
), and
\Gamma^{\lambda}
as in (
6.1
). Consider the following RHP, the
model problem
in the critical regime.

RHP 6.10
.

For a parameter
\tau>0
, find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
with the following properties.

(1)

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma^{\lambda}
.

(2)

The matrix
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
has continuous boundary values
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau,\pm}
along
\Gamma^{\lambda}\setminus\{\lambda\}
, and they are related by
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau,+}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau,-}(\zeta)\mathbf{J}_{\tau}(\zeta)
,
\zeta\in\Gamma^{\lambda}\setminus\{\lambda\}
, where

(6.23)

\mathbf{J}_{\tau}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}}\mathbf{E}_{12},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{0}^{\lambda},\\
\mathbf{I}-\frac{1}{(1-\ee^{-\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)})(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})}\mathbf{E}_{12},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{1}^{\lambda},\\
\left(\mathbf{I}+(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{1}{(1-\ee^{-\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)})(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})}\mathbf{E}_{12}\right),&\zeta\in\Gamma_{2}^{\lambda},\\
\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}}\mathbf{E}_{12}-(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})\mathbf{E}_{21},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{3}^{\lambda},\\
\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{1}{(1-\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)})(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})}\mathbf{E}_{12}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}+(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})\mathbf{E}_{21}\right),&\zeta\in\Gamma_{4}^{\lambda},\\
\mathbf{I}-\frac{1}{(1-\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)})(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})}\mathbf{E}_{12},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{5}^{\lambda}.\end{cases}

(3)

As
\zeta\to\infty
,

(6.24)

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}(\zeta)=\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{\zeta}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}^{(1)}+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-2})\right)\zeta^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\ee^{-\frac{2}{3}\zeta^{3/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}.

(4)

As
\zeta\to\lambda
,

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}(\zeta)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}(1),&\text{if }\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\lambda)}\neq 1,\\
\mathcal{O}(1),&\text{if }\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\lambda)}=1\text{ and }\zeta\notin\Omega_{1}^{\lambda}\cup\Omega_{4}^{\lambda},\\
\mathcal{O}\begin{pmatrix}1&(\zeta-\lambda)^{-1}\\
1&(\zeta-\lambda)^{-1}\end{pmatrix},&\text{if }\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\lambda)}=1\text{ and }\zeta\in\Omega_{1}^{\lambda}\cup\Omega_{4}^{\lambda}.\end{cases}

The conditions in RHP
6.10
(1)–(3) are usual conditions when using model RHPs within the Deift-Zhou nonlinear steepest descent analysis. The form of condition RHP
6.10
(4) is unusual, in the sense that it depends on the nature of the point
\lambda
, but it is needed because when
\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\lambda)}=1
the jump matrix
\mathbf{J}_{\tau}
becomes unbounded as
\zeta\to\lambda
along
\Gamma^{\lambda}_{1}\cup\Gamma^{\lambda}_{2}\cup\Gamma^{\lambda}_{4}\cup\Gamma^{\lambda}_{5}
. This condition will also appear naturally later on, when we discuss the asymptotic analysis for the discrete OPs. Although this condition will follow us throughout the whole analysis, it poses only a technical issue but not conceptual ones, and will more or less resolve itself automatically.

From the very Definition
6.1
of admissible functions
\mathsf{F},\mathsf{P}
and (
6.4
), there exists
\varepsilon>0
for which
1/(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}})
is analytic on
D_{\varepsilon}({\zeta_{\tau}(s)})
, and the term
1/(1+\ee^{\pm\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}})
is either analytic on
D_{\varepsilon}({\zeta_{\tau}(s)})
or it has
{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
as its unique pole in
D_{\varepsilon}({\zeta_{\tau}(s)})
(compare with (
6.29
)). This value
\varepsilon>0
obviously depends on
\tau
, but for now it suffices to consider
\tau
as fixed.

Recall that the sectors
\Omega_{j}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
are displayed in Figure
6
. In the neighborhood
D_{\varepsilon}({\zeta_{\tau}(s)})
, we modify

(6.25)

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}(\zeta)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\mathbf{E}_{12}}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}}\right),&\zeta\in\Omega_{0}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cap D_{\varepsilon}({\zeta_{\tau}(s)}),\\
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}(\zeta)\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\mathbf{E}_{12}}{(1-\ee^{-\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)})(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\mathbf{E}_{12}}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}}\right),&\zeta\in\Omega_{1}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cap D_{\varepsilon}({\zeta_{\tau}(s)}),\\
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}(\zeta)\left(\mathbf{I}+(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\mathbf{E}_{12}}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}}\right),&\zeta\in\Omega_{2}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cap D_{\varepsilon}({\zeta_{\tau}(s)}),\\
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}(\zeta)(\mathbf{I}-(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})\mathbf{E}_{21}),&\zeta\in\Omega_{3}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cap D_{\varepsilon}({\zeta_{\tau}(s)}),\\
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}(\zeta)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\mathbf{E}_{12}}{(1-\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)})(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})}\right),&\zeta\in\Omega_{4}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cap D_{\varepsilon}({\zeta_{\tau}(s)}),\\
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}(\zeta),&\zeta\in\Omega^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{5}\cap D_{\varepsilon}({\zeta_{\tau}(s)}),\end{cases}

and make

(6.26)

\mathcal{H}_{\tau}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}(\zeta+{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})\right]_{21,-},\quad\zeta\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon),

where the operator
\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}
is defined as in (
2.53
). Naturally
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}(\zeta{\,|\,}s)
and
\mathcal{H}_{\tau}(\zeta)=\mathcal{H}_{\tau}(\zeta{\,|\,}s)
also depend on
s
, but we omit from our notation. From (
6.25
), we obtain

\mathcal{H}_{\tau}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}(\zeta+{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})\right]_{21,-},\quad 0<\zeta<\varepsilon,

This function
\mathcal{H}_{\tau}
is central to our work: with the appropriate identification of
\tau
with
N
, it will turn out to be given by
\mathcal{H}_{\tau}=2\pi\mathrm{i}\mathsf{H}_{N}
, with
\mathsf{H}_{N}
being the function appearing in Theorem
2.8
. We now collect some properties that will be relevant for later.

Proposition 6.11
.

Assume that
s
is within the regime (
6.5
).

(i)

The function
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
is analytic in a neighborhood of
\zeta=0
, and
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau,-}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}(\zeta)
for
\zeta>0
.

(ii)

There exists
\kappa>0
, independent of
s,\tau
, such that
\mathcal{H}_{\tau}
extends to a matrix-valued real-analytic function in the real interval
(-\kappa\tau^{2},\kappa\tau^{2})
. Furthermore, it verifies the relation

(6.27)

\mathcal{H}_{\tau}(x-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})=\\
\begin{cases}\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau,-}(x)\right]_{21},&x>{\zeta_{\tau}(s)},\\
-(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(x)})^{2}\left[\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\mathbf{E}_{12}}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(x)}}\right)\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau,-}(x)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\mathbf{E}_{12}}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(x)}}\right)\right]_{12}-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\zeta}(\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})\Big|_{\zeta=x},&x<{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}.\end{cases}

Proof.

The relation between
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
and
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau,-}
claimed in (i) is immediate from (
6.25
).

The definition of
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
is performed in such a way that
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
is the analytic continuation of
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
around the point
{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
, starting from the sector
\Omega_{0}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
. In other words, using the very definition of
\mathbf{J}_{\tau}
as the jump of
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
in (
6.23
), it follows that
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
has no jumps around the point
{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
, and as such
{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
is an isolated singularity of
\mathbf{\Phi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
. Now, sending
\zeta\to{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
, say along
\overline{\Omega_{5}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}}\cap({\zeta_{\tau}(s)},{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}+\varepsilon)
, we see from RHP
6.10
–(4) that this isolated singularity is removable. Thus,
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
is analytic near
{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
, proving (i).

Next, RHP
6.10
–(2) shows that
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
has an analytic continuation from the lower half plane to any interval
J\subset\mathbb{R}
, as long as the functions
\zeta\mapsto\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)
and
\zeta\mapsto 1/(1+\ee^{\tau{\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}})
are analytic in a neighborhood of this interval
J
. From the definition of admissibility and (
6.4
), we see that these latter functions are analytic on intervals of size
\mathcal{O}(\tau^{2})
. This argument suffices to show that for some
\kappa>0
,
\mathcal{H}_{\tau}
is real-analytic in
(-\kappa\tau^{2},\kappa\tau^{2})
.

Equation (
6.27
) for
x>{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
follows directly from (
6.25
), (
6.26
), once one notices that for
x>{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
, we have
x-\mathrm{i}\eta\in\Omega^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{5}
for
\eta>0
sufficiently small.

Now, for
x<{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
, we have
x-\mathrm{i}\eta\in\Omega^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{3}
for
\eta>0
sufficiently small, and from the very definition of
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
in
\Omega_{3}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
we write

\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\mathbf{E}_{12}}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}}}\right)\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau,-}\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\mathbf{E}_{12}}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}}}\right)=(\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})^{\prime}\left[\mathbf{E}_{21}+\frac{\mathbf{\bm{\sigma}}_{3}}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}}}-\frac{\mathbf{E}_{12}}{(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}})^{2}}\right]\\
+\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\mathbf{E}_{12}}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}}}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}-(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}})\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau,-}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}\left(\mathbf{I}+(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}})\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\mathbf{E}_{12}}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}}}\right).

Taking the
(1,2)
-entry, and using the algebraic identity

(6.28)

\left[\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{\beta}\mathbf{E}_{12}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}-\beta\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)M\left(\mathbf{I}+\beta\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{1}{\beta}\mathbf{E}_{12}\right)\right]_{12}=-\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}M_{21},

which is valid for any
2\times 2
matrix
M
and any scalar
\beta\neq 0
, we obtain

\left[\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\mathbf{E}_{12}}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}}}\right)\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau,-}\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\mathbf{E}_{12}}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}}}\right)\right]_{12}=-\frac{(\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}})^{\prime}}{(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}})^{2}}-\frac{1}{(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}})^{2}}\left[\Delta_{\zeta}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau,-}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}\right]_{21},

which is equivalent to the second line of (
6.27
).

∎

The solution
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}=\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}(\cdot{\,|\,}\mathsf{P},\mathsf{F},\lambda,s,u)
depends on several input data. In what follows, we will carry out its asymptotic analysis under the following assumption.

Assumptions 6.12
.

We assume

(a)

(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{F})
is a pair of fixed admissible functions.

(b)

s
is allowed to vary with
\tau
, but within the regime (
6.5
).

(c)

u
is allowed to vary with
\tau
, but within compacts of
(0,+\infty)
.

(d)

The choice
\lambda=\zeta_{\tau}(s)
, with
\zeta_{\tau}(s)
as in Proposition
6.3
.

Remark 6.13
.

It is instructive to keep in mind the approximations (
6.8
), which yield in essence that

(6.29)

\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}}\approx\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}\zeta-s}}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{1-\ee^{\pm\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)}}\approx\frac{1}{1-\ee^{\pm\mathrm{i}u\tau^{3}\pm\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}\zeta}}.

To illustrate our next steps, let us formally replace these approximations in (
6.23
). Recalling the sets
\Omega_{j}^{\lambda}
from (
6.1
), if one transforms

(6.30)

\widetilde{\mathbf{\Phi}}_{\tau}(\zeta)=\begin{cases}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}(\zeta)\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}\zeta-s}}\frac{1}{1-\ee^{-\mathrm{i}u\tau^{3}-\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}\zeta}}\mathbf{E}_{12}\right),&z\in\Omega_{1}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\\
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}(\zeta)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}\zeta-s}}\frac{1}{1-\ee^{\mathrm{i}u\tau^{3}+\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}\zeta}}\mathbf{E}_{12}\right),&z\in\Omega_{4}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\\
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}(\zeta),&\text{elsewhere},\end{cases}

then a direct calculation shows that
\widetilde{\mathbf{\Phi}}_{\tau}
has no jumps along
\Gamma^{\lambda}_{1}\cup\Gamma^{\lambda}_{5}
, and in the remaining parts of
\Gamma^{\lambda}
the jump matrix for
\widetilde{\mathbf{\Phi}}_{\tau}
is

(6.31)

\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}\zeta-s}}\mathbf{E}_{12},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{0}^{\lambda},\\
\mathbf{I}+(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}\zeta-s})\mathbf{E}_{21},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{2}^{\lambda}\cup\Gamma_{4}^{\lambda},\\
\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}\zeta-s}}\mathbf{E}_{12}-(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}\zeta-s})\mathbf{E}_{21},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{3}^{\lambda}.\end{cases}

Also formally ignoring possible poles of the transformation factors in (
6.30
), the transformation
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}\mapsto\widetilde{\mathbf{\Phi}}_{\tau}
does not affect the behavior near
\infty
, that is, we still have

(6.32)

\widetilde{\mathbf{\Phi}}_{\tau}(\zeta)=\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{\zeta}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}^{(1)}+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-2})\right)\zeta^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\ee^{-\frac{2}{3}\zeta^{3/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}},\quad\zeta\to\infty.

Conditions (
6.31
) and (
6.32
) would then say that
\widetilde{\mathbf{\Phi}}_{\tau}
is precisely the solution to the RHP associated to the integro-differential Painlevé II equation. This RHP first appeared in
[
27
]
, in connection with the narrow wedge solution to the KPZ equation, but was realized to be connected to the integro-differential Painlevé II in
[
26
]
, see also
[
55
, Sections 4 and 5]
for the formulation as it appears here.

However, the transformation (
6.30
) involves factors with poles: the factor
1/(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}\zeta-s})
has infinitely many poles at the points
\zeta=(s+(2k+1)\pi\mathrm{i})/\tau
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, which are all located in the regions
\Gamma_{1}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
and
\Gamma_{4}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
where the transformation (
6.30
) is nontrivial. In other words, the matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{\Phi}}_{\tau}
has now poles at these points
\zeta=(s+(2k+1)\pi\mathrm{i})/\tau
, and a proper RHP for it would have to involve a pole condition at these points, and therefore
\widetilde{\mathbf{\Phi}}_{\tau}
is not rigorously comparable with the int-diff PII RHP from the cited works.

Nevertheless, it will turn out that
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
is asymptotically comparable with the int-diff PII. Our next goal is to extract asymptotics of
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
as
\tau\to\infty
. Such asymptotics does depend on how
s
grows with
\tau
, and will be further split into three distinct asymptotic regimes, depending on whether
s/\tau
remains bounded, it is very positive or it is very negative. Each of these three regimes will require a separate asymptotic analysis, and it will turn out that in each of them the asymptotic behavior of
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
has essentially two nontrivial terms, one in direct correspondence with the analogue asymptotic regime of the int-diff PII RHP from
[
26
]
, and one corresponding to the presence of poles of the factor
(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}\zeta-s})^{-1}
.

We now move on to the mentioned asymptotic analysis.

7.
Analysis of related RHPs

The asymptotic analysis of the RHP
6.10
, under Assumptions
6.12
, will display three distinct behaviors, depending on how
s
grows with
\tau
.

Definition 7.1
.

The regime (
6.5
) splits in the following subregimes.

(1)

Subcritical regime
: for any
T>0

sufficiently large
, but fixed, we assume

T\tau\leq s\leq\frac{1}{T}\tau^{3/2}

(2)

Critical regime:
for any
T>0
fixed, we assume

-T\tau\leq s\leq T\tau.

(3)

Supercritical regime:
for any
T>0

sufficiently large
, but fixed, we assume

-\frac{1}{T}\tau^{3/2}\leq s\leq-T\tau.

With the identification
\tau=N^{1/3}
, the subcritical, critical, and supercritical regimes introduced in Definition
7.1
coincide with the regimes in Assumptions
2.7
.

The three different subregimes specified in Definition
7.1
are somewhat explained in terms of the parameter
\zeta_{\tau}(s)
introduced in Proposition
6.3
: the regimes (1), (2) and (3) correspond to whether
\zeta_{\tau}(s)\gg 1
,
\zeta_{\tau}(s)=\mathcal{O}(1)
or
\zeta_{\tau}(s)\ll-1
as
\tau\to\infty
.

In the next section we carry out the asymptotic analysis in each of these regimes. The phenomena that we will observe in each regime may be explained replacing
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
by
\widetilde{\mathbf{\Phi}}_{\tau}
from (
6.30
) and observing the following. In the critical regime, the approximation (
6.31
) suggests that as
\tau\to\infty
,

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{\tau}}\approx\begin{cases}\mathbf{I},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{0}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}=(\zeta_{\tau}(s),+\infty),\\
\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{2}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cup\Gamma_{4}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)},\\
\mathbf{E}_{12}-\mathbf{E}_{21},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{3}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}=(-\infty,-\zeta_{\tau}(s)).\end{cases}

This approximate jump matrix and asymptotic behavior of
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
as
\zeta\to\infty
coincides with the RHP for the Painlevé XXXIV equation. As we will see, in the critical regime the PXXXIV RHP indeed provides a good approximation to
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
away from the point
\zeta_{\tau}(s)
. Near the point
\zeta_{\tau}(s)
, however, we have to devise a local parametrix which, in essence, creates a good approximation to the pole behavior that emerges from the transformation
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}\mapsto\widetilde{\mathbf{\Phi}}_{\tau}
. When performing this approximation, the Painlevé variable
y
corresponds to
y=\zeta_{\tau}(s)\approx\frac{s}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}\tau}
.

When moving from the critical to, say, the subcritical regime, the Painlevé variable
y=\zeta_{\tau}(s)
becomes very large. Rather than still using the PXXXIV RHP as a good approximation for
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
, for technical reasons we have to use instead a RHP related to the integro-differential Painlevé II equation, which in turn in the same asymptotic regime is close to the Airy RHP. Once again, such global parametrix will provide a good approximation for
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
only away from (a scaling of)
{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
, and near this point we have again to construct a local parametrix to essentially handle the poles coming from the factors
(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}})^{-1}
and
1/(1+\ee^{\pm\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}})^{-1}
present in the jump. In the supercritical regime, we use the global parametrix for PXXXIV in the regime
y\to-\infty
, and locally near
{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
we have to use a RHP obtained from a modification of the model problem itself, whose study we postpone to Section
7.3
.

The main goal of the current section is to discuss the RHPs just mentioned, obtaining properties of them that will be needed in the analysis of the model problem.

7.1.
The Painlevé XXXIV RHP

The discussion on the Painlevé XXXIV RHP follows
[
80
]
closely.

Recall that

\Gamma_{2}^{0}=(\infty\ee^{2\pi\mathrm{i}/3},0],\quad\Gamma_{3}^{0}=(-\infty,0],\quad\Gamma_{4}^{0}=(\infty\ee^{4\pi\mathrm{i}/3},0],\quad\text{and set}\quad\Gamma_{\mathbf{\Psi}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Gamma_{2}^{0}\cup\Gamma_{3}^{0}\cup\Gamma_{4}^{0}.

The three rays
\Gamma_{2}^{0}
,
\Gamma_{3}^{0}
and
\Gamma_{4}^{0}
are oriented from
\infty
to the origin. Also, denote

(7.1)

\displaystyle\Omega_{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{\zeta\in\mathbb{C}{\,|\,}|\arg\zeta|<2\pi/3\},

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{\zeta\in\mathbb{C}{\,|\,}2\pi/3<\arg\zeta<\pi\}=\Omega_{2}^{0},

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{\zeta\in\mathbb{C}{\,|\,}-\pi<\arg\zeta<-2\pi/3\}=\Omega_{3}^{0}.

Consider the following RHP.

RHP 7.2
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{\Psi}
with the following properties.

(1)

{\mathbf{\Psi}}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{\Psi}}
.

(2)

{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{+}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Psi}_{-}(\zeta)\mathbf{J}_{0}(\zeta)
,
\zeta\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{\Psi}}
, where

(7.2)

\mathbf{J}_{0}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{2}^{0}\cup\Gamma_{4}^{0},\\
\mathbf{E}_{12}-\mathbf{E}_{21},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{3}^{0}.\end{cases}

(3)

As
\zeta\to\infty
,

(7.3)

\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta)=(\mathbf{I}+\mathrm{i}q\mathbf{E}_{21})\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\mathbf{\Psi}_{1}^{(\infty)}}{\zeta}+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-2})\right)\zeta^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\ee^{-(\frac{2}{3}\zeta^{3/2}+y\zeta^{1/2})\bm{\sigma}_{3}},

where
q\in\mathbb{C}
is a free parameter independent of
\zeta
and that will be chosen appropriately in a moment,
y\in\mathbb{C}
is the Painlevé variable, and
\mathbf{\Psi}_{1}^{(\infty)}=\mathbf{\Psi}_{1}^{(\infty)}(y)
is a
2\times 2
matrix independent of
\zeta
.

(4)

There exists a matrix-valued function
\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}
which is analytic on a disk
D_{\varepsilon}(0)
centered at the origin, and constant matrices
\mathbf{M}_{j}
,
j=0,1,2
, for which

(7.4)

\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}(\zeta)\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\log(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12}\right)\mathbf{M}_{j},\quad\zeta\in D_{\varepsilon}(0)\cap\Omega_{j}.

This is the PXXXIV RHP from
[
80
, Section 2]
, with monodromy data

\alpha=\omega=0.

The solution
\mathbf{\Psi}
and also the term
\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}
both depend on
y
as well, and when needed we write
\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)
and
\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)
etc. In particular,
[
80
, Proposition 1]
ensures that this RHP is solvable for every
y\in\mathbb{R}
. From standard methods, one shows that
y\mapsto\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)
is continuous, hence bounded for
y
within compacts of the real line and
\zeta
within compacts of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{\Psi}}
. Likewise, the matrix
\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}
remains bounded for
y
in compacts of the real line and
\zeta
in a small neighborhood of the origin.

The function
u=u(y)
defined by

u(y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=-\frac{y}{2}-\mathrm{i}\partial_{y}(\mathbf{\Psi}^{(\infty)}_{1}(y))_{12},

satisfies the PXXXIV equation

(7.5)

\partial_{yy}u=4u^{2}+2yu+\frac{(\partial_{y}u)^{2}}{2u}.

Alternatively,
u(y)
can also be related to
\mathbf{\Psi}
through the limit

u(y)=\mathrm{i}\lim_{\zeta\to 0}\zeta\left[\mathbf{\Psi}^{\prime}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)^{-1}\right]_{12},

where here and from now on, the symbol
′
denotes derivative with respect to the (spectral) variable
\zeta
. This solution has no poles on the real axis and has asymptotic behavior

(7.6)

u(y)=\frac{\ee^{-\frac{4}{3}y^{3/2}}}{4\pi y^{1/2}}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(y^{-1/4})\right),\quad y\to+\infty,\qquad\text{and}\qquad u(y)=-\frac{y}{2}-\frac{1}{8y^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(y^{-7/2}),\quad y\to-\infty.

Such asymptotics are stated and proven as above in
[
80
, Theorem 2]
, but follow closely the similar calculations from
[
58
]
performed for slightly different monodromy data.

The solution
u(y)
is classically related to a non-homogeneous Painlevé II equation. With the change of variables
U(y)=2^{1/3}u(-2^{-1/3}y)
, the matrix
U
takes the form

U(y)=\partial_{y}p(y)+p(y)^{2}+\frac{y}{2},\quad\text{where }p\text{ solves the Painlevé II equation}\quad\partial_{yy}p=yp+2p^{3}-\frac{1}{2}.

We need additional information about the RHP above. First of all, the matrices in
\mathbf{\Psi}.4
are determined from

(7.7)

\mathbf{M}_{1}=\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{E}_{21},\quad\mathbf{M}_{0}=\mathbf{M}_{1}(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21})=\mathbf{I},\quad\mathbf{M}_{2}=\mathbf{M}_{0}(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21})=\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21}.

Hence,

\mathbf{M}_{0}=\mathbf{I},\quad\mathbf{M}_{2}=\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21}.

Second, the keen reader will observe that the PXXXIV above does not depend on the factor
q
appearing in
\mathbf{\Psi}{\mathbf{.}3}
. Nevertheless, for any such choice of
q
, possibly depending on
y
, the relations between
u
and
\mathbf{\Psi}
that we just described still hold true. For a particular choice of
q
in
\mathbf{\Psi}{\mathbf{.}3},
it is shown in
[
80
, Equation (45)
et seq.
]
that
\mathbf{\Psi}
is a solution to the Lax pair system

(7.8)

\mathbf{\Psi}^{\prime}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)=\mathbf{A}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta{\,|\,}y),\quad\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)=\mathbf{B}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta{\,|\,}y),

with

\begin{aligned} &\mathbf{A}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\mathbf{A}_{-1}(y)}{\zeta}+\mathbf{A}_{0}(y)-\mathrm{i}\zeta\mathbf{E}_{21},\\
&\mathbf{A}_{-1}(y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\partial_{y}u(y)}{2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}-\mathrm{i}u(y)\mathbf{E}_{12}-\frac{\mathrm{i}\partial_{y}u(y)^{2}}{4u(s)}\mathbf{E}_{21},\\
&\mathbf{A}_{0}(y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{i}\mathbf{E}_{12}-\mathrm{i}(u(y)+y)\mathbf{E}_{21},\end{aligned}\qquad\qquad\begin{aligned} &\mathbf{B}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{B}_{0}(y)-\mathrm{i}\zeta\mathbf{E}_{21},\\
&\mathbf{B}_{0}(y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{i}\mathbf{E}_{12}-i(2u(y)+y)\mathbf{E}_{21}.\end{aligned}

The relevant function
q
for obtaining this Lax pair is given in terms of
u
, namely satisfying the relation

(7.9)

2u(y)=-y-2\partial_{y}q(y).

Hence, for us
q
depends on the Painlevé variable
y
, and from now on we always assume this choice and write
q=q(y)
. Nevertheless, the specific form of
q
will not be important, only the fact that it leads to the Lax pair as stated. Using this function
q(y)
, for later reference it is also useful to express (
7.3
) as

(7.10)

\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta)=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathrm{i}\left(q(y)+\frac{y^{2}}{4}\right)\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-1})\right)\zeta^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\ee^{-\frac{2}{3}(\zeta+y)^{3/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}},\quad\zeta\to\infty.

We need some additional relation between the solution
u
and the solution
\mathbf{\Psi}
that we state as a separate result. For its statement, we introduce

(7.11)

\mathcal{H}(\zeta)=\mathcal{H}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)\right]_{21},&\zeta>0,\\
[(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21})\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{\Psi}_{-}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{E}_{21})]_{21},&\zeta<0,\end{cases}

This function is the analogue of
\mathcal{H}_{\tau}
which we introduced in terms of the model problem in (
6.26
).

Proposition 7.3
.

The function
\zeta\mapsto\mathcal{H}(\zeta)
extends to an analytic function for
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}
in a neighborhood of the origin, which satisfies

\mathcal{H}(0{\,|\,}y)=\pi\mathrm{i}(\partial_{yy}u(y)-6u(y)^{2}-4yu(y)).

Furthermore, the following estimates hold true.

(i)

Given
y_{0}>0
and
\zeta_{0}>0
, there exists
M>0
such that

(7.12)

|\partial_{\zeta}\mathcal{H}(\zeta)|\leq M

for
|\zeta|\leq\zeta_{0}
and
|y|\leq y_{0}
.

(ii)

Given
y_{0}>0
there exist
\eta>0
and
M>0
such that

(7.13)

|\mathcal{H}(\zeta)|\leq M\ee^{-\eta\zeta^{3/2}-2y\zeta^{1/2}}

for
|y|\leq y_{0}
and
\zeta\geq 0
.

(iii)

Given
y_{0}>0
there exist
\eta>0
and
M>0
such that

(7.14)

|\mathcal{H}(\zeta)|\leq M(1+|\zeta|^{3/2})

for
|y|\leq y_{0}
and
\zeta\leq 0
.

Proof.

The matrix
\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}(\zeta{\,|\,}u)
is the matrix from (
7.4
) which is complex-analytic in a neighborhood of
\zeta=0
. Furthermore, from the general theory of RHPs this matrix is also smooth in
y
. Let us expand it in power series near
\zeta=0
,

\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)}+\mathbf{\Psi}_{1}^{(0)}\zeta+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{2}),\quad\zeta\to 0.

Because
\det\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}\equiv 1
, we must have
\det\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)}\equiv 1
as well, and both of these matrices are invertible, with inverses which are smooth functions of
y
.

Using that
\mathbf{M}_{0}=\mathbf{I}
(see (
7.7
)), we compute from RHP
7.2
–(4) that for
\zeta>0
,

(7.15)

\mathcal{H}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)=\left[\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)^{-1}\mathbf{\Psi}^{\prime}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)\right]_{21}=\left[\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)^{-1}\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{\prime}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)\right]_{21},

and therefore

(7.16)

\lim_{\zeta\searrow 0}\mathcal{H}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)=\left[\left(\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{\Psi}_{1}^{(0)}\right]_{21}.

This shows that
\zeta\mapsto\mathcal{H}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)
extends analytically to
\zeta=0
from the right.
In a similar way, since
\mathbf{M}_{2}=\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21}
, we use RHP
7.2
–(4) again to get for
\zeta<0
that

\mathcal{H}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)=[(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21})\mathbf{\Psi}_{-}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)^{-1}\mathbf{\Psi}^{\prime}_{-}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{E}_{21})]_{21}\\
\begin{aligned} &=\left[\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}(\log\zeta)_{-}\mathbf{E}_{12}\right)\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)^{-1}\left(\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)(\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}(\log\zeta)_{-}\mathbf{E}_{12})\right)^{\prime}\right]_{21}&=\left[\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)^{-1}\mathbf{\Psi}^{\prime}_{0}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)\right]_{21}.\end{aligned}

The right-hand side of this expression is analytic in
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}
and smooth in
y
, and agrees with (
7.15
). This shows that
\mathcal{H}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)
is analytic in
\zeta
and smooth in
y
, and also shows the bound (
7.12
).

The bounds (
7.13
) and (
7.14
) follow from the continuity of
\mathcal{H}
as a function of
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
that we just established, together with the asymptotics (
7.3
).

It remains to compute
\mathcal{H}(0{\,|\,}y)
, which we do so using (
7.16
) and with the help of the Lax pair (
7.8
). The identity
\mathbf{\Psi}^{\prime}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{\Psi}
yields

\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{\prime}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\log\zeta}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\mathbf{E}_{12}\right)+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta}\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)\mathbf{E}_{12}=\mathbf{A}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\log\zeta}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\mathbf{E}_{12}\right).

Expanding both sides as
\zeta\to 0
, the terms of order
\zeta^{-1}
and
\zeta^{0}
yield the relations

(7.17)

\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)}\mathbf{E}_{12}=2\pi\mathrm{i}\mathbf{A}_{-1}\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{\Psi}_{1}^{(0)}\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\mathbf{E}_{12}\right)=\mathbf{A}_{0}\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)}+\mathbf{A}_{-1}\mathbf{\Psi}_{1}^{(0)},

which are identities between functions of the variable
y
.
The first equation gives the relation

(\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{-1}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\mathbf{E}_{12}(\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})^{-1}.

Multiplying the second equation in (
7.17
) by
(\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})^{-1}
to the left and using this previous identity, we obtain

(7.18)

\lim_{\zeta\nearrow 0}[(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21})\mathbf{\Psi}_{-}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)^{-1}\mathbf{\Psi}^{\prime}_{-}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{E}_{21})]_{21}=[(\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})^{-1}\mathbf{\Psi}_{1}^{(0)}]_{21}=[(\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{0}\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)}]_{21}.

Our next goal is to express the entries of
\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)}
in terms of the entries of the
\mathbf{A}_{k}
’s. The first row of the first equation in (
7.17
) yields the relations

(7.19)

(\mathbf{A}_{-1})_{11}(\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})_{11}+(\mathbf{A}_{-1})_{12}(\mathbf{\Psi}^{(0)}_{0})_{21}=0,\quad(\mathbf{A}_{-1})_{11}\mathbf{(}\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})_{12}+(\mathbf{A}_{-1})_{12}(\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})_{22}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}(\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})_{11}.

We see these relations as equations on the unknows
(\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})_{jk}
. Let us assume for a moment that
(\mathbf{A}_{-1})_{12}=-\mathrm{i}u(y)\neq 0
, which is true for all but a discrete set of
y\in\mathbb{R}
because
u
is analytic in
y
. Solving the first equation for
(\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})_{21}
we obtain

(7.20)

(\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})_{21}=-(\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})_{11}\frac{(\mathbf{A}_{-1})_{11}}{(\mathbf{A}_{-1})_{12}}.

Also, the second equation writes as

(\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})_{11}=2\pi\mathrm{i}(\mathbf{A}_{-1})_{12}\left((\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})_{22}+\frac{(\mathbf{A}_{-1})_{11}}{(\mathbf{A}_{-1})_{12}}(\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})_{12}\right).

On the other hand, the condition
\det\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)}=1
combined with (
7.20
) yields the relation

(\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})_{11}\left((\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})_{22}+\frac{(\mathbf{A}_{-1})_{11}}{(\mathbf{A}_{-1})_{12}}(\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})_{12}\right)=1.

Thus, we concluded the relations

\left((\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})_{11}\right)^{2}=2\pi\mathrm{i}{(\mathbf{A}_{-1}})_{12}\quad\text{and}\quad\left((\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})_{21}\right)^{2}=\left((\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(0)})_{11}\frac{(\mathbf{A}_{-1})_{11}}{(\mathbf{A}_{-1})_{12}}\right)^{2}=2\pi\mathrm{i}\frac{\left((\mathbf{A}_{-1})_{11}\right)^{2}}{(\mathbf{A}_{-1})_{12}}.

With the help of these equations we express the right-hand side of (
7.18
) solely in terms of the entries of
\mathbf{A}_{0}
and
\mathbf{A}_{-1}
, which leads to the claimed result.
∎

It is instructive to interpret
\mathcal{H}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)
in terms of a correlation kernel. For that, introduce

(7.21)

\mathsf{K}_{\rm P34}(\zeta,\xi)=\mathsf{K}_{\rm P34}(\zeta,\xi{\,|\,}y)\\
\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{1}{\zeta-\xi}\left[(\mathbf{I}+\chi_{(-\infty,0)}(\xi)\mathbf{E}_{21})\mathbf{\Psi}_{-}(\xi{\,|\,}y)^{-1}\mathbf{\Psi}_{-}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)(\mathbf{I}-\chi_{(-\infty,0)}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{21})\right]_{21},\quad\zeta,\xi\in\mathbb{R},\quad\zeta\neq\xi.

Extending
\mathsf{K}_{\rm P34}
to the diagonal
\zeta=\xi
by continuity, we then obtain that

(7.22)

\mathcal{H}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)=2\pi\mathrm{i}\,\mathsf{K}_{\rm P34}(\zeta,\zeta{\,|\,}y),\quad\zeta\in\mathbb{R},\quad y\in\mathbb{R}.

Proposition
7.3
then shows that

\mathsf{K}_{{\rm P34}}(0,0{\,|\,}y)=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{yy}u(y)-3u(y)^{2}-2yu(y)

in terms of the Painlevé XXXIV transcendent
u(y)
, which is the same as (
1.21
).

We now characterize this kernel in a different manner. For that, let us define

\mathbf{\Psi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta),&\zeta\in\Omega_{0},\\
\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta)(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21}),&\zeta\in\Omega_{1},\\
\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta)(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{E}21),&\zeta\in\Omega_{2}.\end{cases}

Then
\mathbf{\Psi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0]
, it has jump

\mathbf{\Psi}_{+}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Psi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}_{-}(\zeta)(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{12}),\quad\zeta<0,

and it coincides with
\mathbf{\Psi}
on
\Omega_{0}
. In particular, the asymptotic behavior of
\mathbf{\Psi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
along the positive real axis is also given by the right-hand side of (
7.3
), from which we conclude that

(7.23)

\psi(\zeta,y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\left(\mathbf{\Psi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)\right)_{11}=(1+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-1}))\frac{\zeta^{-1/4}}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\ee^{-\frac{2}{3}\zeta^{3/2}-y\zeta^{1/2}},\quad\zeta\to+\infty.

The transformation
\mathbf{\Psi}\mapsto\mathbf{\Psi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
does not change the Lax pair system (
7.8
), and the equation
\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Psi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}=\mathbf{B}\mathbf{\Psi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
implies that
\mathbf{\Psi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
is of the form

\mathbf{\Psi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)=\sqrt{2\pi}\begin{pmatrix}\psi(\zeta,y)&\widetilde{\psi}(\zeta,y)\\
-\mathrm{i}\partial_{y}\psi(\zeta,y)&-\mathrm{i}\partial_{y}\widetilde{\psi}(\zeta,y)\end{pmatrix}.

Plugging this identity into (
7.21
), we see that the kernel
\mathsf{K}_{\rm P34}
takes the form expressed in (
1.14
). Still from the second equation in (
7.8
), we obtain that
\partial_{yy}\mathbf{\Psi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}=(\mathbf{B}+\partial_{y}\mathbf{B})\mathbf{\Psi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
, and this identity implies

(7.24)

\partial_{y}^{2}\psi=(\zeta+y+2u(y))\psi.

Equations (
7.23
) and (
7.24
) complete the description claimed in (
1.14
).

7.2.
A simplification of the model RHP and the integro-differential Painlevé II equation

For this section, we set

\Gamma_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{0}\cup\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{2}\cup\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{3}\cup\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{4}.

Consider the following RHP.

RHP 7.4
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}
with the following properties.

(1)

\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}
.

(2)

The matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}
has continuous boundary values
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{\pm}
along
\Gamma_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}\setminus\{{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\}
, and they are related by
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{+}(w)=\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{-}(w)\mathbf{J}_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}(w)
,
w\in\Gamma_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}\setminus\{{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\}
, where

(7.25)

\mathbf{J}_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}(w)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)}}\mathbf{E}_{12},&w\in\Gamma_{0}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)},\\
\mathbf{I}+(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)})\mathbf{E}_{21},&w\in\Gamma_{2}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cup\Gamma_{4}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)},\\
\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)}}\mathbf{E}_{12}-(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)})\mathbf{E}_{21},&w\in\Gamma_{3}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}.\end{cases}

(3)

As
w\to\infty
,

(7.26)

\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}(w)=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(w^{-1})\right)w^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\ee^{-\frac{2}{3}w^{3/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}.

(4)

As
w\to{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
, the matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}
remains bounded.

The RHP for the matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}
differs from the one for
\mathbf{\Psi}_{\tau}
in that there is no jump for
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}
along
\Gamma_{1}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cup\Gamma_{5}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
, and the admissible function
\mathsf{F}
does not appear in the RHP formulation for
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}
. We will use the matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}
in the analysis of the model problem in the subcritical regime.

Similarly as for
\mathbf{\Psi}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}
, we introduce

(7.27)

\widehat{\mathcal{H}}(w)=\widehat{\mathcal{H}}(w{\,|\,}s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{w}\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{-}(w)\right]_{21},&w>{\zeta_{\tau}(s)},\\
\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{w}\left(\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{-}(w)\left(\mathbf{I}-(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)})\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\right)\right]_{21},&w<{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}.\\
\end{cases}

Proposition 7.5
.

For some
\kappa^{+},\kappa^{-}>0
, the function
x\mapsto\widehat{\mathcal{H}}(x)
is analytic on the interval
(-\kappa^{-}\tau^{2},\kappa^{+}\tau)
.

Proof.

The proof is a direct calculation from the RHP for
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}
, and the fact that
\mathsf{P}_{\tau}
- and hence the jump matrix for
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}
- is analytic on a disk of growing radius of order
\mathcal{O}(\tau)
.
∎

If we make the formal replacement
\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)\mapsto-s+\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}w
, the solution
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}
was studied in
[
26
]
, where it was shown to be related to the integro-differential Painlevé II equation and related to the KdV equation, see also
[
55
]
. In our situation, this RHP plays a marginal role, and only its limit as
s\to+\infty
becomes of greater relevance. For that reason, we refer to
[
26
,
55
]
for further details on connections with integrable systems.

When
s\to+\infty
within the subcritical regime from Definition
7.1
, we essentially have the approximation
\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)}\approx 0
. Consequently,
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}
should reduce to the RHP with jumps given by the replacement
\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)}\mapsto 0
, which is the Airy RHP, as we will now discuss.

From (
6.4
), we see that
\mathsf{P}_{\tau}
is analytic on a disk of growing radius of order
O(\tau^{2})
. In particular, since in the subcritical regime we have
{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}=\mathcal{O}(\tau^{1/2})
,
\mathsf{P}_{\tau}
is analytic on the growing disk
D_{\tau}({\zeta_{\tau}(s)})
- the choice of radius
\tau
is made for concreteness, and any radius of order
O(\tau^{\eta})
with
1/2<\eta<2
would suffice for what follows.

We will make a deformation of the RHP
7.4
within the disk
D_{\tau}({\zeta_{\tau}(s)})
, and we now describe the needed geometry. The contour
\Gamma_{2}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
intersects the imaginary axis at the point
w_{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{i}\sqrt{3}{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\in D_{\tau}({\zeta_{\tau}(s)})
. We denote

(7.28)

\gamma^{+}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(\Gamma_{2}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cap\{w\in\mathbb{C}{\,|\,}\re w<0\})\cup[w_{0},0].

That is, when we walk along
\gamma^{+}
starting from
\infty
, we first walk along
\Gamma_{2}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
on the left half plane, until we reach the imaginary axis, and then we walk vertically downwards along the imaginary axis, reaching then the origin. Let also
U^{+}
be the triangular domain determined by the edges
w=w_{0},w=0
and
w={\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
, and
\gamma^{-}
and
U^{-}
be the sets obtained from
\gamma^{+}
and
U^{+}
under complex conjugation, respectively. This construction is illustrated in Figure
7
.

Figure 7
.
On the left, the construction of the regions
U^{\pm}
used for the transformation
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}\mapsto\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
. On the right, the regions determined by the contour
\Gamma^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}=\mathbb{R}\cup\gamma^{+}\cup\gamma^{-}
.

By construction we know that
U^{\pm}\subset D_{\tau}({\zeta_{\tau}(s)})
, and as such the function
\mathsf{P}_{\tau}
is analytic on a neighborhood of
U^{\pm}
. We transform

(7.29)

\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}(w)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}(w)\left(\mathbf{I}\pm(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)}))\mathbf{E}_{21}\right),&w\in U^{\pm},\\
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}(w),&\text{elsewhere}.\end{cases}

Set

\Gamma^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{R}\cup\gamma^{+}\cup\gamma^{-},

with orientation as displayed in Figure
7
.
Then
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
satisfies the following RHP.

RHP 7.6
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
with the following properties.

(1)

\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}
.

(2)

The matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
has continuous boundary values
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{\pm}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
along
\Gamma_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}\setminus\{0\}
, and they are related by
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{+}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}(w)=\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{-}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}(w)\mathbf{J}_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}(w)
,
w\in\Gamma_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}\setminus\{0\}
, where

(7.30)

\mathbf{J}_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}(w)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)}}\mathbf{E}_{12},&w\in(0,\infty),\\
\mathbf{I}+(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)})\mathbf{E}_{21},&w\in\gamma^{+}\cup\gamma^{-},\\
\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)}}\mathbf{E}_{12}-(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)})\mathbf{E}_{21},&w\in(-\infty,0).\end{cases}

(3)

As
w\to\infty
,

(7.31)

\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}(w)=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(w^{-1})\right)w^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\ee^{-\frac{2}{3}\zeta^{3/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}.

(4)

As
w\to 0
, the matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
remains bounded.

To taylor
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}
to our later needs, we now perform a quick asymptotic analysis of it, comparing it with the Airy RHP.

The bare Airy parametrix
{\mathbf{Ai}}_{0}
is given in (
C.1
), and it is used to construct the solution
{\mathbf{Ai}}
to the classical Airy RHP, namely RHP
C.1
. We need to deform the RHP for
{\mathbf{Ai}}
, and for that we repeat the construction of
{\mathbf{Ai}}
but using slightly different sectors on the plane. With
V_{j}
’s being the connected components of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
, labelled as shown in Figure
7
, we define

(7.32)

\widehat{{\mathbf{Ai}}}(w)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}{\mathbf{Ai}}_{0}(w)\times\begin{cases}\mathbf{I},&w\in V_{1},\\
(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{E}_{21}),&w\in V_{2},\\
(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{E}_{12})(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21}),&w\in V_{3},\\
(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{E}_{12}),&w\in V_{4}.\\
\end{cases}

Then
\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}
satisfies the RHP obtained from RHP
7.6
with the replacement
\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)}\mapsto 0
. We emphasize that
\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}
is bounded on bounded subsets of
\mathbb{C}
, and the asymptotic behavior

(7.33)

\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}(w)=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(w^{-1})\right)w^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\ee^{-\frac{2}{3}w^{3/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}},\quad w\to\infty,

still holds true.

We transform

(7.34)

\widehat{\mathbf{R}}(w)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}(w)\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}(w)^{-1},\quad w\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}.

From RHP
7.6
, we obtain that
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}
satisfies the following RHP.

RHP 7.7
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}
with the following properties.

(1)

\widehat{\mathbf{R}}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}
.

(2)

The matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}
has continuous boundary values
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{\pm}
along
\Gamma_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}\setminus\{0\}
, and they are related by
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{+}(w)=\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{-}(w)\mathbf{J}_{\widehat{\mathbf{R}}}(w)
,
w\in\Gamma_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}\setminus\{0\}
, where

(7.35)

\mathbf{J}_{\widehat{\mathbf{R}}}(w)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}+\left(\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)}}-1\right)\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}_{+}(w)\mathbf{E}_{12}\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}_{+}(w)^{-1},&w\in(0,\infty),\\
\mathbf{I}+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)}\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}_{\pm}(w)\mathbf{E}_{21}\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}_{\pm}(w)^{-1},&w\in\gamma^{\pm},\\
\mathbf{I}+\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}_{+}(w)\left[\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)}\mathbf{E}_{11}+\left(\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)}}-1\right)\mathbf{E}_{22}\right]\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}_{+}(w)^{-1},&w\in(-\infty,0).\end{cases}

(3)

As
w\to\infty
,

(7.36)

\widehat{\mathbf{R}}=\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(w^{-1}).

(4)

As
w\to 0
, the matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}
remains bounded.

To conclude the asymptotic analysis, we prove that the jump matrix
\mathbf{J}_{\widehat{\mathbf{R}}}
is asymptotically close to the identity in the appropriate sense.

Proposition 7.8
.

There exists
\eta>0
such that the estimate

\|\mathbf{J}_{\widehat{\mathbf{R}}}-\mathbf{I}\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{\widehat{\mathbf{R}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}})}=\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta s/\tau})

holds true as
\tau\to\infty
, uniformly for
s
within the subcritical regime from Definition
7.1
.

Proof.

We estimate the jump in each part of
\Gamma_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
separately.

To analyze the jump on
(0,\infty)
, we split it into two parts, namely
w>{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}/2
and
w\leq{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}/2
. The asymptotic behavior (
7.33
) shows that

(7.37)

\displaystyle|\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}_{+}(w)\mathbf{E}_{12}\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}_{+}(w)|^{-1}\leq C\ee^{-3w}\leq C\ee^{-w-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}},\quad w\geq\frac{{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}}{2},\qquad\text{and}

(7.38)

\displaystyle|\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}_{+}(w)\mathbf{E}_{12}\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}_{+}(w)|^{-1}\leq C,\quad 0\leq w\leq\frac{{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}}{2},

for some constant
C>0
. The linear factor
3w
in the exponent above is not optimal, but sufficient for our purposes.

On the other hand,

(7.39)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)}}\leq 1,\quad w\geq\frac{{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}}{2},\qquad\text{and}

(7.40)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)}}-1=\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\tau\eta{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}/2}),\quad 0\leq w\leq\frac{{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}}{2},

where for the last estimate we used Proposition
6.9
–(ii). These estimates combined imply that
