\|\mathbf{J}_{\widehat{\mathbf{R}}}-\mathbf{I}\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(0,\infty)}=\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}}).

Next, we prove estimates along
\gamma^{+}
, the estimate along
\gamma^{-}
being analogous. The asymptotic behavior (
7.33
) now yields that

|\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}_{\pm}(w)\mathbf{E}_{21}\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}_{\pm}(w)^{-1}|=\mathcal{O}(1+|w|^{1/2}\ee^{-\frac{4}{3}w^{3/2}}),\quad w\in\gamma^{+}.

In particular, this implies that for some
C>0
,

(7.41)

|\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}_{\pm}(w)\mathbf{E}_{21}\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}_{\pm}(w)^{-1}|\leq C\ee^{-|w|},

for every
w\in\gamma^{+}
.

To continue, recall that
\gamma^{+}
was given in (
7.28
). On one hand, if
w\in\gamma^{+}\cap\Gamma_{2}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
, then certainly
|w-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|\geq|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|={\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
, and Proposition
6.9
–(ii) yields that

(7.42)

|\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)}|\leq C\ee^{-\eta\tau{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}},\quad w\in\gamma^{+}\cap\Gamma_{2}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)},

for some constants
\eta,C>0
. On the other hand, for
w\in\gamma^{+}\setminus\Gamma_{2}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}=[0,w_{0})
, we use (
6.4
) and see that

\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)=-\frac{s}{\tau}+\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}w+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\right),\quad\tau\to\infty,

with uniform error term for
w\in[0,w_{0}]
. Now,
\frac{{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}^{2}}{\tau^{2}}=\mathcal{O}(s^{2}/\tau^{4})
, and because we are in the subcritical regime we can make sure that this term is sufficiently small, which yields that

(7.43)

\re\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)\leq-\frac{s}{\tau}+\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}\re w+1\leq-\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}+2.

Combining (
7.41
), (
7.42
) and (
7.43
), we see that

|\mathbf{J}_{\widehat{\mathbf{R}}}(w)-\mathbf{I}|\leq C\ee^{-|w|-\eta\tau{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}},

for every
w\in\gamma^{+}
. This inequality implies the claim on
L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{\widehat{\mathbf{R}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}})
.

Finally, for
w<0
, we see from (
7.33
) that

\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}_{+}(w)\mathbf{E}_{jj}\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}_{+}(w)^{-1}=\mathcal{O}(1+|w|^{1/2}),

uniformly for
w\in(-\infty,0]
, whereas having in mind that
|w-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|={\zeta_{\tau}(s)}-|w|
, we use once again Proposition
6.9
–(ii) and obtain that

|\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)}|+\left|\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)}}-1\right|\leq C\ee^{-\eta\tau{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}-\eta|w|},

for some constant
C>0
, which finalizes the proof.
∎

Thanks to Proposition
7.8
, the general small norm theory of RHPs yields the required estimate for
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}
. We will use these estimates later.

Theorem 7.9
.

There exists
\eta>0
such that the estimates

\|\widehat{\mathbf{R}}-\mathbf{I}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}})}=\mathcal{O}\left(\ee^{-\eta{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}}\right),\quad\|\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{\pm}-\mathbf{I}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}})}=\mathcal{O}\left(\ee^{-\eta{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}}\right)

as well as

\|\partial_{w}\widehat{\mathbf{R}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}})}=\mathcal{O}\left(\ee^{-\eta{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}}\right),

are valid as
\tau\to\infty
, uniformly for
s
within the subcritical regime.

For later, we also encode an useful analytic continuation of
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}
.

Proposition 7.10
.

For some
\kappa>0
, the function

\widehat{\mathbf{R}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}(w)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\widehat{\mathbf{R}}(w),&w\in V_{4},\;|w|<\kappa\tau^{2}\\
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}(w)\left(\mathbf{I}-\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(w)}\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}(w)\mathbf{E}_{21}\widehat{\mathbf{Ai}}(w)^{-1}\right)&w\in V_{3},\;|w|<\kappa\tau^{2},\end{cases}

extends analytically to
\{w\in\mathbb{C}{\,|\,}-\pi<\arg w<0
,
|w|<\kappa\tau^{2}\}
.

Proof.

All the terms involved are analytic on each of the sectors
V_{3}
and
V_{4}
with
|w|<\kappa\tau^{2}
. The jump condition for
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}
from RHP
7.7
then ensures the claimed expression is analytic across
\gamma_{-}
as well.
∎

7.3.
A modification of the model problem for the supercritical regime

In this section we introduce and study a modification of the model RHP
6.10
, which will be essential in the analysis of the RHP
6.10
itself in the supercritical regime.

Let
\mathsf{F}
and
\mathsf{P}
be admissible functions in the sense of Definition
6.1
, with corresponding point
\zeta_{\tau}(s)
as in Proposition
6.3
. In this section we are only interested in
s
within the supercritical regime from Definition
7.1
. Under such assumption, (
6.6
) ensures the existence of
M=M(T)>0
depending only on
T
for which

(7.44)

-\frac{1}{M}\tau^{1/2}\leq\zeta_{\tau}(s)\leq-M.

The analysis that follows will make extensive use of this restriction on
{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
.

Introduce

(7.45)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(w)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=-s+\tau^{3}\mathsf{P}\left(\frac{w}{\tau^{3}}+\frac{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}{\tau^{2}}\right)=\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}\left(\frac{w}{\tau}+\zeta_{\tau}(s)\right),\quad w\in\mathbb{C},

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(w)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=-u\tau^{3}+\tau^{3}\mathsf{F}\left(\frac{w}{\tau^{3}}+\frac{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}{\tau^{2}}\right)=\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}\left(\frac{w}{\tau}+\zeta_{\tau}(s)\right),\quad w\in\mathbb{C}.

The new model RHP we are about to state has jump matrices given in terms of
\mathcal{F}_{\tau}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\tau}
, and hence it depends on the admissible functions
\mathsf{F},\mathsf{P}
as well as on the variables
\tau,s,u
. Furthermore, it will also depend on a new variable
y>0
. For its statement, recall that
\Gamma_{0}
was introduced in (
6.1
).

RHP 7.11
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}_{\tau}
with the following properties.

(1)

{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}_{\tau}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma^{0}
.

(2)

The matrix
{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}_{\tau}
has continuous boundary values
{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}_{\tau,\pm}
along
\Gamma^{0}\setminus\{0\}
, and they are related by
{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}_{\tau,+}(w)={\mathbf{\Upsilon}}_{\tau,-}(w)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(w)
,
w\in\Gamma^{0}\setminus\{0\}
, where

(7.46)

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(w)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{1+\ee^{{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(w)}}\mathbf{E}_{12},&w\in\Gamma_{0}^{0},\\
\mathbf{I}-\frac{1}{(1-\ee^{-\mathrm{i}{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(w)})(1+\ee^{{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(w)})}\mathbf{E}_{12},&w\in\Gamma_{1}^{0},\\
\left(\mathbf{I}+(1+\ee^{{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(w)})\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{1}{(1-\ee^{-\mathrm{i}{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(w)})(1+\ee^{{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(w)})}\mathbf{E}_{12}\right),&w\in\Gamma_{2}^{0},\\
\frac{1}{1+\ee^{{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(w)}}\mathbf{E}_{12}-(1+\ee^{{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(w)})\mathbf{E}_{21},&w\in\Gamma_{3}^{0},\\
\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{1}{(1-\ee^{\mathrm{i}{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(w)})(1+\ee^{{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(w)})}\mathbf{E}_{12}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}+(1+\ee^{{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(w)})\mathbf{E}_{21}\right),&w\in\Gamma_{4}^{0},\\
\mathbf{I}-\frac{1}{(1-\ee^{\mathrm{i}{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(w)})(1+\ee^{{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(w)})}\mathbf{E}_{12},&w\in\Gamma_{5}^{0}.\end{cases}

(3)

As
w\to\infty
,

(7.47)

\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}(w)=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(w^{-1})\right)w^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\ee^{yw^{1/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}.

(4)

As
w\to 0
,

\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}(w)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}(1),&\text{if }\ee^{\mathrm{i}\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(0)}\neq 1,\\
\mathcal{O}(1),&\text{if }\ee^{\mathrm{i}\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(0)}=1\text{ and }w\notin\Omega_{1}^{0}\cup\Omega_{4}^{0},\\
\mathcal{O}\begin{pmatrix}1&w^{-1}\\
1&w^{-1}\end{pmatrix},&\text{if }\ee^{\mathrm{i}\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(0)}=1\text{ and }w\in\Omega_{1}^{0}\cup\Omega_{4}^{0}.\end{cases}

As said, this new model problem
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}
depends on all the parameters
s,y,u
. When needed to specify one of these parameters, we write
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}(\cdot)=\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}(\cdot{\,|\,}s=s_{0}),\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}(\cdot{\,|\,}y=y_{0})
etc.

The structure of the jump of
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}
is the same as the jump for the model problem
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
from RHP
6.10
: the corresponding jumps are related by a scaling

(7.48)

\mathbf{J}_{\tau}(\zeta)=\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\tau\zeta).

However, the behavior of
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}
and
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
at
\infty
are different.

Introduce

(7.49)

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\tau}(w)=\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\tau}(w{\,|\,}y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{w}\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}(w{\,|\,}y)\right]_{21,-},\quad w>0,y>0,

and for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, also define

(7.50)

\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}(w)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}(w)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\mathbf{E}_{12}}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(w)}}\right),&w\in\Omega_{0}^{0}\cap D_{\varepsilon\tau^{3}}(0),\\
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}(w)\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\mathbf{E}_{12}}{(1-\ee^{-\mathrm{i}\tau\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(w)})(1+\ee^{\tau\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(w)})}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\mathbf{E}_{12}}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(w)}}\right),&w\in\Omega_{1}^{0}\cap D_{\varepsilon\tau^{3}}(0),\\
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}(w)\left(\mathbf{I}+(1+\ee^{\tau\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(w)})\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\mathbf{E}_{12}}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(w)}}\right),&w\in\Omega_{2}^{0}\cap D_{\varepsilon\tau^{3}}(0),\\
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}(w)(\mathbf{I}-(1+\ee^{\tau\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(w)})\mathbf{E}_{21}),&w\in\Omega_{3}^{0}\cap D_{\varepsilon\tau^{3}}(0),\\
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}(w)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\mathbf{E}_{12}}{(1-\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(w)})(1+\ee^{\tau\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(w)})}\right),&w\in\Omega_{4}^{0}\cap D_{\varepsilon\tau^{3}}(0),\\
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}(w),&\zeta\in\Omega^{0}_{5}\cap D_{\varepsilon\tau^{3}}(0).\end{cases}

The next result is the analogue of Proposition
6.11
.

Proposition 7.12
.

Suppose that
y>0
and
s
is within the supercritical regime from Definition
7.1
. The following properties hold.

(i)

The function
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
is the analytic continuation of
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}
from
\Omega_{5}^{0}
to a full neighborhood of the origin.

(ii)

The function
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\tau}(w)
extends analytically from
w>0
to
-\delta\tau^{3}<w<\delta\tau^{3}
, for some
\delta>0
independent of
s,y,\tau
.

Proof.

The expressions in (
7.50
) that define
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
are obtained from the right-hand side of (
6.25
) when we replace
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau},\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau},\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}
by
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau},\mathcal{P}_{\tau}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\tau}
, respectively. With a change of variables, the jumps of
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}
and
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
coincide, see (
7.48
). This means that the same arguments used in the proof of Proposition
6.11
also show that
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
is analytic in a neighborhood of the origin, proving (i).

The proof of (ii) then follows by the identity

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\tau}(w{\,|\,}y)=\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{w}\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}(w{\,|\,}y)\right]_{21},\quad w>0,

the analyticity of
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\scriptscriptstyle\rm mod})}}
, and the fact that
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{F}
are analytic in a neighborhood of the origin of radius
\mathcal{O}(\tau^{3})
.
∎

To better understand the different nature between
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}
and
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
, for
s
within the supercritical regime we state the approximation

\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(w)\approx\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}w\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(w)\approx u_{\tau}+\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}w,\quad u_{\tau}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=-u\tau^{3}+\tau{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}}\zeta_{\tau}(s),

which is formally valid as
\tau\to\infty
. Thus,
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}
depends on
\tau
in an oscillatory way: the factors
\ee^{\pm\mathrm{i}\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(w)}\approx\ee^{\pm\mathrm{i}u_{\tau}}\ee^{\pm\mathrm{i}\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}w}
oscillate with
\tau
for any value of
w
. This is in contrast with the critical regime, where in the appropriate scale we had
|\ee^{\pm\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(w)}|\approx\ee^{\mp\tau{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}}\im w}
so the factors involving
\mathsf{F}_{\tau}
were decaying as
\tau\to\infty
.

We are interested in two major properties of
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}
: (1) the existence of
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}
, and (2) its behavior as
\tau\to\infty
and simultaneously
y\to 0^{+}
in a proper sense. The main outcome of the current section is to carry out the analysis of (2). As a consequence, (1) will follow for
y
sufficiently small. We state it as a formal result, the proof follows from (2) in a standard manner and we skip it.

Theorem 7.13
.

There exists
y_{0}>0
and
\tau_{0}>0
sufficiently large such that a solution
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}
of the RHP
7.11
exists for every
\tau\geq\tau_{0}
, every
y\in(y_{0}/\tau^{3/2},1/y_{0})
, and every
s
within the supercritical regime.

We now carry out the asymptotic analysis for
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}
as
\tau\to\infty
and
y
is small. This asymptotic analysis is similar to the one in
[
26
, Section 7]
. The functions
\mathcal{F}_{\tau}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\tau}
- and hence the jump matrix for
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}
- depend on the function
\zeta_{\tau}(s)
. We assume that for some fixed but arbitrarily large constant
y_{0}>0
,

(7.51)

\frac{y_{0}}{\tau^{3/2}}\leq y\leq\frac{1}{y_{0}},\quad\text{and }s\text{ is within the supercritical regime.}

7.3.1.
Scaling step

As a first step, we scale

(7.52)

\mathbf{V}_{\tau}(\xi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=y^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}\left(\frac{\xi}{y^{2}}\right),\quad\xi\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma^{0}.

Denote the induced scaled functions by

(7.53)

\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{P}_{\tau}\left(\frac{\xi}{y^{2}}\right)=\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}\left(\frac{\xi}{y^{2}\tau}+\zeta_{\tau}(s)\right),\quad\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(\xi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{F}_{\tau}\left(\frac{\xi}{y^{2}}\right)=\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}\left(\frac{\xi}{y^{2}\tau}+\zeta_{\tau}(s)\right),\quad\xi\in\Gamma^{0}.

These functions admit an expansion of the form

(7.54)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)=\frac{\xi}{y^{2}}\mathsf{P}^{\prime}\left(\frac{{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}}{\tau^{2}}\right)\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\xi}{y^{2}\tau^{3}}\right)\right),\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(\xi)=-u\tau^{3}+\tau^{3}\mathsf{F}\left(\frac{{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}}{\tau^{2}}\right)+\frac{\xi}{y^{2}}\mathsf{F}^{\prime}\left(\frac{{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}}{\tau^{2}}\right)\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\xi}{y^{2}\tau^{3}}\right)\right),

valid as
\tau\to\infty
uniformly for
\xi=o(y^{2}\tau^{3})
and
s,y
as in (
7.51
).

It then follows that
\mathbf{V}_{\tau}
satisfies the following RHP.

RHP 7.14
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{V}_{\tau}
with the following properties.

(1)

\mathbf{V}_{\tau}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma^{0}
.

(2)

The matrix
\mathbf{V}_{\tau}
has continuous boundary values
\mathbf{V}_{\tau,\pm}
along
\Gamma^{0}\setminus\{0\}
, and they are related by
\mathbf{V}_{\tau,+}(\xi)=\mathbf{V}_{\tau,-}(\xi)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{V}}(\xi)
,
\xi\in\Gamma^{0}\setminus\{0\}
, where

(7.55)

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{V}}(\xi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(y^{-2}\xi)=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)}}\mathbf{E}_{12},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{0}^{0},\\
\mathbf{I}-\frac{1}{(1-\ee^{-\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(\xi)})(1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)})}\mathbf{E}_{12},&\xi\in\Gamma_{1}^{0},\\
\left(\mathbf{I}+(1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)})\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{1}{(1-\ee^{-\mathrm{i}\tau\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(\xi)})(1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)})}\mathbf{E}_{12}\right),&\xi\in\Gamma_{2}^{0},\\
\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)}}\mathbf{E}_{12}-(1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)})\mathbf{E}_{21},&\xi\in\Gamma_{3}^{0},\\
\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{1}{(1-\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(\xi)})(1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)})}\mathbf{E}_{12}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}+(1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)})\mathbf{E}_{21}\right),&\xi\in\Gamma_{4}^{0},\\
\mathbf{I}-\frac{1}{(1-\ee^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(\xi)})(1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)})}\mathbf{E}_{12},&\xi\in\Gamma_{5}^{0}.\end{cases}

(3)

As
\xi\to\infty
,

(7.56)

\mathbf{V}_{\tau}(\xi)=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(\xi^{-1})\right)\xi^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\ee^{\xi^{1/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}.

(4)

As
\xi\to 0
,

(7.57)

\mathbf{V}_{\tau}(\xi)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}(1),&\text{if }\ee^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(0)}\neq 1,\\
\mathcal{O}(1),&\text{if }\ee^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(0)}=1\text{ and }|\arg\xi|\notin[\frac{\pi}{3},\frac{2\pi}{3}],\\
\mathcal{O}\begin{pmatrix}\xi^{-1}&1\\
\xi^{-1}&1\end{pmatrix},&\text{if }\ee^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(0)}=1\text{ and }|\arg\xi|\in(\frac{\pi}{3},\frac{2\pi}{3}).\end{cases}

The proof that
\mathbf{V}_{\tau}
given in (
7.52
) indeed satisfies this RHP is immediate from the RHP
7.11
satisfied by
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}
, and we skip it.

Construction of global parametrix

As we will see,

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{V}}\to\begin{cases}\mathbf{I},&w\in\Gamma^{0}\cup\Gamma_{1}^{0}\cup\Gamma_{5}^{0},\\
\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21},&w\in\Gamma_{2}^{0}\cup\Gamma_{4}^{0},\\
\mathbf{E}_{12}-\mathbf{E}_{21},&w\in\Gamma_{3}^{0}.\end{cases}

Hence, with

\Gamma_{\mathbf{B}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Gamma_{2}^{0}\cup\Gamma_{3}^{0}\cup\Gamma_{4}^{0},

the global parametrix
\mathbf{B}
we require is the solution to the following RHP.

RHP 7.15
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{B}
with the following properties.

(1)

The matrix
\mathbf{B}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{B}}
.

(2)

The matrix
\mathbf{B}
has continuous boundary values
\mathbf{B}_{\pm}
along
\Gamma_{\mathbf{B}}\setminus\{0\}
, and they are related by
\mathbf{B}_{+}(\xi)=\mathbf{B}_{-}(\xi)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{B}}(\xi)
,
\xi\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{B}}\setminus\{0\}
, where

(7.58)

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{B}}(\xi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21},&\xi\in\Gamma_{2}^{0}\cup\Gamma_{4}^{0},\\
\mathbf{E}_{12}-\mathbf{E}_{21},&\xi\in\Gamma_{3}^{0}.\end{cases}

(3)

As
\xi\to\infty
,

(7.59)

\mathbf{B}(\xi)=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(\xi^{-1})\right)\xi^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\ee^{\xi^{1/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}.

(4)

As
\xi\to 0
,

(7.60)

\mathbf{B}(\xi)=\mathbf{B}_{0}(\xi)\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\log(\xi)\mathbf{E}_{12}\right)\mathbf{M}_{j},\quad\xi\in\Omega_{j},j=0,1,2,

where
\mathbf{B}_{0}
is analytic and invertible in a neighborhood of the origin, and the matrices
\mathbf{M}_{j}
and sectors
\Omega_{j}
are as in (
7.7
) and (
7.1
), respectively.

Obtaining the solution to this RHP is standard, and it is constructed explicitly in terms of Bessel functions, namely

(7.61)

\mathbf{B}(\xi)=\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{3\mathrm{i}}{8}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\begin{pmatrix}I_{0}(\xi^{1/2})&\mathrm{i}K_{0}(\xi^{1/2})\\
\mathrm{i}\xi^{1/2}I_{0}^{\prime}(\xi^{1/2})&-\xi^{1/2}K^{\prime}_{0}(\xi^{1/2})\end{pmatrix}\pi^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\times\begin{cases}\mathbf{I},&\xi\in\Omega_{0},\\
\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{E}_{21},&\xi\in\Omega_{1},\\
\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21},&\xi\in\Omega_{2},\\
\end{cases}

where
I_{\nu}
and
K_{\nu}
are the modified Bessel functions of first and second kind, respectively. For later use, we recall that the Bessel functions appearing above are solutions to the Bessel differential equation,

w^{2}y^{\prime\prime}+wy-(w^{2}+\nu^{2})w=0,\quad y=I_{\nu}(w),K_{\nu}(w),\;\nu\in\mathbb{C},

and they admit the expansions

(7.62)

I_{0}(\xi^{1/2})=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\xi^{k}}{4^{k}(k!)^{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad K_{0}(\xi^{1/2})=-\left(\frac{1}{2}\log\xi-\log 2+\gamma\right)I_{0}(\xi^{1/2})+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\gamma_{k}\xi^{k}}{4^{k}(k!)^{2}},

where here

\gamma_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{1}{k},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\gamma\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(-\log n+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{1}{k}\right),

is the Euler’s constant, and the power series in (
7.62
) are absolutely convergent for
\xi\in\mathbb{C}
. In particular,
\mathbf{B}_{0}
is in fact an entire function.

In a moment, we will also use
I_{1}
, which admits the expansion

(7.63)

I_{1}(\xi^{1/2})=\frac{1}{2}\xi^{1/2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\xi^{k}}{4^{k}k!(k+1)!},\quad\xi\to 0,

the series on the right-hand side is an absolutely convergent series on
\mathbb{C}
, and it satisfies the identity

I_{0}^{\prime}(\xi)=I_{1}(\xi),\quad\xi>0.

A direct calculation shows that

\left[\mathbf{B}(\xi)^{-1}\mathbf{B}(v)\right]_{21}=\pi\mathrm{i}\left(I_{0}(\sqrt{\xi})I_{0}^{\prime}(\sqrt{v})\sqrt{v}-I_{0}(\sqrt{v})I_{0}(\sqrt{\xi})\sqrt{\xi}\right),\quad\xi,v>0,

and in turn this identity implies that

(7.64)

[\mathbf{\Delta}_{\xi}\mathbf{B}(\xi)]_{21}=\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{2}\left(I_{1}(\sqrt{\xi})^{2}-I_{0}(\sqrt{\xi})^{2}\right),\quad\xi>0.

The right-hand side is an entire function, and thus it provides an analytic extension of the (boundary value of the) left-hand side to the whole real line.

Recall that the Bessel kernel
\mathsf{J}_{\nu}(\xi,v)
was defined for
\xi,v>0
in (
2.57
). Using the series expansion

\Jb_{0}(\xi^{1/2})=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}\xi^{k}}{4^{k}(k!)^{2}},

it is straightforward to show that
\mathsf{J}_{0}(\xi,\xi)
extends to an analytic function of
\xi\in\mathbb{C}
, and furthermore that the identity

(7.65)

[\mathbf{\Delta}_{\xi}\mathbf{B}(\xi)]_{21}=-2\pi\mathrm{i}\mathsf{J}_{0}(-\xi,-\xi),\quad\xi>0

holds true. In particular, with this identity we extend
[\mathbf{\Delta}_{\xi}\mathbf{B}(\xi)]_{21,-}
to the whole real line.

7.3.2.
Construction of the local parametrix

Recall the scaled functions
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}
introduced in (
7.53
), set

{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)}},&\xi\in\Gamma_{0}^{0},\\
-\frac{1}{(1-\ee^{-\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(\xi)})(1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)})},&\xi\in\Gamma_{1}^{0}\cup\Gamma_{2}^{0},\\
\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)}}-1,&\xi\in\Gamma_{0}^{3},\\
-\frac{1}{(1-\ee^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(\xi)})(1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)})},&\xi\in\Gamma_{4}^{0}\cup\Gamma_{5}^{0},\end{cases}

and define

(7.66)

\mathsf{B}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma^{0}}\frac{\mathsf{b}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(v)}{v-\xi}\mathrm{d}v,\quad\xi\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma^{0}.

This scalar function
\mathsf{B}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
depends on
\tau
,
s
and
y
, which we recall we are assuming that they satisfy (
7.51
).

The behavior of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
near
\infty
, as described by the relations (
7.53
) and Proposition
6.9
, ensure that
\mathsf{b}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(v)
decays exponentially fast as
v\to\infty
along
\Gamma^{0}
, so the integration is well defined near
\infty
.
Care must be taken at
v=0
: when
\ee^{\mathrm{i}\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(0)}=1
, corresponding to
\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}
, the factor
\mathsf{b}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
has a simple pole sitting in the contour
\Gamma^{0}
. In this case, we interpret the integral along each of the contours
\Gamma^{0}_{1}\cup\Gamma^{0}_{2}
and
\Gamma^{0}_{4}\cup\Gamma^{0}_{5}
in the sense of principal value. Since the density
\mathsf{b}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
is meromorphic along each of these contours, with only a simple pole at
v=0
, then this principal value integral is well defined as a finite complex number.

We collect some properties of this function.

Lemma 7.16
.

Suppose that we are in the supercritical regime from Definition
7.1
. The function
\mathsf{B}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
satisfies the following properties.

(i)

The function
\mathsf{B}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
has continuous boundary values
\mathsf{B}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon},\pm}
along
\Gamma^{0}\setminus\{0\}
, and they satisfy

\mathsf{B}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon},+}(\xi)=\mathsf{B}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon},-}(\xi)\mathsf{b}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi),\quad\xi\in\Gamma^{0}\setminus\{0\}.

(ii)

As
\xi\to 0
, while
\tau,s
are kept fixed,

(7.67)

\mathsf{B}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}(\log\xi),&\text{if }\ee^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(0)}\neq 1,\\
\mathcal{O}(\log\xi),&\text{if }\ee^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(0)}=1\text{ and }|\arg\xi|\notin[\frac{\pi}{3},\frac{2\pi}{3}],\\
\mathcal{O}(\xi^{-1}),&\text{if }\ee^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(0)}=1\text{ and }|\arg\xi|\in[\frac{\pi}{3},\frac{2\pi}{3}],\\
\end{cases}

(iii)

As
\tau\to+\infty
,

\mathsf{B}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)=\mathcal{O}\left(y^{2}\right),

with uniform error term for
\xi
within compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.

Proof.

The proof of (i) follows directly from Plemelj’s formula.

For the proof of (ii), thanks to Proposition
6.9
, in this case we see that the matrix
\mathsf{b}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
is meromorphic along each of the contours
\Gamma^{0}_{1}\cup\Gamma^{0}_{2}
and
\Gamma^{0}_{4}\cup\Gamma^{0}_{5}
, so that the limit in (i) corresponds to the limit of four Cauchy integrals, one along the contour
\Gamma_{0}^{0}=(0,+\infty)
, one along the contour
\Gamma_{3}^{0}=(-\infty,0)
, and the other two over
\Gamma^{0}_{1}\cup\Gamma^{0}_{2}
and
\Gamma^{0}_{4}\cup\Gamma^{0}_{5}
. Along the latter two, each density is meromorphic, with a simple pole at
\zeta=0
when
\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau\widetilde{\mathsf{F}}_{\tau}(0)}=1
, and no other poles. The property (ii) then follows from basic properties of Cauchy transforms and deformation of contours.

Part (iii) is based on a estimate of contours in the style of the classical steepest descent method, and we only indicate the steps. The very definition of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}
in (
7.53
) and Proposition
6.9
ensure that the integrand
\mathsf{b}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
is exponentially decaying for
\xi
away from the origin, and this way for some fixed small neighborhood
D_{\delta}
of the origin and some
\eta>0
, we can estimate

\mathsf{B}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma^{0}\cap D_{\delta}}\frac{\mathsf{b}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(v)}{v-\xi}\mathrm{d}v+\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta/y^{2}})\quad\text{as}\quad\tau\to\infty,

where the error term is uniform for
\xi
in compacts of
\mathbb{C}\setminus D_{\delta}
, and uniform for
s
,
y
as in (
7.51
).

Next, for the integral over
D_{\delta}
we perform the change of variables
v\mapsto r=y^{2}v
. The expansion (
7.54
) ensures the resulting integrand remains bounded as
\tau\to\infty
, whereas the change of differentials
\mathrm{d}v=y^{2}\mathrm{d}r
gives the claimed error estimate.

∎

Recalling the sectors
\Omega_{j}^{0}
displayed in Figure
6
, we finally introduce the needed local parametrix as

(7.68)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{B}(\xi)\mathbf{L}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi),\qquad\text{with }\mathbf{B}\text{ as in \eqref{deff:BesselB} and}

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}(\mathbf{I}+\mathsf{B}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)\mathbf{E}_{12}),&\xi\in\Omega_{0}^{0}\cup\Omega_{1}^{0}\cup\Omega_{4}^{0}\cup\Omega_{5}^{0},\\
(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21})(\mathbf{I}+\mathsf{B}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)\mathbf{E}_{12})(\mathbf{I}-(1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)})\mathbf{E}_{21}),&\xi\in\Omega_{2}^{0},\\
(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{E}_{21})(\mathbf{I}+\mathsf{B}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)\mathbf{E}_{12})(\mathbf{I}+(1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)})\mathbf{E}_{21}),&\xi\in\Omega_{3}^{0},\end{cases}

The introduction of the factor
\mathsf{B}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
is done in order to accomplish

(7.69)

\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon},+}(\xi)=\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon},-}(\xi)\mathbf{J}_{{\mathbf{V}}}(\xi),\quad\xi\in\Gamma^{0}\setminus\{0\}.

Indeed, this jump property follows from a straightforward calculation using Lemma
7.16
–(i), (
7.55
) and (
7.58
).

Furthermore, as a consequence of (
7.67
) we learn that as
\xi\to 0
,

(7.70)

\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}(\log\xi),&\text{if }\ee^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(0)}\neq 1,\\
\mathcal{O}(\log\xi),&\text{if }\ee^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(0)}=1\text{ and }|\arg\xi|\notin[\frac{\pi}{3},\frac{2\pi}{3}],\\
\mathcal{O}\begin{pmatrix}1&\xi^{-1}\\
1&\xi^{-1}\end{pmatrix},&\text{if }\ee^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(0)}=1\text{ and }|\arg\xi|\in[\frac{\pi}{3},\frac{2\pi}{3}].\end{cases}

We also need to control the behavior of
\mathbf{L}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
for
\xi
within a positive distance from the origin. Using (
7.54
) we see that for any compact
K\subset\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
, there exists
\eta=\eta_{K}>0
independent of the other parameters, such that

\re\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)\geq-\eta\frac{1}{y^{2}}|\xi|,\text{ for }\xi\in K\cap(\Omega_{2}^{0}\cup\Omega_{3}^{0}).

With this estimate, we ensure the exponential decay of the factors involving
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}
in (
7.68
), and when combined with Lemma
7.16
–(iii) we obtain that

(7.71)

\mathbf{L}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)=\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}\left(y^{2}\right),\quad\tau\to\infty,

uniformly for
s,y
as in (
7.51
), and uniformly for
\xi
within compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.

7.3.3.
Small norm theory

To conclude the asymptotic analysis, we fix
\varepsilon>0
and introduce

(7.72)

{\mathbf{R}}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{V}_{\tau}(\xi)\mathbf{B}(\xi)^{-1},&\xi\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(\Gamma^{0}\cup\overline{D}_{\varepsilon}(0)),\\
\mathbf{V}_{\tau}(\xi)\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)^{-1},&\xi\in D_{\varepsilon}(0)\setminus\Gamma^{0}.\\
\end{cases}

With the contour

\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(\Gamma^{0}\cup\partial D_{\varepsilon})\setminus D_{\varepsilon},

and where we orient
\partial D_{\varepsilon}
in the clockwise direction, it follows that
\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
satisfies the following RHP.

RHP 7.17
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
with the following properties.

(1)

\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}}
.

(2)

The matrix
\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
has continuous boundary values
\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon},\pm}
along
\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}}
, and they are related by the jump relation
\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon},+}(\xi)=\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon},-}(\xi)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}}(\xi)
,
\xi\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}}
, with

(7.73)

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}}(\xi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}+\frac{\mathbf{B}(\xi)\mathbf{E}_{12}\mathbf{B}(\xi)^{-1}}{1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)}},&\xi\in\Gamma_{0}^{0}\setminus D_{\varepsilon},\\
\mathbf{I}-\frac{\mathbf{B}(\xi)\mathbf{E}_{12}\mathbf{B}(\xi)^{-1}}{(1-\ee^{-\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(\xi)})(1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)})},&\xi\in\Gamma_{1}^{0}\setminus D_{\varepsilon},\\
\left(\mathbf{I}+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)}\mathbf{B}_{+}(\xi)\mathbf{E}_{21}\mathbf{B}_{+}(\xi)^{-1}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\mathbf{B}_{+}(\xi)\mathbf{E}_{12}\mathbf{B}_{+}(\xi)^{-1}}{(1-\ee^{-\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(\xi)})(1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)})}\right),&\xi\in\Gamma_{2}^{0}\setminus D_{\varepsilon},\\
\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{B}_{-}(\xi)\left[\left(\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)}}-1\right)\mathbf{E}_{11}-\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)}\mathbf{E}_{22}\right]\mathbf{B}_{-}(\xi)^{-1},&\xi\in\Gamma_{3}^{0}\setminus D_{\varepsilon},\\
\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\mathbf{B}_{-}(\xi)\mathbf{E}_{12}\mathbf{B}_{-}(\xi)^{-1}}{(1-\ee^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(\xi)})(1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)})}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)}\mathbf{B}_{-}(\xi)\mathbf{E}_{21}\mathbf{B}_{-}(\xi)^{-1}\right),&\xi\in\Gamma_{4}^{0}\setminus D_{\varepsilon},\\
\mathbf{I}-\frac{\mathbf{B}(\xi)\mathbf{E}_{12}\mathbf{B}(\xi)^{-1}}{(1-\ee^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(\xi)})(1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)})},&w\in\Gamma_{5}^{0}\setminus D_{\varepsilon},\\
\mathbf{B}(\xi)\mathbf{L}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)\mathbf{B}(\xi)^{-1},&w\in\partial D_{\varepsilon}.\end{cases}

(3)

As
\xi\to\infty
,

\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)=\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(\xi^{-1}).

The verification that
\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
indeed solves this RHP is straightforward using its definition in (
7.72
), the RHP
7.15
for
\mathbf{B}
, and (
7.68
), (
7.69
), (
7.70
).

Finally, the key estimates to finish the current asymptotic analysis are provided by the next result.

Proposition 7.18
.

The estimate

\left\|\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}}-\mathbf{I}\right\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}})}=\mathcal{O}\left(y^{2}\right)

holds true as
\tau\to\infty
, uniformly for
s,y
satisfying (
7.51
).

Proof.

Throughout this analysis, we use that
\mathbf{B}(\xi)
, as well as its boundary values along
\Gamma_{\mathbf{B}}
, remain bounded on compact components of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. This fact combined with (
7.59
) shows that for any
\varepsilon>0
the estimate

(7.74)

\mathbf{B}(\xi)=\mathcal{O}(1+|\xi|^{1/4})\ee^{\xi^{1/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}

is valid uniformly for
\xi
on
\mathbb{C}\setminus D_{\varepsilon}
.

We verify the decay of the jump in each component of the jump contour
\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}}
.

For
\xi\in\partial D_{\varepsilon}
, which is compact, we estimate using (
7.74
) that
\mathbf{B}(\xi)^{\pm 1}=\mathcal{O}(1)
uniformly, and when combined with (
7.71
) we see that

\left\|\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}}-\mathbf{I}\right\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\partial D_{\varepsilon})}=\left\|\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{L}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}-\mathbf{I})\mathbf{B}^{-1}\right\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\partial D_{\varepsilon})}=\mathcal{O}(y^{2}).

For the remaining jumps, we will now show exponential decay of the
L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}
norms.

On
\Gamma_{0}^{0}\setminus D_{\varepsilon}
we obtain from (
7.74
)

\mathbf{B}(\xi)\mathbf{E}_{12}\mathbf{B}(\xi)^{-1}=\mathcal{O}(w^{1/2}\ee^{2\xi^{1/2}}),

and using (
7.53
) and Proposition
6.9
–(i) we also see that

\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)}}=\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta|\xi|/y^{2}}),

uniformly for
y,s
as in (
7.51
). Such estimate ensures

\left\|\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}}-\mathbf{I}\right\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{0}^{0}\setminus D_{\varepsilon}})=\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta/y^{2}}),

for a possibly different
\eta>0
.
For the analysis on
\Gamma_{1}^{0}\setminus D_{\varepsilon}
, we need to consider three terms. The first term is

(7.75)

\mathbf{B}(\xi)\mathbf{E}_{12}\mathbf{B}(\xi)^{-1}=\mathcal{O}(|\xi|^{1/2}\ee^{2\xi^{1/2}})

as before. The second term is estimated as

(7.76)

\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}}(\xi)}=\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta|\xi|/y^{2}}),

where we used (
7.53
) and Proposition
6.9
–(i).

Finally, for the last term, namely the one involving
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}
, we split into two cases, namely whether
|\xi|\geq y^{2}\tau\varepsilon
or not. For the case
|\xi|\geq y^{2}\tau\varepsilon
we use (
7.53
) again and apply Proposition
6.9
–(iii), obtaining

(7.77)

\frac{1}{1+\ee^{-\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(\xi)}}=\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta|\xi|/y^{2}}),

for a possibly different constant
\eta>0
. Next, for the remaining case
\varepsilon\leq|\xi|\leq y^{2}\tau\varepsilon
, we see from (
7.54
) that

\re\left(\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}(\xi)\right)=\frac{1}{y^{2}}\mathsf{F}^{\prime}\left(\frac{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}{\tau^{2}}\right)\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\tau^{2}}\right)\right)\im\xi=\frac{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}}{y^{2}}\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\tau^{2}}\right)\right)\im\xi.

Since
\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}>0
and we are assuming also that
\xi\in\Gamma_{1}^{0}\setminus D_{\varepsilon}
, the bound above shows that (
7.77
) extends from
|\xi|\geq y^{2}\tau\varepsilon
to the whole set
\Gamma^{1}_{0}\setminus D_{\varepsilon}
. All in all, (
7.75
), (
7.76
) and (
7.77
) combined show that

\left\|\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}}-\mathbf{I}\right\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\Gamma^{0}_{1}\setminus D_{\varepsilon})}=\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta/y^{2}}),

uniformly for
s,y
as in (
7.51
). In a similar manner, one can show that this bound also holds true along
L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{j}^{0}\setminus D_{\varepsilon})
with
j=2,3,4,5
, we skip details.
∎

With Proposition
7.18
established, we immediately obtain

Theorem 7.19
.

The estimates

\|\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}-\mathbf{I}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}})}=\mathcal{O}\left(y^{2}\right),\quad\|\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon},\pm}-\mathbf{I}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}})}=\mathcal{O}\left(y^{2}\right),

as well as

\|\partial_{\zeta}\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}})}=\mathcal{O}\left(y^{2}\right),\quad\|\partial_{\zeta}\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon},\pm}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}})}=\mathcal{O}\left(y^{2}\right),

are valid as
\tau\to\infty
, uniformly for
s,y
satisfying (
7.51
).

Theorem
7.19
finally completes all the steps in the asymptotic analysis of
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}
in the regime (
7.51
).

7.3.4.
Summary of the asymptotic analysis of
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}

We now summarize the asymptotic formulas resulting from the asymptotic analysis we just carried out, and that will be needed for later on.

Next, we now assume that
y,s
satisfy (
7.51
). Unfolding the transformations
\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau}\mapsto\mathbf{V}_{\tau}\mapsto\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
, we obtain that

(7.78)

\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau,-}(w)=y^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon},-}(\xi)\mathbf{B}_{-}(\xi),\quad\xi=y^{2}w,\quad\xi\in\mathbb{R}\setminus D_{\varepsilon}(0),

as well as

(7.79)

\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{\tau,-}(w)=y^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)\mathbf{B}_{-}(\xi)\mathbf{L}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon},-}(\xi),\quad\xi\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon),

where we recall that
\mathbf{L}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
is given in (
7.68
), and we emphasize that its expression depends on whether
\xi>0
or
\xi<0
.
Recall that
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
was introduced in (
7.49
). From the expressions (
7.78
)–(
7.79
), for a given
\delta>0
sufficiently small and
0<w<\tau^{3}\delta
we obtain

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\tau}(w)=y^{2}\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{\xi}\mathbf{B}(\xi)\right]_{21}+y^{2}\left[\mathbf{B}(\xi)^{-1}\mathbf{\Delta}_{\xi}\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)\mathbf{B}(\xi)\right]_{21,-},\quad\xi=y^{2}w.

Using RHP
7.15
–(4), we re-express this identity as

(7.80)

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\tau}(w)=y^{2}\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{\xi}\mathbf{B}(\xi)\right]_{21}+y^{2}\left[\mathbf{B}_{0}(\xi)^{-1}\mathbf{\Delta}_{\xi}\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)\mathbf{B}_{0}(\xi)\right]_{21,-},\quad\xi=y^{2}w,\quad 0<w<\tau^{3}\delta.

All the terms in this identity extend analytically to
w<0
: this is true for
\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
because RHP
7.17
ensures it has no jumps near
\xi=0
, the analyticity of
\mathbf{B}_{0}
is part of RHP
7.15
–(4), the analyticity of the remaining term involving
\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{B}_{-}
on the right-hand side is discussed in (
7.63
)
et seq.
, and the analyticity of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\tau}
is ensured by Proposition
7.12
. Thus, (
7.80
) is in fact valid in a full growing neighborhood
(-\delta\tau^{3},\delta\tau^{3})
of
w=0
.

8.
Asymptotic analysis of the model problem

In this section we carry out the asymptotic analysis of the model problem, RHP
6.10
, in each of the regimes singled out in Definition
7.1
.

8.1.
Asymptotic analysis of the model problem in the critical regime

We now carry out the asymptotic analysis of the model problem in the critical regime of Definition
7.1
. In this regime, the expansion (
6.6
) ensures the existence of
M=M(T)>0
depending only on
T
for which

(8.1)

|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|\leq M.

In this regime, the asymptotic analysis as
\tau\to\infty
does not require any further rescaling of
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
. As we will see, for
\zeta
away from
{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
, the model problem
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
may be well approximated by the Painlevé XXXIV directly. Near the point
\zeta_{\tau}(s)
, we need to construct a local parametrix, which is the major technical step we overcome in this section.

8.1.1.
Construction of the global parametrix

The global parametrix is

(8.2)

\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta-\zeta_{\tau}(s){\,|\,}y=\zeta_{\tau}(s)),

where
\mathbf{\Psi}
is the solution to the PXXXIV RHP
7.2
. This matrix is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus(\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{2}\cup\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{3}\cup\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{4})
, and satisfies the jump

\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{+}(\zeta)=\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{-}(\zeta)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}}(\zeta),\quad\zeta\in\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{2}\cup\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{3}\cup\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{4},

with

(8.3)

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{J}_{{\mathbf{\Psi}}}(\zeta-\zeta_{\tau}(s))=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21},&\zeta\in\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{2}\cup\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{4},\\
\mathbf{E}_{12}-\mathbf{E}_{21},&\zeta\in\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{3}.\end{cases}

For later use, we also record its asymptotics

(8.4)

\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathrm{i}\left(q(\zeta_{\tau}(s))+\frac{\zeta_{\tau}(s)^{2}}{4}\right)\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-1})\right)\zeta^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\ee^{-\frac{2}{3}\zeta^{3/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}},

see (
7.9
) and (
7.10
), as well as the behavior

(8.5)

\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)=\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{0}(\zeta)\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\log(\zeta-\zeta_{\tau}(s))\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\mathbf{M}_{j},\quad\text{as }\zeta\to\zeta_{\tau}(s)\text{ along }\Omega_{j},

where
\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{0}(\zeta)={\mathbf{\Psi}}_{0}(\zeta-\zeta_{\tau}(s){\,|\,}y=\zeta_{\tau}(s))
is analytic near
\zeta=\zeta_{\tau}(s)
and smooth in
y
, and with matrices
\mathbf{M}_{1}=\mathbf{I},\mathbf{M}_{j}=\mathbf{I}+(-1)^{j}\mathbf{E}_{21},j=1,2,
see (
7.4
) and (
7.7
).

8.1.2.
Construction of the local parametrix

The local parametrix needed in the critical regime is a matrix-valued function
\mathbf{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
, which is piecewise analytic on a disk
D_{R}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))
around
\zeta_{\tau}(s)
, with the same jumps as
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}(\zeta)
along the contour
\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cap D_{R}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))
, and asymptotically matching (a shift of) the solution
\mathbf{\Psi}
to the PXXXIV RHP
7.2
on the boundary of a neighborhood of
\zeta_{\tau}(s)
.

The constructive procedure to get to its form is a bit cumbersome, but based on several techniques common in Riemann-Hilbert literature. The basic idea is that, although the jump matrix for
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
has non-trivial components in all its entries, it can essentially be brought to the form
\mathbf{I}+(\ast)\mathbf{E}_{12}
after some formal manipulations. In the following, we just describe the final result of this procedure.

For the calculations that come next, recall that the contours
\Gamma_{j}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
and sectors
\Omega_{j}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
were introduced in (
6.1
) and (
6.2
), respectively, and the functions
\mathsf{F}_{\tau}
and
\mathsf{P}_{\tau}
are given in (
6.4
). As a construction similar to (
7.66
), set

(8.6)

\mathsf{j}_{\tau}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{0}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)},\\
-\frac{1}{(1-\ee^{-\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)})(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{1}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cup\Gamma_{2}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)},\\
\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}}-1,&\zeta\in\Gamma_{3}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)},\\
-\frac{1}{(1-\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)})(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{4}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cup\Gamma_{5}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)},\end{cases}

and define

(8.7)

\mathsf{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}}\frac{\mathsf{j}_{\tau}(w)}{w-\zeta}\mathrm{d}w,\quad\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}.

The growth of
\re\mathsf{P}
and
\im\mathsf{F}
near
\infty
as implied by Proposition
6.2
–(iii),(iv), ensure that
\mathsf{j}_{\tau}(w)
decays exponentially fast as
w\to\infty
along
\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
, so that the integrals defining
\mathsf{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
are absolutely convergent near
\infty
.

Care must be taken at
\zeta=0
when
\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau{\mathsf{F}}_{\tau}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))}=1
, corresponding to
\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}
. Recalling RHP
6.10
–(4), this latter condition is the same that produces a singular behavior of the model problem at
\zeta_{\tau}(s)
. In this case, we interpret the integral along each of the contours
\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{1}\cup\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{2}
and
\Gamma_{3}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cup\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{4}
in the sense of principal value. Since the density
{\mathsf{j}}_{\tau}
is meromorphic along each of these contours, with only a simple pole at
\zeta=\zeta_{\tau}(s)
, this principal value integral is well defined as a finite complex number.

The next lemma is an analogue of Lemma
7.16
.

Lemma 8.1
.

Suppose that we are in the critical regime from Definition
7.1
. The function
\mathsf{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
satisfies the following properties.

(i)

For
\zeta\in\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\setminus\{\zeta_{\tau}(s)\}
, the scalar function
\mathsf{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
satisfies

\mathsf{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{+}(\zeta)=\mathsf{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{-}(\zeta)\mathsf{j}_{\tau}(\zeta).

(ii)

As
\zeta\to{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
while
\tau,s
are kept fixed,

(8.8)

\mathsf{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}(\log(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})),&\text{if}\quad\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))\notin 2\pi\mathbb{Z},\\
\mathcal{O}(\log(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})),&\text{if}\quad\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}\quad\text{and}\quad\zeta\in\Omega_{j}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)},\;j=0,2,3,5,\\
\mathcal{O}\left((\zeta-\zeta_{\tau}(s))^{-1}\right),&\text{if}\quad\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}\quad\text{and}\quad\zeta\in\Omega_{1}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cup\Omega_{4}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}.\end{cases}

(iii)

As
\tau\to+\infty
,

\mathsf{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)=\mathcal{O}\left(\tau^{-1}\right)

with uniform error term for
\zeta
within compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\}
.

Proof.

The claimed property (i) is a consequence of Plemelj’s formula.

Next, we prove (ii). Thanks to Proposition
6.9
, in this case we see that the matrix
{\mathsf{j}}_{\tau}
is meromorphic along bounded arcs of each of the contours
\Gamma^{0}_{0}=[0,+\infty),\Gamma^{0}_{3}=(-\infty,0]
,
\Gamma^{0}_{1}\cup\Gamma^{0}_{2}
and
\Gamma^{0}_{4}\cup\Gamma^{0}_{5}
, and it is
C^{\infty}
along unbounded components. Thus, the limit in (i) corresponds to the limit of four Cauchy integrals near a point where each density is meromorphic, with a simple pole at
\zeta=\zeta_{\tau}(s)
when
\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau{\mathsf{F}}_{\tau}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))}=1
, and no other poles. The property (ii) then follows from basic properties of Cauchy transforms and deformation of contours.

Finally, for the proof of (iii), we observe that Proposition
6.9
provides the estimate

|\mathsf{j}_{\tau}(w)|=\mathcal{O}\left(\ee^{-\eta|w-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|}\right),

uniformly for
w\in\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
. Furthermore, from the very definition of
\mathsf{P}_{\tau}
and
\mathsf{F}_{\tau}
in (
6.4
), it follows that given any compact
K
, there exists
\tau_{0}>0
such that
\mathsf{j}_{\tau}(w)
has an analytic continuation to a neighborhood of any
z\in K\cap\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\setminus\{{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\}
, for any
\tau\geq\tau_{0}
. Part (iii) then follows from standard arguments using the classical steepest descent method for oscillatory integrals, having in mind that the major contribution for each integral along
\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{j}
will always come from the value
w={\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
.
∎

Finally, with

\mathbf{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{I}+\mathsf{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12},\quad\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)},

and
\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
as in (
8.2
), we introduce

(8.9)

\mathbf{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)=\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)\times\begin{cases}\mathbf{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta),&\zeta\in\Omega_{j}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)},\;j=0,1,4,5,\\
(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21})\mathbf{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)(\mathbf{I}-(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})\mathbf{E}_{21}),&\zeta\in\Omega_{2}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)},\\
(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{E}_{21})\mathbf{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)(\mathbf{I}+(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})\mathbf{E}_{21}),&\zeta\in\Omega_{3}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}.\end{cases}

The construction of
\mathbf{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
is as defined in (
8.9
) for the following reasons. First of all, thanks to the jump (
8.3
) and the jump for
\mathbf{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
obtained from (
8.6
) and Lemma
8.1
–(i), the relation

(8.10)

\mathbf{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{+}(\zeta)=\mathbf{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{-}(\zeta){\mathbf{J}}_{\tau}(\zeta),\quad\zeta\in\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cap D_{\delta}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))\setminus\{\zeta_{\tau}(s)\},

follows after a cumbersome but straightforward calculation, where we recall that
\mathbf{J}_{\tau}
is the jump of the model problem given in (
6.23
).

Moreover, using (
8.5
) and (
8.8
), we obtain the behavior as
\zeta\to\zeta_{\tau}(s)

(8.11)

\mathbf{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}((\log(\zeta-\zeta_{\tau}(s)))^{2}),&\text{ if }\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))\notin 2\pi\mathbb{Z},\\
\mathcal{O}((\log(\zeta-\zeta_{\tau}(s)))^{2}),&\text{ if }\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}\text{ and }\zeta\in\Omega_{j}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)},j=0,2,3,5,\\
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log(\zeta-\zeta_{\tau}(s))}{\zeta-\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\right),&\text{ if }\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}\text{ and }\zeta\in\Omega_{1}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cup\Omega_{4}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}.\\
\end{cases}

Furthermore, thanks to Lemma
8.1
–(iii) and the estimates for
\mathsf{P}_{\tau},\mathsf{F}_{\tau}
provided by Proposition
6.9
, we obtain that

\mathbf{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)=\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(\tau^{-1})\right),

uniformly for
\zeta
in compacts of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{\zeta_{\tau}(s)\}
, and uniformly for
s
within the critical regime. Still in the critical regime, the factor
y=\zeta_{\tau}(s)
remains bounded, and the matrix
\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta{\,|\,}y)
is also uniformly bounded for
\zeta
in compacts of
\mathbb{C}\setminus 0
and
y
in compacts of the real line. Thus, the matrix
\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
is also uniformly bounded for
\zeta
in compacts of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{\zeta_{\tau}(s)\}
and
s
within the critical regime. Hence, we can commute the terms in the right-hand side above, obtaining that

(8.12)

\mathbf{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(\tau^{-1})\right)\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta),

valid uniformly for
\zeta
in compacts of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{\zeta_{\tau}(s)\}
and uniformly for
s
within the critical regime.

8.1.3.
Small norm analysis

To conclude the asymptotic analysis, we fix
r>0
and make

(8.13)

\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathrm{i}\left(q(\zeta_{\tau}(s))+\frac{\zeta_{\tau}(s)^{2}}{4}\right)\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\times\begin{cases}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}(\zeta)\mathbf{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)^{-1},&\zeta\in D_{r}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))\setminus\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)},\\
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}(\zeta)\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)^{-1},&\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(\overline{D_{r}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))}\cup\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}),\end{cases}

where we recall that
q=q(y)
is the same factor that appears in (
8.4
).

Denote

\Gamma^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\left(\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cup\partial D_{r}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))\right)\setminus D_{r}(\zeta_{\tau}(s)),\quad\Gamma^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{j}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{j}\setminus\overline{D}_{r}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))=\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}_{j}\cap\Gamma^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})},

with
D_{r}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))
being oriented in the clockwise direction.

The matrix
\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
was defined as in (
8.13
) so that it is the solution to the following RHP.

RHP 8.2
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
with the following properties.

(1)

\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
.

(2)

The matrix
\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
has continuous boundary values
\mathbf{R}_{\pm}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
along
\Gamma^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
, and they are related by the jump relation
\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{+}(\zeta)=\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{-}(\zeta)\mathbf{J}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)
,
\zeta\in\Gamma^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
, with

(8.14)

\mathbf{J}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\\
\begin{cases}\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)\mathbf{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)^{-1},&\zeta\in\partial D_{r}(\zeta_{\tau}(s)),\\
\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}}\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12}\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)^{-1},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{0}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})},\\
\mathbf{I}-\frac{\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12}\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)^{-1}}{(1-\ee^{-\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)})(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})},\\
\left(\mathbf{I}+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{+}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{21}\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{+}(\zeta)^{-1}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{+}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12}\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{+}(\zeta)^{-1}}{(1-\ee^{-\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)})(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})}\right),&\zeta\in\Gamma_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})},\\
\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{-}(\zeta)\left[\left(\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}}-1\right)\mathbf{E}_{11}-\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}\mathbf{E}_{22}\right]\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{-}(\zeta)^{-1},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{3}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})},\\
\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{-}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12}\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{-}(\zeta)^{-1}}{(1-\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)})(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)}\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{-}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{21}\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{-}(\zeta)^{-1}\right),&\zeta\in\Gamma_{4}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})},\\
\mathbf{I}-\frac{\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12}\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)^{-1}}{(1-\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)})(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{5}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}.\end{cases}

(3)

As
\zeta\to\infty
,

\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)=\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-1}).

(4)

The matrix
\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
remains bounded near each point in the finite set
\partial D_{r}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))\cap\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
.

We now summarize how to obtain this RHP for
\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
. In what follows, recall that
\mathbf{J}_{\tau}
is the jump matrix for the model problem
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
, whose explicit expression is found in (
6.23
).

The jumps for
\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
outside
\overline{D}_{r}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))
are obtained from the relations

\mathbf{J}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)=\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{-}(\zeta){\mathbf{J}}_{\tau}(\zeta)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}}(\zeta)^{-1}\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{-}(\zeta)^{-1}=\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{+}(\zeta)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}}(\zeta)^{-1}{\mathbf{J}}_{\tau}(\zeta)\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{+}(\zeta)^{-1}.

With these identities in mind, the matrix
\mathbf{J}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
along
\Gamma^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}\setminus\overline{D}_{r}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))
is computed from (
8.3
) and (
6.23
). The jump on
\partial D_{r}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))
is computed directly from (
8.13
).

Both
\mathbf{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
and
\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
have jumps on the arcs of
\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cap D_{r}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))
, so the matrix
\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
may have jumps along these arcs as well. However, with the aid of the identity

\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{-}(\zeta)^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{+}(\zeta)=\mathbf{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{-}(\zeta){\mathbf{J}}_{\tau}(\zeta)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}}(\zeta)^{-1}\mathbf{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{-}(\zeta)^{-1},\quad\zeta\in\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\cap D_{r}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))\setminus\{\zeta_{\tau}(s)\},

and (
8.10
), we see that
\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
does not have jumps inside
D_{r}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))
, and therefore the point
\zeta=\zeta_{\tau}(s)
is an isolated singularity of
\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
. A combination of (
8.11
) with the local behavior of
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
as
\zeta\to\zeta_{\tau}(s)
ensures that
\zeta_{\tau}(s)
is not an essential singularity. Finally, sending
\zeta\to\zeta_{\tau}(s)
along
\Omega_{0}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
then ensures that this singularity is in fact removable. This argument shows that
\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
is analytic inside
D_{r}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))
.

Also, with (
6.24
) and (
8.4
) in mind, it is straightforward to show RHP
8.2
–(3). Finally, the condition on RHP
8.2
–(4) follows because
\mathbf{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
,
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
,
\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
and their boundary values are all bounded away from
\zeta_{\tau}(s)\notin\partial D_{r}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))\cap\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
.

To conclude the asymptotic analysis, we now show that
\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
is indeed close to the identity matrix.

Proposition 8.3
.

The estimate

\|\mathbf{J}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}-\mathbf{I}\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\Gamma^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})})}=\mathcal{O}(\tau^{-1})

holds true as
\tau\to\infty
, uniformly for
s
within the critical regime from Definition
7.1
.

Along the way of the proof of Proposition
8.3
, and also later, we will need to estimate terms involving
\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
. We use the fact that
\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
and its boundary values
\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{\pm}
are uniformly bounded for
\zeta
in compacts away from
\zeta=\zeta_{\tau}(s)\notin\mathbb{C}\setminus\overline{D}_{r}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))
and
s
within the critical regime. For
\zeta
in unbounded components, we obtain estimates for
\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
using (
8.4
). All in all, and having in mind that
q(y)
is bounded for
y=\zeta_{\tau}(s)
in compacts, it means that the estimates

(8.15)

\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)=\mathcal{O}\left(\zeta^{1/4}\right)\ee^{-\frac{2}{3}\zeta^{3/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)^{-1}=\mathcal{O}\left(\zeta^{1/4}\right)\ee^{\frac{2}{3}\zeta^{3/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}

are valid uniformly for
\zeta\in\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\setminus D_{r}(\zeta_{\tau}(s))
(including for unbounded
\zeta
), and also uniformly for
s
within the critical regime. The same bound also holds true for
\mathbf{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{\pm}
.

Proof of Proposition
8.3
.

We estimate the jumps on (
8.14
) case by case.

The estimate (
8.12
) immediately implies that
\mathbf{J}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}=\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(\tau^{-1})
in
L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\partial D_{r}(\zeta_{\tau}(s)))
.

Next, for
j=1,2
we use Proposition
6.9
–(iii) and (
8.15
) to obtain

\frac{\mathbf{G}_{+}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12}\mathbf{G}_{+}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)^{-1}}{1-\ee^{-\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)}}=\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{1/2})\frac{\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)-\frac{4}{3}\zeta^{3/2}}}{1-\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau\mathsf{F}_{\tau}(\zeta)}}=\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{1/2}\ee^{-\eta\tau|\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|}),\quad\zeta\in\Gamma_{j}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\setminus\overline{D}_{r}({\zeta_{\tau}(s)}),

and when combined with the estimates from Proposition
6.9
–(i),(ii) we get that
\mathbf{J}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}=\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta\tau})
in
L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{j}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\setminus\overline{D}_{r}({\zeta_{\tau}(s)}))
,
j=1,2
, for some new value
\eta>0
. In a completely similar manner, the same estimate also holds true in
L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{j}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\setminus\overline{D}_{r}({\zeta_{\tau}(s)}))
for
j=4,5
.

Finally, the estimate
\mathbf{J}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}=\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta\tau})
in
L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{3}^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\setminus\overline{D}_{r}({\zeta_{\tau}(s)}))=(-\infty,{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}-r)
is a direct consequence of (
8.15
) and Proposition
6.9
–(i).
∎

As a consequence of the estimate for
\mathbf{J}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
, we obtain

Theorem 8.4
.

The estimates

\|\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}-\mathbf{I}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})})}=\mathcal{O}(\tau^{-1}),\quad\|\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{\pm}-\mathbf{I}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})})}=\mathcal{O}(\tau^{-1})

as well as

\|\partial_{\zeta}\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})})}=\mathcal{O}(\tau^{-1}),\quad\|\partial_{\zeta}\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{\pm}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})})}=\mathcal{O}(\tau^{-1})

are valid as
\tau\to\infty
, uniformly for
s
within the critical regime from Definition
7.1
.

Proof.

The result is a consequence of Proposition
8.3
and the general small norm theory for RHPs, we skip details.
∎

8.1.4.
Unwrap of the transformations in the critical regime

Recall that the operator
\mathbf{\Delta}_{z}
in an arbitrary variable
z
was introduced in (
2.53
).

Unwrapping the transformations, we obtain the following identities. For any
r>0
, we obtain that the equality

(8.16)

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau,-}(\zeta)=\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathrm{i}\left(q({\zeta_{\tau}(s)})+\frac{{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}^{2}}{4}\right)\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{-}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Psi}_{-}(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}{\,|\,}y={\zeta_{\tau}(s)}),

holds true for
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}\setminus({\zeta_{\tau}(s)}-r,{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}+r)
. Additionally, the identities

(8.17)

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau,-}(\zeta)=\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathrm{i}\left(q({\zeta_{\tau}(s)})+\frac{{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}^{2}}{4}\right)\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{-}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Psi}_{-}(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}{\,|\,}y={\zeta_{\tau}(s)})(\mathbf{I}+\mathsf{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12})

for
{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}<\zeta<{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}+r
, and

(8.18)

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau,-}(\zeta)=\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathrm{i}\left(q({\zeta_{\tau}(s)})+\frac{{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}^{2}}{4}\right)\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{-}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Psi}_{-}(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}{\,|\,}y={\zeta_{\tau}(s)})(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{E}_{21})\\
\times(\mathbf{I}+\mathsf{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{-}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12})(\mathbf{I}+(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})\mathbf{E}_{21}),

for
{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}-r<\zeta<{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
, also hold.

We use these identities to obtain rough bounds for
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
and
\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
, as well as a suitable representation of the latter.

Starting with the former, observing that
{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
remains bounded for
s
within the critical regime, and using Theorem
8.14
, we estimate

\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathrm{i}\left(q({\zeta_{\tau}(s)})+\frac{{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}^{2}}{4}\right)\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}_{-}(\zeta)=\mathcal{O}(1),\quad\tau\to\infty,

valid uniformly for
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}
. Equation (
8.16
) updates to

(8.19)

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau,-}(\zeta)=\mathcal{O}(1)\mathbf{\Psi}_{-}(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}{\,|\,}y={\zeta_{\tau}(s)}),

valid as
\zeta\to\infty
uniformly for
|\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|>r
.

Next, we use the behavior (
7.4
) of
\mathbf{\Psi}
, and the boundedness of
\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}
for
y={\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
bounded, and express (
8.17
) and (
8.18
) in an unified way as

(8.20)

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau,-}(\zeta)=\mathcal{O}(1)\left[\mathbf{I}+\left(\mathsf{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\log\zeta\right)\mathbf{E}_{12}\right]\left(\mathbf{I}+\chi_{(-r,0)}(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})\mathbf{E}_{21}\right),

valid as
\tau\to\infty
uniformly for
|\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|<r
.

Accounting for the asymptotic behavior (
7.3
), we summarize (
8.19
) and (
8.20
) in the more compact form

(8.21)

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau,-}(\zeta)=\mathcal{O}(1+|\zeta|^{1/4})\ee^{-(\frac{2}{3}\zeta_{-}^{3/2}+{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\zeta_{-}^{1/2})\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\\
\times\left[\mathbf{I}+\chi_{(-r,r)}(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})\left(\mathsf{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta)+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\log\zeta\right)\mathbf{E}_{12}\right]\left(\mathbf{I}+\chi_{(-r,0)}(\zeta-{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})(1+\ee^{\tau\mathsf{P}_{\tau}(\zeta)})\mathbf{E}_{21}\right),

which is valid as
\tau\to\infty
, uniformly for
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}
and uniformly for
{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
in the critical regime.

Next, we move to estimates for
\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
. A direct calculation from (
8.16
) yields

(8.22)

\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau,-}(\zeta+{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})=\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{\Psi}_{-}(\zeta{\,|\,}{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})+\mathbf{\Psi}_{-}(\zeta{\,|\,}{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})^{-1}\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{R}_{-}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta+{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})\mathbf{\Psi}_{-}(\zeta{\,|\,}{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}),

valid for
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}\setminus(-r,r)
.

Thanks to Theorem
8.4
, we obtain that
\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{R}_{-}(\zeta+{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})=\mathcal{O}(\tau^{-1})
uniformly for
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}\setminus(-r,r)
. Furthermore, due to the fact that
y={\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
remains bounded for
s
within the critical regime, the matrix
\mathbf{\Psi}_{-}
is bounded for
\zeta
in compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}\setminus(-r,r)
, also uniformly in
s
within the critical regime. Finally, as
\zeta\to\infty
, the factor
\mathbf{\Psi}_{-}
and its derivative that appear above can be estimated from (
7.3
). All in all, these arguments show that for some
\eta>0
, the rough estimate

\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{\Psi}_{-}(\zeta)=\ee^{\left(\frac{2}{3}-\eta\right)\zeta^{3/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathcal{O}(\zeta)\ee^{-\left(\frac{2}{3}-\eta\right)\zeta^{3/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}},

valid uniformly for
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}\setminus(-r,r)
and uniformly for
s
within the critical regime as
\tau\to\infty
.

Thus, using this bound in (
8.22
), we obtain

(8.23)

\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau,-}(\zeta+{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})=\ee^{\left(\frac{2}{3}-\eta\right)\zeta^{3/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathcal{O}(\zeta)\ee^{-\left(\frac{2}{3}-\eta\right)\zeta^{3/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}},

which again is valid uniformly for
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}\setminus(-r,r)
and uniformly for
s
within the critical regime as
\tau\to\infty
. Such asymptotic formula may be differentiated term by term.

Next, Equation (
8.17
) yields

\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau,-}(\zeta+{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})\right]_{21}=\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta{\,|\,}{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})\right]_{21}+\left[\mathbf{\Psi}_{-}(\zeta{\,|\,}{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})^{-1}\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta+{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})\mathbf{\Psi}_{-}(\zeta{\,|\,}{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})\right]_{21},

for
\zeta\in(0,r)
. The first term on the right-hand side coincides with (
7.11
). The second term can be written in terms of the analytic function
\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}
from (
8.5
), where we recall that therein
\mathbf{M}_{0}=\mathbf{I}
, see (
7.7
). In summary, we then obtain the identity

(8.24)

\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau,-}(\zeta+{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})\right]_{21}=\mathcal{H}(\zeta{\,|\,}{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})+\left[\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}(\zeta{\,|\,}{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})^{-1}\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}(\zeta+{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}(\zeta{\,|\,}{\zeta_{\tau}(s)})\right]_{21},

which is valid for
\zeta\in(0,r)
, and where we emphasize that
r>0
can be made arbitrarily small. Observe that the terms on the right-hand side are all analytic on a full disk
D_{r}(0)
, fact which will be useful later.

The asymptotic formulas (
8.21
) and (
8.23
) and the identity (
8.24
) with the asymptotic decay of
\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm c})}
ensured by Theorem
8.4
are the main outcomes that we will use later.

8.2.
Asymptotic analysis of the model problem in the subcritical regime

As the next step we carry out the asymptotic analysis in the subcritical regime as stated in Definition
7.1
. Under this regime, (
6.6
) ensures the existence of
M=M(T)>0
depending only on
T
for which

(8.25)

M\leq{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}\leq\frac{1}{M}\tau^{1/2}.

We use these bounds on
\zeta_{\tau}(s)
extensively in the analysis that follows.

8.2.1.
Scaling step

As a first step for the asymptotic analysis, we make a scaling of the model problem. Denote

(8.26)

\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{\tau}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathsf{P}_{\tau}\left(\zeta_{\tau}(s)\zeta\right),\quad\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{\tau}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathsf{F}_{\tau}\left(\zeta_{\tau}(s)\zeta\right),

and set

\mathbf{\Phi}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm sb})}_{\tau}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\zeta_{\tau}(s)^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}(\zeta_{\tau}(s)\zeta).

Then the matrix-valued function
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm sb})}
satisfies a RHP which is obtained from a shift and scaling of the RHP
6.10
for
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
, namely the following.

RHP 8.5
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{\Phi}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm sb})}_{\tau}
with the following properties.

(1)

\mathbf{\Phi}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm sb})}_{\tau}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma^{1}
.

(2)

The matrix
\mathbf{\Phi}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm sb})}_{\tau}
has continuous boundary values
\mathbf{\Phi}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm sb})}_{\tau,\pm}
along
\Gamma^{1}\setminus\{1\}
, and they are related by
\mathbf{\Phi}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm sb})}_{\tau,+}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Phi}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm sb})}_{\tau,-}(\zeta)\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{\tau}(\zeta)
,
\zeta\in\Gamma^{1}\setminus\{1\}
, where

(8.27)

\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{\tau}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{\tau}(\zeta)}}\mathbf{E}_{12},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{0}^{1},\\
\mathbf{I}-\frac{1}{(1-\ee^{-\mathrm{i}\tau\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{\tau}(\zeta)})(1+\ee^{\tau\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{\tau}(\zeta)})}\mathbf{E}_{12},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{1}^{1},\\
\left(\mathbf{I}+(1+\ee^{\tau\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{\tau}(\zeta)})\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{1}{(1-\ee^{-\mathrm{i}\tau\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{\tau}(\zeta)})(1+\ee^{\tau\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{\tau}(\zeta)})}\mathbf{E}_{12}\right),&\zeta\in\Gamma_{2}^{1},\\
\frac{1}{1+\ee^{\tau\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{\tau}(\zeta)}}\mathbf{E}_{12}-(1+\ee^{\tau\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{\tau}(\zeta)})\mathbf{E}_{21},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{3}^{1},\\
\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{1}{(1-\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{\tau}(\zeta)})(1+\ee^{\tau\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{\tau}(\zeta)})}\mathbf{E}_{12}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}+(1+\ee^{\tau\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{\tau}(\zeta)})\mathbf{E}_{21}\right),&\zeta\in\Gamma_{4}^{1},\\
\mathbf{I}-\frac{1}{(1-\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{\tau}(\zeta)})(1+\ee^{\tau\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{\tau}(\zeta)})}\mathbf{E}_{12},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{5}^{1}.\end{cases}

(3)

As
\zeta\to\infty
,

(8.28)

{\mathbf{\Phi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm sb})}_{\tau}(\zeta)=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-1})\right)\zeta^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\ee^{-\frac{2}{3}\zeta_{\tau}(s)^{3/2}\zeta^{3/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}.

(4)

As
\zeta\to 1
,

{\mathbf{\Phi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm sb})}_{\tau}(\zeta)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}(1),&\text{if }\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{\tau}(1)}\neq 1,\\
\mathcal{O}(1),&\text{if }\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{\tau}(1)}=1\text{ and }\zeta\notin\Omega_{1}^{1}\cup\Omega_{1}^{4},\\
\mathcal{O}\begin{pmatrix}1&(\zeta-1)^{-1}\\
1&(\zeta-1)^{-1}\end{pmatrix},&\text{if }\ee^{\mathrm{i}\tau\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{\tau}(1)}=1\text{ and }\zeta\in\Omega_{1}^{1}\cup\Omega_{1}^{4}.\end{cases}

To complete the asymptotic analysis for
\mathbf{\Phi}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm sb})}_{\tau}
we need to construct a global parametrix.

8.2.2.
The global parametrix

For the construction of the global parametrix we will use the model problem
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}
which solves RHP
7.4
, setting

(8.29)
