We now estimate the terms on the right-hand side exactly as before. Observing now that
\xi^{1/2}_{-}\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
, from Theorem
7.19
and RHP
7.15
–(3),(4) we bound

(9.18)

[\mathbf{B}_{0}(\xi)^{-1}\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)\mathbf{B}_{0}(\xi)]_{21,-}=\mathcal{O}((1+|\xi|^{1/2})|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|^{2}\tau^{-1}),\quad\tau\to\infty,

uniformly for
-\delta{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}^{2}<\xi<0
and
s
within the supercritical regime.

Next, using RHP
7.15
–(4) and the boundedness of
\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm sp})}(\zeta),\mathbf{A}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm sp})}(\zeta)
, for
-\varepsilon<\xi<0
we obtain

(9.19)

\left[(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21})\mathbf{B}(\xi)^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)^{-1}|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm sp})}(\zeta)\mathbf{A}^{{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm sp})}}(\zeta))|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)\mathbf{B}(\xi)(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{E}_{21})\right]_{21,-}\\
=\left[(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\log\xi}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\mathbf{E}_{12})\mathbf{B}_{0}(\xi)^{-1}\mathcal{O}(|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|)\mathbf{B}_{0}(\xi)(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\log\xi}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\mathbf{E}_{12})\right]_{21,-}=\mathcal{O}(|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|).

Next, to treat the remaining interval
-\delta{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}^{2}<\xi<-\varepsilon
, we use (
7.54
) to obtain

(9.20)

\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)=\frac{\tau}{{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}^{2}}\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}\xi+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}}{\tau}\right),

uniformly in the mentioned interval, and also uniformly for
s
within the supercritical regime. This estimate yields that

(9.21)

\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)}=\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}}{\tau}\right)\right)\ee^{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}\tau\xi/{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}^{2}},\quad|\xi|\leq\delta{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}^{2}.

In particular,
\ee^{\tau{\mathcal{P}}(\xi)}
remains bounded for
-\delta{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}^{2}<\xi<-\varepsilon
. Using then RHP
7.15
–(3) and the boundedness of
\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)
,
\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm sp})}(\zeta)
and
\mathbf{A}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm sp})}(\zeta)
, we get

(9.22)

\Big[(\mathbf{I}+(1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)})\mathbf{E}_{21})\mathbf{B}(\xi)^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)^{-1}|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\\
\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}(\mathbf{R}^{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm sp})}(\zeta)\mathbf{A}^{{\scriptscriptstyle({\scriptscriptstyle\rm sp})}}(\zeta))|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\xi)\mathbf{B}(\xi)(\mathbf{I}-(1+\ee^{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}(\xi)})\mathbf{E}_{21})\Big]_{21,-}=\mathcal{O}(|{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}|(1+|\xi|^{1/2})).

valid uniformly for
-\delta{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}^{2}<\xi<-\varepsilon
, and uniformly for
s
within the supercritical regime.

In summary, combining (
9.18
), (
9.19
) and (
9.22
) into (
9.16
) and (
9.17
), we obtain the second estimate in (iii) for
\zeta<0
, concluding the proof.
∎

This last result concludes the analysis of all the quantities we will need from the model problem, and we now finally turn our attention to the analysis of the discrete OPs themselves.

10.
The Riemann-Hilbert Problem for discrete orthogonal polynomial ensembles

The RHP for continuous weight functions was first introduced by Fokas, Its and Kitaev
[
50
]
, and in combination with the Deift-Zhou nonlinear steepest descent method for its asymptotic analysis
[
41
,
44
,
45
,
46
]
it has become an ubiquitous tool in the asymptotic analysis of models which are solvable by orthogonal polynomials.

The RHP for discrete orthogonality was first introduced by Borodin and Boyarchenko
[
20
]
. In the context of the six-vertex model, this discrete RHP for OPs was notably used by Bleher and Liechty
[
17
,
16
,
15
]
, see also the recent follow-up
[
56
]
by Gorin and Liechty. However, they consider the six-vertex model with boundary conditions and lattices different than the ones we consider here, corresponding to different classes of weights of orthogonality. In related contexts, the discrete RHP for OPs was used by Baik, Kriecherbauer, McLaughlin and Miller
[
5
]
motivated by random tiling models, and by Liechty and Wang
[
71
]
and Buckingham and Liechty
[
25
]
in the context of non-intersecting Brownian bridges on the unit circle. The foundational work of Bleher and Liechty
[
14
]
computes asymptotics for discrete OPs for weights on the full real line; even though our setup here is different in various manners, the streamlined presentation of the asymptotic analysis in
[
14
]
was very helpful to us.

Recall that we are interested in OPs for a deformed discrete weight
\omega^{\sigma}
as in (
2.27
), with the undeformed component
\omega
satisfying Assumptions
2.4
. The Riemann-Hilbert problem for the orthogonal polynomials for the discrete weight
\omega^{\sigma}
is the following.

RHP 10.1
.

Find a matrix-valued function
\mathbf{D}:\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
with the following properties:

(1)

\mathbf{D}
is analytic.

(2)

\mathbf{D}
has simple poles at each point
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, and its residue satisfies

\mathop{\rm Res}_{z=k}\mathbf{D}(z)=\lim_{z\to k}\mathbf{D}(z)\mathbf{E}_{12}\omega^{\sigma}(k).

(3)

For some function
r:\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\to[0,+\infty)
satisfying
r(z)\to 0
as
z\to\infty
, the matrix
\mathbf{D}
admits an asymptotic expansion of the form

\mathbf{D}(z)\sim\left(\mathbf{I}+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbf{D}_{j}}{z^{j}}\right)\begin{pmatrix}z^{N}&0\\
0&z^{-N}\end{pmatrix},\quad z\to\infty\mbox{ with }z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}D_{r}(k),

where the radius
r=r(k)
may depend on the center of the disk
D_{r}(k)
,
\mathbf{I}
is the
2\times 2
identity matrix, and
\mathbf{D}_{k}
is a
2\times 2
matrix independent of
z
.

The corresponding Christoffel-Darboux kernel
\mathsf{K}_{N}^{\sigma}
from (
2.32
) expresses as

\mathsf{K}_{N}^{\sigma}(x,y)=\mathsf{K}_{N}^{\sigma}(x,y{\,|\,}s)=-\frac{1}{x-y}\mathbf{e}_{2}^{T}\mathbf{D}(y{\,|\,}s)^{-1}\mathbf{D}(x{\,|\,}s)\mathbf{e}_{1},\quad x\neq y,

and in the confluent limit
y\to x
this identity becomes

(10.1)

\mathsf{K}_{N}^{\sigma}(x,x{\,|\,}s)=-\mathbf{e}_{2}^{T}\mathbf{D}(x{\,|\,}s)^{-1}\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(x{\,|\,}s)\mathbf{e}_{1},\quad x\in\mathbb{C}.

As a consequence, (
2.33
) yields the relation

(10.2)

\log\frac{\mathsf{Z}_{N}^{\sigma}(S)}{\mathsf{Z}^{\sigma}_{N}(s)}=\int_{s}^{S}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}\ee^{-t(x-aN)-v}\omega(x{\,|\,}v)\mathbf{e}_{2}^{T}\mathbf{D}(x{\,|\,}v)^{-1}\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(x{\,|\,}v)\mathbf{e}_{1}\,\mathrm{d}v.

Our next goal is thus to perform the Deift-Zhou nonlinear steepest descent method for this RHP. The steps in the asymptotic analysis that we will perform here are inspired by
[
5
,
14
]
. However, many technical adaptations in this analysis are necessary, in virtue of the presence of the factor
\sigma
in the weight of orthogonality, and the corresponding poles of the factor
1/\sigma
that appears in this analysis. In a more fundamental contrast with previous works in the literature, we will use the model problem RHP
6.10
in the construction of the so-called local parametrix.

10.1.
Scaling of the RHP

As a first step, we scale the nodes of the RHP. For that, recall that the scaled weight
\Omega
is as in Assumptions
2.4
, and introduce

(10.3)

\sigma(x)=\sigma(x{\,|\,}s,t)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=1+\ee^{-tN(x-\mathsf{a})-s}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\Omega^{\sigma}}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{N}\omega^{\sigma}(Nx)=\sigma(x)\Omega(x).

To streamline notation we mostly write
\sigma=\sigma(x)
, writing the other parameters explicitly as
\sigma=\sigma(x{\,|\,}s),\sigma=\sigma(x{\,|\,}s,t)
etc, solely when necessary to avoid confusion. Also, when we need to evaluate one parameter to a specific value, say
t
at a value
\bullet
, we write this evaluation as
\sigma(x{\,|\,}t=\bullet)=\sigma(x{\,|\,}\bullet,s)
etc.

With
\mathbf{D}
being the solution to the RHP
10.1
, make the change

\mathbf{X}(z)=N^{-N\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{D}(Nz),\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}.

Then
\mathbf{X}
satisfies the following RHP.

RHP 10.2
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{X}:\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
with the following properties.

(1)

\mathbf{X}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.

(2)

\mathbf{X}
has simple poles at each point
x_{k}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{N}k\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, and its residue satisfies

\mathop{\rm Res}_{z=x_{k}}\mathbf{X}(z)=\lim_{z\to x_{k}}\mathbf{X}(z)\mathbf{E}_{12}{\Omega^{\sigma}}(x_{k}).

(3)

For some function
r:\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\to[0,+\infty)
satisfying
r(z)\to 0
as
z\to\infty
, the matrix
\mathbf{X}
admits an asymptotic expansion of the form

\mathbf{X}(z)\sim\left(\mathbf{I}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbf{X}_{k}}{z^{k}}\right)\begin{pmatrix}z^{N}&0\\
0&z^{-N}\end{pmatrix},\quad z\to\infty\mbox{ with }z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}D_{r(x_{k})}(x_{k}),

where for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
\mathbf{X}_{k}
is a
2\times 2
matrix independent of
z
.

Formula (
10.1
) becomes

(10.4)

\mathsf{K}_{N}^{\sigma}(x,x{\,|\,}s)=-\frac{1}{N}\mathbf{e}_{2}^{T}\mathbf{X}\left(\frac{x}{N}{\,|\,}s\right)^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\left(\frac{x}{N}{\,|\,}s\right)\mathbf{e}_{1},

whereas (
10.2
) then updates to

(10.5)

\log\frac{\mathsf{Z}_{N}^{\sigma}(S)}{\mathsf{Z}^{\sigma}_{N}(s)}=\int_{s}^{S}\sum_{x\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}\frac{\sigma(x{\,|\,}u)-1}{\sigma(x{\,|\,}u)}{\Omega^{\sigma}}(x{\,|\,}u)\mathbf{e}_{2}^{T}\mathbf{X}(x)^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\prime}(x)\mathbf{e}_{1}\,\mathrm{d}u.

In what follows, we will need to consider
1/\sigma(z)
for values of
z
away from certain subintervals of the real axis, and for that reason it is convenient to single out certain properties of the poles of this quotient, that is, of the zeros of
\sigma
.

Proposition 10.3
.

The zeros of the function
\sigma
are at the points

(10.6)

w_{k}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{(2k+1)\pi\mathrm{i}}{N}+z_{N}(s),\;k\in\mathbb{Z},\quad z_{N}(s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=-\frac{s}{tN}+\mathsf{a}.

In particular, under Assumptions
2.7
the following holds.

(i)

There exists
\delta>0
independent of
s,t,N
for which

\sigma(z)\neq 0\quad\text{for}\quad\re z>\mathsf{a}+\delta.

(ii)

For any
\theta_{0}\in(0,\pi/2)
, there exist
\rho_{0}>0
and
\delta>0
for which

|\sigma(z_{N}(s)\pm\rho\ee^{\mathrm{i}\theta})|\geq\delta\quad\text{for any }\theta\in[-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}]\text{ and any }\rho\in[0,\rho_{0}].

The values
\rho_{0}
and
\delta
may depend on
s
but are independent of
t
.

Proof.

It follows directly from the definition of
\sigma
.
∎

11.
Asymptotic analysis of the RHP for dOPs

We now apply the Deift-Zhou steepest descent method to the RHP
\mathbf{X}
for OPs described in Section
10.1
. The method itself consists in performing several transformations on the original RHP, with the goal of obtaining a RHP which can be solved by perturbation theory.

The first transformations are in a sense algebraic in nature, and will be carried out in a uniform manner, regardless of the subcritical regime of interest. These steps follow closely the already established roadmap of the Deift-Zhou method for discrete RHPs
[
5
,
14
]
: moving from discrete to a continuous RHP, introduction of the
g
-function and opening of lenses. The main difference in our work here is that we have to cope with the poles of the factor
1/\sigma
throughout all these steps.

As one of the final steps of the analysis, we will need to construct the so-called local and global parametrices. They will be constructed with the same auxiliary functions and model problem, regardless of the regime considered, but their asymptotic behavior will differ depending on which of the subregimes from Assumptions
2.7
that we are considering.

The last step of the asymptotic analysis is the application of the small norm theory, as usual.

11.1.
From discrete to continuous RHP

The first transformation consists of replacing the pole conditions for
\mathbf{X}
to jump conditions for a new matrix
\mathbf{Y}
, and it relies on the structure of the support of the equilibrium measure
\mu_{\mathsf{V}}
.

Set

\Pi_{N}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\sin(\pi Nz)}{\pi N}=\frac{\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}Nz}-\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}Nz}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}N},

which is an analytic function on a neighborhood of the real line that satisfies

\Pi_{N}(x_{k})=0,\qquad\Pi_{N}(z)\neq 0\text{ for }z\in\mathbb{R}_{>}\setminus\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z},\qquad\Pi^{\prime}_{N}(x_{k})=(-1)^{k},

where we recall that
x_{k}=\frac{1}{N}k\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
are the (simple) poles of the matrix
\mathbf{X}
.

We also need to introduce certain contours and regions that will be used for the first transformation. These transformations will depend on the position of the point
z_{N}(s)
introduced in (
10.6
), which we always assume to satisfy

|z_{N}(s)-\mathsf{a}|<\varepsilon,

for some
\varepsilon>0
independent of
N
, which can be made arbitrarily small but will be kept fixed. Recalling (
10.6
), we see that under Assumptions
2.7
this is always true for
N
sufficiently large and independent of the parameters
s,t
, and
\varepsilon>0
can be chosen to ensure that
z_{N}(s)>0
as well.

Fix some
\delta>0
and consider contours
\mathcal{L}^{\Delta}_{\pm}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\nabla}_{\pm}
as follows.

The contour
\mathcal{L}^{\Delta}_{+}
starts at the origin and moves upwards to a point
\mathrm{i}\delta
. It then turns right, moving torwards the real axis, up until it reaches the point
z_{N}(s)
, making an angle
\pi/3
with the interval
[0,z_{N}(s)]
.

The contour
\mathcal{L}^{\nabla}_{+}
starts from
z_{N}(s)
on the upper half plane, with angle
\pi/3
with the interval
[z_{N}(s),\infty)
, and then extends horizontally to
\infty
running asymptotically parallel with the positive real axis, within a positive but small distance from it.

We then set
\mathcal{L}_{-}^{\Delta}
and
\mathcal{L}_{-}^{\nabla}
to be the contours obtained from
\mathcal{L}_{+}^{\Delta}
and
\mathcal{L}_{+}^{\nabla}
by complex conjugation, respectively. These contours are displayed in Figure
9
.

Figure 9
.
The contours and regions used for the transformation
\mathbf{X}\mapsto\mathbf{Y}
of the RHP, corresponding to
s>0
(left) and
s<0
(right).

Also, we consider
\mathcal{S}^{\Delta}_{\pm}
to be the bounded region determined by
\mathcal{L}_{\pm}^{\Delta}
and
[0,z_{N}(s)]
, and
\mathcal{S}^{\nabla}_{\pm}
to be the region between
\mathcal{L}_{\pm}^{\nabla}
and
[z_{N}(s),+\infty)
. These regions are also displayed in Figure
9
. Observe in particular that
\mathsf{a}
is on the interior of the region determined by either
\mathcal{L}^{\Delta}_{+}\cup\mathcal{L}^{\Delta}_{-}
or
\mathcal{L}^{\nabla}_{+}\cup\mathcal{L}^{\nabla}_{-}
, depending on whether
s>0
or
s<0
, we refer again to Figure
9
. Nevertheless, for the first transformation this distinction can still be treated in an uniform manner.

Using these neighborhoods, we transform

(11.1)

\mathbf{Y}(z)=\begin{cases}\mathbf{X}(z)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{{\Omega^{\sigma}}(z)}{\Pi_{N}(z)}\ee^{\pm\mathrm{i}\pi Nz}\mathbf{E}_{12}\right),&z\in\mathcal{S}^{\nabla}_{\pm},\\
\mathbf{X}(z)\Pi_{N}(z)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{1}{{\Omega^{\sigma}}(z)\Pi_{N}(z)}\ee^{\pm\mathrm{i}\pi Nz}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right),&z\in\mathcal{S}^{\Delta}_{\pm},\\
\mathbf{X}(z),&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

The function
\Pi_{N}
has no zeros outside the real axis. Using Proposition
10.3
we see that
{\Omega^{\sigma}}
has no zeros on
\mathcal{S}_{\pm}^{\Delta}
for any
N>0
, so the transformation above is well defined for any
\delta>0
. We will eventually take
\delta>0
sufficiently small but independent of
N
.

The properties of
\Pi_{N}
listed earlier ensure that the poles coming from
1/\Pi_{N}
cancel the poles of
\mathbf{X}
, with the cost of creating jump conditions, so that
\mathbf{Y}
satisfies a continuous RHP that we describe next.

Set

\Gamma_{\mathbf{Y}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{L}_{+}^{\Delta}\cup\mathcal{L}_{-}^{\Delta}\cup\mathbb{R}_{>}\cup\mathcal{L}_{+}^{\nabla}\cup\mathcal{L}_{-}^{\nabla},\quad\mathcal{S}^{\Delta}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{S}^{\Delta}_{+}\cup\mathcal{S}^{\Delta}_{-},\quad\mathcal{S}^{\nabla}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{S}^{\nabla}_{+}\cup\mathcal{S}^{\nabla}_{-},\quad\mathcal{S}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{S}^{\Delta}\cup\mathcal{S}^{\nabla},

with the orientation of the arcs of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{Y}}
as shown in Figure
9
. The matrix
\mathbf{Y}
satisfies the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

RHP 11.1
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{X}:\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{Y}}\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
with the following properties.

(1)

\mathbf{Y}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{Y}}
.

(2)

\mathbf{Y}
has continuous boundary values
\mathbf{Y}_{\pm}
along
\Gamma_{\mathbf{Y}}
, and they are related by

\mathbf{Y}_{+}(z)=\mathbf{Y}_{-}(z)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{Y}}(z),\quad z\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{Y}},

where the jump matrix
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{Y}}
is given by

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{Y}}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}N}{{\Omega^{\sigma}}(z)}\mathbf{E}_{21},&z\in(0,z_{N}(s)),\\
\mathbf{I}-2\pi\mathrm{i}N{\Omega^{\sigma}}(z)\mathbf{E}_{12},&z\in(z_{N}(s),\infty),\\
\mathbf{I}\pm\frac{{\Omega^{\sigma}}(z)}{\Pi_{N}(z)}\ee^{\pm\mathrm{i}\pi Nz}\mathbf{E}_{12},&z\in\mathcal{L}^{\nabla}_{\pm},\\
\Pi_{N}(z)^{\pm\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\pm\frac{\ee^{\pm\pi\mathrm{i}Nz}}{{\Omega^{\sigma}}(z)}\mathbf{E}_{21}&z\in\mathcal{L}^{\Delta}_{\pm}.\end{cases}

(3)

As
z\to\infty
,

\mathbf{Y}(z)=(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}))\begin{pmatrix}z^{n}&0\\
0&z^{-n}\end{pmatrix}.

(4)

As
z\to z_{N}(s)
, the matrix
\mathbf{Y}
has the following behavior,

\mathbf{Y}(z)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}(1),&\text{if }z_{N}(s)\in\mathbb{R}_{>}\setminus\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\\
\mathcal{O}(1),&\text{if }z_{N}(s)\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\text{ and }z\to z_{N}(s)\text{ along }\mathcal{S},\\
\mathcal{O}\begin{pmatrix}1&(z-z_{N}(s))^{-1}\\
1&(z-z_{N}(s))^{-1}\end{pmatrix},&\text{if }z_{N}(s)\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\text{ and }z\to z_{N}(s)\text{ along }\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathcal{S}.\end{cases}

For
\sigma\equiv 1
, the form of the transformation
\mathbf{Y}\mapsto\mathbf{X}
is standard in the RH analysis of discrete OPs, see for instance
[
5
,
14
]
. Due to the presence of the factor
\sigma
we needed to consider the sets
\mathcal{S}^{\Delta}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\nabla}
in such a way that their boundaries emerge from the point
z_{N}(s)
rather than from the endpoint
\mathsf{a}
of
\supp\mu_{\mathsf{V}}
. As a consequence, in the case
z_{N}(s)\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
we do not cancel the pole behavior of
\mathbf{X}
when approaching
z_{N}(s)
from outside
\mathcal{S}^{\Delta}\cup\mathcal{S}^{\nabla}
, and the somewhat nonstandard conditions in RHP
11.1
–(4) have to be placed. The exact form of RHP
11.1
–(4) may be computed directly or, alternatively, in a more systematic way with the aid of Lemma
B.1
.

11.2.
Introduction of the g-function

For the second transformation, we use the equilibrium measure
\mu_{\mathsf{V}}
from Assumptions
2.5
and define

g(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int\log(z-x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathsf{V}}(x),\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,\mathsf{b}],

where the branch of the argument is the principal one. This is the so-called
g
-function associated to
\mu_{\mathsf{V}}
.

Proposition 11.2
.

The
g
-function is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,\mathsf{b}]
and satisfies the following properties

(a)

As
z\to\infty
away from
(-\infty,\mathsf{b}]
,

g(z)=\log z+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}).

(b)

For
x<0
,

g_{+}(x)-g_{-}(x)=2\pi\mathrm{i}.

(c)

For any
x>0
,

g_{+}(x)+g_{-}(x)=-2U^{\mu_{\mathsf{V}}}(x),\quad U^{\mu_{\mathsf{V}}}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int\log\frac{1}{|x-y|}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathsf{V}}(y).

In particular,
g_{+}+g_{-}
is purely real over the positive real axis. In general, for any
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
,

\re g(z)=-U^{\mu_{\mathsf{V}}}(z).

(d)

The variational conditions

\mathsf{V}(x)+2\ell-g_{+}(x)-g_{-}(x)=2U^{\mu_{\mathsf{V}}}(x)+\mathsf{V}(x)+2\ell\begin{cases}>0,&x>\mathsf{b},\\
=0,&\mathsf{a}\leq x\leq\mathsf{b},\\
<0,&0<x<\mathsf{a}\end{cases}

hold true, where
\ell=\ell_{\mathsf{V}}
is the same constant that appears in Assumptions
2.5
.

(e)

For
x\in(0,\mathsf{b})
,

g_{+}(x)-g_{-}(x)=2\pi\mathrm{i}\mu_{\mathsf{V}}([x,\mathsf{b}])=2\pi\mathrm{i}(1-\mu_{\mathsf{V}}([0,x])).

In particular this difference is purely imaginary with positive imaginary part, and on the interval
(0,\mathsf{a})
it reduces to

g_{+}(x)-g_{-}(x)=2\pi\mathrm{i}\left(1-x\right),\quad 0<x<\mathsf{a}.

The proof of Proposition
11.2
is standard in RHP literature, see for instance
[
14
, Section 3]
, and we skip it.

For the transformations that follow, it is convenient to introduce

(11.2)

\widehat{z}_{N}(s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\min\{z_{N}(s),\mathsf{a}\}=\begin{cases}\mathsf{a},&\text{if }s<0,\\
z_{N}(s),&\text{if }s>0,\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad\widecheck{z}_{N}(s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\max\{z_{N}(s),\mathsf{a}\}=\begin{cases}z_{N}(s),&\text{if }s<0,\\
\mathsf{a},&\text{if }s>0.\end{cases}

With
\mathsf{C}\in\mathbb{R}
being the constant from Assumptions
2.4
, the second transformation is

\mathbf{T}(z)=\ee^{(-N\ell+\mathsf{C})\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{Y}(z)\ee^{-Ng(z)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\ee^{(N\ell-\mathsf{C})\bm{\sigma}_{3}},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{T}},\quad\Gamma_{\mathbf{T}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Gamma_{\mathbf{Y}}.

We stress that this transformation does not affect the jump contour, and we refer again to Figure
9
for the display of this contour.

Using the jump properties of the
g
-function as described by Proposition
11.2
, it follows that
\mathbf{T}
satisfies the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

RHP 11.3
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{T}
with the following properties.

(1)

\mathbf{T}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{T}}
.

(2)

The matrix
\mathbf{T}
has continuous boundary values
\mathbf{T}_{\pm}
along
\Gamma_{\mathbf{T}}
, which satisfy the jump relation
\mathbf{T}_{+}(z)=\mathbf{T}_{-}(z)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{T}}(z)
,
z\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{T}}
, with

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{T}}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\ee^{2\pi\mathrm{i}Nz\bm{\sigma}_{3}}-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}N}{{\Omega^{\sigma}}(z)}\ee^{-N(g_{+}(z)+g_{-}(z)-2\ell)-2\mathsf{C}}\mathbf{E}_{21},&0<z<\widehat{z}_{N}(s),\\
\mathbf{I}-2\pi\mathrm{i}N{\Omega^{\sigma}}(z)\ee^{N(g_{+}(z)+g_{-}(z)-2\ell)+2\mathsf{C}}\mathbf{E}_{12},&z>\mathsf{b},\\
\ee^{-N(g_{+}(z)-g_{-}(z))\bm{\sigma}_{3}}-2\pi\mathrm{i}N{\Omega^{\sigma}}(z)\ee^{N(g_{+}(z)+g_{-}(z)-2\ell)+2\mathsf{C}}\mathbf{E}_{12},&\widecheck{z}_{N}(s)<z<b,\end{cases}

and

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{T}}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\ee^{-N(g_{+}(z)-g_{-}(z))\bm{\sigma}_{3}}-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}N}{{\Omega^{\sigma}}(z)}\ee^{-N(g_{+}(z)+g_{-}(z)-2\ell)-2\mathsf{C}}\mathbf{E}_{21},&\text{if }s>0\text{ and }z\in(\widehat{z}_{N}(s),\widecheck{z}_{N}(s)),\\
\ee^{2\pi\mathrm{i}Nz\bm{\sigma}_{3}}-2\pi\mathrm{i}N{\Omega^{\sigma}}(z)\ee^{N(g_{+}(z)+g_{-}(z)-2\ell)+2\mathsf{C}}\mathbf{E}_{12},&\text{if }s<0\text{ and }z\in(\widehat{z}_{N}(s),\widecheck{z}_{N}(s)),\end{cases}

which covers the jumps on the real axis, and

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{T}}(z)=\begin{cases}\Pi_{N}(z)^{\pm\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\pm\frac{\ee^{-N(2g(z)-2\ell\mp\pi\mathrm{i}z)-2\mathsf{C}}}{{\Omega^{\sigma}}(z)}\mathbf{E}_{21},&z\in\mathcal{S}_{\pm}^{\Delta},\\
\mathbf{I}\pm\frac{{\Omega^{\sigma}}(z)}{\Pi_{N}(z)}\ee^{N(2g(z)-2\ell\pm\pi\mathrm{i}z)+2\mathsf{C}}\mathbf{E}_{12},&z\in\mathcal{S}_{\pm}^{\nabla},\end{cases}

which covers the jumps outside the real axis.

(3)

As
z\to\infty
,

\mathbf{T}(z)=\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}).

(4)

As
z\to z_{N}(s)
, the matrix
\mathbf{T}
has the following behavior,

\mathbf{T}(z)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}(1),&\text{if }z_{N}(s)\in\mathbb{R}_{>}\setminus\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\\
\mathcal{O}(1),&\text{if }z_{N}(s)\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\text{ and }z\to z_{N}(s)\text{ along }\mathcal{S},\\
\mathcal{O}\begin{pmatrix}1&(z-z_{N}(s))^{-1}\\
1&(z-z_{N}(s))^{-1}\end{pmatrix},&\text{if }z_{N}(s)\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\text{ and }z\to z_{N}(s)\text{ along }\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathcal{S}.\end{cases}

To further help with the rewriting of the jumps above, we introduce the
\phi
-function

(11.3)

\phi(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{\mathsf{b}}^{z}\left(C^{\mu_{\mathsf{V}}}(s)+\frac{\mathsf{V}^{\prime}(s)}{2}\right)\mathrm{d}s,\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,\mathsf{b}],\quad\text{where }C^{\mu}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu(s)}{s-z},\;z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\supp\mu,

and where the path of integration in the definition of
\phi
does not cross
(-\infty,\mathsf{b}]
. This function
\phi
is well defined and analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,\mathsf{b}]
, and its relevant properties for the subsequent analysis are listed in the next result.

Proposition 11.4
.

We have

\phi(z)=-g(z)+\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{V}(z)+\ell,\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,\mathsf{b}].

Also,

\phi_{+}(z)+\phi_{-}(z)=0,\quad\mathsf{a}<z<\mathsf{b},\qquad\phi_{+}(z)-\phi_{-}(z)=-2\pi\mathrm{i}\mu_{\mathsf{V}}((z,\mathsf{b})),\quad 0<z<\mathsf{b}.

In particular,

\phi_{+}(z)-\phi_{-}(z)=2\pi\mathrm{i}\left(z-1\right),\quad 0<z<\mathsf{a}.

Proof.

Simply note that for
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,\mathsf{b}]
,

g^{\prime}(z)=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathsf{V}}(x)}{z-x}=-C^{\mu_{\mathsf{V}}}(z)=-\phi^{\prime}(z)+\frac{\mathsf{V}^{\prime}(z)}{2},

so for some constant
c

\phi(z)=-g(z)+\frac{\mathsf{V}(z)}{2}+c.

Taking the limit
z\to\mathsf{b}
and using properties of the
g
-function, we get that

\displaystyle 0=2\phi(\mathsf{b})

\displaystyle=-2g_{\pm}(\mathsf{b})+\mathsf{V}(\mathsf{b})+2c

\displaystyle=-g_{+}(\mathsf{b})-g_{-}(\mathsf{b})+\mathsf{V}(\mathsf{b})+2c

\displaystyle=2U^{\mu_{\mathsf{V}}}(\mathsf{b})+\mathsf{V}(\mathsf{b})+2c

\displaystyle=-2\ell+2c,

where for the last identity we used the variational equality. From this, all the remaining properties follow from the properties of
g
.
∎

In terms of the
\phi
function, the jump matrix for
\mathbf{T}
rewrites as

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{T}}(z)=\begin{cases}\ee^{2\pi\mathrm{i}Nz\bm{\sigma}_{3}}-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}N}{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}\ee^{N(\phi_{+}(z)+\phi_{-}(z))}\mathbf{E}_{21},&0<z<\widehat{z}_{N}(s),\\
\mathbf{I}-2\pi\mathrm{i}N\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}\ee^{-2N\phi(z)}\mathbf{E}_{12},&z>\mathsf{b},\\
\ee^{N(\phi_{+}(z)-\phi_{-}(z))\bm{\sigma}_{3}}-2\pi\mathrm{i}N\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}\mathbf{E}_{12},&\widecheck{z}_{N}(s)<z<b,\end{cases}

and

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{T}}(z)=\begin{cases}\ee^{N(\phi_{+}(z)-\phi_{-}(z))\bm{\sigma}_{3}}-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}N}{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}\mathbf{E}_{21},&\text{if }s<0\text{ and }z\in(\widehat{z}_{N}(s),\widecheck{z}_{N}(s)),\\
\ee^{2\pi\mathrm{i}Nz\bm{\sigma}_{3}}-2\pi\mathrm{i}N\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}\ee^{-N(\phi_{+}(z)-\phi_{-}(z))}\mathbf{E}_{12},&\text{if }s>0\text{ and }z\in(\widehat{z}_{N}(s),\widecheck{z}_{N}(s)),\end{cases}

which covers the jumps on the real axis, and

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{T}}(z)=\begin{cases}\Pi_{N}(z)^{\pm\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\pm\frac{\ee^{N(2\phi(z)\pm\pi\mathrm{i}z)}}{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}\mathbf{E}_{21},&z\in\mathcal{L}_{\pm}^{\Delta},\\
\mathbf{I}\pm\frac{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}{\Pi_{N}(z)}\ee^{-N(2\phi(z)\mp\pi\mathrm{i}z)}\mathbf{E}_{12},&z\in\mathcal{L}_{\pm}^{\nabla},\end{cases}

which covers the jumps outside the real axis.

11.3.
Opening of lenses

The next transformation is the opening of lenses. Consider the vertical segment
[\mathsf{b},\mathsf{b}\pm\mathrm{i}\delta]
which connects the point
\mathsf{b}
to the boundary
\mathcal{L}_{\pm}^{\nabla}
. This segment splits
\mathcal{S}^{\nabla}_{\pm}
into two regions, and we denote by
\mathcal{G}_{\pm}^{\nabla}
the bounded one amongst these regions. Additionaly, we also consider the region
\mathcal{T}_{\pm}
, which is the region delimited by the contours
\mathcal{L}_{\pm}^{\Delta},\mathcal{L}_{\pm}^{\nabla}
and the horizontal straight line
[\pm\mathrm{i}\delta,\pm\mathrm{i}\delta+\infty]
. These regions are displayed in Figure
10
.

Figure 10
.
The regions used for the transformation
\mathbf{T}\mapsto\mathbf{S}
of the RHP. For this transformation, there is no distinction between the cases
z_{N}(s)<\mathsf{a}
and
z_{N}(s)>\mathsf{a}

Make the transformation

{\mathbf{S}}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}(-2\pi\mathrm{i}N)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathbf{T}(z)\left(\mathbf{I}\pm\dfrac{\ee^{2N\phi(z)}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}N\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)(-2\pi\mathrm{i}N)^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2},&z\in\mathcal{G}^{\nabla}_{\pm},\\
(-2\pi\mathrm{i}N)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathbf{T}(z)(\mp 2\pi\mathrm{i}N)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\ee^{\mp\pi\mathrm{i}Nz\bm{\sigma}_{3}}(-2\pi\mathrm{i}N)^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2},&z\in\mathcal{S}^{\Delta}_{\pm},\\
(-2\pi\mathrm{i}N)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathbf{T}(z)(1-\ee^{\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i}Nz})^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}}(-2\pi\mathrm{i}N)^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2},&z\in\mathcal{T}_{\pm},\\
(-2\pi\mathrm{i}N)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathbf{T}(z)(-2\pi\mathrm{i}N)^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2},&\text{elsewhere}.\end{cases}

Set

\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Gamma_{\mathbf{T}}\cup[\mathsf{b},\mathsf{b}+\mathrm{i}\delta]\cup[\mathsf{b},\mathsf{b}-\mathrm{i}\delta],\quad\mathcal{G}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{G}_{+}^{\Delta}\cup\mathcal{G}_{-}^{\Delta},\quad\mathcal{T}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{T}_{+}\cup\mathcal{T}_{-},

with the orientation of the segments
[\mathsf{b},\mathsf{b}\pm\mathrm{i}\delta]
taken from
\mathsf{b}
to
\mathsf{b}\pm\mathrm{i}\delta
, and the remaining parts of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}
inheriting the orientation from
\Gamma_{\mathbf{T}}
, see Figure
10
.

Using the properties of the
\phi
-function just proved in Proposition
11.4
, we arrive at the following RHP for
\mathbf{S}
.

RHP 11.5
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{S}
with the following properties.

(1)

\mathbf{S}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}
.

(2)

The matrix
\mathbf{S}
has continous boundary values
\mathbf{S}_{\pm}
along
\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}
, satisfying the relation
\mathbf{S}_{+}(z)=\mathbf{S}_{-}(z)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{S}}(z)
,
z\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}
. Recalling (
11.2
), this jump matrix is given by

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{S}}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}-\mathbf{I}-\dfrac{\ee^{N(\phi_{+}(z)+\phi_{-}(z))}}{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}\mathbf{E}_{21},&0<z<\widehat{z}_{N}(s),\\
\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}\mathbf{E}_{12}-\dfrac{1}{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}\mathbf{E}_{21},&\widecheck{z}_{N}(s)<z<\mathsf{b},\\
\mathbf{I}+\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}\ee^{-2N\phi(z)}\mathbf{E}_{12},&z>\mathsf{b},\\
-\mathbf{\ee}^{N(\phi_{+}(z)-\phi_{-}(z)-2\pi\mathrm{i}z)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}-\frac{1}{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}\mathbf{E}_{21},&z\in(\widehat{z}_{N}(s),\widecheck{z}_{N}(s))\;(\text{when }s<0),\\
\frac{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}{\ee^{N(\phi_{+}(z)+\phi_{-}(z))}}\mathbf{E}_{12}-\dfrac{\ee^{N(\phi_{+}(z)+\phi_{-}(z))}}{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}\mathbf{E}_{21},&z\in(\widehat{z}_{N}(s),\widecheck{z}_{N}(s))\;(\text{when }s>0),\end{cases}

which covers the jumps on the real axis,

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{S}}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}+\frac{\ee^{2N\phi(z)}}{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}\frac{1}{1-\ee^{\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i}Nz}}\mathbf{E}_{21},&z\in\partial\mathcal{S}_{\pm}^{\Delta}\cap\partial\mathcal{T}_{\pm},\\
\left(\mathbf{I}+\ee^{-2N(\phi(z)-\pi\mathrm{i}z)}\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}\mathbf{E}_{12}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\ee^{2N\phi(z)}}{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}\frac{1}{1-\ee^{2\pi\mathrm{i}Nz}}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right),&z\in\partial\mathcal{G}_{+}^{\nabla}\cap\partial\mathcal{T}_{+},\\
\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\ee^{2N\phi(z)}}{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}\frac{1}{1-\ee^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}Nz}}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}+\ee^{-2N(\phi(z)+\pi\mathrm{i}z)}\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}\mathbf{E}_{12}\right),&z\in\partial\mathcal{G}_{-}^{\nabla}\cap\partial\mathcal{T}_{-},\end{cases}

which covers the jumps along the contours emanating from
z_{N}(s)
away from the real axis,

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{S}}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}-\frac{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)-2N(\phi(z)\mp\pi\mathrm{i}z)}}{1-\ee^{\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i}Nz}}\mathbf{E}_{12},&z\in[\mathsf{b}\pm\mathrm{i}\delta,\pm\mathrm{i}\delta+\infty),\\
\mathbf{I}+\frac{\ee^{2N\phi(z)}}{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}\mathbf{E}_{21},&z\in(\mathsf{b},\mathsf{b}\pm\mathrm{i}\delta),\end{cases}

which covers the jumps along the contours emanating from
\mathsf{b}
away from the real axis, and finally

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{S}}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}(1-\ee^{\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i}Nz})^{\pm\bm{\sigma}_{3}}+\frac{\ee^{2N\phi(z)}}{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}\mathbf{E}_{21},&z\in\partial\mathcal{S}_{\pm}^{\Delta}\setminus\partial\mathcal{T}_{\pm},\\
(1-\ee^{\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i}z})^{\pm\bm{\sigma}_{3}},&z\in\partial\mathcal{T}_{\pm}\cap[\pm\mathrm{i}\delta,\pm\mathrm{i}\delta+\infty),\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&\dfrac{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)-2N(\phi(z)-\pi\mathrm{i}z)}}{1-\ee^{2\pi\mathrm{i}Nz}}\\
\dfrac{\ee^{2N\phi(z)}}{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}&1-\dfrac{1}{1-\ee^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}Nz}}\end{pmatrix},&z\in\partial\mathcal{G}_{+}^{\nabla}\cap[\mathrm{i}\delta,\mathrm{i}\delta+\infty),\\
\begin{pmatrix}1-\dfrac{1}{1-\ee^{2\pi\mathrm{i}Nz}}&\dfrac{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)-2N(\phi(z)+\pi\mathrm{i}z)}}{1-\ee^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}Nz}}\\
\dfrac{\ee^{2N\phi(z)}}{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}&1\end{pmatrix},&z\in\partial\mathcal{G}_{-}^{\nabla}\cap[-\mathrm{i}\delta,-\mathrm{i}\delta+\infty),\\
\end{cases}

which covers the remaining parts of the jump contour.

(3)

As
z\to\infty
,

\mathbf{S}(z)=\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}).

(4)

As
z\to z_{N}(s)
,

\mathbf{S}(z)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}(1),&\text{if }z_{N}(s)\in\mathbb{R}_{>}\setminus\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\\
\mathcal{O}(1),&\text{if }z_{N}(s)\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\text{ and }z\to z_{N}(s)\text{ along }\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{G},\\
\mathcal{O}\begin{pmatrix}(z-z_{N}(s))^{-1}&1\\
(z-z_{N}(s))^{-1}&1\end{pmatrix},&\text{if }z_{N}(s)\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\text{ and }z\to z_{N}(s)\text{ along }\mathcal{T}.\end{cases}

The next proposition ensures that the jumps of
\mathbf{S}
away from
[0,\mathsf{b}]
are exponentially small as
N\to+\infty
.

Proposition 11.6
.

For
\delta>0
sufficiently small, there exists a constant
\eta>0
such that the function
\phi
satisfies the following properties.

(i)

For
\delta\leq z\leq\mathsf{a}-\delta
, we have
\re\left(\phi_{+}(z)+\phi_{-}(z)\right)\leq-\eta
.

(ii)

For
z\geq\mathsf{b}+\delta
, we have
\re\phi(z)\geq\eta
.

(iii)

For
z\in\partial\mathcal{S}_{\pm}^{\Delta}\setminus(D_{\delta}(\mathsf{a})\cup\mathbb{R})
, we have
\re\phi(z)\leq-\eta
.

(iv)

For
z\in(\partial\mathcal{G}_{\pm}^{\nabla}\cup\partial S_{\pm}^{\nabla})\setminus(D_{\delta}(\mathsf{a})\cup D_{\delta}(\mathsf{b})\cup\mathbb{R})
, we have
\re\phi(z)\leq-\eta
, as well as
\re(\phi(z)\mp\pi\mathrm{i}z)\geq\eta
.

Proof.

With standard arguments, all these properties follow from the properties of the
g
-function as ensured by Proposition
11.2
, and we skip the details.
∎

Thanks to Proposition
11.6
, the jump matrix
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{S}}(z)
decays to the identity matrix as
N\to\infty
, as long as
z
stays within a positive distance from the interval
[0,\mathsf{b}]
. We state this fact as a formal result.

Proposition 11.7
.

There exists
\delta>0
and
\eta>0
such that

\|\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{S}}-\mathbf{I}\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}\setminus([0,b]\cup D_{\delta}(\mathsf{a})\cup D_{\delta}(\mathsf{b})))}=\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta n}),\quad n\to\infty,

uniformly for
s
within any of the regimes from Assumptions
2.7
.

Proof.

The proof follows by inspection, using Proposition
11.6
in each component of
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{S}}
as defined in RHP
11.5
–(2). We skip details.
∎

As usual in the asymptotic analysis of OPs with the RHP approach, we now proceed to the construction of global and local parametrices to handle the jumps of
\mathbf{S}
on
[0,\mathsf{b}]
and near
z_{N}(s)
and
\mathsf{b}
, which are not decaying to the identity uniformly.

11.4.
The global parametrix

As we will see later, the jump matrix for
\mathbf{S}
decays to the identity matrix away from the interval
[0,\mathsf{b}]
. If we ignore these decaying jumps, we are lead to considering the following RHP, the
global parametrix RHP
.

RHP 11.8
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{G}
with the following properties.

(1)

\mathbf{G}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,\mathsf{b}]
.

(2)

\mathbf{G}_{+}(z)=\mathbf{G}_{-}(z)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{G}}(z)
,
z\in(0,\mathsf{b})\setminus\{{z_{N}(s)}\}
, where

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{G}}(z)=\begin{cases}-\mathbf{I},&0<z<{z_{N}(s)},\\
\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}\mathbf{E}_{12}-\dfrac{1}{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}\mathbf{E}_{21},&{z_{N}(s)}<z<\mathsf{b}.\end{cases}

(3)

As
z\to\infty
,

\mathbf{G}(z)=\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}).

(4)

The entries of
\mathbf{G}
are locally integrable or square-integrable near
z=0,{z_{N}(s)},\mathsf{b}
.

The RHP above could have been stated replacing the endpoint
{z_{N}(s)}
by
\mathsf{a}
. However, it turns out convenient to state it this way, to ensure that certain quantities remain bounded later on. Since this global parametrix will only be used in the conclusion of the RHP analysis for values away from
\mathsf{a}
, and
{z_{N}(s)}\to\mathsf{a}
, such distinction does not play a prominent role and, as said, it is only convenient for asymptotic estimates that will be neded later.

To solve for
\mathbf{G}
explicitly, we first transform its jumps to constant jumps, seeking for a solution of the form

\mathbf{G}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\ee^{-\mathsf{g}(\infty)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{H}(z)\ee^{\mathsf{g}(z)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}.

This way, we enforce
\mathsf{g}
to solve the scalar RHP:

RHP 11.9
.

Find a function
\mathsf{g}:\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,\mathsf{b}]\to\mathbb{C}
with the following properties.

(1)

\mathsf{g}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,\mathsf{b}]
,

(2)

\mathsf{g}_{+}(z)+\mathsf{g}_{-}(z)=-\mathsf{E}(z)-\log\sigma(z)
for
{z_{N}(s)}<z<\mathsf{b}
,

and

\mathsf{g}_{+}(z)-\mathsf{g}_{-}(z)=-\pi\mathrm{i}
for
0<z<{z_{N}(s)}
.

(3)

\ee^{\mathsf{g}(z)}
has integrable singularities near
z=0
, and it is bounded near
z={z_{N}(s)},\mathsf{b}
.

(4)

As
z\to\infty,

\mathsf{g}(z)=\mathsf{g}(\infty)+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1})
, for some finite constant
\mathsf{g}(\infty)\in\mathbb{C}
.

and
\mathbf{H}
should solve the following RHP:

RHP 11.10
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{H}
with the following properties.

(1)

\mathbf{H}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus[{z_{N}(s)},\mathsf{b}]
,

(2)

\mathbf{H}_{+}(z)=\mathbf{H}_{-}(z)(\mathbf{E}_{12}-\mathbf{E}_{21})
,
{z_{N}(s)}<z<\mathsf{b}
.

(3)

\mathbf{H}
has square-integrable singularities at the endpoints
z={z_{N}(s)},\mathsf{b}
,

(4)

\mathbf{H}(z)=\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1})
as
z\to\infty
.

Observe that
\sigma
is strictly positive over the real axis, so its real logarithm as used above is well defined.

11.4.1.
Solution of the RHP for H

The solution
\mathbf{H}
is found in a standard way: after diagonalizing the jump matrix
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{H}}
, solving the RHP for
\mathbf{H}
is essentially reduced to solving a scalar RHP, see
[
41
, Section 7.3]
for details. It takes the form

(11.4)

\mathbf{H}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{U}_{0}^{-1}\,\mathsf{m}(z)^{\sigma_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0},\quad\mathsf{m}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{z-{z_{N}(s)}}{z-\mathsf{b}},\quad\mathbf{U}_{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathrm{i}\\
\mathrm{i}&1\end{pmatrix},

with the principal branch of the root. In particular, this function satisfies

\mathsf{m}(z)^{1/4}_{+}=\mathrm{i}\mathsf{m}(z)^{1/4}_{-},\quad{z_{N}(s)}<z<\mathsf{b}.

For later use, we also record that
\mathbf{H}
satisfies the identities

(11.5)

\mathbf{H}(z)=\mathbf{U}_{0}\mathsf{m}(z)^{-\sigma_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}^{-1}\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma_{1}\mathbf{H}(z)\sigma_{1}=\mathbf{U}_{0}\mathsf{m}(z)^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}^{-1}.

11.4.2.
Solution of the scalar RHP

The scalar Riemann-Hilbert problem for
\mathsf{g}
is solved with standard methods, we briefly outline them.
First, make the ansatz that
\mathsf{g}=\mathsf{g}_{1}+\mathsf{g}_{2}
, where
\mathsf{g}_{1}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,{z_{N}(s)}]
,
\mathsf{g}_{2}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus[{z_{N}(s)},\mathsf{b}]
, and both are
\mathcal{O}(1)
as
z\to\infty
. This way, the jump conditions for
\mathsf{g}
translate into

\mathsf{g}_{1,+}(z)-\mathsf{g}_{1,-}(z)=-\pi\mathrm{i},\quad 0<z<{z_{N}(s)},

and

\mathsf{g}_{2,+}(z)+\mathsf{g}_{2,-}(z)=-\mathsf{E}(z)-\log\sigma(z)-2\mathsf{g}_{1}(z),\quad{z_{N}(s)}<z<\mathsf{b}.

The RHP for
\mathsf{g}_{1}
can be solved using Plemelj’s formula (or by inspection) and yields

\mathsf{g}_{1}(z)=\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{z}{z-{z_{N}(s)}}\right),\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,{z_{N}(s)}],

where the branch of the root is the principal one, so that
\left(\log\left(\frac{z}{z-{z_{N}(s)}}\right)\right)_{\pm}=\log\left|\frac{z}{z-{z_{N}(s)}}\right|\mp\pi\mathrm{i}
for
0<z<{z_{N}(s)}
.

Having
\mathsf{g}_{1}
at hand, we now solve for
\mathsf{g}_{2}
. In contrast with
\mathsf{g}_{1}
whose jump comes as a difference, the jump for
\mathsf{g}_{2}
comes as a sum. But multiplying
\mathsf{g}_{2}
by
((z-{z_{N}(s)})(z-\mathsf{b}))^{1/2}
(with principal branch), we turn the jump for
\mathsf{g}_{2}
from a sum into a difference, and solve the resulting RHP once again using Plemelj’s formula. The final outcome is then that

\mathsf{g}(z)=\frac{((z-{z_{N}(s)})(z-\mathsf{b}))^{1/2}}{2\pi}\int_{{z_{N}(s)}}^{\mathsf{b}}\frac{\mathsf{E}(x)+\log\sigma(x)+\log\left(\frac{x}{x-{z_{N}(s)}}\right)}{\sqrt{(\mathsf{b}-x)(x-{z_{N}(s)})}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{x-z}\\
+\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{z}{z-{z_{N}(s)}}\right),\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,\mathsf{b}],

where
(\cdot)^{1/2}
is the principal branch of the square root, and
\sqrt{\cdot}
is the standard positive root for positive real numbers. This solution satisfies

(11.6)

\mathsf{g}(z)=\mathsf{g}(\infty)+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}),\,z\to\infty,\quad\text{with}\quad\mathsf{g}(\infty)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{{z_{N}(s)}}^{\mathsf{b}}\frac{\mathsf{E}(x)+\log\sigma(x)+\log\left(\frac{x}{x-{z_{N}(s)}}\right)}{\sqrt{(\mathsf{b}-x)(x-{z_{N}(s)})}}\mathrm{d}x.

From the procedure outlined above, we are ensured that
\mathsf{g}
satisfies the conditions of the RHP
11.9
, except that it is not immediate whether it satisfies the condition RHP
11.9
–(3). To verify that condition, we re-express
\mathsf{g}
. When
z\to 0
, it is immediate that

\mathsf{g}(z)=\frac{1}{2}\log z+\mathcal{O}(1),\quad\text{and therefore}\quad\ee^{\mathsf{g}(z)}=z^{1/2}(\eta+\mathit{o}(1)),\;z\to 0,

for some constant
\eta\neq 0
.

As for the remaining endpoints, a residue calculation shows that

\int_{{z_{N}(s)}}^{\mathsf{b}}\frac{\log\frac{x}{x-{z_{N}(s)}}}{\sqrt{(\mathsf{b}-x)(x-{z_{N}(s)})}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{x-z}\\
\begin{aligned} &=-\pi\mathop{\rm Res}_{s=z}\frac{\log\left(\frac{s}{s-{z_{N}(s)}}\right)}{((s-{z_{N}(s)})(s-\mathsf{b}))^{1/2}(s-z)}+\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}}\int_{0}^{{z_{N}(s)}}\frac{\left(\log\left(\frac{s}{s-{z_{N}(s)}}\right)\right)_{+}-\left(\log\left(\frac{s}{s-{z_{N}(s)}}\right)\right)_{-}}{((s-{z_{N}(s)})(s-\mathsf{b}))^{1/2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s-z}\\
&=-\frac{\pi\log\left(\frac{z}{z-{z_{N}(s)}}\right)}{((z-{z_{N}(s)})(z-\mathsf{b}))^{1/2}}+\pi\int_{0}^{{z_{N}(s)}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{({z_{N}(s)}-s)(\mathsf{b}-s)}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s-z},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,\mathsf{b}].\end{aligned}

We emphasize that in the last equality we also used that
((s-{z_{N}(s)})(s-\mathsf{b}))^{1/2}=\sqrt{({z_{N}(s)}-s)(\mathsf{b}-s)}
for
0<s<{z_{N}(s)}
. Using this identity, we simplify
\mathsf{g}
to

(11.7)

\mathsf{g}(z)=\frac{((z-{z_{N}(s)})(z-\mathsf{b}))^{1/2}}{2\pi}\int_{{z_{N}(s)}}^{\mathsf{b}}\frac{\mathsf{E}(x)+\log\sigma(x)}{\sqrt{(\mathsf{b}-x)(x-{z_{N}(s)})}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{x-z}\\
+\frac{((z-{z_{N}(s)})(z-\mathsf{b}))^{1/2}}{2}\int_{0}^{{z_{N}(s)}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{(\mathsf{b}-x)({z_{N}(s)}-x)}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{x-z},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,\mathsf{b}].

The remaining integrals on the right-hand side are Cauchy transforms of densities that blow up as square root at the endpoints
\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}
and, therefore, the integrals too blow up as square roots. This way it follows that
\mathsf{g}
is bounded as
z\to\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}
.

For later purposes, it is convenient to split the parts of
\mathsf{g}
that depend on
s
in a nontrivial way. Recall that
\sigma(x)=\sigma(x{\,|\,}s)
depends on
s
, which gives rise to the dependence of
\mathsf{g}
on
s
as well. Set

\mathsf{g}_{N}(z,s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{((z-{z_{N}(s)})(z-\mathsf{b}))^{1/2}}{2\pi}\int_{{z_{N}(s)}}^{\mathsf{b}}\frac{\log\sigma(x{\,|\,}s)}{\sqrt{(\mathsf{b}-x)(x-{z_{N}(s)})}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{x-z},

as well as

(11.8)

\mathsf{g}_{0}(z)=\frac{((z-{z_{N}(s)})(z-\mathsf{b}))^{1/2}}{2}\\
\times\left(\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{z_{N}(s)}}^{\mathsf{b}}\frac{\mathsf{E}(x)}{\sqrt{(\mathsf{b}-x)(x-{z_{N}(s)})}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{x-z}+\int_{0}^{{z_{N}(s)}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{(\mathsf{b}-x)({z_{N}(s)}-x)}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{x-z}\right).

This way, the decomposition

(11.9)

\mathsf{g}(z)=\mathsf{g}_{N}(z,s)+\mathsf{g}_{0}(z)

follows, and we now provide estimates for each of these terms, as well as for
\mathsf{g}(\infty)
, as
N\to\infty
.

The factor
\mathsf{g}_{0}
and
\mathsf{g}(\infty)
still depend on
N
through
\mathsf{E}
, as well as through the endpoint
{z_{N}(s)}
of the interval of integration. Likewise,
\mathsf{g}(\infty)
depends on
\mathsf{E}
and
{z_{N}(s)}
, and also in the factor
\sigma
which remains bounded in the interval of integration in (
11.6
). A simple argument then shows that

(11.10)

\mathsf{g}_{0}(z)=\mathcal{O}(1),\quad\text{as well as}\quad\mathsf{g}(\infty)=\mathcal{O}(1)\quad\text{as }N\to\infty,

where the estimate for
\mathsf{g}_{0}
is valid uniformly for
z
within compacts of
\mathbb{C}
that do not contain the points
\mathsf{b}
and
0
, and both error terms are uniform for
s
within any of the regimes. Furthermore,
\mathsf{g}_{0}
is analytic on both the upper and lower half planes, and a direct calculation shows that

(11.11)

\mathsf{g}_{0,+}(z)-\mathsf{g}_{0,-}(z)=-\pi\mathrm{i},\;0<z<{z_{N}(s)},\quad\mathsf{g}_{0,+}(z)+\mathsf{g}_{0,-}(z)=-\mathsf{E}(z),\;{z_{N}(s)}<z<\mathsf{b}.

From standard properties of Cauchy transforms we also obtain

(11.12)

\mathsf{g}_{0}(z)=\mathcal{O}(1),\quad z\to{z_{N}(s)}.

Next, we provide an estimate on
\mathsf{g}_{N}(z,s)
, which we encode in the next lemma.

Lemma 11.11
.

For
\beta>-1
, set

\mathsf{L}_{\beta}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}v^{\beta}\log(1+\ee^{-v})\mathrm{d}v.

Suppose that a function
f
is smooth on a real neighborhood of the interval
[\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}]
. Then the estimate

(11.13)

\int_{{z_{N}(s)}}^{\mathsf{b}}(x-{z_{N}(s)})^{\beta}f(x)\log\sigma(x{\,|\,}s)\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{f({z_{N}(s)})}{t^{1+\beta}N^{1+\beta}}\left(\mathsf{L}_{\beta}+\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})\right)

holds true uniformly for
s
within any of the regimes from Assumptions
2.7
.

Proof.

Let us write the integral on the left-hand side of (
11.13
) as

f({z_{N}(s)})\int_{{z_{N}(s)}}^{\mathsf{b}}(x-{z_{N}(s)})^{\beta}\log\sigma(x{\,|\,}s)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{{z_{N}(s)}}^{\mathsf{b}}(x-{z_{N}(s)})^{\beta}\log\sigma(x{\,|\,}s)(f(x)-f({z_{N}(s)}))\mathrm{d}x.

From the smoothness of
f
, we obtain that
|f(x)-f({z_{N}(s)})|\leq M(x-{z_{N}(s)})
for some
M
independent of
s,t
, and every
x
in the interval of integration. A standard argument
a là
Watson’s Lemma for exponential integrals shows that

\int_{{z_{N}(s)}}^{\mathsf{b}}(x-{z_{N}(s)})^{\nu}\log\sigma(x{\,|\,}s)\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{t^{1+\nu}N^{1+\nu}}\mathsf{L}_{\nu}+\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta Nt(\mathsf{b}-{z_{N}(s)})}),

and the lemma follows from this estimate applied to both
\nu=\beta
and
\nu=\beta+1
.
∎

With an application of Lemma
11.11
, we obtain the estimate

(11.14)

\mathsf{g}_{N}(z,s)=-\frac{1}{t^{1/2}N^{1/2}}\frac{\mathsf{L}_{-1/2}}{2\pi\sqrt{\mathsf{b}-{z_{N}(s)}}}\left(\frac{z-\mathsf{b}}{z-{z_{N}(s)}}\right)^{1/2}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})\right)

where the error term is uniform for
z
in compacts that do not contain
\mathsf{a}
and
\mathsf{b}
, and also uniform for
s
in any of the regimes from Assumptions
2.7
.

As a consequence, we obtain that the estimate

(11.15)

\mathsf{g}(z)=\mathsf{g}_{0}(z)+\mathcal{O}(N^{-1/2}),

is valid uniformly for
z
in compacts of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b},0\}
, and also uniformly for
s
within any of the regimes from Assumptions
2.7
.

11.4.3.
Final form of the global parametrix

Summarizing, the solution to the RHP
11.8
takes the form

(11.16)

\mathbf{G}(z)=\ee^{-\mathsf{g}(\infty)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{U}_{0}^{-1}\mathsf{m}(z)^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\ee^{\mathsf{g}(z)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}=\ee^{-\mathsf{g}(\infty)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{H}(z)\ee^{\mathsf{g}(z)\bm{\sigma}_{3}},

with
\mathbf{H}
as in (
11.4
),
\mathsf{g}
as in (
11.7
), or alternatively as in (
11.9
), and
\mathsf{g}(\infty)
as in (
11.6
). For later convenience, we book-keep the error term when moving between
\mathsf{g}
and
\mathsf{g}_{0}
in this formula. We keep denoting by
U_{\mathsf{a}}
a small neighborhood of
\mathsf{a}
, sufficiently small such that it does not contain
0
and
\mathsf{b}
in its closure.

The following observations will be crucial for later. First off, fix a neighborhood
U_{\mathsf{a}}
of
\mathsf{a}
that does not contain
\mathsf{b}
. From (
11.15
),

\ee^{\mathsf{g}(z)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}\left(N^{-1/2}\right)\right)\ee^{\mathsf{g}_{0}(z)\bm{\sigma}_{3}},\quad N\to\infty,

uniformly for
s,t
as in Assumptions
2.7
, and also uniformly for
z
in compacts of
U_{\mathsf{a}}\setminus\{\mathsf{a}\}
. The matrix
\mathbf{H}
is bounded for
z
on compacts of
U_{\mathsf{a}}\setminus\{\mathsf{a}\}
, and it is independent of
s,N,t
. So it can be commuted with the error above. Likewise,
\mathsf{g}(\infty)
remains bounded as
N\to\infty
(see (
11.10
)), and we obtain

(11.17)

\mathbf{G}(z)=\ee^{-\mathsf{g}(\infty)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{H}(z)\ee^{\mathsf{g}(z)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}\left(N^{-1/2}\right)\right)\ee^{-\mathsf{g}(\infty)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{H}(z)\ee^{\mathsf{g}_{0}(z)\bm{\sigma}_{3}},\quad N\to\infty,

again valid uniformly for
s,t
as in Assumptions
2.7
and uniformly for
z
in compacts of
U_{\mathsf{a}}\setminus\{\mathsf{a}\}
.

Second, expressing
\mathsf{g}=\mathsf{g}_{1}+\mathsf{g}_{2}
, we may write

(11.18)

\mathbf{G}(z)=\ee^{-\mathsf{g}(\infty)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathcal{O}(1)\ee^{\mathsf{g}_{1}(z)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}=\mathcal{O}(1)z^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2},\quad N\to\infty,

where this expansion is valid uniformly for
z
in closed sets of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}\}
, and it may be differentiated term by term.

11.5.
Construction of the local parametrix near
z=\mathsf{a}

The local parametrix near
z=\mathsf{a}
is the solution to a local RHP with the same jumps as
\mathbf{S}
in a neighborhood
U_{\mathsf{a}}
of
z=\mathsf{a}
, and matching
\mathbf{G}
as
N\to\infty
for values
z\in\partial U_{\mathsf{a}}
. This neighborhood
U_{\mathsf{a}}
of
\mathsf{a}
will be made as small as needed, it is independent of
N
and
s
, but it is not necessarily a disk. Since
z_{N}(s)\to\mathsf{a}
as
N\to\infty
, we assume without loss of generality that
N
is sufficiently large so that
\widehat{z}_{N}(s),\widecheck{z}_{N}(s)\in U_{\mathsf{a}}
.

We assume that the neighborhood
U_{\mathsf{a}}
is sufficiently small so that
|z-\mathsf{a}|<\varepsilon
for every
z\in U_{\mathsf{a}}
, with
2\varepsilon<\min\{\delta,|\mathsf{a}-\mathsf{b}|,|\mathsf{a}|\}
. This ensures that
\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}\cap U_{\mathsf{a}}
consists of subarcs of the six contours of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}
that emanate from
z_{N}(s)
, see Figure
11
.

Figure 11
.
The region
U_{\mathsf{a}}
used for the local parametrix near
\mathsf{a}
, and the jump contours for this local parametrix in solid inside
U_{\mathsf{a}}
, compare with Figure
10
. Although not displayed, the point
\mathsf{a}
is inside
U_{\mathsf{a}}
, either to the left or to the right of
z_{N}(s)
, depending on whether
s<0
or
s>0
, respectively.

The initial local parametrix problem is the following. We seek for a matrix
\mathbf{P}
which solves the following RHP.

RHP 11.12
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{P}
with the following properties.

(1)

\mathbf{P}
is analytic on
U_{\mathsf{a}}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}
.

(2)

\mathbf{P}_{+}(z)=\mathbf{P}_{-}(z)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{S}}(z)
,
z\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}\cap U_{\mathsf{a}}

(3)

For
z\in\partial U_{\mathsf{a}}
,

\mathbf{P}(z)=(\mathbf{I}+\mathit{o}(1))\mathbf{G}(z),\quad N\to\infty.

(4)

As
z\to z_{N}(s)
,

\mathbf{P}(z)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}(1),&\text{if }z_{N}(s)\in\mathbb{R}_{>}\setminus\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\\
\mathcal{O}(1),&\text{if }z_{N}(s)\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\text{ and }z\to z_{N}(s)\text{ along }\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{G},\\
\mathcal{O}\begin{pmatrix}(z-z_{N}(s))^{-1}&1\\
(z-z_{N}(s))^{-1}&1\end{pmatrix},&\text{if }z_{N}(s)\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\text{ and }z\to z_{N}(s)\text{ along }\mathcal{T}.\end{cases}

The first step is to remove the
\phi
-function from the jumps of
\mathbf{P}
. For that, introduce

(11.19)

\widetilde{\phi}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\pi\mathrm{i}(z-1)-\phi(z),&\im z>0,\\
-\pi\mathrm{i}(z-1)-\phi(z),&\im z<0.\end{cases}

Proposition 11.13
.

The function
\widetilde{\phi}
satisfies

\widetilde{\phi}_{+}(z)-\widetilde{\phi}_{-}(z)=0,\quad\mathsf{a}-\varepsilon<z<\mathsf{a},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widetilde{\phi}_{+}(z)+\widetilde{\phi}_{-}(z)=0,\quad\mathsf{a}<z<\mathsf{a}+\varepsilon.

Furthermore, for the constant
\mathsf{c}_{0}>0
from (
2.26
), it satisfies

\widetilde{\phi}(z)=\frac{2\mathsf{c}_{0}}{3}(\mathsf{a}-z)^{3/2}(1+\mathcal{O}(z-\mathsf{a})),\quad z\to\mathsf{a},\;z\in U_{\mathsf{a}}\setminus(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}+\varepsilon).

Proof.

The proof is essentially a consequence of the relation of
\widetilde{\phi}
with
\phi
from (
11.19
) and Proposition
11.4
. The exact value
\frac{2\pi\mathsf{c}_{0}}{3}
of the constant upfront is obtained from (
11.3
) and (
2.26
).
∎

The jumps of
\mathbf{S}
near
z=\mathsf{a}
are then expressed in terms of
\widetilde{\phi}
as

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{S}}(z)=\begin{cases}-\mathbf{I}-\dfrac{\ee^{-N(\widetilde{\phi}_{+}(x)+\widetilde{\phi}_{-}(x))}}{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}\mathbf{E}_{21},&0<z<\widehat{z}_{N}(s),\\
\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}\mathbf{E}_{12}-\dfrac{\ee^{-\mathsf{E}(z)}}{\sigma(z)}\mathbf{E}_{21},&z>\widecheck{z}_{N}(s),\\
-\ee^{-N(\widetilde{\phi}_{+}(z)-\widetilde{\phi}_{-}(z))\bm{\sigma}_{3}}-\frac{1}{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}\mathbf{E}_{21},&z\in(\widehat{z}_{N}(s),\widecheck{z}_{N}(s))\;(\text{if }s<0),\\
\frac{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}{\ee^{-N(\widetilde{\phi}_{+}(z)+\widetilde{\phi}_{-}(z))}}\mathbf{E}_{12}-\frac{\ee^{-N(\widetilde{\phi}_{+}(z)+\widetilde{\phi}_{-}(z))}}{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}\mathbf{E}_{21},&z\in(\widehat{z}_{N}(s),\widecheck{z}_{N}(s))\;(\text{if }s>0),\\
\end{cases}

which covers the jumps on
U_{\mathsf{a}}\cap\mathbb{R}
, and

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{S}}(z)=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}-\frac{\ee^{-2N\widetilde{\phi}(z)}}{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}\frac{1}{1-\ee^{\mp 2\pi\mathrm{i}Nz}}\mathbf{E}_{21},&z\in\partial\mathcal{S}_{\pm}^{\Delta}\cap\partial\mathcal{T}_{\pm},\\
\left(\mathbf{I}+\ee^{2N\widetilde{\phi}(z)}\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}\mathbf{E}_{12}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\ee^{-2N\widetilde{\phi}(z)}}{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}\frac{1}{1-\ee^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}Nz}}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right),&z\in\partial\mathcal{S}_{+}^{\nabla}\cap\partial\mathcal{T}_{+},\\
\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\ee^{-2N\widetilde{\phi}(z)}}{\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}}\frac{1}{1-\ee^{2\pi\mathrm{i}Nz}}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}+\ee^{2N\widetilde{\phi}(z)}\sigma(z)\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)}\mathbf{E}_{12}\right),&z\in\partial\mathcal{S}_{-}^{\nabla}\cap\partial\mathcal{T}_{-},\end{cases}

which covers the remaining jumps on
U_{\mathsf{a}}
.

We now transform

(11.20)

\mathbf{L}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{P}(z)\ee^{(N\widetilde{\phi}(z)+\mathsf{E}(z)/2\pm\pi\mathrm{i}/2)\bm{\sigma}_{3}},\quad z\in U_{\mathsf{a}}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}},\quad\pm\im z>0.

Strictly speaking, this transformation
\mathbf{L}
is not necessary, as we could (and in fact will) construct
\mathbf{P}
directly. However, for the sake of clarity we prefer to proceed the discussion with
\mathbf{L}
instead, as its jump connects to the jump of the model problem in a more clear manner.
Then
\mathbf{L}
should solve the following problem.

RHP 11.14
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{L}
with the following properties.

(1)

\mathbf{L}
is analytic on
U_{\mathsf{a}}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}
.

(2)

\mathbf{L}_{+}(z)=\mathbf{L}_{-}(z)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{L}}(z)
,
z\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}\cap U_{\mathsf{a}}
, with

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{L}}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}-\dfrac{1}{\sigma(z)}\mathbf{E}_{21},&z\in(0,z_{N}(s))\cap U_{\mathsf{a}},\\
\sigma(z)\mathbf{E}_{12}-\dfrac{1}{\sigma(z)}\mathbf{E}_{21},&z\in(z_{N}(s),+\infty)\cap U_{\mathsf{a}},\end{cases}

and

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{L}}(z)=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{\sigma(z)}\frac{1}{1-\ee^{\mp 2\pi\mathrm{i}Nz}}\mathbf{E}_{21},&z\in\partial\mathcal{S}_{\pm}^{\Delta}\cap\partial\mathcal{T}_{\pm},\\
\left(\mathbf{I}-\sigma(z)\mathbf{E}_{12}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{\sigma(z)}\frac{1}{1-\ee^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}Nz}}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right),&z\in\partial\mathcal{S}_{+}^{\nabla}\cap\partial\mathcal{T}_{+},\\
\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{\sigma(z)}\frac{1}{1-\ee^{2\pi\mathrm{i}Nz}}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}-\sigma(z)\mathbf{E}_{12}\right),&z\in\partial\mathcal{S}_{-}^{\nabla}\cap\partial\mathcal{T}_{-},\end{cases}

(3)

For
z\in\partial U_{\mathsf{a}}
with
\pm\im z>0
,

\mathbf{L}(z)=(\mathbf{I}+\mathit{o}(1))\mathbf{G}(z)\ee^{(N\widetilde{\phi}(z)+\mathsf{E}(z)/2\pm\pi\mathrm{i}/2)\bm{\sigma}_{3}},\quad N\to\infty.

(4)

As
z\to z_{N}(s)
,

\mathbf{L}(z)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}(1),&\text{if }z_{N}(s)\in\mathbb{R}_{>}\setminus\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\\
\mathcal{O}(1),&\text{if }z_{N}(s)\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\text{ and }z\to z_{N}(s)\text{ along }\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{G},\\
\mathcal{O}\begin{pmatrix}(z-z_{N}(s))^{-1}&1\\
(z-z_{N}(s))^{-1}&1\end{pmatrix},&\text{if }z_{N}(s)\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\text{ and }z\to z_{N}(s)\text{ along }\mathcal{T}.\end{cases}

We will now construct this matrix
\mathbf{L}
, matching it with the model RHP
6.10
. Several ingredients will be needed, namely

(i)

A change of coordinates
z\mapsto\zeta
, mapping
U_{\mathsf{a}}
to a new
\zeta
-plane, and with a “blown-up” effect that identifies
\partial U_{\mathsf{a}}
in the
z
-plane with the point at
\infty
in the
\zeta
-plane.

(ii)

A pair of admissible functions
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{F})=(\mathsf{P}_{0},\mathsf{F}_{0})
in the sense of Definition
6.1
.

(iii)

An identification of
z_{N}(s)
with the central point
\lambda={\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
used for the fomulation of RHP
6.10
.

(iv)

We also need to make sure that
\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}\cap U_{\mathsf{a}}
, under the change
z\mapsto\zeta
, matches the jump contour
\Gamma^{\lambda}=\Gamma^{\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
.

(v)

Ingredients (i)–(iv) just outlined will ensure that, under
z\mapsto\zeta
, conditions RHP
11.14
–(1),(2),(4) match with conditions RHP
6.10
–(1),(2),(4). To conclude RHP
11.14
–(3) matching it with RHP
6.10
–(3) , we will also need to construct a matrix-valued function
\mathbf{A}
with specific properties (the so-called analytic prefactor).

We now proceed to accomplish each step above. For what comes next, we identify
\tau=N^{1/3}
. Under this identification, the regimes from Assumptions
2.7
are equivalent to the ones from Definition
7.1
.

For (i), we will need a conformal map constructed in a canonical way, as given by the next proposition.

Proposition 11.15
.

The function

(11.21)

\varphi(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\left(\frac{3}{2}\widetilde{\phi}(z)\right)^{2/3}

is a conformal map from a neighborhood of
\mathsf{a}
to a neighborhood of the origin, with an expansion of the form

(11.22)

\varphi(z)=-\mathsf{c}_{\varphi}(z-\mathsf{a})(1+\mathcal{O}(z-\mathsf{a})),\quad z\to\mathsf{a},\quad\mathsf{c}_{\varphi}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\left(\mathsf{c}_{0}\right)^{2/3}>0,

and where
\mathsf{c}_{0}>0
is as in (
2.26
).

Proof.

The result is a consequence of Proposition
11.13
.
∎

Remark 11.16
.

The constant
\mathsf{c}_{\varphi}
coincides with the constant
\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{V}}
introduced earlier in (
2.34
). But from now on we prefer to adopt the notation
\mathsf{c}_{\varphi}
, to make it transparent that this constant emerges from the local expansion of the conformal map
\varphi
.

To accomplish (i), we now set

(11.23)

\zeta=N^{2/3}\varphi(z),\qquad\text{so that}\quad N\widetilde{\phi}(z)=\frac{2}{3}\zeta^{3/2},\quad\text{and}\quad\zeta>0\text{ for }z<\mathsf{a}.

Thanks to Proposition
11.15
,
z\mapsto\zeta
constructed this way is a bona fide change of coordinates.

Next, to accomplish (ii) we set

\mathsf{P}_{0}(w)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=-t(\varphi^{-1}(w)-\mathsf{a})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{F}_{0}(w)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=-2\pi\varphi^{-1}(w)+2\pi\mathsf{a}.

These functions are defined in terms of the inverse conformal map
\varphi^{-1}
, and therefore are analytic in a neighborhood of the origin and satisfy

\mathsf{P}_{0}(w)=\frac{t}{\mathsf{c}_{\varphi}}w(1+\mathcal{O}(w)),\quad\mathsf{F}_{0}(w)=\frac{2\pi}{\mathsf{c}_{\varphi}}w+\mathcal{O}(w^{2}),\quad w\to 0.

Hence, the pair
(\mathsf{P}_{0},\mathsf{F}_{0})
is admissible in the sense of Definition
6.1
.
The functions
\mathsf{P}_{\tau}
and
\mathsf{F}_{\tau}
from (
6.4
) are identified with the functions
\mathsf{P}_{N}
and
\mathsf{F}_{N}
given explicitly by

(11.24)

\mathsf{P}_{N}(\zeta)=\mathsf{P}_{N}(\zeta{\,|\,}s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=-\frac{s}{N^{1/3}}+N^{2/3}\mathsf{P}_{0}\left(\frac{\zeta}{N^{2/3}}\right),\quad\mathsf{F}_{N}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=-2\pi\mathsf{a}N^{2/3}+N^{2/3}\mathsf{F}_{0}\left(\frac{\zeta}{N^{2/3}}\right),

so that under the change of coordinates
z\mapsto\zeta=\zeta(z)

(11.25)

\sigma(z{\,|\,}s)=1+\ee^{-Nt(z-{z_{N}(s)})}=1+\ee^{N^{1/3}\mathsf{P}_{N}(\zeta)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\ee^{2\pi\mathrm{i}Nz}=\ee^{-\mathrm{i}N^{1/3}\mathsf{F}_{N}(\zeta)}.

With these identifications, we have the correspondence of parameters
\tau=N^{1/3}
as previously said,
s
in this section remains the same as in the previous sections, and the values
\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{u}
from Definition
6.1
and (
6.4
) are

(11.26)

\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{P}}=\frac{t}{\mathsf{c}_{\varphi}},\quad\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{F}}=\frac{2\pi}{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{\varphi}}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{u}=2\pi\mathsf{a}.

This concludes the completion of ingredient (ii).

Still under the correspondence
z=z(\zeta)\mapsto\zeta=\zeta(z)
, and taking into account the definition of
z_{N}(s)
from Proposition
10.3
,

(11.27)

{z_{N}(s)}=z({\zeta_{N}(s)}),\qquad\text{for the value }{\zeta_{N}(s)}={\zeta_{\tau}(s)}=N^{2/3}\varphi({z_{N}(s)})\text{ for which }\quad\mathsf{P}_{N}({\zeta_{N}(s)})=0.

Hence,
{\zeta_{N}(s)}
as above is identified with
\lambda={\zeta_{\tau}(s)}
from Proposition
6.3
for
\mathsf{P}_{\tau}=\mathsf{P}_{N}
, and ingredient (iii) is completed.

Ingredient (iv) can be accomplished by simply making sure that the intersection of the boundaries
\partial\mathcal{G}^{\nabla}_{\pm},\partial\mathcal{S}^{\Delta}_{\pm}
of the lenses with
U_{\mathsf{a}}
are contained in the image of
\Gamma^{\lambda={\zeta_{\tau}(s)}}
under
\varphi
.

For ingredient (v), recall that the matrix
\mathbf{H}
and the function
\mathsf{g}_{0}
were defined in (
11.4
) and (
11.8
), respectively. For
z
in a neighborhood of
z=\mathsf{a}
, we introduce

\mathbf{A}_{0}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\ee^{-\mathsf{g}(\infty)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{H}(z)\ee^{(\mathsf{E}(z)+2\mathsf{g}_{0}(z)\pm\pi\mathrm{i})\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathbf{U}_{0}^{-1}\left(\varphi(z)-\varphi({z_{N}(s)})\right)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4},\quad\pm\im z>0,

and

(11.28)

\mathbf{A}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{A}_{0}(z)N^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/6},\quad\pm\im z>0.

Proposition 11.17
.

The matrix-valued functions
\mathbf{A}_{0}
and
\mathbf{A}
are analytic on a neighborhood of
z=\mathsf{a}
. Furthermore,

\mathbf{A}_{0}(z)=\mathcal{O}(1)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{A}_{0}^{\prime}(z)=\mathcal{O}(1)\quad\text{as}\quad N\to\infty,

where the error terms are uniform for
z
in a neighborhood of
z=\mathsf{a}
.

Proof.

A routine calculation using the jumps for
\mathbf{H}
and
\mathsf{g}_{0}
(recall for instance (
11.11
)) shows that the jump
\mathbf{A}_{0,-}(z)^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{0,+}(z)
along each of the intervals
({z_{N}(s)}-\delta,{z_{N}(s)})
and
({z_{N}(s)},{z_{N}(s)}+\delta)
is the identity matrix. This means that
\mathbf{A}_{0}
is in fact analytic across each of these intervals, and as such it has an isolated singularity at
z={z_{N}(s)}
. Again from the very definition of
\mathbf{A}_{0}
and (
11.12
), we then verify that this singularity is in fact removable. This shows the analyticity of
\mathbf{A}_{0}
, and the analyticity of
\mathbf{A}
follows from it.

The claimed boundedness for
\mathbf{A}_{0}
follows from its expression and the fact that all the terms involved are bounded in
N
, and the boundedness of its derivative then follows from Cauchy’s integral formula.
∎

Finally, with all these notations and identifications, we are ready to obtain
\mathbf{L}
or, equivalently,
\mathbf{P}
from (
11.20
).

Theorem 11.18
.

The matrix-valued function

(11.29)

\mathbf{P}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{A}(z)\bm{\sigma}_{1}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}(\tau^{2}\varphi(z))\bm{\sigma}_{1}\ee^{-(N\widetilde{\phi}(z)\pm\pi\mathrm{i}/2+\mathsf{E}(z)/2)\bm{\sigma}_{3}},\quad\pm\im z>0,\quad z\in U_{\mathsf{a}},\quad\tau=N^{1/3},

solves RHP
11.12
, with RHP
11.12
–(3) being improved to

(11.30)

\mathbf{P}(z)=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(N^{-1/3})\right)\mathbf{G}(z),\quad N\to\infty,

uniformly for
z\in\partial U_{\mathsf{a}}
, and also uniformly for
s
and
t
satisfying Assumptions
2.7
.

Proof.

With the correspondence (
11.24
)–(
11.26
) in mind, and stressing that the conformal map
z\mapsto\zeta
has a rotation effect by
\pi
(see (
11.22
)), the jump matrix
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{L}}
writes as

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{L}}(z)=\sigma_{1}\mathbf{J}_{\tau}(\zeta)^{-1}\sigma_{1},

where we recall that
\mathbf{J}_{\tau}
is precisely the jump matrix for the model RHP
6.10
. Since
\mathbf{A}
is analytic, and accounting for the relation (
11.20
) and the RHP
6.10
for
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
, this is enough to show that
\mathbf{P}
determined from (
11.29
) indeed solves RHP
11.12
–(1),(2),(4).

It remains to verify the matching condition (
11.30
). The very definition of
\mathbf{A}
, the identity (
11.21
) and the condition in RHP
6.10
–(3) when combined yield

\mathbf{P}(z)=\ee^{-\mathsf{g}(\infty)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{H}(z)\ee^{(\mathsf{E}(z)+2\mathsf{g}_{0}(z)\pm\pi\mathrm{i})\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathbf{U}_{0}^{-1}\left(\varphi(z)-\varphi({z_{N}(s)})\right)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}N^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/6}\\
\times\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}\left(N^{-2/3}\right)\right)N^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/6}\varphi(z)^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\ee^{-(\mathsf{E}(z)\pm\pi\mathrm{i})\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}

where the error is uniform for
s,t
as in Assumptions
2.7
and
z\in\partial U_{\mathsf{a}}
. The terms
\mathbf{U}_{0}
and
(\varphi(z)-\varphi({z_{N}(s)}))
remain bounded as
N\to\infty
and
z\in\partial U_{\mathsf{a}}
, so they commute with the error term above, simplying it to

\mathbf{P}(z)=\ee^{-\mathsf{g}(\infty)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{H}(z)\ee^{(\mathsf{E}(z)+2\mathsf{g}_{0}(z)\pm\pi\mathrm{i})\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}\left(N^{-1/3}\right)\right)\\
\times\mathbf{U}_{0}^{-1}\left(1-\frac{\varphi({z_{N}(s)})}{\varphi(z)}\right)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\ee^{-(\mathsf{E}(z)\pm\pi\mathrm{i})\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}

Using that
{z_{N}(s)}=\mathsf{a}+\mathcal{O}(s/N)
and the fact that
\varphi
is continuous and independent of
s,t
, we estimate
\varphi({z_{N}(s)})/\varphi(z)=\mathcal{O}(s/N)=\mathcal{O}(-N^{1/2})
, and this last estimate simplifies to

\mathbf{P}(z)=\ee^{-\mathsf{g}(\infty)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{H}(z)\ee^{\mathsf{g}_{0}(z)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(N^{-1/3})\right).

The proof is finally completed observing that
\mathbf{H}
,
\mathsf{g}_{0}(z)
and
\mathsf{g}(\infty)
are all bounded, and using (
11.17
).
∎

11.6.
Construction of the local parametrix near
z=\mathsf{b}

As it was also the case for
z=\mathsf{b}
, near
z=\mathsf{a}
the jumps of
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{S}}
do not convergence uniformly to the identity and a local parametrix is needed. This jump matrix
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{S}}
still involves the factor
\sigma
deforming the original weight of orthogonality. But unlike near
z=\mathsf{a}
, this factor
\sigma
becomes nonvanishing in a fixed neighborhood of
z=\mathsf{b}
, so we can remove this factor with simple considerations, and the local parametrix can then be constructed using Airy functions in a canonical way.

We only describe the outcome.

The bare Airy parametrix
{\mathbf{Ai}}_{0}
is given in (
C.1
), and it is used to construct the solution
{\mathbf{Ai}}
to the classical Airy RHP, namely RHP
C.1
. We need to deform the RHP for
{\mathbf{Ai}}
, in a similar way as we have already done in (
7.32
). Define

(11.31)

\widetilde{{\mathbf{Ai}}}(\xi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}{\mathbf{Ai}}_{0}(\xi)\times\begin{cases}\mathbf{I},&0<\arg\xi<\frac{\pi}{2},\\
(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{E}_{21}),&\frac{\pi}{2}<\arg\xi<\pi,\\
(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{E}_{12})(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21}),&-\pi<\xi<-\frac{\pi}{2},\\
(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{E}_{12}),&-\frac{\pi}{2}<\xi<0.\\
\end{cases}

Then
\widetilde{{\mathbf{Ai}}}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus(\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}\cup\mathbb{R})
, and its jumps are given by

\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{12}\text{ on }(0,+\infty),\quad\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21}\text{ on }\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{E}_{12}-\mathbf{E}_{21}\text{ on }(-\infty,0).

In the above, we assume that
(0,\pm\mathrm{i}\infty)
are oriented from
0
towards
\infty
, and the subintervals of
\mathbb{R}
inherit the natural orientation from
\mathbb{R}
. Furthermore

\widetilde{\mathbf{Ai}}(\xi)=\xi^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(\xi^{-3/2})\right)\ee^{-\frac{2}{3}\xi^{3/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}},\quad\xi\to\infty.

Set

\widetilde{\varphi}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\left(\frac{3}{2}\phi(z)\right)^{2/3},

which is a conformal map near
z=\mathsf{b}
. Recalling that
\mathsf{g}_{0}
was introduced in (
11.8
) and that
\mathbf{H}
was given in (
11.4
), introduce

\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{G}(z)\sigma(z)^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\ee^{\mathsf{E}(z)\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathbf{U}_{0}^{-1}(N^{2/3}\widetilde{\varphi}(z))^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}.

A direct calculation, using (
11.11
) and RHP
11.8
, shows that
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}
is analytic near
z=\mathsf{b}
. Finally, fix a small neighborhood
U_{\mathsf{b}}
of
\mathsf{b}
and set

(11.32)

{\mathbf{P}}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(z)\widetilde{\mathbf{Ai}}(\xi)\sigma(z)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\ee^{-\mathsf{E}(z)\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\ee^{-N\phi(z)\bm{\sigma}_{3}},\quad z\in U_{\mathsf{b}}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}},\quad\xi\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=N^{2/3}\widetilde{\varphi}(z).

Then
{\mathbf{P}}
is analytic on
z\in U_{\mathsf{b}}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}
, and its jumps along
U_{\mathsf{b}}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}
are given precisely by
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{S}}
. Furthermore, it satisfies the matching condition

(11.33)

{\mathbf{P}}(z)=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})\right)\mathbf{G}(z),\quad z\in\partial U_{\mathsf{b}},\quad N\to\infty.

11.7.
Last step and small norm theory

In summary, the global parametrix
\mathbf{G}
was constructed in Section
11.4
, culminating in the expression (
11.16
), the local parametrix
\mathbf{P}
on a neighborhood
U_{\mathsf{a}}
of
\mathsf{a}
was constructed in Section
11.5
, resulting in the expression (
11.29
), and the local parametrix
\mathbf{P}
on a neighborhood
U_{\mathsf{b}}
of
\mathsf{b}
was constructed in Section
11.6
, resulting in the expression (
11.32
).

The last transformation
\mathbf{S}\mapsto\mathbf{R}
involves the functions just mentioned, and it is given by

(11.34)

\mathbf{R}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{S}(z)\mathbf{P}(z)^{-1},\quad z\in U_{\mathsf{a}}\cup U_{\mathsf{b}}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}},\\
\mathbf{S}(z)\mathbf{G}(z)^{-1},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\left(\overline{U}_{\mathsf{a}}\cup\overline{U}_{\mathsf{b}}\cup\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}\right).\\
\end{cases}

Because the jumps of
\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{G}
coincide on
(0,\mathsf{b})
, it follows that
\mathbf{R}
is analytic across this interval. Likewise, the jumps of
\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{P}
inside
U_{\mathsf{a}}
and
U_{\mathsf{b}}
coincide, so
\mathbf{R}
has no jumps there as well. For these considerations and for what follows, we make sure that
N
is chosen sufficiently large, in such a way that
{z_{N}(s)}\in U_{\mathsf{a}}
; thanks to (
10.6
) and Assumptions
2.7
, we can always do so uniformly in
s
, for a neighborhood
U_{\mathsf{a}}
of
\mathsf{a}
which is independent of
s
.

In particular, we see that
\mathbf{R}
has no jumps near
\mathsf{a}
,
\mathsf{b}
and
{z_{N}(s)}
. Since
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{S}
remain bounded as
z\to\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}
, naturally
\mathbf{R}
too remains bounded near these points. Even though
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{S}
may blow up as
z\to{z_{N}(s)}
, we do know that these two functions remain bounded as
z\to{z_{N}(s)}
along the real axis (see RHP
11.5
and RHP
11.12
–(4)). Therefore,
\mathbf{R}
has a removable singularity at
z={z_{N}(s)}
.

Set

\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}\cup\partial U_{\mathsf{a}}\cup\partial U_{\mathsf{b}})\setminus([0,\mathsf{b}]\cup U_{\mathsf{a}}\cup U_{\mathsf{b}}).

Summarizing, it follows that
\mathbf{R}
solves the following RHP.

RHP 11.19
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{R}
with the following properties.

(1)

\mathbf{R}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}}
.

(2)

The matrix
\mathbf{R}
has continous boundary values
\mathbf{R}_{\pm}
along
\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}}
, satisfying the relation
\mathbf{R}_{+}(z)=\mathbf{R}_{-}(z)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{R}}(z)
,
z\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}}
, with

(11.35)

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{R}}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{P}(z)\mathbf{G}(z)^{-1},&z\in\partial U_{\mathsf{a}}\cup\partial U_{\mathsf{b}},\\
\mathbf{G}(z)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{S}}(z)\mathbf{G}(z)^{-1},&z\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}}\setminus(U_{\mathsf{a}}\cup\partial U_{\mathsf{b}}).\end{cases}

(3)

As
z\to\infty
,

\mathbf{R}(z)=\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}).

To conclude the asymptotic analysis, we now obtain estimates for
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{R}}
.

Proposition 11.20
.

There exists
\eta>0
such that

\|\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{R}}-\mathbf{I}\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}}\setminus(\partial U_{\mathsf{a}}\cup\partial U_{\mathsf{b}}))}=\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta n}),\quad n\to\infty,

as well as

\|\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{R}}-\mathbf{I}\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\partial U_{\mathsf{a}})}=\mathcal{O}(N^{-1/2}),\quad\|\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{R}}-\mathbf{I}\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\partial U_{\mathsf{b}})}=\mathcal{O}(N^{-1}),\quad N\to\infty,

all valid uniformly for
s
within any of the regimes from Assumptions
2.7
.

Proof.

The estimates on
\Gamma_{\mathbf{G}}\setminus(\partial U_{\mathsf{a}}\cup U_{\mathsf{b}})
follow from Proposition
11.7
and the fact that
\mathbf{G}
remains bounded as
n\to\infty
, uniformly for
z
in closed sets of
\mathbb{C}\setminus[a,b]
. The estimates on
\partial U_{\mathsf{b}}
follow from (
11.33
). The estimates on
\partial U_{\mathsf{a}}
follow from (
11.30
).
∎

As a result, we obtain.

Theorem 11.21
.

As
N\to\infty
, the matrix
\mathbf{R}
satisfies the estimates

\|\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{I}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}})}=\mathcal{O}(N^{-1/3}),\quad\|\mathbf{R}_{\pm}-\mathbf{I}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}})}=\mathcal{O}(N^{-1/3}),

as well as

\left\|\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}})}=\mathcal{O}(N^{-1/3}),\quad\|\mathbf{R}_{\pm}^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}})}=\mathcal{O}(N^{-1/3}),\quad\text{with}\quad^{\prime}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z},

uniformly for
s
within any of the regimes from Assumptions
2.7
.

Theorem
11.21
concludes the steepest descent analysis, and now we move to collecting its main consequences.

12.
The multiplicative statistics in terms of the Riemann-Hilbert Problem

With the asymptotic analysis of the orthogonal polynomials completed, in this section we unwrap all the transformations
\mathbf{X}\mapsto\cdots\mapsto\mathbf{R}
, in order to express the quotient
\mathsf{Z}_{N}^{\sigma}(s)/\mathsf{Z}^{\sigma}_{N}(S)
in terms of the error matrix
\mathbf{R}
and the model problem
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\tau}
, amongst other quantities. Later, in the next section, we then use such expression to analyze
\mathsf{Z}_{N}^{\sigma}(s)/\mathsf{Z}^{\sigma}_{N}(S)
asymptotically as
N\to\infty
.

Fix a small value
\delta>0
, and let
s,S
be values satisfying

(12.1)

-\delta N^{1/2}\leq s\leq S\leq\delta N^{1/2}.

This way, we are ensured that in the integration in (
10.5
) over a dummy variable
u\in[s,S]
, the whole interval of integration
[s,S]
lies within the regimes appearing in Assumptions
2.7
